# A Home or an Asset? A World History of Housing Ideologies

Oct 7, 2026 · Miguel Puche

## Introduction

A house is two things at once. It is a place to live, as basic a need as food or clothing, and it is usually the most valuable asset a family will ever own. No other object in ordinary life carries both roles so heavily. Bread is a necessity, but nobody saves for retirement in bread. Shares are an investment, but nobody sleeps in them. A home is both, and almost every disagreement about housing comes from which of these two natures a person treats as primary.

Those who see the asset first tend to want a free market in which homes are bought, sold and invested in like shares or gold. Rising prices are then a sign of success and the reward for prudent saving, and the proper task of government is to protect property and stay out of the way. Those who see the need first tend to want housing shielded from that market. For them a rising price is a rising barrier, access to a decent home is a right of citizenship, and the proper task of government is to guarantee it, whether by building, regulating or owning.

Put so simply, the argument sounds like a contest between right and left, and it is often conducted as one. The history told in this book suggests something more tangled. The most complete system of state housing in the capitalist world was built by the pro-business government of Singapore. The highest rates of private home ownership on earth are found in countries that spent forty years under communism. The tax that economists of the free market school admire most was devised by a nineteenth century radical who wanted to abolish private income from land. Rent control was introduced across Europe by wartime governments of every colour, and the zoning rules that libertarians blame for high prices are defended most fiercely by conservative homeowners. Anyone who expects housing ideologies to line up neatly will be confused by the evidence within a few pages.

The confusion clears a little once the question is divided into three. The first part of it concerns purpose. What is a house for? It can be regarded as an investment, as the material basis of family and community, or as a basic need. Each answer implies a different measure of success. An investor asks whether values are rising, a conservative whether families are settled and independent, a social reformer whether anyone is badly housed.

The second part concerns land. A house is a building standing on a piece of ground, and the two behave differently. Buildings can be produced in any quantity, wear out with time and cost roughly the same to construct in one town as in another. Land in a particular place cannot be produced at all, does not wear out, and varies in value by a factor of thousands depending on what surrounds it. When a city grows and a plot becomes valuable through no effort of its owner, somebody receives that gain. Whether it should be the owner, the state or the community at large is perhaps the oldest question in political economy, and a great deal of housing policy that is not presented as being about land turns out to depend on the answer.

The third part concerns allocation. Homes have to be distributed among households somehow. One method is price, which gives each dwelling to whoever will pay most for it. The alternatives are rules, queues, lotteries, need assessed by officials, membership of a cooperative or a workplace, or inheritance and custom. Those who trust prices tend to fear the arbitrary power of officials. Those who trust public institutions tend to fear the private power of landlords. Each fear has ample historical justification.

This book follows these three questions across the world and through some four thousand years. It tries to explain each tradition as its own supporters understood it, to describe what happened when it was put into practice, and to set out the objections its critics have raised. It is not a work of advocacy. The reader will find that every approach has produced at least one striking success and at least one disaster, and that the same policy has had opposite results in different times and places. The aim is to equip the reader to recognise the arguments when they reappear, as they do in every generation, and to judge them with some knowledge of where they have led before.

A word on evidence. The book covers a very wide range of periods and countries, and it draws on the general historical and economic literature. Dates, quantities and proportions are given to convey scale and should be treated as approximate. Where scholars disagree about what happened or why, the text tries to say so.

## How the book is organised

The book has ten parts of three chapters each. The first two parts are historical and set the scene. Part One describes the long ages in which land was not a commodity in the modern sense: the cities of the ancient world, the systems of tenure and obligation that governed land in medieval Europe, the Islamic lands, China, Japan, India, Africa and the Americas, and the process of enclosure and colonisation by which land was turned into property that could be freely bought and sold. Part Two covers the liberal century that followed, with the philosophers who justified and questioned property in land, the industrial city that tested their ideas, and the first attempts at reform through sanitation law, philanthropy, cooperation and the garden city.

The next five parts take the main modern traditions in turn. Part Three examines the market liberal and libertarian view that housing is a commodity best supplied by free exchange. Part Four follows the transformation of the home into a financial product, from the invention of the long mortgage to the global crisis of 2008 and the rise and fall of the Chinese property sector. Part Five treats the conservative ideal of a property-owning democracy in Britain, the United States and the Catholic and authoritarian traditions of continental Europe. Part Six describes the social democratic alternative of public and non-profit housing within a regulated market, from Red Vienna to the Nordic countries and the rise and decline of council housing. Part Seven turns to the socialist attempt to remove housing from the market altogether, in the Soviet Union, Eastern Europe, China and Cuba, and to its present day descendants in cooperatives and community land trusts.

Part Eight is devoted to the land question itself, which cuts across the other traditions: the ideas of Henry George, the long political struggle over the taxation of land values, and the cities that have kept their land in public ownership. Part Nine looks beyond Europe and North America, to the developmental states of East Asia, to Latin America with its doctrine of the social function of property, and to Africa, India and the Middle East, where most housing is built by its occupants outside the law. Part Ten returns to the present. It considers housing as a legal right, surveys the issues over which the traditions now collide, from rent control to tourist lets, and ends with Spain as a country in which nearly all of them have left their mark, followed by a general conclusion.

The chapters can be read in order, but each is intended to make sense on its own.

## Part One: Land Before the Market

### Chapter 1: The First Cities

It is tempting to suppose that the buying and selling of houses is a modern habit and that the ancient world knew only custom and command. The surviving records say otherwise. As soon as there were cities there were house sales, rents, mortgages, landlords, building rules and disputes between neighbours. What the ancient world lacked was not a property market but the belief that the market should be the main principle by which land is distributed. Alongside the contracts stood temples and palaces that held great estates, kin groups that could not be dispossessed, and rulers who from time to time cancelled the whole accumulated structure of debt. The tension between treating a house as merchandise and treating it as a family's place in the world is as old as the written record.

#### Mesopotamia, Egypt and ancient Israel

The cities of southern Mesopotamia, in what is now Iraq, have left us the oldest housing documents in existence. From the early second millennium before the common era, in cities such as Ur, Nippur and Sippar, thousands of clay tablets record the sale, lease, inheritance and division of houses. A typical sale tablet gives the area of the house in a standard measure, names the neighbours on each side, states the price in silver, records that the seller has been paid in full and will raise no future claim, and lists the witnesses. Rental agreements set an annual rent and sometimes oblige the tenant to repair the roof, which in a land of mud brick and flat roofs needed replastering every year. Families divided houses among heirs room by room, and archaeologists excavating residential quarters at Ur have traced doorways blocked up and reopened as shares were split and recombined across generations.

These transactions took place within an economy dominated by large institutions. The temple of a city's god and the palace of its king held extensive lands, employed great numbers of people and housed many of them. Private property in houses was real, but it was one form of holding among several. The law took a close interest in building. The collection of legal rulings issued under Hammurabi of Babylon in the eighteenth century before the common era contains a group of clauses on the liability of builders. If a house collapses and kills its owner, the builder is to be put to death. If it kills the owner's son, the builder's son is to die. If it destroys goods, the builder must replace them and rebuild at his own expense. The principle is harsher than any modern building code, but its purpose is the same: the person who profits from construction must answer for its safety.

Mesopotamian rulers also practised something with no real modern equivalent. On coming to the throne, and at intervals afterwards, kings of the Old Babylonian period proclaimed edicts that cancelled certain debts, freed people who had been bound into servitude for debt and returned land and houses that had been surrendered to creditors. The edicts applied to debts contracted out of need, not to commercial partnerships between merchants. Their logic was partly religious, since the king presented himself as a restorer of justice, and partly practical. A population of free householders owed labour and military service to the crown. If their land and persons passed into the hands of creditors, the king lost his subjects to private magnates. The clean slate was a way of preventing property from concentrating beyond a certain point, and it expressed a conviction that a family's hold on its home ranked above a lender's claim.

Egypt presents a different pattern. In principle the whole land belonged to the pharaoh, and in practice much of it was held by temples and administered by officials, although private sales of houses and plots are recorded from an early date. The state itself was a great builder of housing for those who worked on its projects. The town now called Lahun was laid out around 1880 before the common era for the people who built and maintained a royal pyramid. It was planned on a grid, with rows of small identical houses for workers in one quarter and a few very large mansions for officials in another, separated by a wall. Several centuries later the craftsmen who cut and decorated the royal tombs near Thebes lived in a purpose built walled village at the place now known as Deir el-Medina. The state provided the houses, delivered rations of grain, fish and water, and the positions and dwellings passed from father to son. When rations were late, in the reign of Ramesses III, the workmen laid down their tools and marched out in what is often described as the first recorded strike. Housing supplied by an employer, with all the security and dependence that implies, is among the oldest arrangements there is.

The Hebrew scriptures preserve the clearest ancient statement of the opposing view, that land is not merchandise at all. The book of Leviticus has God declare that the land must not be sold permanently, because it belongs to him and the Israelites are only strangers and tenants upon it. Land could be transferred to meet a debt, but a kinsman had the right to redeem it, and in the fiftieth year, the Jubilee, every holding was to return to the family to which it had originally been allotted. A sale of farmland was therefore, in effect, a lease for the years remaining until the next Jubilee, and the price was to be reckoned accordingly. Scholars doubt that the Jubilee was ever fully observed, but the ideal was influential, and it has been invoked by land reformers from the English radicals of the seventeenth century to modern campaigners for debt relief.

Two details of the biblical law are worth noticing. The first is a story. In the first book of Kings, King Ahab asks a man named Naboth to sell or exchange a vineyard that lies beside the palace. Naboth refuses, saying that God forbids him to give up the inheritance of his fathers. The queen has him falsely accused and killed, the king takes the vineyard, and the prophet Elijah pronounces doom on the royal house. The story assumes that even a king cannot compel the sale of ancestral land, and that to engineer it is a crime crying out for punishment. The second detail is an exception. Leviticus provides that a dwelling house inside a walled city may be redeemed only within one year of its sale. After that it belongs permanently to the buyer and does not return in the Jubilee. The law thus distinguishes farmland, which is a family's livelihood and must not be lost, from urban houses, which may circulate freely. Three thousand years ago legislators had already noticed that property in cities behaves more like a commodity than property in the countryside, and had decided to let it.

#### Greece: the household and the citizen's land

In the Greek city states the house was bound up with citizenship. The basic unit of society was the oikos, a word that means at once the house, the household and the family estate, and from which the word economy is derived. A citizen was, almost by definition, a man who headed such a household and held land in the territory of the city. To lose the land was to risk losing one's standing, and the political history of the Greek cities is in large part a history of struggles over debt and the distribution of land.

Athens offers the best documented case. At the start of the sixth century before the common era the city was close to civil war. Poor farmers had fallen into debt to the rich, pledging their land and ultimately their own bodies, and many had been sold into slavery. In about 594 the Athenians gave a respected citizen named Solon the power to settle the crisis. His measures became known as the shaking off of burdens. He cancelled existing debts, forbade loans secured on the person of the borrower, brought back Athenians who had been sold abroad, and pulled up the stone markers that had been planted on encumbered fields to record the creditor's claim. Solon boasted in his own verses that the earth, formerly enslaved, was now free. He refused, however, the demand of the poor for a redistribution of land. His settlement protected the small owner against losing what he had without giving land to those who had none, and that compromise between security and equality was to be repeated many times in later history.

Classical Athens developed an active property market within firm political limits. Houses and land were sold, leased and offered as security for loans, and the boundary stones reappeared, inscribed with the name of the lender and the amount of the debt, as a public register of mortgages. But the right to own land and houses in Athenian territory was restricted to citizens. The many resident foreigners, known as metics, who made up much of the commercial and artisan population, could not buy a home unless the assembly voted them the privilege as a special honour. They rented, and a class of buildings arose to accommodate them. The Athenians called such a building a synoikia, a shared house, divided into rooms or apartments let to different tenants, and wealthy citizens held them as investments. The orators mention estates composed partly of lodging houses, and one speaker observes that land lying idle yields nothing while a house can always be let.

The restriction troubled some observers. The writer Xenophon, in a pamphlet on how Athens might improve its revenues, proposed that metics of good standing should be allowed to build and own houses on the vacant plots that lay within the city walls. More foreigners would settle, he argued, and the city would grow richer. It is perhaps the earliest surviving proposal to solve a housing question by relaxing the rules on who may own and build, and it was not adopted. Citizens regarded the land of the city as the collective patrimony of the citizen body, and they were not prepared to admit outsiders to it for the sake of economic growth.

Sparta stood at the opposite extreme, at least in its own legend. The Spartans attributed to their lawgiver Lycurgus a division of the land into equal lots, one for each citizen, which could not be sold and which supported the citizen while he devoted himself to military training. The reality was less tidy. Lots passed by inheritance and could be given or bequeathed, wealth concentrated over the generations, and since a Spartan who could not pay his contribution to the common mess lost his full citizenship, the number of citizens fell from several thousand to a few hundred. In the third century two reforming kings, Agis and Cleomenes, attempted to cancel debts and redistribute the land in order to restore the citizen body. The first was executed and the second was driven into exile. Their story became, for later writers, a parable of the fate of land reformers.

The philosophers drew general lessons from these experiences. Plato, in the Republic, denied private property of any kind to the guardian class that would rule his ideal city, on the grounds that owners of houses and land would pursue their private interests against the common good. In his later and more practical work, the Laws, he designed a city of exactly 5,040 households. Each would hold an inalienable lot, consisting of two parcels, one near the city and one near the frontier, with a house on each. The lots could not be sold, divided or combined, and no citizen would be allowed to accumulate movable wealth beyond four times the value of his lot. Plato understood the lot as held in trust from the city and from the gods, and he regarded the buying and selling of land as the root of civil strife.

Aristotle, who had been Plato's pupil, replied with what has remained the standard defence of private ownership. Property held in common, he observed, is neglected, because people take most care of what is their own and least of what they share with others. Common ownership also breeds quarrels, as those who work more resent those who work less and receive the same. Property should therefore be private. But Aristotle added that its use should be in some measure common, through generosity, friendship and public spirit, and he thought that the legislator should aim to prevent extremes of wealth and poverty, since a city with a large middle class of moderate owners is the most stable. He also drew a distinction that later moralists would rely upon, between acquiring goods in order to live well, which has a natural limit, and acquiring money for its own sake, which has none.

The Greeks also made a contribution of a more practical kind. From the fifth century new cities and rebuilt quarters were laid out on a regular grid, a method associated with the planner Hippodamus of Miletus, who designed the port of Athens at Piraeus. At Olynthus in northern Greece, excavated in the twentieth century, the residential district consists of uniform blocks, each divided into ten house plots of equal size. Whether or not the equal plots expressed a democratic ideal, as some scholars think, they made land easy to measure, allocate and transfer. The grid would return at Roman colonies, in the Spanish towns of the Americas, in the survey of the American West and in the street plan of Manhattan, and in each case it served the same purpose of turning ground into standard units that could be distributed or sold.

#### Rome: the apartment block and the landlord

Rome was the first city in history to reach a population of something like a million, and it housed most of those people in a way that anticipates the modern metropolis more closely than anything else in the ancient world. The rich lived in the domus, a house of one or two storeys arranged around internal courts. Everyone else lived in the insula, a block of rented apartments rising to four, five or more floors, with shops on the street front and progressively poorer tenants on each level above. A catalogue of the city compiled in the fourth century counts fewer than two thousand private houses against more than forty thousand apartment blocks. Rome was a city of tenants.

The insulae were built as investments. A wealthy owner, often a senator, would put up or buy a block and lease the whole of it to a contractor for a fixed sum. The contractor then sublet the apartments and rooms and kept the difference, and an agent, frequently a slave or freedman of the owner, collected the rents. The owner thus enjoyed a steady income without dealing with tenants, the middleman had every incentive to squeeze them, and responsibility for the condition of the building was divided among several parties. The upper floors were commonly built of timber and of a cheap construction of rubble and wattle that the architect Vitruvius condemned as a standing invitation to fire. There was no running water above ground level, and cooking was done on open braziers.

The literature of the period is full of the consequences. The satirist Juvenal has a character explain why he is leaving the city: one lives in a building propped up with slender poles, the agent papers over the cracks and tells the tenants to sleep soundly, and the man on the top floor, with nothing between him and the rain but the roof tiles, is the last to learn that the stairs below are burning. The poet Martial describes his own climb of three flights and the annual upheaval of the first of July, when leases ended and the streets filled with the carts of families moving their few possessions. Cicero, who drew a good part of his income from urban property, wrote to his friend Atticus that two of his shops had fallen down and the rest were cracking, so that not only the tenants but even the mice had moved out. He added cheerfully that he had a plan to turn the loss to profit through rebuilding.

Fire and collapse were also opportunities. Plutarch reports that Marcus Crassus, the richest Roman of his day, kept a force of several hundred slaves trained as builders and firefighters. When a building caught fire he would arrive and offer to buy it, together with the neighbouring properties that were threatened, at a small fraction of their value. If the owners accepted, his men would put out the flames, and he would rebuild on the site. In this way, Plutarch says, the greater part of Rome came into his hands. The story may be exaggerated, but the Romans who repeated it clearly found it plausible that a fortune could be made from the dangers of the housing market.

Roman law gave the tenant little protection. A lease was a contract like any other, and its terms were whatever the parties agreed, which in practice meant whatever the landlord offered. The landlord had an automatic claim over the furniture and goods the tenant had brought into the dwelling as security for the rent, and he could shut the tenant out and hold the goods if payment was late. A tenant could be turned out if the owner wished to use or rebuild the property. The jurists did allow a tenant to leave without penalty, or to deduct from the rent, if the dwelling became uninhabitable, for instance if a neighbour's new building blocked the light or the landlord failed to repair doors and windows. But there was no control of rents and no security of tenure in the modern sense.

Rents became a political issue at moments of crisis. During the civil war of the early forties before the common era, credit collapsed and many tenants could not pay. A magistrate named Caelius Rufus, bidding for popular support, proposed a year's remission of rent and was driven from office. Julius Caesar, more cautiously, then granted exactly such a remission for one year, limited to dwellings below a set annual rent, with a higher threshold in Rome than in the rest of Italy. It was a temporary measure and was not repeated as policy, but it shows that the idea of government intervening between landlord and tenant in an emergency was already available.

The emperors preferred to regulate buildings instead of rents. Augustus limited the height of new structures facing public streets to about seventy Roman feet, roughly twenty metres, and organised a permanent force of watchmen to fight fires. After the great fire of the year 64, which destroyed a large part of the city, Nero issued a code for the rebuilding. Streets were to be broad and regular, buildings were to be of limited height and to stand free of one another without shared walls, a proportion of each was to be of fireproof stone, and porches were to be added to the fronts of the apartment blocks so that fires could be fought from their roofs. Trajan later reduced the height limit further. The handsome brick blocks that survive at the port of Ostia, with their regular windows and internal courtyards, show what a well built insula of the following century looked like, and they would not seem out of place in a modern Italian town.

Rome's rulers accepted responsibility for feeding the urban population, through the distribution of free or cheap grain to a list of some two hundred thousand citizens, and for entertaining it and supplying it with water and baths. They did not accept responsibility for housing it. That remained a private matter between owner and occupier, subject to rules about safety. The attempt to do something more fundamental about property had been made earlier and had failed. In 133 before the common era the tribune Tiberius Gracchus proposed to enforce an old limit on the amount of public land any individual could occupy and to distribute the surplus in small farms to landless citizens, many of whom had drifted to the city. He was killed by a group of senators, and his brother Gaius, who revived and extended the programme ten years later, met the same end. The land question in the countryside went unresolved, the dispossessed continued to crowd into Rome, and the insula was the result.

#### Roman law and its legacy

The most durable thing Rome left to the history of housing was not a building but a set of legal ideas. Roman jurists developed a conception of ownership more absolute than anything known before. The owner of a thing, in their analysis, had the right to use it, to take its fruits and to dispose of it, including by neglecting or destroying it, and this bundle of powers was in principle exclusive, perpetual and good against the whole world. Other legal systems thought of land in terms of layered and overlapping claims held by lords, kin and communities. Roman law began from a single owner with full control and treated every other interest as a limited deduction from it.

The jurists then worked out a vocabulary of such limited interests, and it is still in use. A usufruct gave a person the right to occupy a property and take its income for life without owning it, a device widely used to provide for widows. A servitude bound one piece of land for the benefit of another, as when an owner was forbidden to build above a certain height so that his neighbour kept his light, or was obliged to let a neighbour's rainwater drain across his plot. A pledge of land secured a debt. A long lease of the kind called emphyteusis gave the holder, in exchange for an annual payment, a right so durable and transferable that it approached ownership, and it was much used by cities, temples and later the Church to obtain income from land they were forbidden or unwilling to sell.

One rule deserves particular attention because of its bearing on the modern argument about land. The Romans held that whatever is built on land becomes part of the land and belongs to its owner. A man who built a house on another's plot did not own the house. The principle expressed the superior status of land over anything that human effort placed upon it. To meet the practical need for building on land one did not own, the law developed a separate right called superficies, under which a person could hold, inherit and transfer a building standing on someone else's ground in return for a ground rent. Here, in embryo, is the separation between ownership of the site and ownership of the structure that reappears in the building leases of Georgian London, the ninety-nine year flats of Singapore and the community land trusts of the present day.

Roman law also recognised that the freedom of one owner ends where it damages another, and the later empire legislated in some detail on the point. A constitution of the emperor Zeno, issued for Constantinople in the late fifth century, laid down minimum distances between buildings, limits on projecting balconies and rules on height. It also protected a neighbour's existing view of the sea, which the inhabitants of the capital evidently prized, and Justinian in the following century closed a loophole by which builders had been evading the rule. An entitlement to a sea view, enforced by imperial law against a neighbour who wishes to build, is not very far from the disputes over development that fill the planning committees of modern coastal cities.

Under Justinian, between 529 and 534, the accumulated law was collected and edited into the great compilation later known as the body of civil law. It was largely forgotten in western Europe for five centuries. Then, at the end of the eleventh century, scholars at Bologna began to study and teach it, and its concepts spread through the universities to the courts and chanceries of the continent. When the states of Europe came to write modern codes of law in the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries, they turned to Roman categories. The definition of ownership in the French civil code of 1804 as the right to enjoy and dispose of things in the most absolute manner is a Roman idea in revolutionary dress, and through the French, German and Spanish codes it travelled to Latin America, Japan, Turkey and much of Africa and Asia.

The legacy was double edged. On one side, the Roman conception gave owners a clarity and security that made land easy to sell, mortgage and develop, and it armed the reformers who wished to sweep away the tangle of feudal and customary claims. On the other, it had little to say about the person who occupied a dwelling without owning it. The tenant in Roman law was merely a party to a contract, with no interest in the property itself, and legal systems built on Roman foundations long continued to regard him in that light. Much of the housing legislation of the twentieth century, with its rent tribunals and its protection from eviction, can be understood as an effort to give the occupier a standing that the Roman tradition had denied.

It is worth pausing over what the ancient city had already shown. Private ownership and a free market in urban property had housed an enormous population, with a vigour that no ancient government could have matched, and had done so at a quality that horrified contemporaries. The remedy of the authorities was to regulate construction and, in emergencies, to suspend the rights of creditors and landlords. More radical proposals, to limit holdings or redistribute land, had been advanced by philosophers and reformers and defeated by those with property to lose. Religion and custom meanwhile preserved the conviction that a family's home and land were more than merchandise. Every element of the later debate was present. What would change, over the following two thousand years, was the balance among them.

### Chapter 2: Tenure and Obligation

For roughly a thousand years after the fall of the western Roman empire, and for much longer in most of the world, land was held on terms quite different from the absolute ownership of the Roman jurists. A person's right to a house and a field was typically one claim among several on the same ground. It was acquired by birth, by service or by allocation more often than by purchase, and it carried duties to a lord, a kin group, a religious foundation or a ruler. This chapter surveys the main forms such arrangements took in Europe, the Islamic lands, East Asia and the societies of India, Africa, the Americas and the Pacific. The variety is great, but one feature recurs: almost nowhere was land regarded as simply a thing to be bought and sold.

#### Feudal Europe: the manor, the commons and the town

The organising idea of land law in medieval western Europe was tenure, from the Latin word for holding. Nobody but the king owned land outright. Everyone else held it of someone above them, in exchange for something. After the Norman conquest of England in 1066 the principle was applied with unusual thoroughness. William claimed the whole kingdom by right of conquest and granted estates to his followers in return for a fixed quota of knights. They in turn granted portions to their own men on similar terms, and so on downwards. The great survey of 1086 known as Domesday Book recorded, manor by manor, who held what and from whom. English law preserves the theory to this day, since the most complete form of ownership it recognises is still technically an estate held of the Crown.

At the base of this structure was the manor, and within it the peasant household. A villager who was unfree, as most were in the twelfth and thirteenth centuries, held his cottage and his strips in the open fields from the lord in return for labour on the lord's own land, payments in kind and a series of fines on occasions such as marriage and inheritance. He could not leave, and he could not sell. Yet his position was more secure than this suggests. His holding passed to his heir on payment of a customary fine, and the terms on which he held it were fixed by the custom of the manor, recorded in the rolls of the manorial court and binding on the lord as well as the tenant. Later lawyers described such a man as holding at the will of the lord according to the custom of the manor, a phrase in which the second half took much of the force out of the first.

The holding included more than the house and the strips. It carried rights over the land that the village used in common: the right to graze a set number of animals on the pasture and on the stubble after harvest, to gather fallen wood for fuel and timber for repairs, to cut turf or peat, to turn pigs into the woods to feed on acorns. For the poorest villagers, who held a cottage and little or no arable land, these rights could be the difference between subsistence and destitution. They belonged to the dwelling, not to the person, so that whoever occupied a particular cottage enjoyed its rights of common. In England the Charter of the Forest, issued in 1217 as a companion to Magna Carta, confirmed such rights against royal encroachment, and it remained on the statute book for more than seven centuries.

The feudal order therefore did not lack property so much as divide it. The king, the lord and the peasant each had a recognised interest in the same acre, and none could dispose of it without regard to the others. A system of this kind was poorly suited to a market. An important step towards one was taken in England in 1290, when a statute known from its opening words as Quia Emptores allowed any free tenant to sell his land without his lord's consent, the buyer simply stepping into the seller's place. Freehold land thereby became freely transferable among the living, three centuries before most of the continent, and historians have seen in the measure one root of the early development of an English land market.

The towns were islands of a different law. Kings and lords who founded towns, or granted charters to existing ones, wished to attract settlers, and they offered plots on terms far freer than those of the countryside. Under burgage tenure, as it was called in England, a townsman held his plot for a fixed money rent, owed no labour services, and could sell, mortgage or bequeath it as he pleased. The long narrow burgage plot, with a short frontage on the market street and a deep yard behind, still determines the layout of many European town centres. In Germany a saying held that town air makes a person free, and it became a rule in many places that a serf who lived in a town for a year and a day without being reclaimed could no longer be taken back. The medieval town was thus the first place in Europe where an ordinary person might own a house in something like the modern sense.

Urban property quickly became an instrument of finance as well as a place to live. The Church condemned lending money at interest, but it permitted a transaction in which a person with capital bought from a householder the right to receive a fixed annual payment secured on the house. To the householder it was a way of raising money, and to the buyer it was a safe income. In the towns of Germany, the Low Countries and northern France such rent charges were created, sold, inherited and donated to religious houses in great numbers, until many houses carried several layers of them and the holders of the charges, not the occupants, enjoyed most of the value. Municipal governments eventually legislated to make the charges redeemable, so that owners could buy themselves free. The medieval city had invented something very like the mortgage backed security, and had discovered the difficulty of a housing stock weighed down by perpetual claims.

Religion also supplied the main alternative to the market for those who could not house themselves. Monasteries, guilds and wealthy individuals founded almshouses and hospitals, in the older sense of places of shelter, where the aged poor were lodged free in return for prayers for the founder's soul. Some of these foundations, such as the Hospital of St Cross at Winchester, established in the twelfth century, still fulfil their purpose. In the towns of the Low Countries, communities of lay religious women known as beguines lived in enclosed courts of small houses, supporting themselves by their work. The best known foundation of the kind is the Fuggerei at Augsburg, built from 1516 by the banker Jakob Fugger for respectable Catholic citizens of the town who had fallen into need. It consists of several dozen small terraced houses within its own walls, and its residents still pay an annual rent equal to one Rhenish guilder, now less than one euro, and undertake to say three prayers a day for the Fugger family. Housing the poor was understood as a work of charity that benefited the giver's soul. It created no right in the recipient, but it established a tradition of housing held permanently outside the market, from which the non-profit housing associations of modern Europe are in part descended.

#### The Islamic world: revived land and the endowment

Islamic law took shape in the cities of Arabia, Syria and Iraq in the first centuries after the death of the Prophet in 632, and it developed a body of property rules that governed housing from Spain to India for more than a millennium. Its starting point was theological. The earth belongs to God, and human beings hold it as stewards. Within that framework the jurists recognised private ownership fully, protected it strongly and surrounded it with obligations to neighbours, kin and the community of believers.

The law distinguished several categories of land. Property in full private ownership could be sold, given, inherited and built upon at will, and it included most houses, shops and gardens in towns. Agricultural land in the conquered territories was generally treated differently. The ultimate title was held by the state on behalf of the Muslim community, and cultivators had a heritable right to use it in return for tax. A third category was dead land, meaning ground that was unowned and uncultivated. A saying attributed to the Prophet held that whoever brings dead land to life becomes its owner. A person who cleared, irrigated or built upon waste ground thereby acquired it, though the schools of law differed on whether the ruler's permission was needed. The doctrine anticipates by a thousand years the argument of John Locke that property arises from mixing one's labour with the soil, and it remains relevant today in debates over the status of informal settlements built on vacant land at the edges of cities in the Muslim world.

The most distinctive institution was the endowment, known in Arabic as the waqf. An owner could declare that a property was henceforth dedicated in perpetuity to a pious purpose. From that moment it could not be sold, given away, inherited or seized, and its income was to be applied forever to the purpose named. The founder appointed an administrator and laid down the rules of succession to the post. Endowments were of two broad kinds. Charitable endowments supported mosques, schools, hospitals, public fountains, bridges, soup kitchens and lodgings for travellers and the poor. Family endowments reserved the income for the founder's descendants, with a charitable purpose named only as the final beneficiary should the line die out. The family form allowed an owner to keep an estate intact, to protect it from confiscation by rulers, and to provide for relatives outside the fixed shares of the inheritance law.

The typical endowed asset was urban property. A founder who wished to establish a school would build it and then endow it with shops, baths, warehouses and houses whose rents would pay the teachers and maintain the building. Over the centuries the accumulation was immense. In the great cities of the Ottoman empire a very large share of all real estate, by some estimates a third or more, was held in endowments by the eighteenth century, and much of the public life of the city, from water supply to education, was financed from their rents instead of from taxation. For the ordinary townsman this meant that the landlord of his shop or dwelling was very often a pious foundation, charging a customary rent and legally incapable of selling the building from under him.

Perpetuity had costs, and they are instructive. A property that can never be sold cannot easily be adapted when circumstances change. Administrators lacked the means or the motive to repair ageing buildings, and after a fire or an earthquake an endowment might be left with a ruined plot and no capital to rebuild. The jurists responded with devices that preserved the letter of the law while admitting private investment. Under one arrangement, common in Egypt and Syria, a tenant took a very long lease of endowed land at a low ground rent and owned whatever he built on it. Under another, which spread in Istanbul after the great fires of the sixteenth and seventeenth centuries, the tenant paid a large sum at the outset, close to the value of the property, and a small annual rent thereafter, and received a right he could pass to his children. The endowment kept its nominal title, and the tenant acquired something close to ownership. The pattern, in which land is held permanently by a public or charitable body while the building is privately owned, was thus reinvented in the Islamic world to solve the problem its own law had created.

Islamic law also gave neighbours and co-owners a standing that Roman law had not. Under the right of pre-emption, when a share in a property or, according to some schools, an adjoining property was sold to an outsider, the co-owner or neighbour could step in and take the place of the buyer by paying the same price. The rule protected households from the intrusion of strangers and tended to keep property within the family and the quarter. Judges heard disputes about windows that overlooked a neighbour's courtyard, about doors opened onto a shared lane, and about walls that cut off light and air, applying a general maxim that there should be neither harm nor the returning of harm. The result was a city shaped less by plans imposed from above than by an accumulation of agreements and judgments among neighbours, with its winding lanes, blind alleys held in common by the houses that opened onto them, and inward facing courtyard dwellings.

This system met the European idea of property in the nineteenth century. The Ottoman Land Code of 1858 sought to bring order to rural holdings by requiring those who used state land to register their rights and receive a title document. The aim was to identify the taxpayer and encourage investment. In practice many peasants feared that registration would expose them to taxes and conscription, and allowed their land to be entered in the name of a local notable, a tribal chief or a city merchant. A reform designed to secure the cultivator thus converted customary users into tenants of absentee owners in parts of Syria, Palestine and Iraq, with consequences that are still felt. In the same period the endowments came under attack from reformers who saw them as a dead hand on the economy, and in the twentieth century the governments of Turkey, Egypt, Syria and other states abolished the family endowment and took the charitable ones under the control of ministries. The largest body of housing ever held outside the market by non-state institutions was absorbed by the state or released for sale.

#### China and Japan: allotment, contract and the tenement

Chinese political thought contained, from an early date, the idea that the ruler should ensure that every household had land enough to live on. The philosopher Mencius, in the fourth century before the common era, described a system he attributed to the sage kings of antiquity. Land was divided into squares of nine equal plots, like the Chinese character for a well. Eight families each farmed one of the outer plots for themselves and worked the central plot together for the lord. Historians doubt that the well field system ever existed in this tidy form, but for two thousand years it served Chinese reformers as an image of the just distribution that had been lost.

The nearest approach to it in practice was the equal field system, introduced by the Northern Wei dynasty in 485 and continued under the Sui and the Tang. The state assigned farmland to each household according to the number and age of its members. Most of the allotment returned to the state for reassignment when the holder grew old or died, while a smaller portion, planted with mulberry trees and other long term crops, could be inherited. The purpose was to secure a broad base of taxpaying farmers and to deny great families the opportunity to engross land and people. The system depended on accurate registers and on a supply of land for redistribution, and it broke down in the upheavals of the eighth century. From 780 the Tang government taxed households according to the property they actually held, which amounted to recognising private ownership and the inequality that went with it.

Under the Song dynasty, which ruled from 960 to 1279, China developed one of the most active land markets of the premodern world. The state did not try to limit holdings. It taxed transfers and required that a deed of sale be presented for an official stamp, so that a contract bearing the red seal was proof of title. Printed contract forms circulated, and proverbs remarked that a field changed owners many times in a thousand years. The freedom to sell was nevertheless qualified by claims of kinship and neighbourhood. Custom, and for long periods the law, required a seller to offer land first to relatives and adjoining owners. A widely used form of transaction was the conditional sale, under which the seller received most of the value but kept the right to redeem the property by repaying the price, sometimes many decades later. Families in difficulty preferred it because it avoided the shame and finality of parting with ancestral land, and Chinese courts spent centuries adjudicating claims by the descendants of sellers to recover property or to demand a supplementary payment.

In the south, rights in the same field were often split between two parties. One held the subsoil, paid the land tax and received a rent. The other held the surface, with a permanent right to cultivate that he could sell, pledge or bequeath without the consent of the first. The arrangement, known as one field with two owners, gave the working tenant a secure and marketable interest and is one more example of the layered property that absolute ownership would later displace. Lineages also held land collectively. The scholar and statesman Fan Zhongyan founded a charitable estate at Suzhou in 1050, endowing it with fields whose rents were to provide grain, clothing, and the costs of weddings, funerals and schooling for all members of his clan. The estate was inalienable, was managed under written rules, and survived for some nine centuries. Such trusts, which multiplied in later dynasties, were a Chinese counterpart of the Islamic family endowment.

The Chinese city passed through a comparable change. The Tang capital at Chang'an, the largest city in the world in its day, was divided into more than a hundred walled wards, whose gates were shut at dusk, and commerce was confined to two official markets. In the Song capital of Kaifeng the ward walls came down, shops and houses opened directly onto the streets, and a commercial market in urban accommodation emerged. Officials posted to the capital, merchants and labourers commonly rented, and writers of the time complained of the cost of lodging. The government was itself one of the great landlords. An office of state managed thousands of rental premises in the capital, the income from which went to the treasury, and emperors are recorded as remitting several days' rent to the tenants during heavy snow or at festivals. A state that lets housing commercially and occasionally forgives the rent as an act of benevolence is neither a free market landlord nor a welfare authority, and the example shows how poorly modern categories fit the past.

Japan borrowed the Chinese model and then departed from it. The reforms of the mid seventh century declared all rice land to belong to the emperor and instituted periodic allotment to households on the Tang pattern. Within two centuries the system had decayed, as temples, shrines and noble families obtained exemptions for the lands they reclaimed and built up private estates. Under the Tokugawa shoguns, who governed from 1603 to 1868, the regime sought stability above all. A decree of 1643 prohibited the permanent sale of farmland, with the aim of keeping peasants on their holdings and preserving the tax base, although villagers evaded it by pawning land without redeeming it.

The towns of Tokugawa Japan were another matter. Edo, the present Tokyo, had about a million inhabitants in the eighteenth century and was probably the largest city on earth. Roughly half its people were commoners, crowded into a small fraction of its area. Merchant landowners held plots that faced the street, where they built shops and their own residences. Behind, along narrow alleys, stood the long single storey tenements called nagaya, divided into one room dwellings of about ten square metres each, with a shared well, privy and rubbish pit. The owner usually entrusted the tenement to a resident manager, who collected the rent, vouched for the tenants to the authorities, settled quarrels, and stood in a relationship to them that was described as that of parent to child. Part of his income came from selling the contents of the shared privy to farmers as fertiliser. Edo burned repeatedly, the tenements were regarded as temporary, and a culture of cheap, quickly rebuilt housing took root that still marks Japan.

The new government that took power in 1868 swept the old restrictions away within a few years. The ban on selling farmland was lifted in 1872. In 1873 a land tax reform issued certificates of ownership to those recognised as holders and imposed an annual tax of three per cent of the assessed value of the land, payable in money. Land became private property that could be freely sold and mortgaged, and the tax on it financed much of Japan's early industrialisation. Reformers elsewhere in Asia, including the followers of Henry George, would later point to the Japanese tax on land values as a model.

#### India, Africa, the Americas and the Pacific

In the Indian subcontinent the question of who owned the land had no single answer before the British insisted on one. Under the Mughal emperors, who ruled most of India in the sixteenth and seventeenth centuries, the state claimed a share of the produce of every field, commonly reckoned at a third or more, and the whole apparatus of government rested on collecting it. Between the emperor and the cultivator stood intermediaries of many kinds, some of them local chiefs with ancient hereditary positions and others officials assigned the revenue of a district for a term in lieu of salary. Beneath them the village community regulated the use of fields, wells, grazing and house sites among its members according to caste and custom. The cultivator had a heritable right to remain on his land so long as he paid, and the intermediary had a right to collect. Neither was an owner in the Roman sense, and European observers argued for generations about whether the true proprietor was the sovereign, the intermediary or the peasant. The dispute was more than academic, because the answer the British eventually gave reshaped Indian society, as the next chapter describes.

In most of sub-Saharan Africa before colonial rule, land was understood to belong to a community that included the dead and the unborn as well as the living. A chief or council of elders held it in trust and allocated the right to use it. A man who needed a place to build and fields to cultivate asked for them and received them as a member of the lineage or village, or as a stranger accepted into it. What he received was a secure right to use the land and to pass that use to his heirs, and it lasted as long as the land was occupied. He could not sell it to an outsider, because it was not his to sell. A Nigerian chief, giving evidence to a colonial committee on land in 1912, expressed the idea in words that have been quoted ever since: the land, he said, belongs to a vast family, of whom many are dead, few are living and countless numbers are yet unborn.

The variety within this general pattern was considerable. Among the Asante of what is now Ghana, land was attached to the stools, or thrones, of chiefs, and the reverence owed to the ancestors who lay in it made outright sale unthinkable, though rights of use were readily granted. In highland Ethiopia a system known as rist gave every descendant of the original founder of a community, traced through either parent, a claim to a share of its land, so that holdings were continually divided and reallocated among kin, while the nobility and the church held rights to tribute from the same land. On the Swahili coast and in the trading cities of the Sahel, by contrast, Islamic law applied and houses were bought, sold and endowed. Where land was plentiful and people were scarce, as in much of the continent, control over followers mattered more than control over acres, and there was little reason to treat ground as a scarce commodity.

The great states of the Americas organised land on comparable principles. In the Aztec realm of central Mexico the basic unit was the calpulli, a community with its own territory, temple and leaders. Its council assigned plots to member households for their support. A family kept its plot for as long as it worked the land and could pass it to its children, but could not sell it, and a holding left uncultivated for two years returned to the community to be assigned to someone else. Other lands were set aside to support the temples, the ruler and the nobility. In the Andes the corresponding unit was the ayllu, a group of related households that held land collectively and reallocated it periodically according to the size of each family. The Inca state required labour from these communities to work the lands of the ruler and the cult of the sun and to build roads, terraces and storehouses, and in return it fed the workers and held reserves against famine. In neither society was there a market in land.

North of Mexico the peoples of the continent held land in ways as varied as their economies. Among the Haudenosaunee, or Iroquois, of the north eastern woodlands, the longhouse in which several related families lived belonged to a clan traced through the female line, the fields were worked by the women and controlled by them, and a husband moved into the house of his wife's kin. Hunting peoples recognised territories belonging to bands or families without any notion that the ground itself could be transferred. When Europeans proposed to buy land, the two sides frequently meant different things by the transaction. The newcomers believed they were acquiring exclusive and permanent ownership. Those who accepted their goods often understood that they were admitting the strangers to share the use of the land. The Shawnee leader Tecumseh, who tried in the early nineteenth century to unite the nations against further cessions, is reported to have argued that no single group had the right to sell what belonged to all, and to have asked why, if the land could be sold, the air, the clouds and the sea should not be sold as well.

In the islands of the Pacific the bond between people and land was commonly expressed in the language of kinship. The Maori word for land also means the placenta, which was buried in ancestral ground after a birth, and a group's identity was stated by naming its mountain, its river and its meeting place. Rights to cultivate, fish and gather belonged to descent groups and were allocated among families by their leaders. Similar conceptions prevailed in Hawaii, Fiji and Samoa, and several Pacific states today retain the great majority of their land in customary ownership, inalienable by law.

Across all these societies certain features recur. A person's claim to a home and the means of subsistence arose from membership of a group, not from a purchase. The claim was conditional on use, so that land left idle returned to the common stock. It could be inherited but not sold to outsiders. And authority over land was a form of trusteeship exercised on behalf of past and future generations. Such systems gave their members great security against landlessness, and offered correspondingly little freedom to the individual who wished to leave, to borrow against his land or to accumulate. To the European lawyers and administrators who encountered them, they appeared either as the absence of property or as an obstacle to progress. The collision between these conceptions and the idea of land as a commodity is the subject of the next chapter.

### Chapter 3: The Making of Property

Between about 1500 and 1900 the layered and conditional rights described in the last chapter were replaced, across much of the world, by a single form: exclusive private ownership of a defined parcel, recorded in a document, free of customary claims and transferable to anyone for money. The change was not a natural evolution. It was carried out by acts of parliament, revolutionary decrees, colonial ordinances and surveyors' chains, usually against resistance, and it produced both a great release of economic energy and a great dispossession. Those who lost their customary hold on land became the wage earners and tenants of the industrial age, and it is from their situation that the modern housing question arises.

#### Enclosure in the British Isles

England led the process, and the word that names it is English. To enclose land was to fence or hedge it and to extinguish the rights that others had exercised over it, converting open fields and commons into separate farms under one person's control. Lords had been permitted since the thirteenth century to enclose portions of manorial waste, provided they left sufficient pasture for their free tenants. The movement gathered pace in the late fifteenth and sixteenth centuries, when the high price of wool tempted landowners to turn arable villages into sheep runs. Thomas More, in Utopia in 1516, wrote bitterly that sheep, once so meek, had become devourers of men, consuming fields, houses and whole towns. Tudor governments, fearing depopulation and disorder, legislated against the conversion of tillage to pasture, to little effect. A rising in Norfolk in 1549, led by Robert Kett, began with the tearing down of hedges, and so did a revolt in the Midlands in 1607.

The most radical protest came in 1649, in the aftermath of the civil war. A small group led by Gerrard Winstanley began to dig and plant the common land on St George's Hill in Surrey, declaring that the earth had been made a common treasury for all and that the poor had as much right to it as the rich. The Diggers, as they were called, argued that the buying and selling of land was the root of oppression and that the victory over the king meant nothing unless the common people were free to use the commons. Local landowners had their huts pulled down and their crops trampled, and within a year the colony was gone. Winstanley's pamphlets were rediscovered in the nineteenth century and have been claimed as ancestors by socialists, anarchists and the followers of Henry George alike.

The decisive phase came later and proceeded by law. From about 1750 to 1850 Parliament passed some four thousand private acts, each authorising the enclosure of a particular parish. Commissioners surveyed the open fields and commons, extinguished all common rights and allotted the land in compact blocks to those who could prove a legal entitlement, in proportion to their previous holdings. In this way roughly a fifth of the surface of England, close to seven million acres, passed from shared use into exclusive ownership. Agricultural writers of the time were enthusiastic. Enclosed farms could be drained, fertilised and bred upon selectively in ways the open fields did not allow, and they fed a population that doubled within a century.

The cost fell on those whose claims were customary and unwritten. A cottager who had kept a cow and a few geese on the common, and gathered his fuel there, might receive nothing if he was a tenant, since any allotment went to his landlord, or a plot too small to be worth the expense of fencing, which he then sold. The agricultural improver Arthur Young, who had done as much as anyone to promote enclosure, later admitted that in the great majority of cases the poor had been injured, and he quoted them as saying that all they knew was that they had once had a cow and an act of parliament had taken it from them. Historians still dispute the scale of the harm, but the direction is not in doubt. A class of villagers who had combined wages with the produce of the commons became wholly dependent on wages.

Enclosure bore directly on housing. In the open village a poor family could often put up a cottage on the edge of the waste. An Elizabethan statute had tried to forbid the building of any cottage without four acres of land attached, in order to prevent the growth of a landless class, but it was widely ignored and was repealed in 1775. A tradition found in Wales and parts of England held that a person who could raise a dwelling on common land between sunset and sunrise, with smoke rising from the chimney by morning, acquired the right to remain. Once the commons were enclosed there was nowhere left to build. In many parishes the dominant landowner went further. The poor law made each parish responsible for relieving its own paupers, and a landowner who controlled a whole village could reduce the charge on himself by pulling down cottages and refusing to build new ones, so that labourers had to live in a neighbouring parish and walk miles to work. Those cottages that remained on the estate were commonly tied to employment. A labourer who lost his place, or offended his employer, lost his home on the same day. The tied cottage survived in English agriculture until the late twentieth century.

In the Highlands of Scotland the process was more abrupt. Clan chiefs who had once valued their lands for the fighting men they supported came, after the defeat of the Jacobite rising in 1746, to regard themselves as commercial landlords. From the later eighteenth century, and most notoriously on the Sutherland estate in the 1810s, they removed the townships of small tenants from the interior glens to make way for sheep, resettling some on poor plots by the coast and leaving the rest to emigrate. Houses were unroofed or burned to prevent return. The tenants had no leases, and the law gave them no protection. Their descendants, the crofters, organised in the 1880s, withheld rent and resisted evictions, and after a royal commission the Crofters Act of 1886 granted them security of tenure, fair rents fixed by a tribunal and the right to bequeath their holdings.

Ireland supplied the precedent that would matter most for the future. There the land was owned by a small class, largely of English and Protestant descent, and farmed by tenants who could be evicted at will and who received no compensation for the houses and improvements they had made themselves. After the famine of the 1840s and a further agricultural crisis in the late 1870s, tenants combined under the Land League in a campaign of rent strikes and social ostracism, from one of whose targets, an agent named Captain Boycott, the English language acquired a word. Their demands were summarised as the three Fs: fair rent, fixity of tenure and free sale of the tenant's interest. The Land Act of 1881 conceded all three, creating courts to fix rents. Further acts, culminating in that of 1903, went beyond regulation and lent tenants the money to buy their farms from the landlords, on terms that made the repayments lower than the former rent. Within a generation most of the land of Ireland had passed to those who worked it. A British government had accepted that the market in land could be overridden by rent tribunals and that the state might finance a wholesale transfer of ownership, and both ideas were soon to be applied to housing in the towns.

#### Revolution and reform: the continent, Mexico and Russia

On the European continent the old order of landholding was ended not by a slow accumulation of private acts but by revolution and by reforming states that copied or feared it. The model was France. On the night of 4 August 1789, with châteaux burning in the provinces, the National Assembly voted to abolish the feudal regime. Personal servitude and the lords' private courts disappeared at once. The dues that peasants owed on their land were at first declared redeemable by purchase and then, in 1793, cancelled without compensation. The peasant who had held land under a lord became its owner, free of all charges. The Declaration of the Rights of Man of the same year named property among the natural rights and, in its final article, called it inviolable and sacred.

The revolution also carried out one of the largest transfers of real estate in European history. The lands and buildings of the Church, and later those of nobles who had fled abroad, were confiscated as national property and sold to raise money for the state. The property amounted to perhaps a tenth of the land of France and a far larger share of the buildings in Paris and other cities, where convents and monasteries had occupied whole districts. The sales were by auction and in large lots, so that most went to those who already had means: merchants, lawyers, prosperous farmers. A new class of owners thereby acquired a personal stake in the permanence of the revolution, and no later regime dared undo the sales.

The legal expression of the new order was the civil code promulgated under Napoleon in 1804. Its article 544 defined ownership as the right to enjoy and dispose of things in the most absolute manner, provided that no use is made of them that the law forbids. The code gave no recognition to collective or customary rights, treated the lease as a simple contract, and required estates to be divided among all children. French armies and French prestige carried it across Europe, and it became the template for the codes of Belgium, the Netherlands, Italy, Spain, Portugal and most of Latin America. With it travelled an idea of property as the unrestricted power of one individual over one thing, which later generations would find it necessary to qualify.

Other states reformed from above to avoid the French fate or to match French strength. Prussia, after its defeat by Napoleon, abolished hereditary serfdom by an edict of 1807 and allowed land to be bought and sold by any class. The peasants paid heavily for their freedom, since most had to cede a third or a half of their land to the lord in compensation, and the estates of the east grew larger as a result. Throughout central Europe the revolutions of 1848 completed the abolition of labour services and feudal dues.

Spain followed its own path, and the episode left a mark on the country that is still discussed. A large proportion of Spanish land was held in what was called dead hands, by the Church, the municipalities and entailed noble estates, none of which could sell. Liberal governments, chronically short of money and convinced that land must circulate to be productive, set about releasing it. The measures of the minister Mendizábal in 1836 and 1837 confiscated the property of the religious orders and sold it at auction. A general law promoted by Pascual Madoz in 1855 extended the sales to the lands of the towns and villages, including the commons on which poorer inhabitants pastured animals and gathered wood. Buyers could pay in instalments or in depreciated government bonds, and the purchasers were overwhelmingly the urban middle class and existing landowners. In the cities, former convents were demolished or converted, and their sites provided the land for new streets, squares and apartment buildings. In the countryside of the south the great estates grew greater and the landless labourers more numerous. The reformer Joaquín Costa argued at the end of the century that the liberals had destroyed a tradition of collective property that had protected the rural poor, and the grievance fed the agrarian conflicts that preceded the civil war of 1936.

Mexico repeated the experiment with more explosive results. The liberal reformers who came to power in the 1850s shared the Spanish conviction that corporate property was an obstacle to progress. A law of 1856, named after the minister Miguel Lerdo de Tejada, obliged all civil and ecclesiastical corporations to sell their real estate. The primary target was the Church, which was the largest owner of urban buildings and the principal lender in the country. But the law applied equally to the Indian villages, which had held their lands in common since before the Spanish conquest and had been confirmed in them by the Spanish crown. Over the following half century, and especially under the long rule of Porfirio Díaz, village lands were divided, sold, surveyed away by companies entitled to a share of whatever they measured, or simply taken by neighbouring haciendas. By 1910 the great majority of rural families owned no land at all. The revolution that broke out in that year was fought in the south, under Emiliano Zapata, with the slogan of land and liberty. Its constitution of 1917 declared in article 27 that the ownership of land and water is vested originally in the nation, which has the right to impose on private property the forms that the public interest requires. On that basis the state restored and created communal holdings known as ejidos, which eventually covered about half the farmland of Mexico and whose members could use and inherit their plots but, until a reform of 1992, could not sell them. Many of the informal settlements that surround Mexican cities today stand on former ejido land.

Russia attempted to free its peasants while keeping the collective. The emancipation of 1861 ended serfdom for more than twenty million people, but it did not make them individual proprietors. The land allotted to the former serfs was vested in the village commune, the mir, which was jointly responsible for taxes and for the payments, spread over forty-nine years, by which the peasants were to reimburse the state for compensating the landlords. In most of Russia the commune periodically redistributed the strips of arable among households according to the number of workers or mouths in each. Conservatives valued the commune as a guarantee of order, since no peasant could become landless, and the populist revolutionaries valued it as the germ of a native socialism. Economists blamed it for backward farming. After the revolution of 1905 the minister Pyotr Stolypin reversed the policy and encouraged peasants to withdraw their land from the commune as consolidated private farms, hoping to create a class of sturdy owners loyal to the regime. He described the plan as a wager on the strong. It was cut short by his assassination and by war, and when the old order collapsed in 1917 the peasants seized the remaining estates and in many places revived the commune.

These continental experiences have a common shape. Reformers who believed in the individual owner dissolved the collective and institutional forms of property, expecting a society of independent proprietors to emerge. Where the land went to those who worked it, as in France, the result was a conservative peasantry attached to the new order. Where it went to those who could pay, as in Spain and Mexico, the result was concentration, a dispossessed rural population and eventually violent conflict. The same choice, between distributing property to its occupants and selling it to the highest bidder, would confront governments again whenever they disposed of public housing.

#### Empire, survey and title

Outside Europe the making of property was bound up with conquest. European powers that acquired territory overseas had to decide whose the land was, and they answered in ways that suited settlement and revenue. The legal doctrines they devised, the surveys they carried out and the registers they established still define the map of ownership in much of the world, and the disputes they created are far from settled.

The first step was to deny or diminish the title of the existing inhabitants. English colonists in North America found a justification in an argument that John Locke, himself a secretary to the proprietors of Carolina, set out at length. Property, Locke held, arises when a person mixes labour with the land by enclosing and cultivating it. Peoples who hunted over a territory, or farmed it without fences and moved on, had not appropriated it in this sense, and the land could be regarded as lying in common, open to anyone who would improve it. The Supreme Court of the United States gave the position legal form in 1823, ruling that discovery had vested ultimate title in the European sovereign and its successors, and that the native nations retained only a right of occupancy which the government alone could purchase or extinguish. In Australia the British went further and treated the continent as land belonging to no one, so that no purchase or treaty was thought necessary. That doctrine stood until 1992, when the High Court of Australia held in the Mabo case that native title had survived the assertion of British sovereignty wherever it had not been lawfully extinguished.

The second step was to measure the land and divide it into parcels that could be sold. The United States devised the most systematic method. The Land Ordinance of 1785 provided that the western territory should be surveyed before settlement into townships six miles square, each subdivided into thirty-six sections of one square mile, with one section in every township reserved to support public schools. The lines ran due north and south and east and west, regardless of rivers and hills, and the resulting chequerboard is visible from the air across two thirds of the country. Land so described could be identified by a few numbers, sold at a distant land office and resold without ambiguity. It became a commodity before anyone lived on it. The government at first sold it for revenue and later, under the Homestead Act of 1862, offered a quarter section of 160 acres free to any citizen who would live on it for five years and build a dwelling. The homestead embodied a political ideal, the republic of independent farmers, and it distributed some 270 million acres. Much of the best land nevertheless went to railways and speculators, and all of it had first been taken from others.

The same faith in individual ownership was then applied to the native nations themselves. The General Allotment Act of 1887, known as the Dawes Act, authorised the president to break up reservations held in common and allot a parcel to each family, with the remainder declared surplus and opened to settlers. Its sponsors sincerely believed that private property would turn hunters into farmers and citizens. One of them complained that tribal ownership lacked selfishness, which he held to be at the bottom of civilisation. The allotments, once the restrictions on sale expired, were often lost to debt, taxes or fraud. Between 1887 and the repeal of the policy in 1934, the land in Indian ownership fell from about 138 million acres to about 48 million. The experiment is frequently cited by those who doubt that granting individual title to communal land benefits the people who receive it.

The third step was to create a reliable record of who owned what. Under English law a purchaser had to examine the chain of deeds by which land had passed from hand to hand, a slow and uncertain business in which a single defect could invalidate a title. Robert Torrens, a customs official who became premier of South Australia, proposed to treat land as ships were treated, with a public register that was itself conclusive proof of ownership. Under the system he introduced in 1858, each parcel has a single entry, every transfer and mortgage is recorded upon it, and the state guarantees the result. Torrens title spread to the rest of Australia, New Zealand, western Canada, and many British territories in Africa and Asia, and comparable registers were developed in Germany and elsewhere.
