Registration made land cheap to buy, sell and borrow against, and it is a precondition of the modern mortgage market. It also made final whatever had been entered, including the exclusion of unwritten customary claims.

In India the British East India Company wanted a dependable revenue and a class of loyal proprietors. By the Permanent Settlement of Bengal in 1793, the governor general Lord Cornwallis declared the zamindars, who had been hereditary collectors of revenue, to be the owners of the land, on condition that they paid the government a sum fixed forever. Cornwallis hoped they would behave like improving English landlords. Instead many defaulted and were sold up, their estates passed to financiers in Calcutta, and the cultivators beneath them, who had possessed customary rights of occupancy, became tenants with little protection against rising rents. In other provinces the British experimented with settlements made directly with the peasant or with the village as a body. All these systems had one effect in common. Land became an asset that could be mortgaged and seized for debt, and by the late nineteenth century the transfer of peasant holdings to moneylenders had become so alarming that the colonial government restricted the sale of agricultural land to non-agriculturalists in the Punjab.

In Africa the colonial powers typically created two systems side by side. Land wanted for European settlement, mining or towns was declared the property of the crown or the state and granted to settlers under registered title. The remainder was left under what was called customary law, administered by chiefs whose powers the colonial government defined and often enlarged. In Kenya the best agricultural land of the central highlands was reserved for white farmers. In South Africa the Natives Land Act of 1913 prohibited Africans, who were over two thirds of the population, from buying or renting land outside reserves that amounted to about seven per cent of the country, a share later raised to thirteen. The writer Sol Plaatje, who travelled the country recording the evictions that followed, opened his account by saying that on the morning the act came into force the African found himself not actually a slave but a pariah in the land of his birth. In the towns, Africans were treated as temporary sojourners, permitted to live only in designated locations and denied the right to own their homes, a policy that shaped South African cities for the rest of the century.

Similar measures were taken elsewhere. In Algeria a French law of 1873 subjected tribal lands to the French civil code so that individual shares could be bought by colonists. In New Zealand a Native Land Court established in 1865 converted Maori customary tenure into individual titles, which could then be sold, and within a few decades most of the North Island had changed hands. In Hawaii a division of lands in 1848 introduced private ownership at the urging of foreign advisers, and much of the land soon belonged to foreign planters. In each case the new form of property was presented as progress and security, and in each case its first consequence was to make the land available for transfer out of the hands of those who had held it.

#### The urban land market takes shape

While property in the countryside was being remade by enclosure and revolution, the growing cities of the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries were developing the institutions through which urban housing would be produced for the next two hundred years: the landed estate that sold the right to build, the speculative builder who worked for an unknown future occupant, the investor who held houses for their rents, and the municipal rule book that governed what could be erected. London, Paris and New York each contributed a characteristic form.

London grew westward in the seventeenth century over fields that belonged to a handful of aristocratic families. These owners were mostly bound by family settlements that prevented them from selling, and they had neither the capital nor the wish to build on their own account. The solution they adopted was the building lease. The estate laid out streets and squares, divided the frontages into plots, and let each plot to a builder for a long term at a low ground rent. The builder put up a house at his own expense, to a standard the lease prescribed, and sold his interest to an occupier or investor. When the lease expired, originally after thirty or forty years and later typically after ninety-nine, the land and the house upon it reverted to the estate without payment. The Earl of Bedford developed Covent Garden in this manner in the 1630s, and his descendants built Bloomsbury. The Grosvenor family laid out Mayfair from the 1720s and Belgravia a century later. The Portman, Cadogan and Howard de Walden estates followed the same practice, and several of them still own the freeholds.

The system had notable advantages for the appearance and order of the city. A single owner with a permanent interest planned whole districts, insisted on uniform facades, provided squares and gardens and excluded noxious trades. It also embodied a particular division of the gains from urban growth. The builder and the occupier bore the cost and risk of construction and enjoyed the house for a term. The increase in the value of the site, as the city spread around it, accrued to the landowner, who recovered the whole property at the end and could let it again at the full current rent. By the nineteenth century the wealth this conferred on a few families had become a political issue. John Stuart Mill had the London ground landlords in mind when he wrote of those who grow richer in their sleep, and campaigns to tax ground rents, and to give leaseholders the right to buy their freeholds, ran for a hundred years before the latter succeeded in 1967.

The man who built on these estates was a new economic type. After the Great Fire of 1666, which destroyed most of the old City of London, a physician turned developer named Nicholas Barbon pioneered the methods of the speculative builder. He took building leases on a large scale, laid out streets of standardised brick houses with interchangeable parts, sublet the plots to tradesmen who built with materials supplied on credit, and sold the finished houses to anyone who would buy. He was litigious and unscrupulous, on one occasion sending his workmen to fight the lawyers of Gray's Inn who objected to a development beside their gardens, and he died insolvent. He also founded the first fire insurance office in London and wrote a treatise on trade that argued for the benefits of building and consumption. The rebuilding after the fire was governed by an act of 1667 that sorted houses into four classes according to the width of the street, fixed the number of storeys and the thickness of walls for each class, and required brick or stone in place of timber. Standard regulations and the standard terraced house developed together, and speculative building on leased land remained the normal way of housing Londoners of every class until the First World War.

Paris developed a different building type and a different pattern of ownership. Confined by its walls and by a tradition of dense building, the city grew upwards. From the eighteenth century investors erected what was called the house of revenue, an apartment building of five or six storeys designed from the start to be let in separate dwellings. Shops occupied the ground floor. The grandest apartment was on the first or second floor, with high ceilings and a balcony, and the accommodation became smaller and cheaper with each flight of stairs, ending with servants and the poor in the attics under the roof. All classes thus lived at the same address, separated vertically, an arrangement that novelists from Balzac to Zola used to portray the whole of society in a single staircase. The building belonged to one proprietor, for whom it was a safe investment akin to government bonds, and everyone else was a tenant. Rents were paid quarterly, and the quarter days, when those who could not pay moved out, sometimes secretly by night, were a recurring drama of Parisian life.

New York applied the logic of the American land survey to a city. In 1811 a state commission adopted a plan that covered the island of Manhattan, most of it still farms and rocky hills, with a uniform grid of twelve avenues and one hundred and fifty-five cross streets. The commissioners explained their choice with unusual candour. A city, they observed, is composed principally of the habitations of men, and straight sided and right angled houses are the cheapest to build and the most convenient to live in. The blocks were divided into lots twenty-five feet wide and one hundred feet deep, a module that could be bought, sold and combined like any other standard article. No squares or public buildings interrupted the pattern except a parade ground, and the large park at the centre of the island was added only in the 1850s after a public campaign.

The grid made possible the greatest fortune in American real estate. John Jacob Astor, an immigrant from Germany who had grown rich in the fur trade, began buying land on Manhattan beyond the edge of settlement in the early years of the century and continued for four decades, often acquiring it cheaply from owners in financial distress. He seldom built. He leased his lots for terms of about twenty years to others who put up houses, and at the end of the term he could buy the building at a valuation or renew at a higher rent. When he died in 1848 he was the richest man in the United States, and he is said to have remarked that if he could live his life again he would buy every foot of land on the island. His descendants drew rents from some of the most crowded tenement districts in the world. Astor had contributed nothing to the growth of New York except his foresight in getting there first, and it was the spectacle of fortunes of this kind that led Henry George, a generation later, to his theory of land.

By the early nineteenth century, then, the elements of the modern housing market were in place. Land had become private property that could be measured, registered, mortgaged and sold. In the countryside of the most advanced economies, the customary rights that had given the poor a foothold on the land had been extinguished, and a population without property was moving towards the towns. In the towns, landowners, builders and investors had developed efficient methods of producing housing for profit, and the rules governing them were concerned with the safety and regularity of buildings, not with who could afford to live in them. Thinkers had begun to ask whether the private ownership of land could be justified, and on what terms. Then the factory arrived, the towns multiplied in size within a single lifetime, and the arrangements described in this part were tested as never before. That is the subject of Part Two.

## Part Two: The Liberal Century

### Chapter 4: The Philosophers of Property

The transformation of land into a commodity was accompanied, and in part driven, by an argument about whether it could be justified. Between the seventeenth and the nineteenth centuries the question of how any individual could rightfully own a portion of the earth was debated more intensely than at any time before or since. The answers given then still supply nearly every position in the modern debate on housing. Those who defend the owner's freedom to do as he likes with his house, those who would tax away the value of land, those who would abolish the landlord and those who would make every family a proprietor are all, whether they know it or not, repeating arguments first set out in this period.

#### Natural right and its critics

The problem was posed in its modern form by the theorists of natural law in the seventeenth century. Writers such as the Dutch jurist Hugo Grotius and the German Samuel Pufendorf began from the scriptural premise that God had given the earth to humanity in common. If that was so, the private ownership they saw around them needed explaining. Their solution was consent. In the earliest age, they supposed, people had taken what they needed from the common stock. As population grew and goods became scarce, mankind had agreed, expressly or tacitly, to divide the earth and to respect each person's share. Property on this view was a human convention, founded on agreement, and it retained a trace of its origin in the principle that a person in extreme need might take what was necessary to survive, since nobody could be presumed to have consented to starve.

Thomas Hobbes drew a starker conclusion from similar premises. In the natural condition of humanity, he argued, there is no mine and thine at all, only what each can seize and hold. Property comes into existence with the sovereign, who alone can make and enforce the rules that distinguish one person's goods from another's. It follows that the sovereign who creates property may also regulate, tax and redistribute it, and that no subject can plead a right of property against the state. The argument was repugnant to the landed classes of England, who had just fought a civil war against a king who taxed without consent.

John Locke gave them the theory they wanted. In the Second Treatise of Government, published in 1689, he accepted that the earth was originally common but denied that private property required anyone's agreement. Every person, he argued, owns himself, and therefore owns his labour. When he mixes that labour with something that nature has provided, by gathering acorns, tilling a field or building a house, he joins to it something that is his and so makes it his own. No consent is needed, for if it were, mankind would have starved amid the plenty that God provided. Property is thus a natural right that precedes government, and the chief purpose for which people establish governments is to protect it. A ruler who invades the property of his subjects without their consent breaks the trust on which his authority rests.

Locke attached conditions to this right, and they have proved as influential as the right itself. A person may appropriate from the common only where there is enough, and as good, left for others. And he may take only as much as he can use before it spoils, since nothing was made by God for man to waste. In a world of abundant land these limits pinch nobody. Locke then removed their force for his own society with a further argument. The invention of money, which does not spoil, allows a person to accumulate without waste, and since people have agreed to use money they have tacitly agreed to the unequal possessions that result. As for those left without land, the labour of the industrious has so increased the productivity of the earth that a day labourer in England is better housed and fed than a king among peoples who hold their land in common.

The weaknesses of the argument were noticed early. If labour creates the title, it creates a title to what labour has added, which is the crop or the building, and it is not obvious why it should extend to the ground itself, which no one made. If the proviso means what it says, then once all the land has been taken there is not enough and as good left for those who come later, and their exclusion needs some further justification. Locke's theory explains very well why the builder should own the house. It explains much less well why the first occupier of a site in what later becomes the centre of a great city should own, forever, the value that the city confers upon it. The whole subsequent debate on land may be read as a series of attempts to deal with this gap.

Jean-Jacques Rousseau attacked from the other side. In his Discourse on the Origin of Inequality, published in 1755, he wrote that the true founder of civil society was the first man who, having enclosed a piece of ground, thought of saying that it was his and found people simple enough to believe him. How many crimes, wars and miseries, he continued, would have been spared the human race by anyone who had pulled up the stakes and cried out to his fellows to beware of the impostor, for they were lost if they forgot that the fruits of the earth belong to all and the earth itself to nobody. Rousseau did not propose to abolish property, which he elsewhere called the most sacred of the citizen's rights. His point was that property is a social creation that has brought inequality and dependence with it, and that the community which guarantees it may set its limits. In The Social Contract he added that the right of each individual over his own land is always subordinate to the right of the community over all.

In Britain the natural right theory was undermined by sceptics who nonetheless defended property. David Hume argued that there is nothing natural about ownership. It is a convention that people have gradually adopted because stable possession is so obviously useful to everyone, much as they have adopted languages and money without any formal agreement. Rules of property are justified by their utility and by nothing else. Jeremy Bentham put the point more sharply. Property and law, he wrote, are born together and die together, and before laws there was no property. To speak of natural rights was, in his phrase, nonsense upon stilts. Bentham held that the legislator should above all protect the security of established expectations, because people will not work, build or save unless they are confident of keeping the results. But he also held that the aim of legislation is the greatest happiness of the greatest number, and that a given sum adds more to the happiness of a poor person than of a rich one. A utilitarian could therefore support any reform of property that would increase the general welfare without too greatly disturbing security.

The lawyers meanwhile described what the propertied classes believed they had. William Blackstone, in his Commentaries on the Laws of England in the 1760s, declared that nothing so strikes the imagination and engages the affections of mankind as the right of property, which he defined as that sole and despotic dominion which one man claims and exercises over the external things of the world, in total exclusion of the right of any other individual in the universe. He admitted in the next breath that few people cared to look too closely into the origin of this right, for fear of finding some defect in their title. By the end of the eighteenth century, then, two views were available. On one, the owner of a house and land held them by a right older than the state, which the state could not justly infringe. On the other, he held them by a convention that society maintained for its own benefit and could amend. The politics of housing has moved between these poles ever since.

#### The economists and the problem of rent

While philosophers disputed the origin of property, a new science set out to explain how the income of society is divided among those who own land, those who own capital and those who work. The founders of political economy were, almost without exception, friends of private property and free exchange. It is therefore striking that they reached a conclusion about land that has embarrassed defenders of the landlord ever since. The income from land, they found, differs in kind from wages and profits. It is not a reward for any contribution to production, and it can be taxed without harm.

The idea first appeared in France in the middle of the eighteenth century, among a group of thinkers who called themselves the economists and are known to history as the Physiocrats. Their leader, François Quesnay, was physician to the court of Louis XV. They held that agriculture alone yields a surplus over the cost of production, that this surplus accrues to the owners of land as rent, and that all taxes, however levied, are ultimately paid out of it. It followed that the multitude of taxes on trade and consumption that burdened France should be replaced by a single tax on the net product of land. The statesman Turgot, who shared many of their views, tried as minister to move in that direction and was dismissed. The Physiocrats' analysis of agriculture as the sole source of wealth did not survive, but their proposal of a single tax on rent was to be revived, on different grounds, a century later.

Adam Smith knew the Physiocrats personally and admired them with reservations. In The Wealth of Nations, published in 1776, he treated rent as a monopoly price. The landlord, he observed, demands for the use of his land the highest sum the tenant can afford to pay, and this has no relation to anything the landlord may have laid out in improving it. Landlords, like all other men, love to reap where they never sowed. Smith was the first to analyse the rent of houses in particular, and he divided it into two parts. The building rent is the return on the capital spent in constructing the house, and it must be enough to give the builder the ordinary rate of profit, or houses will not be built. The ground rent is whatever is paid above this, and it goes to the owner of the site for an advantage of situation that he did nothing to create. It is highest in the capital and in those parts of it where demand is greatest.

From this Smith drew a conclusion about taxation that bears directly on the modern argument. A tax on the building rent, he reasoned, would fall partly on the occupier and would discourage construction. A tax on the ground rent would fall wholly on the owner of the ground, who always charges as much as he can get and could not raise his price merely because he was taxed. Such a tax would not raise the rent of houses or reduce their supply. Ground rents, he wrote, are a species of revenue that the owner in many cases enjoys without any care or attention of his own, and they owe their existence to the good government of the state. Nothing could be more reasonable than that a fund which arises from good government should be taxed peculiarly to support it. The passage is quoted to this day by advocates of a land value tax, who take pleasure in claiming the founder of free market economics as their own.

David Ricardo gave the theory of rent its rigorous form in 1817. Suppose, he argued, that a growing population must bring poorer or more distant land into use. The price of produce must then rise far enough to cover the cost of working that marginal land, which pays no rent. Owners of better land receive the same price for produce that costs them less, and the difference is rent. Rent is therefore not a cause of high prices but a result of them, and it rises automatically with the progress of population and wealth, without the landlord lifting a finger. Ricardo drew the inference that the interest of the landlord is always opposed to that of every other class in the community. Though he wrote about farmland, the reasoning applies with still greater force to cities. The marginal site is the field at the edge of town, and every plot nearer the centre commands a premium that reflects its advantage of position. As the city expands, the margin moves outward and the premiums on all the land within it grow.

The practical conclusion was drawn by Ricardo's friend James Mill, who suggested that in a country where land was newly being brought into private ownership, the state might reserve to itself all future increases in rent. His son John Stuart Mill made the idea a cause. In his Principles of Political Economy, first published in 1848 and for forty years the leading textbook in the English speaking world, the younger Mill wrote that the ordinary progress of a society that increases in wealth tends at all times to augment the incomes of landlords, independently of any trouble or outlay on their part. They grow richer, as it were, in their sleep, without working, risking or economising. What claim have they, he asked, on the general principle of social justice, to this accession of riches? He proposed that the land of the country should be valued and that any future increase in its value not attributable to the owner's improvements should be taken by taxation. He called this increase the unearned increment, and in 1870 he founded the Land Tenure Reform Association to campaign for its appropriation. Present values, which owners had bought in good faith, he would leave untouched.

More radical voices had already gone further. Thomas Spence, a schoolmaster in Newcastle, proposed in a lecture in 1775 that the land of each parish should belong to the parish as a corporation. The inhabitants would pay rent to the parish for the houses and land they occupied, and the parish would use the income to meet all public expenses, with no other taxes, dividing any surplus equally among the residents. Spence spent much of his life in and out of prison for publishing such ideas. The Scottish professor William Ogilvie argued in 1781 that every citizen had a birthright to an equal share of the original value of the soil. Thomas Paine, in Agrarian Justice in 1797, distinguished as Smith had done between the value of improvements, which belongs to the improver, and the value of the earth itself, which belongs to all. Every proprietor, he reasoned, owes the community a ground rent for the land he holds. Paine proposed to collect it through a tax on inheritances and to pay from the proceeds a lump sum to every person on reaching the age of twenty-one, and an annual pension to everyone over fifty, as compensation for the loss of their natural inheritance. He insisted that this was a right and not a charity.

By the middle of the nineteenth century, therefore, respectable economic opinion held a position that few governments have ever acted upon. Private property in what people make, including the houses they build, is both just and useful, because it rewards effort and encourages production. Private property in the value of land is a different matter. That value arises from nature and from the growth of the community, it increases without effort on the owner's part, and the public has a claim upon it. The conclusion was reached not by enemies of the market but by its principal theorists. It explains why proposals to tax land have continued to find support among economists of every school, and why the argument over who should profit from rising urban land values has never been confined to the political left.

#### Socialists and anarchists

The economists wished to correct the distribution of rent while leaving the system of private enterprise intact. A different current of thought, which took shape in the first half of the nineteenth century, held that the trouble lay deeper, in private ownership of the means of life as such. Its early representatives were less interested in theory than in demonstration. They proposed to show, by building model communities, that people housed and employed on cooperative principles would be healthier, happier and more productive than those left to the competitive market.

Robert Owen was a successful cotton manufacturer who took over the mills at New Lanark in Scotland in 1800. He found a workforce living in squalor and set about improving its conditions as a matter of both humanity and business. He enlarged the workers' houses, organised the cleaning of the streets, opened a store that sold goods at near cost, and founded a school for the children, whom he withdrew from the mill until the age of ten. The enterprise remained profitable, and New Lanark became a place of pilgrimage for reformers from all over Europe. Owen concluded that human character is formed by circumstances, and that poverty and vice could be abolished by placing people in rationally designed communities of about a thousand persons, living in a great quadrangle of dwellings with common kitchens and dining rooms, surrounded by the land they farmed. He spent a large part of his fortune on an attempt to found such a community at New Harmony in Indiana in 1825. It collapsed within three years, amid quarrels over who should do the work.

In France, Charles Fourier imagined the cooperative community in still greater detail. His phalanstery was to be a vast palace housing some sixteen hundred people, with private apartments of varying size for families of different means, linked by heated galleries to common workshops, dining halls, libraries and an opera house. Residents would change tasks several times a day according to their inclinations. Fourier waited in vain for a rich patron to finance the scheme, and the colonies founded by his followers in the United States soon failed. One disciple succeeded. Jean-Baptiste Godin, a manufacturer of cast iron stoves at Guise in northern France, built beside his factory from 1859 a large residential complex that he called the Familistère, or social palace. It contained several hundred apartments arranged around glass roofed courtyards, with a cooperative shop, a nursery, schools, a theatre and a swimming bath, and Godin eventually transferred ownership of both factory and dwellings to an association of the workers. It functioned on that basis until 1968, the most durable of all the utopian experiments.

Pierre-Joseph Proudhon supplied the movement's most famous phrase. In a pamphlet of 1840 he asked what property is and answered that it is theft. The formula was more precise than it sounds. Proudhon distinguished between possession, by which he meant a person's control over the house he lives in, the land he works and the tools he uses, and property, by which he meant the legal power to draw an income from things one does not use oneself, in the form of rent, interest or profit. The first he regarded as the foundation of liberty. The second was an exaction on the labour of others, made possible only by the law. A peasant's ownership of his farm or a craftsman's of his workshop was therefore legitimate, and the landlord's ownership of a tenanted house was not. Proudhon was no believer in the state, which he feared more than the proprietor, and he later wrote that property, in the hands of the small independent owner, was the only force capable of counterbalancing it.

Applied to housing, Proudhon's doctrine led to a concrete proposal. Rent should be treated as a payment towards purchase. Each sum a tenant paid would be credited against the value of his dwelling, and when the total equalled the price he would become its owner. Landlordism would thereby be extinguished gradually, without violence and without state ownership, and society would come to consist of independent households, each possessing its own home. The idea had wide appeal among French and German workers in the 1860s, and versions of it have reappeared ever since, in the rent to buy schemes of housing reformers and in the sale of public housing to its tenants.

Karl Marx regarded all this as sentimental. In his analysis, the central fact of modern society is the separation of the mass of the population from any means of supporting itself except the sale of its labour. The chapters of Capital on what he called primitive accumulation tell the story of the English enclosures as a history of expropriation, written, as he put it, in letters of blood and fire. The landless labourer did not arise because some people were thrifty and others idle. He was made by driving the peasantry from the soil. Landed property is thus the precondition of capitalism, and ground rent is a portion of the surplus produced by workers that the landowner is able to intercept by virtue of his monopoly of a natural resource. Marx accepted Ricardo's account of the rent that arises from differences in fertility and location, and added that the owner of even the worst land in use can exact a payment simply because he can withhold it. He noted that in the cities the gain accrues to the owner of the ground, not the builder, and he cited the testimony of a large London speculative builder that his profit came from the improved ground rents, not from the houses. The first measure listed in the programme of the Communist Manifesto in 1848 was the abolition of property in land and the application of all rents of land to public purposes.

The specific application to housing was made by Friedrich Engels. His first book, The Condition of the Working Class in England, published in 1845 when he was twenty-four, described the districts of Manchester house by house and court by court, and argued that the arrangement of the city allowed the prosperous to travel from their suburbs to their offices without ever seeing how the workers lived. In 1872, during an acute shortage in the newly unified Germany, he returned to the subject in a series of articles later published as The Housing Question. They were directed against the followers of Proudhon and against middle class reformers who promised to solve the problem within the existing order.

Engels made three arguments. First, the housing shortage is not peculiar to the working class or to the present. It is a recurring result of the rapid growth of cities under capitalism, and it concerns reformers only because it has begun to affect the lower middle class. Second, the relation between landlord and tenant is not like that between employer and worker. It is an ordinary sale of a commodity, the use of a dwelling, and the tenant is cheated only in the sense that any buyer may be. To make workers the owners of their houses would chain them to one place and one employer, as the cottage and potato patch had chained the rural weaver, and would enable employers to pay lower wages. Third, the methods by which the bourgeoisie deals with slums merely move them. He named the procedure after Haussmann. A notorious quarter is demolished, to general self congratulation, and it reappears at once somewhere nearby. The only solution, Engels concluded, lies in the abolition of the capitalist mode of production. He added, almost in passing, that enough dwellings already existed in the large cities to remedy any real shortage immediately, if they were rationally used, and that a victorious working class would begin by quartering the homeless in the houses of the wealthy. The remark was remembered in Russia in 1918.

#### The positions at the end of the century

In the last decades of the nineteenth century the land question, as it was called, stood near the centre of political debate in Britain, Ireland, Germany, the United States and Australasia, and thinkers of very different temperaments converged on the view that something was wrong with private property in land. The diversity of the company is itself revealing.

Herbert Spencer was the most uncompromising individualist of the Victorian age, an opponent of state education, public sanitation and the poor law. Yet in his first book, Social Statics, published in 1851, he reasoned from his own first principle to a radical conclusion. If every person has an equal right to freedom, then every person has an equal right to the use of the earth, for no one can exercise any freedom without a place to stand. Private ownership of land, which allows some to exclude the rest, is therefore inconsistent with equal liberty, and the land should be held by society as a whole and let to individuals. Spencer later retreated, arguing that the cost of compensating existing owners made the change impractical, and he removed the offending chapter from later editions. His opponents continued to quote the original with relish.

The naturalist Alfred Russel Wallace, who had arrived at the theory of evolution by natural selection independently of Darwin, founded the Land Nationalisation Society in 1881. He proposed that the state should become the owner of all land and that occupiers should hold their plots from it as secure tenants, owning their houses and improvements outright and paying a ground rent to the public. In the same years the American Henry George published Progress and Poverty and conducted lecture tours of Britain and Ireland that drew enormous audiences. George's solution, a tax absorbing the whole rental value of land while leaving titles in private hands, is examined at length in Part Eight. Here it is enough to note that his book did more than any other single work to make land the leading social question of the 1880s, and that many who went on to found the British labour movement dated their political awakening from reading it or hearing him speak.

Among the academic economists the same conclusion was reached in more measured language. Léon Walras, one of the founders of modern mathematical economics and a firm believer in free competition, held throughout his life that land should belong to the state. Individuals, he argued, are entitled to the products of their faculties, and the community is entitled to the gifts of nature, so that rent should supply the public revenue and wages should go untaxed. Alfred Marshall, the dominant figure in English economics, distinguished in the value of a site between what was due to the owner's outlay and what he called its public value, due to the growth of the surrounding population. He regarded a tax on the second as free from the usual objections and recommended that towns levy what he termed a fresh air rate on land values, to pay for parks and open spaces.

Political liberalism was changing in the same direction. A generation of British thinkers who called themselves New Liberals, among them L. T. Hobhouse and J. A. Hobson, argued that liberty required more than the absence of legal restraint. A person without decent housing, education or security against unemployment was not free in any sense that mattered, and the state was justified in providing these conditions. To pay for them they looked first to the forms of wealth that society itself creates. Hobhouse wrote that the value of a site in London is due to London, not to the landlord, and that the community has the right to reclaim what it has made. These ideas passed directly into the programme of the Liberal government elected in 1906, whose attempt to tax land values is described later in this book.

The Catholic Church entered the argument in 1891 with the encyclical Rerum Novarum, issued by Pope Leo XIII. It was written against socialism, and it affirmed in Lockean terms that a man who has cultivated or built upon a portion of the earth has made it his own. But it rejected with equal firmness the notion that the market might be left to itself. Workers were owed a wage sufficient to support a family in frugal comfort and to allow a thrifty man to save and acquire property of his own. The law, it declared, should favour ownership, and its policy should be to induce as many as possible of the people to become owners. Property was a natural right, and precisely for that reason it should be widely distributed and not concentrated in a few hands. The document became the charter of Christian democratic housing policy in the twentieth century, with its preference for the family home, the small proprietor and the voluntary association over both the landlord and the state.

It is useful to set out the positions as they stood around 1900, since each corresponds to a tradition traced in the remainder of this book. One school, descended from Locke by way of Blackstone and the Napoleonic code, held that property in land and houses was an absolute right and that the market should be left to allocate both. A second, descended from Aristotle and restated by Leo XIII and by conservatives of many countries, held that property was good because of its effects on character and social stability, and that policy should spread it as widely as possible. A third, descended from Smith, Ricardo and Mill and popularised by George, accepted private property in buildings and rejected it in the value of land, which was to be recovered for the community through taxation. A fourth, represented by the New Liberals and the reformist wing of the labour movement, accepted the market but called on the state to regulate it and to provide directly what it failed to supply. A fifth, descended from Marx and Engels, held that the housing problem was inseparable from capitalism and would be resolved only by collective ownership. And a sixth, descended from Owen, Proudhon and the cooperative movement, looked to voluntary associations of the occupiers themselves.

None of these schools had yet been able to try its ideas on any scale. In 1900 the housing of the people in every industrial country was still provided almost entirely by private landlords and builders under the first set of principles, tempered by sanitary regulations. To understand why the other five gained a hearing, it is necessary to look at what that system had produced.

### Chapter 5: The Industrial City

The nineteenth century created a kind of settlement that had not existed before. In 1800 there was one city in Europe with a million inhabitants, and perhaps one person in forty across the continent lived in a town of more than a hundred thousand. By 1900 there were nine cities of over a million in Europe and three in the United States, and in Britain three quarters of the population was urban. Places such as Manchester, Essen, Chicago and Lodz grew from villages to great cities within a lifetime. The people who filled them had to be housed at once, by a building industry of small firms working for profit, on land whose price rose with every new arrival, and for tenants whose wages were low and irregular. What resulted was studied, photographed and denounced as no social condition had been before, and the housing ideologies of the twentieth century were formed in reaction to it.

#### Britain: from the Manchester cellar to outcast London

Britain industrialised first and met the problem first. Manchester had about seventy-five thousand people in 1801 and more than three hundred thousand fifty years later. Liverpool, Leeds, Birmingham, Sheffield and Glasgow grew at comparable rates. Housing was supplied by small investors, typically shopkeepers and tradesmen who put their savings into a few cottages, and by builders who ran up as many dwellings as possible on each plot. No authority controlled what they built. In the northern towns the characteristic product was the back to back house: two rows of dwellings built under a single roof and sharing a rear wall, so that each house had windows and a door on one side only. They were arranged around narrow unpaved courts entered through a tunnel from the street, with a single privy and a standpipe, if there was one, shared among twenty families. Leeds went on building back to backs into the twentieth century.

Below the back to back in the scale of misery was the cellar. In Liverpool in the 1840s something like a fifth of the working class population, a large part of it recent arrivals from famine stricken Ireland, lived in underground rooms without light, drainage or ventilation. In Glasgow and Edinburgh the tradition of building in stone tenements produced a different form of overcrowding. Families lived in a single room, known as a single end, in tall blocks crammed into the back courts of older buildings. As late as 1911 well over half of the population of Glasgow lived in dwellings of one or two rooms, and the city authorities fixed metal plates to the doors of the smallest houses stating the number of occupants permitted, and sent inspectors by night to count the sleepers.

The first systematic account came from an official. Edwin Chadwick, secretary to the Poor Law Commission, published in 1842 a Report on the Sanitary Condition of the Labouring Population, compiled from the returns of doctors across the country. Its statistics shocked a public that had not thought of the matter in numbers. In Manchester, Chadwick reported, the average age at death among professional persons and gentry was thirty-eight, and among mechanics and labourers seventeen, a figure dragged down by the death of more than half of all working class children before the age of five. In rural Rutland the corresponding figures were fifty-two and thirty-eight. The difference, he argued, was caused by filth: by the absence of drains, the accumulation of refuse and the contamination of water. Chadwick was a disciple of Bentham and no sentimentalist. His case was that disease made widows and orphans who had to be supported from the rates, and that prevention would be cheaper than relief. Cholera, which reached Britain in 1832 and returned in 1848, 1853 and 1866, reinforced the argument by killing the prosperous as well as the poor.

The economics of the slum were well understood by contemporaries. Land in the centre of a town was dear, because commerce competed for it, and it was there that casual labourers had to live in order to be on hand at the docks or markets at dawn. The only way a poor family could afford such a location was by occupying very little of it. An investor would therefore take a decayed house built for a single family, let it room by room, and collect in aggregate a rent far higher than any respectable tenant would have paid for the whole. A middleman, known in London as a house farmer, often took the lease and did the subletting, with no interest in the fabric beyond the term of his lease. Rents were collected weekly, tenants could be turned out at a week's notice, and a family that fell behind would move by night and start again elsewhere. For the poorest fifth of the urban population, rent absorbed a quarter or more of income and bought a single room.

Public improvements made matters worse. The new streets that Victorian cities cut through their centres were deliberately routed through the worst districts, on the theory that slums would thereby be removed. Railway companies, obliged to buy land for their lines and terminals, chose the routes where property was cheapest, which were also the most densely inhabited, and the construction of the London railways alone is estimated to have displaced some hundred thousand people in the second half of the century. No provision was made for those evicted. They crowded into the adjacent streets, which became more congested than before, and rents there rose. The process that Engels named after Haussmann was observable in every British city.

London, the largest city in the world, became the focus of concern in the 1880s. In 1883 a pamphlet by a Congregationalist minister, Andrew Mearns, appeared under the title The Bitter Cry of Outcast London. It described in plain terms the conditions in the courts of the inner districts, including families of eight in a single room and the immorality that the author believed to follow, and it caused a sensation. A royal commission on the housing of the working classes was appointed the following year, with the Prince of Wales among its members. It found that overcrowding in central London was growing worse, that rents were rising while wages were not, and that the laws already on the statute book were not enforced, partly because the members of the local boards responsible were frequently the owners of the property in question.

The most thorough investigation was the work of a shipowner. Charles Booth, sceptical of socialist claims that a quarter of Londoners lived in poverty, set out in 1886 to establish the facts, and spent seventeen years on a survey that eventually filled seventeen volumes. His investigators classified every street in London according to the condition of its inhabitants and coloured the results on maps, from black for the lowest class, described as vicious and semi criminal, through shades of blue for the poor, to red and gold for the comfortable and wealthy. Booth found that the true proportion in poverty was not a quarter but closer to a third. He concluded that the poorest, whose labour was irregular and whose presence dragged down the rest, could not be decently housed by the market at any rent they could pay, and he was led, against his own individualist convictions, to propose that the state should take responsibility for them. When a conservative businessman reached that conclusion from the evidence, the assumption that private enterprise could be left to house the urban population had begun to lose its hold.

#### Paris, Berlin, Vienna and Barcelona

The cities of the continent grew later than those of Britain and in a different shape. Most were still enclosed by fortifications in the early nineteenth century, land within the walls was scarce, and the established habit was to live in flats. Growth therefore took the form of large multi storey blocks, and the housing question presented itself as one of density and of the power of the urban landowner.

Paris doubled its population in the first half of the century within a medieval street plan. The centre was a maze of alleys in which cholera killed some eighteen thousand people in 1832 and a similar number in 1849, and in which barricades had gone up in 1830 and 1848. When Louis Napoleon made himself emperor in 1852 he resolved to rebuild the capital, and in the following year he appointed Georges-Eugène Haussmann as prefect of the Seine. Over the next seventeen years Haussmann drove broad straight boulevards through the old quarters, built sewers, aqueducts and parks, annexed the surrounding suburbs and imposed on the new streets the uniform stone facades that still define the city. The work was financed by borrowing against the expected rise in land values and carried out by expropriating property along the new routes. About three hundred and fifty thousand people are estimated to have been displaced.

The transformation was justified on grounds of health, circulation and public order, and all three motives were real. Its social effect was to sort the population by income as it had not been sorted before. The new apartment buildings along the boulevards were let at rents beyond the reach of workers, the vertical mixture of classes in the old houses declined, and the poor moved outward to the annexed districts of the north and east, to Belleville and Ménilmontant, where speculators housed them in hastily built tenements and where no boulevards came. Owners whose property was taken were generously compensated by sympathetic juries, and those whose property adjoined the new streets were enriched. Tenants received little or nothing. When the people of the eastern districts rose in the Commune of 1871, one of their first acts was to decree the remission of rents that had fallen due during the Prussian siege. Critics and admirers of Haussmann have argued ever since over whether the improvement of a city can be separated from the removal of its poor.

Berlin acquired the reputation of being the largest tenement city in the world. Its population rose from about four hundred thousand in 1850 to two million by 1900 and nearly four million in the enlarged city of 1920. The pattern of growth was set by a plan drawn up in 1862 by a young engineer, James Hobrecht, which laid out wide streets and very large blocks over the surrounding fields but left the interior of the blocks to the owners. The building regulations of the time required little more than that internal courtyards be large enough for a fire engine to turn, a square of some five metres. Owners accordingly covered their deep plots with a front building of five storeys and a succession of rear buildings of the same height around a series of small courts, into which the sun scarcely reached. The Berliners called these blocks rental barracks. A census in 1905 found that the average building plot in Berlin housed more than seventy people, compared with eight in London.

Hobrecht had defended the tenement as an instrument of social harmony, on the grounds that the official in the front apartment and the workman in the rear court would use the same entrance and their children attend the same school. Later commentators attributed the form to the land market. Because the plan designated a huge area for dense building in advance, land on the edge of the city was priced on the expectation that it would carry a five storey block, and at that price nothing lower could pay. Companies were floated to buy and hold building land, and much of the gain from the growth of the capital was absorbed by those who had bought fields at the right moment. Within the tenements, families who could not meet the rent sublet sleeping space. At the end of the century a large proportion of Berlin households took in lodgers who rented a bed, or a share of one for certain hours, and had no right to be in the dwelling at other times. Evictions provoked riots in the summer of 1872, when the destruction of a shanty settlement outside the city gates and the removal of a tenant in the Blumenstrasse set off several days of fighting with the police. It was this crisis that prompted Engels to write on the housing question.

Vienna, the capital of an empire of fifty million, was by common consent worse housed than Berlin. The typical working class dwelling consisted of one room and a kitchen lit only from the corridor, with water and lavatories shared on the landing. Rents took a quarter of a worker's wage and leases could be ended at two weeks' notice. Landlords' agents were among the most hated figures in the city, and the bed lodger was as common as in Berlin. On the eve of the First World War tens of thousands of Viennese had no dwelling of their own at all, and the homeless shelters and warming rooms were full, among others, of a young man from the provinces named Adolf Hitler. These conditions explain the intensity with which the socialist city government of the 1920s was to throw itself into building.

Barcelona offers the most poignant case, because its growth was planned by a man who wished to prevent exactly what occurred. The old city, confined within walls that the military authorities refused to demolish until 1854, was among the densest in Europe, and the engineer Ildefons Cerdà made a detailed statistical study of its working class, relating mortality to the space and air available in each street. His plan for the extension of the city, approved by the government in Madrid in 1859, covered the plain beyond the walls with a grid of identical square blocks with cut off corners. Cerdà intended that each block should be built upon on only two sides, to a moderate height, leaving more than half its area as garden, and that all classes should be distributed evenly across a city with no privileged centre. He was one of the first to use the word urbanisation, and he conceived the plan as an instrument of equality and public health.

The owners of the land had other ideas, and the municipality gave way to them. Over the following decades the permitted building depth and height were repeatedly increased. The blocks were built up on all four sides, then raised by additional storeys, and their interior gardens were filled with workshops and warehouses. The Eixample, as the extension is called, became a district of elegant apartment buildings for the middle classes, with a density several times what Cerdà had proposed, while the workers remained in the old city or settled in the industrial suburbs and, later, in shanties on the hill of Montjuïc. Madrid followed a similar course under the plan of Carlos María de Castro. The experience suggested a lesson that later planners would relearn: a layout of streets, however enlightened, cannot determine who will live in a city or how well, so long as the value of every plot is left to be maximised by its owner.

#### New York and the American city

The United States was supposed to be different. Land was abundant, wages were higher than in Europe, and the national ideal was the independent family in its own house. In much of the country the ideal was within reach. But the great ports that received the immigrants, and New York above all, reproduced the conditions of the European city with an intensity of their own.

The standard Manhattan lot of twenty-five feet by a hundred had been designed for a single family row house. As the well to do moved uptown from the 1830s, their former houses near the docks were divided among several families, and extra buildings were put up in the back yards. Builders then began to erect structures intended from the start for multiple occupation, covering nearly the whole of the lot to a height of five or six storeys, with four apartments to a floor. The rooms ran in a line from front to back, and only those at each end had a window. The interior rooms, where people slept, had neither light nor air. Such buildings were called tenant houses and later tenements, a word that in American usage came to mean a slum dwelling. By 1865 there were about fifteen thousand of them in the city, housing half a million people.

The first regulation, a state law of 1867, required a window in every sleeping room, and owners complied by cutting an opening into the next room. In 1878 a trade journal held a competition for a better design that could be built on the standard lot. The winning plan, narrowed in the middle to form an air shaft between adjoining buildings, was nicknamed the dumbbell from its shape, and a law of 1879 effectively made it the standard. Reformers regarded it as an advance. In practice the shaft, about five feet wide and six storeys deep, admitted little light, carried noise and cooking smells from one flat to another, served as a chute for refuse and acted as a flue in a fire. Tens of thousands of dumbbell tenements were built in the next two decades. By the 1890s the Tenth Ward on the Lower East Side held more than seven hundred people to the acre, a density greater than any district of London, Paris or, as far as was known, Bombay.

These conditions were brought before the public by a Danish immigrant who had himself slept in police station lodging houses on arriving in New York. Jacob Riis worked as a police reporter in the tenement districts, and in the late 1880s he began to use the newly invented magnesium flash to photograph their interiors at night: lodgers packed on the floor of a room at five cents a spot, families making cigars or sewing garments in their kitchens, children asleep in doorways. His book How the Other Half Lives, published in 1890, combined the pictures with statistics, anecdote and a moral appeal to the conscience and the self interest of the comfortable. Riis was no radical. He believed in private enterprise and placed his hopes in model tenements, parks, schools and the enforcement of the law. But his images made it impossible to deny what the market had produced, and they won him the friendship of Theodore Roosevelt, then head of the city's police board.

The result, after an exhibition organised by the reformer Lawrence Veiller and a state commission that he directed, was the Tenement House Act of 1901. It prohibited the dumbbell for new buildings, required that every room have a window opening on the street, the yard or a court of substantial size, limited the proportion of the lot that could be covered, and demanded a lavatory in each apartment, running water and fireproof stairs. It also required the alteration of existing buildings, and a new city department was created to inspect them. The act became the model for legislation in other American cities. It did what regulation can do and showed what it cannot. The new law tenements were far better than the old, they needed two lots where one had served before, and their rents were correspondingly higher. The poorest families stayed in the old buildings, many of which remained in use for another half century.

American reformers of this generation set their faces firmly against the remedy that Europeans were beginning to consider. Veiller, the most influential of them, held that it was no business of government to build or own housing, that municipal tenements would be badly managed and corruptly let, and that public competition would drive private capital out of the field. Restrictive legislation, in his view, was the proper and sufficient role of the state. That conviction governed American policy until the 1930s and has never ceased to influence it.

Elsewhere in the country the picture was more varied. Philadelphia, the second city, was known as the city of homes. Its small brick row houses, built by the thousand on narrow streets, were within the means of skilled workers, in part because of a local system by which the buyer of a house paid the landowner a perpetual annual ground rent in place of a purchase price for the land, and in part because of the building and loan associations that originated there in 1831. These were mutual societies in which working people saved in monthly instalments and borrowed in turn to buy a house, and by the end of the century there were several thousand of them across the United States. Baltimore followed the Philadelphia pattern. In Boston and the mill towns of New England the typical dwelling was the three decker, a wooden building with one flat on each of three floors, often owned by a family that lived on one floor and let the other two to pay the mortgage. In Chicago, the invention in the 1830s of the light timber frame held together by machine made nails, which could be put up in days by workers with little skill, made the detached wooden cottage cheap enough for labourers, and the city spread across the prairie in a low sea of small houses on small lots.

These differences are a reminder that the tenement was not the inevitable fate of the industrial worker. Where land was not monopolised or hemmed in, where building methods were cheap and where institutions existed to lend small sums to people of small means, a remarkable proportion of working class families became owners of their homes. By 1900 rather less than half of American households owned their dwellings overall, but the share was far higher in the newer cities of the interior than in New York, where it was about one in eight. The contrast shaped American thinking about housing. The slum came to be seen as an exception, a disease of a few old and crowded cities, to be cured by regulation and by helping families to escape to a house of their own. The idea that a large part of the population might be permanently and decently housed as tenants, by public or cooperative landlords, never took root in the United States as it did in Europe.

#### Beyond the North Atlantic

The industrial city was not confined to Europe and the United States. Steamships, railways and the telegraph tied ports on every continent into a single trading system in the second half of the nineteenth century, and cities such as Bombay, Calcutta, Shanghai, Buenos Aires, Rio de Janeiro, Johannesburg and Melbourne grew as fast as Manchester had. Each developed its own form of cheap rental housing built for profit, and each discovered the same connection between overcrowding, disease and unrest.

In Bombay the mill owners and private landlords housed the workers who came from the countryside to the cotton factories in buildings called chawls. A chawl was a block of three or four storeys containing rows of single rooms, each about three metres square, opening onto a common corridor or veranda, with shared latrines and taps at the end. A room was commonly occupied by a family and several lodgers, or by a group of men from the same village who slept in shifts. In 1896 bubonic plague arrived in the city, and over the following years it killed many tens of thousands. The colonial government reacted with a ferocity it would not have dared to use in Britain, searching houses, burning bedding and demolishing buildings, and a large part of the population fled. In 1898 it established the Bombay Improvement Trust, with powers to clear congested areas, lay out new streets and build sanitary housing. The Trust demolished far more dwellings than it built and rehoused few of those it displaced, and overcrowding in the remaining chawls increased. It was the first of many improvement trusts in Indian cities, and its record anticipated the criticisms later made of slum clearance everywhere.

Calcutta, the capital of British India, presented the contrast between the two halves of a colonial city in its sharpest form. European officials and merchants lived in spacious houses in planned districts. The majority of the Indian population lived in settlements known as bustees, clusters of huts of mud and thatch or tile built on land rented from a landlord, with the hut itself frequently owned by a third party who let it to the occupier. Three layers of interest, the landowner, the hut owner and the tenant, made any improvement difficult, since none would pay for drains that benefited the others. The same layering of rights, and the same paralysis, persisted in the bustees for the following century.

Shanghai developed one of the most thoroughly commercial housing markets in the world. After the opening of the port to foreign trade in the 1840s, areas outside the Chinese city were set aside for foreign residence under foreign administration. When rebellion in the surrounding provinces drove hundreds of thousands of Chinese refugees into these settlements in the 1850s and 1860s, foreign merchants found that building houses for them was more profitable than trading in tea and silk. They developed a standard product, rows of two storey terraced houses arranged along narrow lanes that ran off the main street through an arched gateway, combining an English terrace layout with the internal courtyard of a Chinese house. These lane neighbourhoods, known as lilong, eventually housed the majority of the city's inhabitants. The land was held by a small number of firms and families, among them the Sassoons and the Hardoons, whose fortunes rested on urban rents, and the houses were sublet room by room through principal tenants who lived on the difference. By the 1930s a house designed for one family commonly held five or six.

In Latin America the equivalent of the tenement was a building of one or two storeys with a long central patio lined on both sides with single rooms. In Buenos Aires it was called a conventillo, in Mexico City a vecindad, in Rio de Janeiro a cortiço, and in Santiago and Lima by other names. Many were old colonial mansions abandoned by the wealthy and subdivided, and others were purpose built by investors. Each room housed a family, and the patio, with its shared tap, washing troughs and latrines, was the common living space. Buenos Aires, which grew from under two hundred thousand people in 1870 to a million and a half by 1914 on the strength of immigration from Italy and Spain, had some two thousand conventillos at the turn of the century, sheltering perhaps a quarter of the population in the central districts.

It was there that one of the first great tenants' movements took place. In 1907, when landlords raised rents after an increase in municipal taxes, the residents of a conventillo refused to pay. The strike spread within weeks to some two thousand buildings and more than a hundred thousand tenants in Buenos Aires and other Argentine cities. It was organised largely by women, with the support of anarchist and socialist groups, and when the police came to carry out evictions they were met by crowds of women and children armed with brooms, who declared that they were sweeping away the landlords. Some owners agreed to reductions, the leaders who were foreign born were deported under a recently passed law, and the movement subsided. It left a tradition of tenant organisation and a model that was followed in the rent strikes of New York, Glasgow, Barcelona and Veracruz over the next two decades.

Rio de Janeiro underwent its own version of Haussmann's programme. Between 1902 and 1906 the prefect Francisco Pereira Passos, who had studied in Paris, cut a grand avenue through the old centre and demolished hundreds of buildings, most of them cortiços, in a campaign that the population called the tearing down. At the same time the public health authorities under Oswaldo Cruz attacked yellow fever, plague and smallpox with compulsory disinfection and, in 1904, compulsory vaccination, which provoked several days of rioting. The poor who were expelled from the centre climbed the nearby hills and built shacks from the debris of the demolitions. One of the first hills to be settled had been occupied a few years earlier by soldiers returned from a campaign in the interior, who named it after a plant from the region where they had fought. The name was favela, and it became the general term for the self built settlements that have grown beside Brazilian cities ever since.

The British settler colonies stood at the other extreme. Melbourne in the 1880s was one of the largest and richest cities in the empire, and its workers enjoyed wages and conditions unknown in Europe. Land was plentiful, the detached single storey house on its own plot was the norm, and building societies advanced the money. Perhaps four households in ten owned their homes, a proportion far above that of any British city, and visitors called the country a working man's paradise. The same decade showed the danger that accompanies such a system. A speculative mania in suburban land, fed by British capital and by building societies and land banks that lent with abandon, drove prices to heights that bore no relation to rents. The boom broke in 1891. Banks and societies failed by the dozen, thousands of depositors were ruined, unemployment was severe, and the city's population fell. The colony took a generation to recover, and the episode has a strong claim to be considered the first modern housing bubble.

Seen together, these cases show how general the pattern was. Wherever commerce and industry drew people into cities faster than incomes rose, private investors met the demand by dividing space ever more finely, and the single room let to a family became the standard dwelling of the urban poor from Glasgow to Shanghai. Epidemics forced the authorities to intervene. They did so by demolition, which removed the buildings without addressing the poverty of the occupants, and the occupants moved on to recreate the problem elsewhere. Where there were tenants in large numbers, they eventually organised. And where land was cheap and credit was easy, ordinary people became owners, at the risk of speculative excess. All the main themes of the century that followed were present by 1900.

### Chapter 6: Reform Before the State

The generation that documented the industrial city also tried to mend it. Between about 1840 and 1914 reformers experimented with almost every method that has since been used to improve housing, with one exception. They regulated buildings, cleared slums, endowed charitable trusts, built model villages, founded cooperatives and savings institutions and designed new towns. What they did not do, until the very end of the period and then on a small scale, was to spend public money on building homes. The prevailing belief was that housing was properly a matter for private enterprise, and that the task of reform was to guide, shame or assist enterprise into doing better. The history of these efforts is the history of how that belief was tested and gradually abandoned.

#### Sanitary law, building rules and the first municipal housing

The earliest and least controversial form of intervention was sanitary. Even the strictest believer in the freedom of contract could accept that a man's liberty to build as he pleased did not extend to poisoning his neighbours. Chadwick's report of 1842 led, after six years of resistance from ratepayers and local interests, to the Public Health Act of 1848, which allowed towns in England to set up local boards with powers over sewers, water and refuse. Legislation remained permissive and patchy until the great consolidating Public Health Act of 1875, which obliged every district to appoint a medical officer and a sanitary inspector, and empowered local authorities to make by-laws governing new streets and buildings.

The by-laws changed the face of English towns. Model regulations issued by the central government laid down that every new street must be of a minimum width, that every house must have an open space at the rear, that rooms must be of a minimum height with windows of a minimum size, and that walls, foundations and drains must meet stated standards. Back to backs and enclosed courts were prohibited in most places. The result was the by-law street: long straight rows of terraced houses, each with its own yard and privy and a back alley for the removal of refuse, monotonous to the eye and incomparably healthier than what had gone before. Urban death rates in England began a sustained fall from the 1870s, and the sewer, the water main and the by-law house deserve much of the credit.

Regulation of new building did nothing for the existing slums, and Parliament approached these with reluctance. An act of 1868 sponsored by William Torrens allowed local authorities to require the owner of an insanitary house to repair or demolish it at his own cost. A more ambitious act of 1875, promoted by the home secretary Richard Cross, empowered the authorities of large towns to buy up and clear whole unhealthy areas and to sell or let the sites for the erection of new dwellings for the working classes. The authority itself was not expected to build. Birmingham under its radical mayor Joseph Chamberlain used the act to drive a grand commercial thoroughfare, Corporation Street, through its central slums, and rehoused almost none of the inhabitants. In London the Metropolitan Board of Works cleared sites and offered them to the philanthropic housing companies at a fraction of their cost. The compensation paid to slum owners was so generous that clearance became an expensive way of rewarding those who had profited from neglect, and a later act had to stipulate that an owner should receive no more than the value of his property as it would be if it were not overcrowded.

The difficulty with all such measures was arithmetical, and it was clearly stated at the time. Every improvement in standards raised the cost of a dwelling. A house built to the by-laws cost more than a back to back, and a slum cleared and rebuilt to decent standards housed fewer people at higher rents than before. Those who had lived in the condemned property could not afford its replacement. Enforcing the law against overcrowding simply turned families into the street. The medical officers who were charged with enforcement knew this and commonly refrained from using their powers. As long as wages were what they were, sanitary reform could lift the standard of housing for the regularly employed, and it did, but it could not house the poor. Either their incomes had to rise or someone had to provide dwellings at less than cost.

The second possibility was admitted into British law by degrees. The city of Liverpool, which had the worst housing in England, built a block of tenements known as St Martin's Cottages in 1869, generally reckoned the first council housing in the country. The Housing of the Working Classes Act of 1890 consolidated the earlier legislation and added a provision allowing local authorities to build and manage dwellings on their own account. The newly created London County Council, controlled by a progressive alliance of radicals and labour men, used it to clear a notorious district of Bethnal Green and erect in its place the Boundary Street estate, completed in 1900. Its red brick blocks, designed by young architects influenced by the arts and crafts movement and grouped around a raised central garden with a bandstand, were intended to show that public housing could be dignified. They were also let at rents that the former inhabitants could not pay, and only a handful of the five thousand people displaced returned to live there. By 1914 local authorities in Britain had built about twenty-four thousand dwellings, a fraction of one per cent of the stock. The principle had been conceded. The practice awaited a subsidy.

Germany pursued reform by a different route, through the powers of its cities over land. German municipalities were governed by professional mayors with long terms and wide discretion, and several used them boldly. Frankfurt under Franz Adickes pioneered the zoning of the city into districts with different permitted heights and densities, and obtained a law allowing the compulsory pooling and redistribution of awkwardly shaped plots on the urban fringe so that they could be built upon in an orderly manner. The city also levied a tax on increases in land value at the time of sale. Above all, German cities bought land. Ulm, under its mayor Heinrich von Wagner, acquired over two decades the greater part of the land within its boundaries, on which it built small houses that it sold to workers on condition that the city could repurchase them at the original price, so that the buyer could not profit from speculation. Other cities leased their land on long building leases under a right, written into the new civil code of 1900, that revived the Roman idea of owning a building on another's ground. The object in each case was to keep the increase in land values in public hands and to moderate the price of building plots.

Other countries passed framework laws to encourage non-profit building without committing the state to build. Belgium led in 1889 with an act that allowed the national savings bank to lend at low interest to societies providing workers' dwellings. France followed in 1894 with the law named after Jules Siegfried, which offered tax exemptions and cheap credit to societies building what were called cheap dwellings. Italy adopted a similar law in 1903. The Dutch Housing Act of 1901 was the most far reaching. It required municipalities to draw up building regulations and extension plans, gave them powers to condemn unfit houses and to expropriate land, and provided for government loans and subsidies to approved associations working exclusively in the interest of public housing. Little was built under any of these laws before 1914. But they created the legal and financial machinery, and the network of recognised non-profit bodies, through which the mass housing programmes of the following decades would be carried out.

#### Philanthropy and the model dwelling

If the state was not to build, and unaided enterprise built badly, there remained the possibility that the wealthy might build well out of a sense of duty. The Victorian middle classes had great faith in organised philanthropy, and from the 1840s they applied it to housing. Their aim was not to give dwellings away, which they believed would demoralise the recipients and undercut the honest builder. It was to demonstrate that sound, sanitary homes for working people could be provided on business principles at a modest profit, in the hope that ordinary investors would then follow the example.

The first bodies of this kind were founded in London in the early 1840s. One of them, the Society for Improving the Condition of the Labouring Classes, enjoyed the patronage of Prince Albert, who had a pair of model cottages built to its design beside the Great Exhibition of 1851, where they were inspected by a quarter of a million visitors. The societies and companies erected blocks of flats in the central districts, each dwelling self contained with its own water supply and lavatory, which was a novelty.
