They limited their dividends, usually to five per cent, at a time when slum property was expected to yield ten or more. The approach was summed up in a phrase that became proverbial: philanthropy and five per cent.

The movement received its greatest endowment in 1862, when George Peabody, an American banker who had made his fortune in London, gave a hundred and fifty thousand pounds, later increased to half a million, to ameliorate the condition of the poor of the city. His trustees decided to devote the fund to housing. The Peabody estates that rose over the following decades were stern five storey blocks of yellow brick around asphalt courtyards, with shared sculleries and lavatories on each landing. Tenants were required to be vaccinated, to refrain from keeping animals or taking in washing, and to be indoors by eleven at night, when the gates were locked. The rents were moderate, the buildings were solid and the death rate among the residents was well below that of the surrounding streets. By the 1880s the trust housed about twenty thousand people. The Improved Industrial Dwellings Company, founded by the printer Sydney Waterlow, housed more, and the brewing heir Edward Guinness established a further trust in 1890. All three bodies still exist as providers of social housing.

The limitation of the model dwellings was apparent within a few years. To pay even a restricted dividend on the cost of land and sound construction in central London, the companies had to charge rents that only artisans, clerks, policemen and others in steady work could afford. The trustees also selected their tenants with care, preferring families of regular habits and secure income. The casual labourers, hawkers and sweated workers who occupied the slums were neither able to pay nor likely to be accepted. Worse, when the companies built on sites cleared under the Cross Act, the blocks they erected displaced poor residents in favour of a more prosperous class. The royal commission of 1885 acknowledged that the model dwellings, admirable as they were, had not touched the problem of those in greatest need.

Octavia Hill took a different approach, founded on management instead of construction. In 1865, with money advanced by the critic John Ruskin, she bought the leases of three run down houses in a court in Marylebone that was ironically named Paradise Place. She did not rebuild. She repaired the drains and roofs, cleaned the passages, and then set about what she regarded as the real work, the reform of the relationship between landlord and tenant. She or one of the ladies she trained called every week to collect the rent in person, insisted on punctual payment, and used the visit to get to know each family, arrange small repairs, find work for the unemployed and encourage cleanliness and thrift. Arrears were not tolerated, and those who would not pay were evicted. Improvements were made gradually out of the rents as the tenants showed that they would respect them. Ruskin received his five per cent.

Hill's method spread to thousands of dwellings in London and was imitated in Europe and America, and the women she trained formed the nucleus of a new profession. Her philosophy was severely individualist. She believed that bad housing was as much a matter of bad tenants and bad landlords as of bad buildings, that the poor needed friendship and discipline more than bricks, and that any subsidy would sap their independence. She opposed municipal housing and old age pensions to the end of her life. Yet her insistence that tenants were owed a personal and continuing responsibility by those who housed them passed into the practice of the public and non-profit landlords she distrusted. She was also among the founders, in 1895, of the National Trust, and campaigned for the preservation of open spaces within reach of the urban poor.

The continent and the United States had their equivalents. In Berlin the conservative reformer Victor Aimé Huber inspired a public benefit building society in 1847. In France, Louis Napoleon financed a model block for workers in Paris, with a nursery and a wash house, which the intended residents avoided on the grounds that its locked gates and regulations resembled a barracks. A more successful French experiment took place at Mulhouse in Alsace, where from 1853 a group of textile manufacturers led by Jean Dollfus built a workers' quarter of more than a thousand small houses with gardens, which the occupants bought by instalments over fifteen years. Mulhouse became the reference point for all who argued that the solution lay in making workers into owners, and Engels singled it out for attack. In Brooklyn the businessman Alfred Tredway White built model tenements in the 1870s with open stairways and a large shared garden, and published his accounts to prove that they paid. The City and Suburban Homes Company of New York, founded in 1896, became the largest American enterprise of the kind.

The verdict on five per cent philanthropy has to be mixed. It never came near to matching the scale of need. All the model dwelling companies and trusts in London together had housed fewer than one in twenty of the working class population by 1914, and ordinary investors, who could obtain better returns elsewhere, did not follow where the philanthropists led. On the other hand, the movement proved that decent urban housing was a technical possibility, set standards of construction and equipment that later became general, and created institutions devoted to housing as a permanent social purpose and not as a speculation. Its failure was as instructive as its achievements. By demonstrating that even capital content with half the market return could not house the poor without loss, it showed that the gap between what a sanitary dwelling cost and what low wages could pay was real, and that it could be closed only with someone else's money.

#### The employer as landlord

One group of capitalists had a direct interest in how workers were housed. Employers who built factories, mines or railways away from existing towns had to provide accommodation or have no labour, and some of those in the towns concluded that healthy, settled and contented workers were worth the outlay. Company housing in the nineteenth century ranged from rows of mean cottages thrown up beside a pit to elaborate model communities that drew visitors from across the world. At its best it offered conditions far above anything the market supplied. In all its forms it raised a question that has never gone away: what happens to a person's freedom when the same party controls both the job and the home.

The early cotton spinners set the pattern. Mills driven by water had to be placed on fast streams in remote valleys, and their owners built villages around them, as Robert Owen's father in law had done at New Lanark. In the United States the Boston investors who founded the textile city of Lowell in Massachusetts in the 1820s recruited the daughters of New England farmers and lodged them in company boarding houses under the supervision of respectable matrons, with a curfew, compulsory church attendance and dismissal for misconduct. The arrangement was designed to reassure parents that factory work would not compromise their daughters' character, and for a time Lowell was shown to foreign visitors as proof that industry need not produce a degraded working class.

The most complete British example was Saltaire, built from 1853 by Titus Salt, a manufacturer of alpaca cloth. Salt moved his whole enterprise out of smoky Bradford to a site on the River Aire and erected beside an immense new mill a town of some eight hundred stone houses, laid out on a grid, with a church, a school, a hospital, almshouses, baths, a park and an institute with a library and lecture hall. The houses were graded in size according to the rank of the occupant in the mill. There was no public house, since Salt disapproved of drink, and tenants were forbidden to hang washing across the streets. The death rate was a fraction of that in Bradford. Salt was made a baronet, and his town was taken as the model of enlightened industrial paternalism.

Later ventures were more spacious and more self conscious. William Lever, a soap manufacturer, began Port Sunlight near Liverpool in 1888, housing his workers in cottages of varied picturesque design set among lawns and avenues, with an art gallery, a theatre and an open air swimming pool. He described the scheme as prosperity sharing. Instead of distributing a share of profits to his employees, who he said would only spend it on whisky and sweets, he would use the money to provide them with everything that makes life pleasant. George Cadbury, a Quaker chocolate maker, moved his factory to the countryside outside Birmingham and from 1895 developed the village of Bournville, with low density housing, large gardens planted with fruit trees and generous public open space. Cadbury differed from Lever in an important respect. He did not confine the houses to his own employees, and in 1900 he handed the whole estate to an independent trust so that it should not depend on the firm. Joseph Rowntree did the same at New Earswick near York.

The heavy industrialists of Germany built on a larger scale and with a plainer purpose. The Krupp steel company at Essen began constructing colonies for its workforce in the 1860s and by 1914 housed many thousands of families, in settlements that grew progressively more attractive in design. The company also ran its own shops, hospitals, schools, pension fund and sickness insurance. Alfred Krupp made no secret of his object. He wished to bind a core of skilled and loyal workers to the firm and to keep them out of the hands of the socialists. A worker who joined a strike or was dismissed for political activity lost his house and his pension rights together. Similar systems prevailed in the coal fields of the Ruhr, northern France, Belgium and South Wales, and in the textile colonies established along the rivers of Catalonia, where the Güell family built a complete village with a church designed by Antoni Gaudí.

The dangers of the arrangement were exposed most dramatically in the United States. George Pullman, the manufacturer of railway sleeping cars, built from 1880 a model town bearing his name on the prairie south of Chicago. It had brick houses with gas, water and indoor lavatories, paved and lighted streets, a park, a shopping arcade, a library, a theatre and a single church, whose rent proved too high for any congregation. Everything belonged to the company and was expected to return six per cent. No resident could buy his house, there was no elected local government, and company inspectors could enter any dwelling. An economist who visited in 1885 praised the physical conditions and concluded that the place was nevertheless un-American, a benevolent feudalism in which the inhabitants had everything provided for them except the right to manage their own affairs.

When depression struck in 1893, Pullman cut wages by a quarter or more and made no reduction in rents, which were deducted from pay before the workers received it. Some found that after the deduction they had almost nothing left. A delegation that asked for redress was told that the company's role as employer had nothing to do with its role as landlord, and three of its members were dismissed. The strike that followed in May 1894 was taken up by the American Railway Union, whose boycott of Pullman cars paralysed the railways of the western United States until federal troops were sent in and the union's leaders were imprisoned. A commission appointed by the president condemned the company's conduct, and in 1898 the supreme court of Illinois ruled that the company had no legal power to own a town and ordered it to sell all property not required for manufacturing. Pullman himself died the year before, and was buried at night under tons of concrete for fear that his former employees would desecrate the grave.

The lesson drawn from Pullman influenced housing policy in several ways. Trade unions in most countries became deeply suspicious of housing tied to employment, and pressed for dwellings provided by public authorities or by their own cooperatives instead. In the mining districts of the United States, where companies owned the houses and the only store and often paid in their own tokens, the eviction of strikers' families into tent camps remained a regular feature of labour disputes into the 1930s. On the other side, the better company villages showed what low density housing with gardens and open space could achieve for health and contentment, and Bournville and Port Sunlight directly inspired the garden city movement. And the general principle that had emerged, that those who house people acquire power over them and that such power needs some check, was to be applied in due course to private landlords, to municipal housing departments and to the state itself.

#### Self-help, cooperation and the garden city

The remaining tradition of reform owed nothing to the state, the philanthropist or the employer. It was created by working people for themselves, through institutions that pooled small savings, and it produced both the modern mortgage lender and the housing cooperative. At the end of the century it was joined by a movement that proposed to solve the problem of the city by starting again somewhere else.

The building society was an English invention of the late eighteenth century. In its original form a group of perhaps twenty or thirty men, usually meeting at a public house, agreed to pay a fixed sum each month into a common fund. When enough had accumulated, land was bought and a house was built, and the members drew lots or bid to decide who should have it. All continued to pay until every member was housed, and the society then came to an end. From the 1840s a new type appeared, the permanent society, which accepted deposits from savers who did not wish to borrow and made advances to borrowers who had not previously saved, and which never needed to wind up. It was, in effect, a specialised bank owned by its customers. The Halifax, founded in a Yorkshire town in 1853, grew to be the largest of them and eventually the largest mortgage lender in the world.

The societies had a political dimension that is now forgotten. Before 1867 the right to vote in English county elections belonged to men who owned freehold land worth forty shillings a year. In the 1840s radicals organised freehold land societies, which bought estates on the edges of towns, divided them into plots just large enough to qualify, and sold them to members by instalments, with the express object of manufacturing voters. Home ownership was promoted as a means of working class emancipation by liberals and radicals before it was adopted by conservatives as a defence against them. By the end of the century, building societies in Britain had well over half a million members. They served the better paid: clerks, shopkeepers, skilled artisans. In towns where wages were good and land cheap, such as the cotton and woollen districts of Lancashire and Yorkshire and the mining valleys of South Wales, a substantial minority of workers owned their houses in 1914. In London and Scotland hardly any did.

The idea crossed the Atlantic early. The first American building and loan association was founded near Philadelphia in 1831 by immigrants from the English textile districts, and the form spread until almost every town had one. Germany developed a parallel network of credit cooperatives inspired by Hermann Schulze-Delitzsch and Friedrich Raiffeisen. In each country the mutual savings institution became the principal source of loans for the purchase of modest homes, and its ethos of thrift, sobriety and independence coloured the idea of ownership itself.

The housing cooperative grew from the same root and took a different direction. In a cooperative the members did not become individual owners. The society built and owned the dwellings, and each member held a share that gave him the right to occupy one, at a rent that covered costs without profit, together with a vote in its affairs. The model appealed to workers who could not afford to buy and did not wish to remain at the mercy of a landlord. One of the first enduring examples was the Workers' Building Association of Copenhagen, founded in 1865 by employees of a shipyard at the suggestion of a doctor who had been shocked by a cholera epidemic. In Germany the movement expanded rapidly after a law of 1889 limited the liability of cooperative members to the value of their shares, and after the new national insurance funds for old age and invalidity were permitted to lend their reserves to non-profit housing bodies at low interest. By 1914 Germany had more than a thousand building cooperatives, and they had begun to produce housing of high architectural quality. In Sweden, Switzerland and Austria cooperatives were to become a pillar of the housing system. In Britain a variant called co-partnership housing, in which tenants were shareholders in the society that owned their estate, built several attractive suburbs before the war, though it never achieved comparable importance.

The most original proposal of the period came from an obscure shorthand writer employed to record the debates of Parliament. Ebenezer Howard had spent some years in the United States, had read Henry George and the utopian novelist Edward Bellamy, and had concluded that the fundamental evil was the concentration of people in great cities, where the pressure of numbers forced up rents for the benefit of the landowner. In 1898 he published a small book, reissued four years later under the title Garden Cities of To-morrow. He proposed that a company should buy some six thousand acres of ordinary farmland at agricultural prices and build upon a sixth of it a complete new town for about thirty-two thousand people, with its own factories, shops and public buildings. Each house would have a garden, the town would be limited in size, and the remainder of the estate would be kept as a permanent belt of farms and woods.

The heart of the scheme was financial. The land would never be sold. It would be held in trust for the community, and residents and businesses would pay a ground rent. As the town was built and the land rose in value, as it was bound to do once thirty thousand people had come to live on it, the rents would rise accordingly. After paying interest on the original loan, limited to a fixed rate, and gradually repaying the capital, the whole of the increase would belong to the town and would pay for its roads, schools, parks and pensions. The unearned increment that in London flowed to the ground landlords would be captured for those who created it, without confiscating anything from anyone. Howard described this as a peaceful path to real reform, and he believed that once one garden city had demonstrated its advantages, others would follow, until the great cities were drained of their excess population and the value of their land collapsed.

Howard was a persuasive man, and with the support of Cadbury, Lever and other backers a company was formed that bought an estate at Letchworth, about fifty kilometres north of London, in 1903. The architects Raymond Unwin and Barry Parker laid out a town of cottages grouped around greens and culs de sac at about twelve houses to the acre, a standard that Unwin defended in a pamphlet entitled Nothing Gained by Overcrowding. Letchworth grew slowly, since capital was short and industry hesitant, and its early residents were mocked as cranks devoted to vegetarianism and sandals. But it survived, and its land is still owned by a foundation that spends the income for the benefit of the town. A second garden city was begun at Welwyn in 1920. The design vocabulary of Letchworth, and of Hampstead Garden Suburb which Unwin planned in 1907, was immediately taken up around the world, generally without the collective ownership of land that Howard had thought essential. In Spain a comparable vision had been put forward independently by the engineer Arturo Soria, who from 1894 built on the outskirts of Madrid the first section of a linear city strung along a tramway, under a motto promising every family a house and every house an orchard and a garden.

By 1914, then, almost every instrument of later housing policy had been invented and tried. Regulation had raised the standards of new building. Philanthropic trusts and limited dividend companies had shown what good management and sound construction could achieve. Employers had built model communities. Mutual societies had made owners of the better paid, and cooperatives had begun to offer a collective alternative. Planners had devised the garden suburb and proposed to recover land values for the community. A handful of municipalities had started to build. Yet all these efforts together had housed only a small minority, and the mass of the urban working class in every industrial country still lived as tenants of private landlords in conditions that the reformers themselves considered a disgrace. What was lacking was not ideas but money and compulsion on a scale that only governments command, and governments were not yet willing. The war that began in August 1914 changed their minds. How each political tradition used the opportunity is the subject of the parts that follow.

## Part Three: The Market Tradition

### Chapter 7: The Case for the Market

The market tradition holds that housing is best provided in the way that food, clothing and most other necessities are provided: by private owners and producers responding to prices, with the state confining itself to the protection of property, the enforcement of contracts and such help to the poor as can be given without disturbing the mechanism. For most of the nineteenth century this was not so much a doctrine as an assumption. It became a self-conscious school of thought only when governments began to override it. The modern case for the housing market was formulated as a criticism of rent control, and its later developments have each been a response to some further intervention: public housing, town planning, zoning, subsidy. This chapter traces the argument, and the two that follow examine its application to land use and its record in practice.

#### Wartime rent control and the liberal reaction

Until 1914 the rent of a dwelling in every industrial country was whatever landlord and tenant agreed. Within three years, across Europe and beyond, it was fixed by law. The change came about through the pressures of war and not through any theory. In France, a moratorium declared in the first days of the conflict suspended the payment of rent by the families of mobilised soldiers. In Britain, as described earlier, the Glasgow rent strike of 1915 forced an act that froze rents and mortgage interest on working class houses at the level of August 1914 and forbade eviction except for specified causes. Germany, Austria-Hungary, Italy, Russia, the Scandinavian neutrals and most of the British dominions took similar steps. In the United States, where shortages followed the war instead of accompanying it, New York passed emergency rent laws in 1920 and the Supreme Court upheld comparable legislation for the District of Columbia the following year, Justice Holmes reasoning that a public emergency could justify a temporary regulation of an essential of life.

Every one of these measures was described as temporary, and almost none was promptly repealed. The reason lay in what happened to prices. Wartime inflation, and in central Europe the catastrophic inflation that followed, reduced the real value of frozen rents to a fraction of what it had been. By the early 1920s a worker in Vienna or Berlin was paying a few per cent of his wage for his dwelling, where before the war he had paid a fifth or a quarter. To restore rents to an economic level would have meant multiplying them several times over at a stroke, in countries where revolution was a live possibility. No government dared. Tenants, who were a large majority of urban voters, had acquired an interest in control that they have defended ever since, and the question of how to escape from a rent freeze without social upheaval has troubled policy makers in one country after another for a century.

The consequences that followed were set out most clearly in Austria, where the problem was most extreme, by a group of economists who had every reason to study it closely. Ludwig von Mises, the leading figure of the Austrian school, was an adviser to the Vienna chamber of commerce, and his younger colleague Friedrich Hayek lectured on the subject in 1928 and 1930. Their analysis became the template for all subsequent criticism. A rent held below the level that would balance supply and demand, they argued, has predictable effects on both sides of the market. Tenants who would have economised on space at the full price have no reason to do so at a nominal one. The widow stays in the large flat where she raised her family, the couple whose children have left do not move, and rooms that would have been released are retained. Hayek found that the average number of occupants per dwelling in Vienna had fallen substantially since before the war, at the very time when thousands could find nowhere to live.

The effects on mobility were as serious. A protected tenancy was a valuable possession that was lost by moving, since any new dwelling had to be obtained on the uncontrolled fringe of the market or through an official queue. Workers therefore declined to move to where jobs were available, or travelled long distances from the flat they could not afford to give up. The housing stock was distributed according to who had happened to occupy what in 1914, not according to present need or willingness to pay. Those without a controlled tenancy, above all the young and the newly arrived, bore the whole burden of the shortage, and a shadow market arose in which outgoing tenants sold the right to succeed them for a lump sum described as payment for the furniture.

On the side of supply, the landlord whose receipts no longer covered taxes and repairs ceased to maintain his building, and the fabric of the older districts decayed. More important for the future, nobody would invest in building houses to let. Even where new construction was formally exempt from control, investors had seen what the state could do to property in an emergency and expected it to do so again. Private rental building, which had housed nearly the whole urban population before 1914, never recovered in most of Europe. The critics pointed out a consequence that the supporters of control had not intended. If private capital would no longer build, the state would have to, and the taxation needed to finance public building would fall on the same economy that control had already distorted. Rent control, in their view, did not merely respond to a shortage. It perpetuated the shortage and created the case for further intervention.

The experience of other countries supplied further illustrations. In France, where control was maintained in various forms from 1914 until a partial reform in 1948, rents in Paris fell to trivial levels in real terms. Very little housing was built in the capital between the wars, a large part of the stock lacked a bathroom or an inside lavatory as late as the 1950s, and tens of thousands of buildings were in advanced decay. Britain attempted a gradual decontrol, releasing dwellings as they fell vacant under acts of 1923 and 1933, and enjoyed a great private building boom in the 1930s, though this was a boom of houses for sale. Controls were reimposed in full in 1939. A Conservative government lifted them from the more valuable properties in 1957 in the hope of reviving investment. The supply of rented homes did not increase, since owners took the opportunity to sell, and the exposure of a London landlord named Peter Rachman, who used intimidation to drive out protected tenants so that he could relet at higher rents, gave the English language a new word for the abuse of tenants and discredited decontrol for a generation.

The liberal critics did not claim that tenants gained nothing from control. Those in possession plainly gained a great deal, at the expense of their landlords. The claim was that the gain was arbitrary in its distribution, since it went to the sitting tenant regardless of need and was denied to the homeless family regardless of need, and that it was purchased at the cost of a shrinking, deteriorating and frozen housing stock. The proper remedy for a shortage, they concluded, was to let rents rise so that space would be used sparingly and new building would be called forth, and to assist those who could not pay by giving them money. That proposition has been the core of the market case from that day to this.

#### Roofs or Ceilings and the economists' verdict

The argument reached the English speaking public in 1946 through a pamphlet of some twenty pages written by two young economists at the University of Minnesota, Milton Friedman and George Stigler. Both would later win the Nobel prize, and Roofs or Ceilings? was the first publication in which either addressed a general audience. The United States had frozen rents in most cities during the war, millions of servicemen were returning, and the newspapers were full of stories of veterans sleeping in cars and chicken coops. Congress was debating whether to extend controls. Friedman and Stigler set out to show that the ceilings were the cause of the difficulty.

They built the case around a comparison. In April 1906 an earthquake and the fire that followed destroyed more than half the housing in San Francisco in three days. About a quarter of a million people were made homeless. Yet the authors, searching the newspapers of the following weeks, found no mention of a housing shortage. They found advertisements. In the first issue of a leading daily after the disaster there were dozens of offers of houses and rooms to let and only a handful of requests for accommodation. Rents had risen, families had doubled up, rooms had been let, people had moved out of the city for a time, and the reduced stock had been shared out without queues. In 1946 the same city had more housing per head than in 1940, and nothing had been destroyed. But the advertisements now ran the other way, with hundreds of people seeking homes for every home on offer. The difference, they concluded, was that in 1906 the available space had been rationed by price and in 1946 it was rationed by chance and favouritism.

Rationing by rent, they argued, is the only method that gives everyone an incentive to economise. Under a ceiling, housing goes to those who already have it, who have friends, who can pay a bribe disguised as a purchase of furniture, or who are simply lucky. Landlords, forbidden to charge more, choose among applicants on other grounds, and prefer tenants without children or of a favoured race. Owners sell houses to occupiers instead of letting them, since selling prices are not controlled, so that the supply of rental housing shrinks precisely when it is most needed. No new housing for rent is built. The authors accepted that removing ceilings would raise rents and that the poor would be hard pressed, but they held that the proper response to low incomes was to supplement incomes. They added that if the country wished to ration housing fairly it should do so openly, by official allocation of space, which no one was prepared to propose.

The pamphlet was controversial even among those who agreed with it. Its publisher, a newly founded free market institute, objected to a passage in which the authors declared themselves in favour of greater equality of income and inserted a footnote dissociating itself from the sentiment. The novelist Ayn Rand attacked the authors as collectivists for discussing the matter in terms of social utility instead of the landlord's rights. The estate agents' association distributed half a million copies. Federal rent control was wound down over the next few years, though New York retained a system of its own, and the pamphlet became the most widely read statement of the case.

Similar essays followed in other countries. The French writer Bertrand de Jouvenel described Paris in 1948 in a piece entitled No Vacancies. He reported that the average Parisian household spent less on rent than on tobacco, that a month's rent for a working class flat cost about as much as a few packets of cigarettes, and that in consequence nothing was repaired and nothing was built. Young couples lived with their parents or in hotel rooms, and the only way to find a flat was to learn of a death before anyone else did. In Britain the economist Frank Paish and in Sweden Sven Rydenfelt and Assar Lindbeck wrote in the same vein. Lindbeck's remark that rent control appears in many cases to be the most efficient technique yet known for destroying a city, next to bombing, became the most quoted sentence in the literature. A collection of these essays published in London in 1972 presented the verdict of half a century and several countries as unanimous.

Among professional economists it very nearly was. A survey of American economists in 1992 found that more than nine in ten agreed, wholly or with reservations, that a ceiling on rents reduces the quantity and quality of housing available, a level of agreement approached on few other questions. Later polls of leading academic economists have returned similar results. Textbooks of every political colouring use rent control as the standard illustration of the effects of a price ceiling, and economists of the left such as Gunnar Myrdal and Paul Krugman have endorsed the conclusion as firmly as those of the right.

The consensus needs to be stated with care, however, because the policy it condemns is not quite the policy that most places now operate. The controls of the wartime generation were hard freezes that fixed the money rent of a dwelling indefinitely, whoever occupied it. From the 1970s many jurisdictions in North America and Europe adopted more flexible systems, often called rent stabilisation or tenancy regulation. Such systems typically allow annual increases linked to inflation or costs, exempt new buildings permanently or for a period of years, permit the rent to return to the market level when a tenant leaves, and guarantee the landlord a reasonable return. Their purpose is less to hold rents below the market in general than to protect the sitting tenant from sudden large increases and from eviction without cause.

The economic analysis of these milder regimes is less clear cut. An influential survey by the economist Richard Arnott in 1995 argued that the textbook model of a competitive market did not fit housing well. Tenants face heavy costs in moving, landlords have some power over sitting tenants as a result, and a contract that limits increases within a tenancy can be understood as a form of insurance that both sides might value. Well designed regulation of this kind, he suggested, might do more good than harm, and the confident hostility of the profession owed more to the memory of the old freezes than to evidence about the new systems. Empirical studies since then, some of which are discussed in Part Ten, have tended to find that modern controls deliver real security to those they cover and also reduce the supply of rental housing to some degree, with the balance depending on the details.

The market tradition has generally not accepted the distinction as more than one of degree. Any ceiling that binds, its adherents reply, creates a gap between the regulated rent and the rent a newcomer would pay, and that gap produces the familiar results: tenants who do not move, landlords who economise on upkeep or seek to convert their buildings to other uses, and investors who build something else. A regulation that does not bind is unnecessary. They point out, too, that every flexible system is under constant political pressure to become stricter, since sitting tenants vote and future tenants do not, and that exemptions for new construction are worth only as much as investors' confidence that they will last. On this view the lesson of 1914 stands. A measure that begins as a modest protection for tenants ends by making it unprofitable to house them.

#### The philosophical foundations

The economists' objection to rent control was practical: it did not work. Behind it stood a broader set of convictions about knowledge, liberty and the nature of government, developed in the middle decades of the twentieth century by thinkers who saw the housing policies of their time as instances of a general error.

Friedrich Hayek supplied the most systematic account. His central idea was that the knowledge needed to run an economy is not available to any single mind or office. It is dispersed among millions of people, each of whom knows particular facts about his own circumstances, preferences and opportunities that no planner could collect. Prices are the means by which this scattered knowledge is brought to bear. A rise in the rent of flats in one district tells builders to build there, tells some tenants to look elsewhere, and tells owners of large houses that it would pay to divide them, without any of these people needing to know why the rise occurred. To fix the price by decree is to switch off the signal, and the official who then tries to allocate dwellings by rule must do so in ignorance of nearly everything that matters.

Hayek devoted a chapter of The Constitution of Liberty, published in 1960, to housing and town planning, and it is more qualified than his reputation might suggest. He accepted that city life generates effects on neighbours that the price system does not capture, that some framework of rules governing building and land use is therefore legitimate, and that the general principle of private property offers no simple answer to the problems of urban land. What he opposed was the attempt to supersede the market. Rent control he regarded as having done more than any other single measure, except perhaps inflation, to restrict freedom and prosperity. It made a large part of the population dependent on administrative decisions for the most important item in their lives, and accustomed them to look to authority for what they had formerly arranged for themselves. Public housing, if provided to the poor alone, would have to be worse than what unassisted workers could afford, or it would be unjust to them. If provided to everyone, it would make the state the general landlord. Either way it would have to be allocated by officials, with all the arbitrary power that implies.

A second line of argument was moral and owed more to Locke. The American philosopher Robert Nozick, in Anarchy, State and Utopia in 1974, maintained that justice in holdings is a matter of history and not of pattern. If a person acquired his property without wronging anyone, or received it by voluntary transfer from someone who had, then he is entitled to it, and no one may take it from him to bring about a preferred distribution, however benevolent the purpose. To tax one person's earnings in order to house another is to make the first work for the second without consent. On this view the landlord who charges what the market will bear wrongs nobody, since the tenant is free to refuse, and a law that compels him to accept less takes what is his. Nozick dealt with Locke's proviso about leaving enough and as good for others by reading it narrowly. An appropriation is illegitimate only if it leaves others worse off than they would have been had the thing remained unowned, and since the system of private property has made nearly everyone far better off than they would be without it, the proviso is rarely infringed.

The economist Murray Rothbard carried the same premises to their limit. He held that all legitimate property derives from original homesteading, the first use of an unowned resource, and from voluntary exchange thereafter, and that the state has no rightful role in housing or in anything else. Streets, parks and public housing estates should be handed to those who use them or sold. Zoning, building codes and planning are violations of the owner's rights, and conflicts between neighbours should be resolved by contract and by the ordinary law against nuisance. Few followed Rothbard all the way, but his position marks the pole from which more moderate proposals for privatisation and deregulation take their bearings.

A third contribution came from the economists who applied the assumption of self interest to politics. The school of public choice, associated with James Buchanan and Gordon Tullock, argued that it was naive to compare the imperfect market with an ideal government staffed by disinterested servants of the public. Voters, politicians and officials pursue their own ends like everyone else. Policies are shaped by groups that are organised and have much at stake, at the expense of the unorganised majority, each of whom loses a little. Applied to housing, the analysis predicts that rent regulation will be designed for sitting tenants and not for those seeking a home, that land use rules will be written for existing owners and not for would be residents, that public housing agencies will serve their staff and their political patrons, and that subsidies will be captured by builders, lenders and landowners. The proper comparison is between the market as it is and the state as it is, and on that comparison, the school maintained, the market usually comes off better.

These general ideas were reinforced by particular studies of what planners had done. In 1961 Jane Jacobs published The Death and Life of Great American Cities, an attack on the orthodoxies of urban renewal by a writer who lived in an old and crowded district of New York that the authorities wished to clear. Jacobs was not a libertarian and had no objection to public spending on cities. But her argument ran parallel to Hayek's. The lively and safe neighbourhoods that planners condemned as slums, she contended, were the product of countless small decisions by residents, shopkeepers and owners, and they possessed an intricate order that the planners could not see and did not understand. The estates of towers set in empty lawns that replaced them destroyed the mixture of uses, the old buildings with low rents and the constant presence of people on the street on which urban life depends. Three years later the economist Martin Anderson published a study of the American urban renewal programme under the title The Federal Bulldozer. He calculated that in its first dozen years the programme had demolished well over a hundred thousand dwellings, nearly all of them low rent, and had built about a quarter as many, most of them at rents the former residents could not afford. The displaced were disproportionately black. A programme enacted to improve the housing of the poor had reduced the supply available to them.

The free market position also drew on the work of Ronald Coase, who argued in 1960 that conflicts over land use, such as a factory whose smoke damages its neighbours, need not be resolved by regulation. If rights are clearly defined and bargaining is cheap, the parties will reach the efficient arrangement by agreement, whichever of them the law favours. The lawyer Bernard Siegan applied the idea to an actual city. Houston, the fourth largest in the United States, has never adopted a zoning ordinance, its voters having rejected the proposal on several occasions. Siegan showed in a study published in 1972 that land uses there were nonetheless orderly. Factories sought out sites near transport, shops gathered at main intersections, and residential neighbourhoods protected themselves through private covenants written into property deeds. Housing, particularly apartments, was cheaper and more plentiful than in comparable zoned cities. The market, he concluded, performed the legitimate functions of zoning without its exclusionary effects.

Taken together these writers gave the market tradition a coherent philosophy. Its elements were that no authority can know enough to allocate housing as well as prices do, that owners have rights that a just government must respect, that political intervention is steered by the interests of insiders, and that what looks like the disorder of the market is often a form of order too complex for planners to perceive. In the 1970s and 1980s, as the public housing estates of the postwar era fell into disrepute and governments ran short of money, these ideas moved from the seminar room into policy.

#### Filtering, vouchers and the market's answer to poverty

The hardest question for the market tradition has always been the one that defeated the Victorian philanthropists. A new dwelling built to a decent standard costs more than a poor household can pay. If the state is neither to build for the poor nor to hold down their rents, how are they to be housed? The tradition gives two answers, one describing how the market works and the other prescribing what government should do.

The descriptive answer is called filtering. New houses, the argument runs, are almost always built for people of above average income, because they are the ones who can afford the cost of new construction. But the families who move into them leave other homes behind. Those homes are occupied by households a step lower on the income scale, who vacate dwellings in their turn, and the chain continues downward. Meanwhile every building ages. As it grows older and less fashionable its price or rent falls relative to newer stock, and it passes to occupants of progressively lower income. The poor are thus housed in the market much as people of modest means obtain cars, by acquiring second hand what the better off bought new. The concept was developed in the 1930s by American land economists, among them Homer Hoyt, who observed that the fashionable districts of cities migrate steadily outward and that the mansions of one generation become the rooming houses of the next.

The policy implication is that anything which increases the total supply of housing helps the poor, even if not one new dwelling is built for them, and that anything which restricts supply harms them, even if it is aimed at the rich. A luxury tower, on this view, relieves pressure on the whole market. Critics have long objected that the process is slow, that housing may deteriorate into unfitness before it becomes cheap, and that in a growing and prosperous city filtering can run in reverse, as the well off buy up and renovate old working class districts. Recent research has given the theory more empirical support than it once had. Studies that follow the chains of moves set off by new apartment buildings in American cities have found that within a few years each hundred new market rate units opens up a substantial number of vacancies in neighbourhoods of below average income. A long run analysis of the American housing stock found that the typical dwelling does pass to poorer occupants as it ages, at a rate of a couple of per cent a year, though more slowly in regions where building is restricted. Filtering is real. Whether it is fast enough and reaches far enough down is the point in dispute.

The prescriptive answer is that the problem of poor people's housing is a problem of poverty and should be treated as one. If a family cannot afford a decent home, the remedy is to give it the means to pay and let it choose for itself in the ordinary market. Friedman argued that the best form of assistance was cash, through what he called a negative income tax, since the recipients knew their own needs better than any agency did. If the public insisted that the money be spent on housing, the next best form was a voucher that covered part of the rent of any dwelling the family found. Either was preferable to public housing projects, which in his view cost more per family than the market equivalent, concentrated the poor in one place, tied them to a particular dwelling and created a bureaucracy with an interest in its own survival.

The United States tested the idea in one of the largest social experiments ever conducted. Beginning in 1970 the federal government spent a decade and several hundred million dollars on the Experimental Housing Allowance Program, which gave cash payments for housing to some thirty thousand households in a dozen cities and studied the results. The main fears on both sides proved unfounded. An allowance offered to every eligible household in two entire metropolitan areas did not drive up rents to any measurable extent, because the recipients spent only a small part of the extra money on housing and treated the rest as general income. That finding reassured those who thought landlords would pocket the subsidy, and disappointed those who had hoped that allowances would greatly improve housing conditions. Congress had already acted. The Housing and Community Development Act of 1974 created the programme known as Section 8, under which a low income tenant pays a fixed share of income, now thirty per cent, towards the rent of a privately owned dwelling and the government pays the remainder up to a local ceiling. Vouchers of this kind gradually became the largest form of American housing assistance, serving more than two million households, while the construction of public housing all but ceased.

Europe moved in the same direction for reasons of its own. By the 1970s the great postwar shortages had been overcome, the budgets devoted to building subsidies had become very large, and it was observed that much subsidised housing was occupied by people who were no longer poor. Governments concluded that it would be cheaper and fairer to subsidise households according to their means than to subsidise buildings regardless of who lived in them. West Germany introduced a general housing allowance in 1965. France reformed its system in 1977, in a change described as a shift from aid to stone to aid to the person. Britain created housing benefit in 1982. In each country spending on personal allowances rose over the following decades as spending on construction fell, and the allowance became the principal instrument of housing policy.

The results have not fully borne out the hopes of the reformers, and the reason bears on the central dispute between the market tradition and its critics. An allowance works well where the supply of housing can expand in response to the extra spending power. Where supply is tight, the additional money bids up rents, and the subsidy passes through the tenant to the landlord. A French study of the extension of housing allowances in the early 1990s estimated that between half and four fifths of each additional franc was absorbed by higher rents. Research on British housing benefit found a similar pattern. In the United States, by contrast, where vouchers reach only about a quarter of eligible households and supply in most regions is more elastic, the effect on rents has been small. Allowances also have a tendency to grow. British spending on housing benefit multiplied several times in real terms after rents were deregulated, until it far exceeded what the state had ever spent on building.

Supporters of the market draw from this the conclusion that the two halves of their programme must go together. Subsidising demand without freeing supply enriches landlords, and the answer is to free supply. Their opponents draw the conclusion that the market cannot be relied on to supply housing for the poor and that public money is better spent on dwellings that remain permanently affordable. Both agree that an allowance by itself, poured into a market that cannot or will not build, is an expensive way of standing still. That shared recognition explains why the attention of the market tradition has shifted, over the past generation, from rent control to the rules that determine how much housing can be built at all.

### Chapter 8: Zoning, Planning and the Supply Argument

The leading claim of the market tradition today is that housing is expensive in successful cities because governments prevent it from being built. The claim concerns land use regulation: the body of rules, adopted mainly in the twentieth century, that determine what may be constructed on each plot. These rules were introduced for reasons that seemed compelling at the time, and they are defended today by people across the political spectrum. This chapter describes how they arose in the United States and Britain, sets out the evidence that they raise the cost of housing, considers the objections, and follows the political movement that has grown up to change them.

#### The origins of American zoning

Before the twentieth century an American landowner could build more or less what he liked on his own ground, subject to the law of nuisance and to fire and tenement regulations. The idea of dividing a city into districts, each with its own permitted uses and building forms, was imported from Germany, where Frankfurt and other cities had adopted it in the 1890s. German zoning was mainly concerned with density and height, and it allowed dwellings of different kinds to be mixed. The American version developed a different emphasis.

The first comprehensive zoning ordinance in the United States was enacted by New York City in 1916. Two concerns lay behind it. The owners of the fashionable shops on Fifth Avenue were alarmed by the northward advance of garment factories, whose immigrant workers crowded the pavements at lunchtime and, it was feared, drove away customers. And the completion in 1915 of the Equitable Building in lower Manhattan, a block of offices rising forty storeys straight up from the edge of its plot and casting a shadow over several acres, had shown what unregulated construction could do to the light and value of neighbouring property. The ordinance divided the city into residential, commercial and unrestricted districts and limited the height and bulk of buildings by formulas that produced the stepped silhouette of the skyscrapers of the following decades. Its purposes were to stabilise property values and to protect owners from one another, and it was promoted by the real estate interests of the city.

Zoning spread across the country with remarkable speed. Herbert Hoover, as secretary of commerce, appointed a committee that published in 1922 a model law for states to adopt, authorising their municipalities to zone. By the end of the decade several hundred cities and towns containing most of the urban population had done so. The constitutional question was settled in 1926 in a case from Euclid, a suburb of Cleveland, where a property company complained that an ordinance restricting most of its land to houses had destroyed three quarters of its value. The Supreme Court upheld the ordinance as a reasonable exercise of the power to protect public health, safety and welfare. The opinion contained a passage that set the tone for American land use law. In a district of detached houses, Justice Sutherland wrote, an apartment building is very often a mere parasite, constructed to take advantage of the open spaces and attractive surroundings created by the residential character of the neighbourhood. A nuisance, he added, may be merely a right thing in the wrong place, like a pig in the parlour instead of the barnyard. The apartment house was thereby assimilated to the pig.

Race was entangled with zoning from the start. Baltimore adopted in 1910 an ordinance that prohibited black residents from moving onto blocks where the majority was white, and the reverse, and a number of southern and border cities copied it. The Supreme Court struck down such laws in 1917, on the grounds that they interfered with the right of an owner to sell his property to whom he pleased. The purpose was then pursued by other means. Developers wrote into the deeds of new subdivisions covenants forbidding sale or lease to members of named races, and these were enforced by the courts until 1948. Municipalities meanwhile discovered that zoning by type of dwelling could accomplish much of what zoning by race could not. An ordinance that reserved a district for detached houses on large lots, and excluded apartments and small dwellings, kept out everyone who could not afford such a house, and in the America of the time that meant most black families. Some of the planners who drafted the early ordinances said so openly.

The system that emerged had a characteristic structure. At the top stood the district of single family detached houses, protected from every other use. Two family houses, apartments, shops and industry were assigned to districts of successively lower status. In the great wave of suburban building after 1945, the single family zone became the overwhelmingly dominant category. In most American metropolitan areas today it is illegal to build anything other than a detached house for one family on roughly three quarters of the residential land. To the rule about use, suburban governments added requirements for minimum lot sizes of half an acre, an acre or more, minimum floor areas, wide setbacks from the street, and two or more parking spaces for each dwelling. Each requirement could be defended as protecting the character of the community. Their combined effect was to set a floor under the price of entry.

The arrangement was reinforced by the fragmentation of American local government. A metropolitan area typically consists of dozens or hundreds of independent municipalities, each controlling its own land use and financing its schools from local property taxes. A suburb that admits apartments or small houses must educate the children who come to live in them and receives relatively little tax in return. A suburb that permits only expensive houses obtains high revenues and low costs. The incentive to exclude is therefore built into the structure, quite apart from any prejudice. In 1975 the supreme court of New Jersey ruled, in a case brought on behalf of poor black and Hispanic residents of the township of Mount Laurel, that a municipality could not use its zoning power to shut out low and moderate income households and must make realistic provision for its fair share of the region's need. The decision was a landmark, and four decades of litigation and legislation have followed in the attempt to enforce it.

In the 1970s a further layer was added, especially on the west coast. The environmental movement had made Americans conscious of the costs of growth, and communities that had welcomed development began to resist it. The town of Petaluma in California capped the number of dwellings that could be approved each year, and the courts allowed it. California's environmental quality act, passed in 1970 to require the study of the effects of public works, was extended by judicial decision to private development, so that any substantial project could be delayed for years by litigation over its impact on traffic, views or parking. Cities imposed growth boundaries, design review, historic districts and requirements that developers pay for new infrastructure. Proposition 13, adopted by the voters in 1978, limited property taxes to one per cent of a home's purchase price, with only small annual increases until the next sale. It gave long established owners low and falling tax bills relative to the value of their homes, and removed much of the fiscal reason for a city to welcome new residents.

The economist William Fischel has offered the most influential explanation of why these controls tightened when they did. For most American families the home is the largest asset they own, it cannot be diversified, and its value depends heavily on what happens in the immediate neighbourhood. Homeowners therefore behave in local politics like anxious shareholders in a company whose only asset is the town, and they vote accordingly, against any change that carries even a small risk to property values. Fischel called them homevoters. As inflation in the 1970s made housing an increasingly valuable investment, their vigilance increased. The outcome, in the prosperous coastal regions, was that the construction of new housing fell far behind the growth of employment, and prices began the long divergence from the rest of the country that defines the American housing problem today.

#### Britain's planning system and the green belt

Britain arrived at a comparable result by a very different route. Where the United States regulates land use through local zoning maps that say in advance what may be built on each plot, Britain has since 1947 operated a system in which almost nothing may be built anywhere without the specific permission of the local authority, which decides each application on its merits. The system was created by a socialist government for socialist purposes, and it has been maintained for three quarters of a century largely by conservative voters.

The background was the building boom of the 1930s. With cheap money, cheap land and little control, private builders erected nearly three million houses in England and Wales between the wars, most of them semi detached homes for sale along the arterial roads and around the stations of the expanding suburban railways. London doubled in area. To the families who bought them, the houses represented space, gardens and independence at a price that a clerk or skilled worker could afford, and the period was the nearest Britain has come to a free market in housing supply. To planners, preservationists and much of educated opinion, the result was a disaster: ribbon development along every main road, the countryside eaten up, and the capital spreading like a stain. The architect Clough Williams-Ellis compared the process to an octopus strangling England.

The Town and Country Planning Act of 1947, passed by the Labour government elected at the end of the war, was the response. Its central provision was that ownership of land would no longer carry the right to develop it. That right was taken into the hands of the state. An owner could continue to use his land as before, but any building or change of use required planning permission from the county or borough council, guided by a development plan. A fund was set aside to compensate owners for the loss of development value, and when permission was granted the owner was to pay a charge equal to the whole of the resulting increase in the value of the land, so that the gain created by a public decision would return to the public. The charge proved unworkable, since owners had no reason to sell land for development if they kept none of the profit, and a Conservative government abolished it in 1953. The requirement of permission remained. The effect of removing one half of the scheme was that the state now decided where development could occur, and whoever owned the favoured land collected the windfall.

The second pillar was the green belt. The planner Patrick Abercrombie, in a plan for Greater London prepared in 1944, proposed to stop the outward growth of the capital by surrounding it with a ring of protected countryside several miles wide, and to accommodate the overflow of population in new towns built beyond it. The new towns were duly built by public corporations, beginning with Stevenage in 1946, and eventually housed more than two million people. In 1955 the government invited other cities to establish green belts of their own. They now cover about an eighth of the land of England, considerably more than the area that is built upon, and they encircle London, Birmingham, Manchester, Leeds, Bristol and the university cities of Oxford and Cambridge. The purpose stated in policy is to prevent sprawl and the merging of towns. The designation says nothing about the beauty or ecological value of the land, much of which is ordinary farmland, and it has proved extremely popular.

For the first two decades the system was accompanied by building on a large scale, much of it by the state. Local authorities and new town corporations built between a third and a half of all new homes, on land they acquired at close to its existing use value, and total completions in the United Kingdom reached a peak of more than four hundred thousand in 1968. From then on the trend was downward. Council building was run down in the 1970s and virtually ended in the 1980s. Private builders did not fill the gap, and annual completions in England have seldom exceeded two hundred thousand since. Over the same period the number of households grew steadily, real incomes roughly doubled and credit became far easier to obtain. Real house prices in Britain have risen roughly four to five times over since 1970, faster than in any other large developed economy.

Economists of the market school attribute the rise chiefly to planning. Their argument is that the supply of land with permission to build has been rationed far below the level of demand, and that the scarcity value of permission has been capitalised into prices. The evidence they cite is the gap between the value of land with and without it. A hectare of farmland in the south east of England may be worth some twenty-five thousand pounds. The same hectare with permission for housing may be worth several million. The difference is the price of the planning consent, and it accounts for a large part of the cost of a new home. The economists Paul Cheshire and Christian Hilber have estimated that regulatory constraints account for a substantial share of the rise in English house prices over recent decades, and that prices would be markedly lower if the south east had been as accommodating as the north. An official review led by the economist Kate Barker in 2004 reached the conclusion that England needed to build far more than it was building to bring the growth of prices down to the European average, and that the planning system took too little account of prices as a signal of where homes were wanted.

The discretionary character of the system is said to compound the problem. Because every application is decided individually, by elected councillors who answer to existing residents, the outcome is uncertain, and objectors have many opportunities to delay or defeat a scheme. Uncertainty and delay favour large developers with the resources to wait and to appeal, and the British housebuilding industry has become concentrated in a handful of firms. Critics contrast the arrangement with the rule based systems of much of the continent, where a plan with legal force states what may be built and a proposal that complies is entitled to approval.

Defenders of planning dispute both the diagnosis and the remedy. They point out that the countryside around English cities is a public good that the market would not preserve, that the compact form of British towns saves energy and supports public transport, and that the alternative on view in the United States is not obviously preferable. They observe that developers hold large stocks of land that already has permission and build it out slowly, at the rate that will not depress local prices, so that granting more permissions need not produce more homes. They argue that the long rise in prices owes at least as much to the liberalisation of mortgage lending in the 1980s, the fall in interest rates, the tax advantages of ownership and the withdrawal of the state from building as to any constraint on land. And they note that the period in which Britain built most was the period in which the public sector built a large share on cheaply acquired land, which suggests that the missing ingredient is not deregulation but a public builder. The dispute between those who would loosen the planning system and those who would use it more actively has become the central division in British housing policy.

#### The evidence

The proposition that regulation raises the price of housing can be tested, and over the past quarter century it has generated a large body of research. The findings are more robust than those on most contested questions of economic policy, though they do not settle everything that the market tradition claims.

The modern literature begins with a simple observation by the economists Edward Glaeser and Joseph Gyourko. In a competitive market the price of a house should be close to the cost of building it plus the value of the land it needs. Across most of the United States around the year 2000 that was the case. In the cities of the interior and the south, houses sold for roughly what they cost to construct. In a group of coastal metropolitan areas, including San Francisco, Los Angeles, Boston and New York, they sold for far more. The authors showed that the gap could not be explained by the intrinsic value of land. In the expensive areas an additional quarter acre of garden added little to the price of a house, whereas the right to place a house on a separate plot was worth an enormous sum. What was scarce was not land but permission, and they described the excess of price over cost as a regulatory tax. In Manhattan they estimated that flats sold for around twice the cost of adding another floor to a building.

Later studies refined the picture. Albert Saiz measured how readily the supply of housing in each American city responds to rising prices, and found that it depends on two things: the amount of land made unbuildable by water and steep slopes, and the stringency of local regulation as recorded in surveys of planning officials. Cities constrained in either way see demand translated into higher prices, and unconstrained cities see it translated into more homes. Other researchers examined the wider consequences. For most of the twentieth century Americans moved from poorer regions to richer ones, and incomes across the states converged. Around 1980 the convergence stopped. Peter Ganong and Daniel Shoag attributed the change to housing costs. A janitor or a waiter could once improve his standard of living by moving to New York or California, and no longer can, because the higher wage is more than consumed by the higher rent. Chang-Tai Hsieh and Enrico Moretti estimated that if the most productive American cities had allowed housing to expand, enough workers would have moved to them to raise national output by a substantial margin. On this view restrictive land use policy harms not only those priced out but the whole economy.

The international comparison most often cited is Japan. Japanese land use law is set by the national government. It divides urban land into a dozen categories that run from low rise residential to industrial, and each category permits everything allowed in the more restrictive ones, so that houses and small shops may be built almost anywhere and apartment buildings across most of a city. A proposal that conforms to the rules for its zone must be approved, and neighbours have no power to block it. After the collapse of the property bubble at the start of the 1990s, the government relaxed the rules further in order to stimulate construction, culminating in a law of 2002 that allowed very tall buildings in designated districts of the major cities. Tokyo has since added well over a hundred thousand dwellings in a typical year. The population of the metropolitan area has continued to grow, yet average prices and rents changed little in real terms for two decades, and a household on an ordinary income can rent a small flat within a reasonable journey of the centre. The contrast with London, New York, Sydney and Vancouver over the same period is striking.

Within the United States, comparisons between Texas and California make a similar point. Both states have grown rapidly. The large Texan cities permit building readily on their outskirts and, increasingly, within them. Houston, which as noted earlier has no zoning ordinance, reduced its minimum lot size in the central area in 1998 from five thousand square feet to fourteen hundred, and later extended the change citywide. Tens of thousands of narrow three storey townhouses have since been built on plots that formerly held one house each. Median house prices in the Texan cities have remained a fraction of those in coastal California, and in Austin a surge of apartment construction in the early 2020s was followed by a marked fall in rents, at a time when they were rising in most of the country.

Germany is sometimes offered as a European example. Real house prices there were almost flat from the 1970s until about 2010, while they multiplied in Britain, France and Spain. The explanation usually given combines several elements: a plan based system in which conforming proposals are entitled to approval, a system of local finance that rewards municipalities for attracting residents, a large and well regulated rental sector that reduces the pressure to buy, and cautious mortgage lending. Since 2010, however, prices and rents in the major German cities have risen sharply as building has fallen short of demand, which suggests that the earlier stability depended on conditions, including a stagnant population, that no longer hold.

The evidence has not persuaded everyone, and the objections deserve a hearing. The first is that the studies establish a correlation between regulation and price without fully establishing its direction. Places that are attractive and prosperous tend both to be expensive and to adopt strict rules, and it is difficult to separate the effect of the rules from the effect of the attractions. The second is that the price of housing is the price of an asset and depends on the cost and availability of credit. The British economist Josh Ryan-Collins and others argue that the long rise in house prices across the developed world since the 1980s coincided with the deregulation of mortgage lending and a prolonged fall in interest rates, and occurred in countries with very different planning systems. On their account the supply of credit, flowing into a stock of land that is inherently limited, matters more than the supply of permits. Spain and Ireland, which built at extraordinary rates before 2008 and experienced extraordinary rises in price at the same time, are cited as cases in which supply did not restrain a credit driven boom.

The third objection comes from within the affected neighbourhoods. Residents of poorer urban districts often maintain that new market rate building raises nearby rents by signalling that the area is changing, and so accelerates the displacement of those already there. The claim has been examined closely in the past few years. Studies in several American cities that compare the surroundings of new apartment buildings with similar areas where building was delayed have mostly found that a new building slightly lowers rents in the blocks around it and reduces the rate at which existing residents are forced out, because it absorbs higher income newcomers who would otherwise have competed for the older stock. A review by three housing scholars at New York University concluded that the scepticism about supply found among many tenant advocates is not supported by the evidence, while agreeing that new supply alone will not provide for the poorest and that subsidised housing remains necessary.

A reasonable summary of the present state of knowledge would be this. Restrictions on building do raise prices and rents, substantially so in the most constrained cities, and relaxing them does allow more housing to be built and does moderate costs over time. The effect is gradual, since even a high rate of construction adds only one or two per cent to the stock each year, and it operates within a market also moved by incomes, interest rates and migration. Abundant supply is a condition of affordable housing in a growing city. The evidence does not show that it is sufficient to house people on the lowest incomes, and the market tradition's critics are entitled to point that out.

#### The YIMBY movement and the wave of reform

For several decades the supply argument was the property of economists and of the building industry, neither of which commands much public affection. Opposition to development, by contrast, had the support of neighbours, environmentalists and a good part of the left, and its opponents coined a sneering acronym for it: NIMBY, for not in my back yard. What changed the politics was the appearance, in the 2010s, of a constituency that wanted building and was willing to turn up to meetings to say so.

The movement began in the San Francisco Bay Area, where the growth of the technology industry had created hundreds of thousands of well paid jobs in a region that added very few homes. Rents for a one bedroom flat in San Francisco passed three thousand dollars a month. In 2014 a mathematics teacher named Sonja Trauss began writing to the city's planning commission in support of every proposed apartment building, on the principle that any new housing was better than none, and attending hearings to speak for the people who would live in buildings that did not yet exist. She and her allies then started suing suburban municipalities that turned down housing schemes in breach of a little used state law. They adopted the name YIMBY, yes in my back yard, and the idea spread quickly among young professionals who earned good salaries and could not afford to buy a home. Groups formed in Seattle, Boston, New York, London, Sydney, Vancouver and elsewhere, linked by social media and a shared reading list of urban economics.

The movement's analysis was that of the market tradition, but its tone and its membership were different. Most of its activists were renters, many identified with the political left, and they presented the freedom to build as a matter of fairness to the young, to newcomers and to those excluded from prosperous places by the price of admission. They pointed to the racial history of zoning and described the single family district as a wall around privilege. They argued that dense cities are better for the climate than sprawling ones. The case for deregulation, which had been associated with developers and the right, acquired progressive credentials.

In California the main arena was the state legislature, since the cities themselves had shown that they would not act. A bill introduced in 2018 by the state senator Scott Wiener, which would have overridden local zoning to permit apartment buildings of four or five storeys near railway stations and frequent bus routes, failed, and so did its successor. Narrower measures passed. A series of laws from 2016 onward obliged cities to allow a small second dwelling in the garden or garage of any house, and such units grew from a negligible number to around a fifth of all homes permitted in the state. A law of 2021 allowed most single family plots to be divided in two with a pair of dwellings on each. Other legislation shortened approval procedures, limited the grounds on which a conforming project could be refused, and strengthened a rule under which a city that fails to adopt an adequate housing plan loses the power to reject developments that include affordable units. The state began to sue municipalities that defied these requirements.

Elsewhere reform took the form of ending the exclusive single family zone.
