In 2018 the city council of Minneapolis adopted a plan allowing up to three dwellings on every residential plot, the first large American city to do so, although the plan was subsequently tied up in litigation on environmental grounds. Oregon passed a law in 2019 requiring its larger cities to permit two to four units on plots formerly reserved for one house. California, Washington, Maine, Vermont and Montana followed with statutes of their own, the last adopted in 2023 by a conservative legislature with support from both parties. Cities including Austin, Portland and Seattle reduced minimum lot sizes, removed parking requirements and allowed taller buildings along main streets.

The most thorough test of the idea took place in New Zealand. Auckland, the largest city, adopted in 2016 a new plan that rezoned about three quarters of its residential land to allow townhouses and apartments where only detached houses had been permitted. The number of dwellings approved roughly doubled within five years, most of the increase consisting of attached housing in the newly rezoned areas, and studies by economists at the University of Auckland estimated that rents in the city were substantially lower by the early 2020s than they would have been without the change. In 2021 the two main parties in the national parliament jointly sponsored a law requiring all the larger cities to allow three dwellings of three storeys on almost any plot. The consensus did not last, and the centre right party later made the rule optional for councils, but the episode showed that a reform of this kind could be enacted nationally.

Britain came to the question from its own direction. The Conservative governments of the 2010s set ambitious targets for housebuilding and repeatedly retreated from reforms to the planning system when their own voters in the suburbs and shires objected. The loss of a safe parliamentary seat near London in a by-election in 2021, attributed to anger at proposed planning changes, ended one such attempt. The Labour government elected in 2024 made the issue central. It promised one and a half million homes in five years, restored compulsory targets for local authorities, proposed to release poorer quality land within the green belts, which it called grey belt, and set about limiting the opportunities for legal challenge. Canada's federal government offered money to cities that liberalised their zoning, and the Australian states of New South Wales and Victoria overrode local controls around railway stations.

The politics of the movement cut across the usual lines. On the left, some see new private building as a necessary part of the answer, and others regard it as a gift to developers that brings wealthier residents into poor neighbourhoods, insisting that only public or non-profit housing serves those in need. Tenant organisations in several American cities have opposed YIMBY proposals on these grounds. On the right, those who favour property rights and economic growth support the freedom to build, and those who favour local self government and the protection of established neighbourhoods oppose the imposition of density from above. In the American election campaign of 2020 the president warned suburban voters that his opponents intended to abolish the suburbs. The effective division on the question often runs not between parties but between those who already own a home in a desirable place and those who do not.

It is too early for a verdict on the reforms. Where they have been in force longest, as in Auckland, Houston and Minneapolis, construction has increased and rents have risen more slowly than in comparable cities. In much of California the response has so far been modest, partly because the laws contained many exceptions and partly because high interest rates and construction costs made building unprofitable for reasons unconnected with zoning. That experience points to a limit of the supply argument. Removing a prohibition permits houses to be built. It does not cause them to be built, and when the market for other reasons will not build, a policy that relies on the market has nothing further to offer.

### Chapter 9: Market Experiments

Theories are tested when governments act on them. Since the 1970s a number of countries have tried, with varying degrees of thoroughness, to reorganise their housing systems on market principles: withdrawing the state from building, removing controls on rents and lending, converting subsidies into payments to households, and giving formal title to people who held their homes informally. This chapter examines four such experiments. None was a pure case, and each has been claimed as a success by its supporters and as a failure by its critics, sometimes on the basis of the same facts.

#### Chile and the subsidy to demand

The military government that seized power in Chile in September 1973 inherited a housing policy shaped by two decades of increasing state involvement. Successive governments had built public housing through a state corporation, regulated rents, and, under the socialist president Salvador Allende, tolerated or encouraged the occupation of urban land by organised groups of homeless families. By 1973 something like a sixth of the population of Santiago lived in settlements that had originated in such seizures, known as campamentos. The new regime entrusted economic policy to a group of economists who had studied at the University of Chicago, and housing was among the fields they set out to remake.

Their approach had three parts. The first concerned land. A national policy adopted in 1979 declared that urban land was not a scarce resource and that its apparent scarcity resulted from the artificial boundary that planners had drawn around the city. The boundary of Santiago was accordingly abolished and the area open to development was multiplied several times over, in the expectation that the price of land would fall. It did not. Owners at the edge held their land in anticipation of the gains to come, prices rose, and the policy was modified in 1985 with an acknowledgement that land was scarce after all. The episode is regularly cited in debates about whether releasing land at the periphery lowers prices.

The second part concerned the informal settlements. Between 1979 and 1985 the government carried out a programme of what it termed eradication. Some thirty thousand families living in campamentos in the wealthier districts of Santiago, where land was valuable, were moved to new housing in a handful of poor municipalities on the southern and western fringe of the city. Families in settlements on less coveted land were given title to their plots and connected to services. The policy cleared the eastern districts for private development and sorted the population of the capital by income more completely than before. Santiago became, and has remained, one of the most segregated large cities in Latin America.

The third part was the system of finance, and it is this that made Chile a model. The state would cease to be a builder and lender. Instead it would give each eligible family a single capital grant, a voucher, to be applied to the purchase of a dwelling built and sold by the private sector. To qualify, a family had to open a dedicated savings account and accumulate a deposit. Applicants were ranked by a points system that rewarded the amount and duration of saving and took account of need. The successful family combined its savings, the grant and a mortgage loan to buy a house. The scheme was summed up in the initials of the Spanish words for saving, grant and credit. Its designers argued that it was transparent, that it left the choice of dwelling to the family, and that it harnessed the efficiency of private builders.

In its early years the system worked poorly for the poor. Banks would not lend small sums to people with irregular incomes, private firms preferred to build for the middle class, and most of the grants went to households well above the bottom of the scale. The state found itself organising the construction of basic dwellings after all, by commissioning them from contractors and allocating them to voucher holders, and lending directly through a public agency. After the return to democracy in 1990 the centre left governments that followed kept the model and greatly enlarged it. For most of the 1990s Chile built around a hundred thousand subsidised dwellings a year, in a country of some fourteen million people. The officially measured shortage of housing fell by about half between 1990 and 2006, a result no other Latin American country came near, and the campamentos shrank to a small fraction of the urban population. International agencies recommended the Chilean system as the pattern for the developing world, and versions of it were introduced in Costa Rica, Colombia, Ecuador, Peru, Mexico, Brazil and South Africa.

The costs of the achievement became evident in the following decade. Because the grant was a fixed sum and builders competed on price, the dwellings were erected where land was cheapest, in very large uniform estates far from the centre of the city, and they were made as small as the rules allowed, often less than forty square metres. Many were poorly built. In the winter of 1997 the rains penetrated the walls of a large new estate in the south of Santiago, and television showed blocks of flats wrapped by their residents in sheets of plastic. The estates had few schools, clinics, shops or jobs within reach, and their inhabitants spent hours each day on buses. Crime and drug dealing took hold in some of them. Borrowers fell into arrears on a large scale, and the state eventually wrote off most of the debts.

Two Chilean researchers, Alfredo Rodríguez and Ana Sugranyes, gave the outcome a name in a study published in 2005. The housing problem, they wrote, was no longer that of people without a roof. It was the problem of those with a roof: several hundred thousand families who had become the owners of dwellings that were too small to adapt, too distant to sell and surrounded by neighbours as poor as themselves. Surveys found that a majority of residents in the large estates wished to leave and could not. A policy that had succeeded brilliantly by the measure it set for itself, the number of housing units delivered, had in the authors' view manufactured new ghettos at public expense.

Chilean governments responded by raising minimum standards, giving larger grants for sites near transport and services, funding the improvement of the worst estates and, in a few cases, demolishing blocks built only fifteen years earlier. Subsidies for renting were introduced in 2013. The underlying model remains in place. In the years after 2019, with house prices rising faster than incomes and immigration increasing, the number of families living in campamentos climbed back to its highest level in decades.

The Chilean experience is instructive for both sides of the larger argument. It shows that a system of grants to buyers, working through private builders, can produce formal housing for the poor in very large quantities and at a cost a middle income country can bear, which state building programmes elsewhere in the region had failed to do. It also shows what the market delivers when the subsidy is small and the buyer has no real choice: the cheapest possible dwelling on the cheapest possible land. The location of a home, and the quality of the neighbourhood around it, turned out to matter as much as the possession of a title, and those were the things the mechanism was least able to provide.

#### Deregulation in the rich countries

In the developed world the turn to the market was less abrupt than in Chile, but between about 1980 and 2000 it altered the housing systems of most countries. The common elements were the removal of rent controls on new lettings, the ending or reduction of subsidies for construction, the sale of public housing, the freeing of mortgage finance, and the replacement of state building by allowances to tenants and incentives to private investors. The sale of council houses to their tenants in Britain, the best known of these measures, belongs to the story of the property-owning democracy and is treated in Part Five. The present section concerns the attempt to revive the private landlord and to shift provision for the poor onto market mechanisms.

Britain's private rented sector had been shrinking since 1915. It housed nine households in ten before the First World War and fewer than one in ten by the late 1980s, squeezed between owner occupation, which was favoured by the tax system, and council housing, and discouraged by rent regulation and by the near impossibility of recovering possession from a tenant. The Housing Act of 1988 set out to reverse the decline. All new private tenancies would be at whatever rent the parties agreed. A new form of letting, the assured shorthold tenancy, allowed the landlord to recover the property at the end of a fixed term, which could be as short as six months, without giving any reason. A minister of the day summarised the philosophy by saying that rents should be allowed to rise to market levels and that housing benefit would take the strain for those who could not pay.

The act achieved its immediate purpose more completely than almost any other housing measure of the period. From 1996 lenders began to offer mortgages designed for small investors buying a property to let, and over the next two decades some two million individuals became landlords. The private rented sector doubled in size, to around a fifth of all households in England, and came to house not only students and the mobile young, as intended, but several million families with children and a growing number of pensioners. Supporters regard this as proof that controls had been suppressing a supply that the market was ready to provide. Critics point to what came with it. Tenants could be required to leave at two months' notice for no stated cause, the ending of a private tenancy became the leading cause of homelessness, and the cost of housing benefit, as the minister had foreseen, rose to more than twenty billion pounds a year, much of it paid to private landlords for dwellings that had once been council houses. In 2025 Parliament passed legislation abolishing eviction without cause, three and a half decades after introducing it.

The Nordic countries offer a pair of contrasting cases. Finland had regulated private rents since the Second World War and saw its private rental sector contract steadily. Between 1991 and 1995 it removed the controls, first on new contracts and then on existing ones. The supply of private rented dwellings, which had been falling for decades, began to grow again, and Finnish economists generally count the reform a success, noting that it was accompanied by a substantial housing allowance and by continued public support for a large non-profit sector. Sweden took a different course. A centre right government elected in 1991, facing a severe financial crisis, abolished the ministry of housing and withdrew most of the interest subsidies and tax concessions that had supported construction since the 1940s. It did not, however, free rents, which continued to be set by collective negotiation with the tenants' union at levels related to the cost and quality of the dwelling and not to its location. Building became much more expensive for those who undertook it and no more rewarding for those who let the result. Housing construction fell to its lowest level in half a century and remained low for twenty years, while the queues for rental flats in Stockholm lengthened. Advocates of the market cite Sweden as an illustration of what happens when a government removes the supports of a regulated system without removing the regulation.

New Zealand conducted the most radical experiment on the market principle that assistance should go to people and not to dwellings. Since the 1930s the state had let houses to tenants on low incomes at rents related to what they could afford. In 1991 a reforming government announced that state tenants would henceforth pay the full market rent for their homes, phased in over several years, and that all low income households, whether in public or private housing, would instead receive a cash supplement covering part of their housing costs. The state housing agency was turned into a company expected to earn a commercial return, and it sold about a sixth of its stock. The logic was impeccable in its own terms: equal treatment of public and private tenants, and choice for both. In practice the supplement covered only a portion of the increase, state tenants found their rents doubled or more, and studies during the decade reported families moving in with relatives, growing overcrowding in the poorer districts of Auckland and a rise in diseases associated with it. A Labour government elected in 1999 restored rents related to income as one of its first acts.

In the United States there was less to dismantle. In 1973 the Nixon administration suspended all federal programmes for the construction of subsidised housing, and when support resumed it took the form of the rental vouchers described in an earlier chapter. Public housing was capped by law at its existing number of units in 1998. Under a programme begun in 1992, some of the most troubled estates were demolished and replaced with smaller developments mixing subsidised and market rate homes, with the displaced tenants receiving vouchers. Roughly a hundred thousand public dwellings were removed in this way, and far fewer were rebuilt for the original residents. The principal means of producing new low rent housing became a tax credit created in 1986. The federal government allots credits to the states, the states award them to developers, private or non-profit, who undertake to let a proportion of their units at restricted rents for a period of thirty years, and the developers sell the credits to corporations and banks seeking to reduce their tax bills. The programme has financed well over three million dwellings. It is defended as having enlisted private capital and management in a field where public agencies had failed, and criticised as a complex and costly way of doing through intermediaries what the government could do directly, with the further drawback that the affordability of each building eventually expires.

Similar changes occurred elsewhere. West Germany abolished in 1990 the tax privileged status of its non-profit housing companies, following the bankruptcy of a huge housing enterprise owned by the trade unions, and many municipalities subsequently sold their housing to investment funds. The Netherlands cut its housing associations loose from government finance in the mid 1990s. Spain freed new rental contracts from control in 1985.

The record of these reforms does not lend itself to a single judgment. Where the aim was to restore a private rental sector, deregulation succeeded, sometimes spectacularly. Where the aim was to lower public spending, it frequently failed, because allowances paid at market rents proved costlier than the subsidies they replaced. Security for tenants declined almost everywhere. And the expectation that private investment, once freed, would add substantially to the total supply of housing was borne out in some countries and not in others, depending in large part on whether land for building was available, which is to say on the questions considered in the previous chapter.

#### Hernando de Soto and the titling of informal property

In the cities of the developing world the market tradition faced a different situation. There the difficulty was not that the state had displaced private enterprise in housing. It was that most housing had been produced by private enterprise of a kind the law did not recognise. Families had occupied land, built their own dwellings and traded them among themselves without titles, permits or mortgages. The Peruvian economist Hernando de Soto proposed to see in these settlements not a failure of the market but a market struggling to be born, and his ideas became for two decades the most influential free market doctrine in development policy.

De Soto's first book, The Other Path, appeared in 1986. Its title was a rejoinder to the Maoist insurgency known as the Shining Path, then at its height, and its argument was that the true revolutionaries of Peru were the migrants who had built the shanty towns of Lima and created a vast unofficial economy of workshops, street markets and minibuses. They operated outside the law, he contended, not from choice but because the law made legality impossibly expensive. His institute had tested the proposition. Its researchers set up a small garment workshop and attempted to register it by the book, without paying bribes unless there was no alternative. The process took the better part of a year. They then traced the procedure by which a group of families might lawfully obtain state land on which to build homes. It involved more than two hundred administrative steps in dozens of offices and would have taken, by their reckoning, nearly seven years, followed by several more to secure permission to build. The poor, de Soto concluded, were kept out of the legal system by a thicket of regulation that served the interests of those already inside it.

His second book, The Mystery of Capital, published in 2000, generalised the thesis. Why, he asked, does capitalism flourish in the West and fail almost everywhere else? The answer could not be a lack of assets. He estimated that the poor of the developing and formerly communist countries held real estate worth more than nine trillion dollars, many times the total of all foreign aid and investment they had ever received. But these assets were held in defective form. A house without a registered title cannot be mortgaged to raise a loan for a business. It cannot be sold except to neighbours who know and trust the occupier. Its owner cannot easily obtain utilities, cannot be held to contracts by reference to a fixed address, and must spend time and effort guarding it. Such property is, in his phrase, dead capital. In the West, by contrast, every parcel is represented by a document that allows it to lead a second life as collateral, as an address and as a tradable interest. De Soto pointed out that the United States itself had once been a country of squatters, and that it had become prosperous by enacting laws that recognised the claims people had already established on the ground. The task for developing countries was to do likewise: to discover the informal rules by which the poor already allocated property among themselves and to incorporate them into a unified legal system.

Peru put the programme into effect. A public agency established in 1996 simplified the procedure for recognising urban plots and issued more than a million titles in the settlements of Lima and other cities within a few years, at a low cost per title. The World Bank and other donors financed comparable projects in dozens of countries, from Thailand, where a national titling programme had begun in the 1980s, to Rwanda, which surveyed and registered some ten million parcels between 2009 and 2013. De Soto was received by presidents and praised by politicians of both right and left, since his message combined respect for the poor with faith in property.

The Peruvian programme and others like it have been evaluated with unusual care, and the results confirm some of de Soto's claims while contradicting the one that attracted most attention. The economist Erica Field found that families in Lima who received titles increased the number of hours they worked outside the home, because someone no longer needed to stay behind to protect the plot from rival claimants, and that fewer children were sent out to work. Households with titles also invested more in improving their houses. A study in Argentina by Sebastian Galiani and Ernesto Schargrodsky took advantage of a natural experiment. In a squatter settlement outside Buenos Aires, a law had transferred ownership to the occupants, but some of the former landowners contested the compensation in the courts, so that for many years certain families had titles and their neighbours, who had arrived at the same time and were otherwise identical, did not. The titled families had built substantially better houses, had fewer children, and kept those children in school longer.

What neither study found was any significant increase in access to credit. Banks proved unwilling to make small loans against shanty town houses, which are costly to value and almost impossible, politically and practically, to repossess. The poor, for their part, were reluctant to stake their one substantial asset on a loan. The central mechanism of de Soto's theory, the transformation of dead capital into collateral, scarcely operated. The benefits that did appear stemmed from security of possession, and critics observed that security could be provided in simpler ways. The geographer Alan Gilbert, studying the settlements of Bogotá, found that families there invested in their homes and bought and sold them freely once it was clear that the authorities would not evict them and had begun to supply water and electricity, whether or not a formal title had been issued. What mattered was the confidence of staying, and a government could confer that through recognition and services at far less expense than through a full cadastral survey.

Other objections were raised. Where land is held under customary arrangements, a titling programme must decide whose name goes on the document, and has often recorded the male head of the household at the expense of wives, or the well connected at the expense of those with weaker claims. A formal title makes a plot easier to sell, and in well located settlements that can lead to the gradual replacement of the original residents by wealthier buyers, a process some describe as displacement by the market. Titles also bring obligations, including property taxes and service charges, that some poor households cannot meet. And registration systems must be maintained. In several countries the records fell out of date within a decade, as owners sold or bequeathed their plots without paying to register the change, and informality returned.

De Soto's lasting contribution lay less in his remedy than in his description. He persuaded governments and international agencies to regard the inhabitants of informal settlements as builders and proprietors whose efforts deserved recognition, and not as invaders to be removed, and the wholesale demolition of such settlements became harder to justify. He also drew attention to the way in which excessive regulation of land and building excludes the poor from legality, a point that connects his work with the argument about zoning in rich countries. The stronger claim, that a paper title would by itself release the wealth of the poor and set off development, has not been borne out.

#### Argentina, and an assessment

The most recent experiment took place in Argentina and concerns the oldest question in this part of the book. In 2020 the Argentine congress passed, with support from most parties, a law intended to give tenants greater security. It fixed the minimum term of a residential lease at three years where it had been two. It provided that rents could be adjusted only once a year, by an official index combining inflation and wages. It limited the deposits and advance payments a landlord could demand and required contracts to be registered with the tax authority. In a country with stable prices these would have been moderate provisions, comparable to those in force in much of Europe.

Argentina did not have stable prices. Inflation was running at around fifty per cent a year when the law took effect and passed two hundred per cent in 2023. A landlord signing a three year contract with one adjustment a year faced the certainty that the real value of the rent would fall by a large fraction between each adjustment and the next. Owners reacted as the market tradition would predict. Many withdrew their properties from the long term rental market, offering them for sale, letting them to tourists in dollars or simply leaving them empty. Those who continued to let set the opening rent high enough to anticipate a year of inflation. The number of flats advertised for rent in Buenos Aires fell steeply, by some estimates to less than half its former level, opening rents rose faster than prices in general, and tenants found themselves competing for a dwindling pool. A partial amendment in 2023 did little to change matters. By then economists of nearly every persuasion in Argentina agreed that the law had hurt the people it was meant to help.

Javier Milei, a libertarian economist who described himself as an admirer of Rothbard, was elected president in November 2023 on a promise to dismantle the regulatory state. In December he issued an emergency decree that, among several hundred other provisions, repealed the rental law outright. Landlord and tenant were left free to agree any term, any currency, including the dollar, and any schedule of adjustment. The effect on supply was rapid. Within a year the number of properties listed for rent in the capital had roughly tripled, according to the main property websites. Contracts became shorter, typically two years with adjustments every three or four months. Rents for new contracts rose more slowly than inflation, so that in real terms they fell appreciably.

Supporters of the market, in Argentina and abroad, hailed the episode as the cleanest demonstration since San Francisco in 1906 that controls create scarcity and freedom relieves it, and it was widely reported in those terms. A more cautious reading is possible without denying the facts. The law of 2020 was a poorly designed regulation imposed in conditions of extreme inflation, and its failure says less about tenancy regulation in general than about the folly of fixing nominal contracts in an unstable currency. The recovery of supply consisted largely of dwellings returning to a market they had left, not of new construction. Tenants gained more flats to choose from and lost security, since a landlord may now decline to renew for any reason, and rents continued to absorb a rising share of incomes in a deep recession. The experiment showed convincingly that a badly conceived control can make a shortage worse, and that removing it brings quick relief. It could not show what kind of regulation, if any, serves tenants well in normal conditions.

With the evidence of this and the preceding chapters in view, it is possible to attempt a balance on the market tradition as a whole. Its strengths are considerable, and its critics have often been slow to concede them. It was right about hard rent control. The freezes of the first half of the twentieth century did shrink and degrade the rental stock of European cities, did distribute the remaining dwellings arbitrarily, and did prove extremely difficult to unwind. It was right that the knowledge required to allocate housing administratively is hard to come by, and that queues, points systems and official discretion produce their own injustices. It was right that programmes carried out in the name of the poor, such as urban renewal, were frequently captured by other interests and left the poor worse housed. And it has been substantially vindicated in its most important contemporary claim, that restrictions on building in prosperous cities raise the cost of housing for everyone and that the beneficiaries are existing owners. That argument has changed the terms of debate in a dozen countries and has won adherents well beyond the tradition's usual supporters.

The weaknesses are equally clear. The tradition has never had a convincing account of how the market houses those at the bottom. Filtering is slow and reaches the poorest last. Allowances are effective only where supply is elastic, they are expensive, and in most countries they reach only a fraction of those eligible. No society that has relied on the market alone has avoided a residue of slums, overcrowding or homelessness, and the countries with the least regulated housing systems are not those with the best housed populations. The tradition also tends to treat the tenant as a customer who can take his business elsewhere, when a family that must move loses its neighbours, its children's school and often its employment. The insecurity that follows from full freedom of contract is a real cost, even if it does not appear in the price.

There is a deeper difficulty concerning land. The market tradition defends the owner's right to the full value of his property, and it also demands that owners be free to build. But the value of a house in a restricted city consists in large part of the scarcity that the restrictions create. An owner who votes against development is behaving precisely as a rational holder of an asset should. The tradition asks property owners, in the name of the market, to give up the most valuable privilege that their property confers, and it should not be surprised that they decline. Its natural allies on this question turn out to be people without property, whose interests it has not usually championed.

Finally, the tradition has had comparatively little to say about what happens when housing is not merely traded in a market but becomes the principal object of a financial system: when the price of homes depends on the terms of credit, when households treat their dwelling as their main investment, and when the solvency of banks rests on the assumption that values will not fall. Those developments, which did more than any regulation to shape housing in the late twentieth century, are the subject of Part Four.

## Part Four: Housing as Finance

### Chapter 10: The Invention of the Mortgage

A house costs several times what a household earns in a year, and very few people have ever been able to buy one from savings. Whether ordinary families own or rent, and at what price, therefore depends on the terms on which money can be borrowed against property. Those terms are not facts of nature. The loan that most people in rich countries now take for granted, repaid in level monthly instalments over twenty-five or thirty years and covering most of the purchase price, was designed by governments in the 1930s, and the machinery that supplies it has been repeatedly rebuilt since. This chapter tells how the mortgage came to be what it is, and argues that the change did more than any ideology to turn the home into a financial asset.

#### From the dead pledge to the covered bond

Lending on the security of land is as old as the records. The boundary stones of Athens and the clay tablets of Babylon, described in the first chapter, recorded it. The word used in English comes from the law French of the Middle Ages and means a dead pledge. A twelfth century treatise explains the term. Under a living pledge the lender took possession of the land and its produce went to reduce the debt. Under a dead pledge the produce did not count against the debt, so that the pledge did nothing to release itself. In the form that became usual in England, the borrower conveyed his land outright to the lender, on condition that it would be returned if he repaid on the appointed day. If he was a day late, the land was lost and the debt, in strict law, remained.

The harshness of the rule was tempered in the seventeenth century by the court of Chancery, which held that a mortgage was in substance only a security and that the borrower could redeem his land by paying what he owed, with interest, even after the date had passed. This right, the equity of redemption, could be cut off only by a court order, and no clause in the contract could exclude it. The doctrine made the borrower the true owner and the lender merely the holder of a charge, and it is the foundation of the modern law of mortgages in the common law world. It also meant that taking and enforcing a mortgage was a slow and costly business, suited to lending by one gentleman to another and not to small loans on small houses.

The continent of Europe contributed the institutions that allowed mortgage lending to draw on the savings of the public at large. The first arose in Prussia after the Seven Years' War, which had left the noble estates of Silesia devastated and their owners unable to borrow. In 1769 Frederick the Great authorised the landowners of the province to form an association whose members were jointly liable for one another's debts. A member in need of funds received from the association a bond secured on his estate, which he could sell to investors. Because every bond was backed by the collective guarantee and by all the pledged land, it was safe, and it traded at a low rate of interest. The instrument was called a Pfandbrief, a pledge letter. In the nineteenth century German mortgage banks adapted the technique to urban property. A bank made loans secured on buildings, to a cautious proportion of their value, and financed them by issuing bonds backed by the whole pool of loans, which remained on its books. If the bank failed, the bondholders had first claim on the pool. No Pfandbrief has defaulted in more than two hundred years, and the covered bond, as the type is now known, remains the main source of housing finance in Germany, Denmark, Sweden, Spain and several other European countries.

Denmark developed its own version after a fire destroyed a quarter of Copenhagen in 1795. To finance the rebuilding, a group of citizens founded a mortgage credit association two years later, and the system that grew from it has a feature found nowhere else in so pure a form. Each loan to a homeowner is matched by a bond of the same amount, interest rate and term, sold on the market, so that the institution bears no risk from changes in interest rates. A Danish borrower can obtain a loan for thirty years at a fixed rate and may repay it at any time by buying back the corresponding bonds. France created its national mortgage bank, the Crédit Foncier, in 1852, at the outset of the Second Empire, and it financed a large part of the rebuilding of Paris under Haussmann. Spain established a Banco Hipotecario on the French model in 1872.

These institutions lent mainly to the owners of estates, apartment buildings and commercial property. The financing of small houses for their occupiers developed separately, through the mutual societies whose origins were described in Part Two. The British building societies by the late nineteenth century were making loans repayable in regular instalments over fifteen or twenty years, covering two thirds or three quarters of the value of a house, at a rate of interest the society could vary. The loan was amortising: each payment covered the interest due and reduced the principal a little, so that at the end of the term the debt was extinguished. This was the essential innovation. It converted the purchase of a house into a series of payments resembling rent, with the difference that the payer ended as the owner.

The United States, outside the building and loan associations, lagged behind. Before the 1930s a typical American mortgage from a bank or insurance company ran for three to five years and covered no more than half the value of the property. The borrower paid interest only during the term, and the whole principal fell due at the end in what was called a balloon payment. It was assumed that the loan would then be renewed. A buyer who could not put down half the price took out a second mortgage, and sometimes a third, at much higher rates. The system worked while property values rose and lenders were willing to renew. It contained no provision for the possibility that both might fail at once.

That is what happened after 1929. As incomes collapsed and banks came under pressure, lenders refused to roll over maturing loans and demanded repayment. Borrowers could neither pay nor refinance, and the forced sales that followed drove down prices, which impaired the security for every other loan. By the spring of 1933 about half of all home mortgage debt in the United States was in default, and foreclosures were running at something like a thousand a day. Farmers in the Midwest gathered at foreclosure auctions to intimidate bidders, and several states declared moratoria. The mortgage system had become an engine for transmitting a financial crisis into mass dispossession. The response of the Roosevelt administration created the modern housing finance system, and it is examined in the next section.

#### The New Deal and the long loan

The American government entered the mortgage market in 1933 as an emergency measure and never left it. Its first instrument was the Home Owners' Loan Corporation, created in June of that year. The corporation bought mortgages that were in default from the banks and other lenders who held them, paying with its own government guaranteed bonds, and then issued the homeowner a new loan in place of the old. Within three years it had refinanced about a million mortgages, roughly one in five of those on owner occupied homes outside farms. The terms of the new loans were as significant as their number. They ran for fifteen years, later extended to twenty-five, at a fixed rate of five per cent, and they were fully amortising, with no balloon at the end. About a fifth of the borrowers eventually lost their homes all the same. The corporation was wound up in 1951 having made a small profit, and it had demonstrated that the long level payment loan could work on a national scale.

The second instrument made that kind of loan the standard. The Federal Housing Administration, established in 1934, did not lend. It insured private lenders against loss on mortgages that met its conditions, in return for a premium paid by the borrower. The conditions were that the loan be amortised over a long term, at first twenty years and soon thirty, at a capped rate of interest, and that it cover a high proportion of the value of the house, initially eighty per cent and later ninety or more. A lender protected against default could safely accept a deposit of a tenth where it had demanded a half. To qualify for insurance a house had to meet minimum standards of construction and an appraisal of its neighbourhood, and the administration's manuals thereby set national norms for the design of dwellings and subdivisions.

The third instrument addressed the supply of funds. The Federal National Mortgage Association, soon known as Fannie Mae, was chartered in 1938 to buy insured mortgages from the institutions that originated them. A bank in a fast growing region where demand for loans exceeded local deposits could sell its mortgages and lend again, while investors in regions with surplus savings acquired a safe asset. A national market in mortgages came into being where there had been thousands of local ones. Alongside it the government chartered and insured the deposits of the savings and loan associations, and regulated the interest they could pay, so that they enjoyed a steady supply of cheap funds to lend on houses. After 1944 the Veterans Administration guaranteed loans to returning servicemen, often with no deposit at all.

The consequences were enormous. A family that before 1930 would have needed to save half the price of a house and refinance every few years could now buy with a small deposit and a fixed payment that it knew in advance for three decades. In many areas the monthly cost of buying a new house fell below the rent of a comparable one. The proportion of American households that owned their homes had stood at under half for as long as records existed. It rose from forty-four per cent in 1940 to sixty-two per cent in 1960. The long fixed rate mortgage is in one sense a peculiar product, since the lender bears the whole risk that interest rates will rise while the borrower may refinance if they fall, and no private market has ever offered it on a large scale without public support. It exists in the United States because the government created the institutions that make it possible, and Americans have come to regard it as a birthright.

The system also carried a defect that is discussed more fully in Part Five. The appraisers of the Home Owners' Loan Corporation graded the neighbourhoods of every city on maps that coloured the lowest grade red, and the presence of black residents, or of immigrants and Jews, was treated as a mark of decline. The Federal Housing Administration's underwriting manual warned against the mixing of what it called inharmonious racial groups and recommended restrictive covenants. For three decades the benefits of federally supported home ownership went almost entirely to white families in new suburbs, and older urban districts and their residents were denied credit.

Other countries reached mass home ownership by different financial routes, and the differences have lasting effects. In Britain the building societies remained the dominant lenders until the 1980s. They operated as a recognised cartel, agreeing among themselves the rates they would pay savers and charge borrowers, and when funds were short they rationed loans by making applicants wait. Their mortgages carried a variable rate, so that the borrower and not the lender bore the risk of rising interest. During the 1930s, with interest rates low, the societies offered loans over twenty-five years with deposits as small as five per cent, the builder guaranteeing part of the risk, and this financed the great suburban expansion between the wars. The state assisted through the tax system. Interest on a mortgage could be deducted from taxable income, a concession that became more valuable as tax rates rose and that survived until 2000.

Germany developed a method that reflects a different attitude to debt. The Bausparkasse, or building savings bank, first established in the 1920s, operates on a contract. A customer undertakes to save a fixed sum each month for a number of years at a low rate of interest. When he has accumulated a set proportion of his target, usually between forty and fifty per cent, he becomes entitled to borrow the remainder at a low rate fixed at the outset. The loans to those whose contracts have matured are financed by the deposits of those still saving, so that the system is a closed circle largely insulated from the capital market. Combined with mortgage bank loans limited to sixty per cent of a conservatively assessed value, it means that a German buyer typically puts down a substantial deposit accumulated over years. Austria adopted the same model, and France introduced a state supported scheme of housing savings in 1965. In Germany the consequence has been that people buy later in life, if at all, that the rate of home ownership is among the lowest in Europe, and that house prices were for decades unusually stable.

Japan set up a Government Housing Loan Corporation in 1950, which lent at fixed rates below those of the market for the construction and purchase of homes, drawing on the deposits of the postal savings system. For half a century it financed a large share of Japanese owner occupied housing. Spain, Italy and the other countries of southern Europe had weakly developed mortgage markets until the 1980s. Families there acquired homes with the help of relatives, by building in stages, or through subsidised schemes, and their very high rates of ownership were achieved with little debt.

These national differences deserve emphasis because they are easily mistaken for differences of culture. It is often said that Germans prefer to rent and that the English are attached to ownership. But in 1950 a smaller proportion of households owned their homes in England than in West Germany. The divergence since then follows the structure of finance and taxation in each country: where loans were long and deposits low, and where the tax system favoured the borrower, ownership spread and prices rose, and where credit was cautious and renting well regulated, it did not. The preference followed the system at least as much as the system followed the preference.

#### Securitisation and the deregulation of credit

For a generation after the Second World War, housing finance in the rich countries was a quiet and segregated business. Specialised institutions, whether savings and loan associations, building societies or mortgage banks, collected the savings of households and lent them on houses, under rules that limited what they could pay, what they could charge and how much they could lend. Banks proper did other things. Credit for house purchase was often rationed, and a would be borrower was expected to have saved with the institution for some time. The arrangements were dull, and they were stable. Between the late 1960s and the late 1980s they were dismantled, and the financing of homes was merged with the global capital market.

The change began in the United States with a technical innovation. In 1970 a federal agency, the Government National Mortgage Association, guaranteed the first security of a new type. A lender assembled a pool of several hundred insured mortgages and sold shares in the pool to investors, who received each month their proportion of the interest and principal paid by the homeowners. The mortgages passed off the lender's books entirely. In the same year Congress created a second government sponsored company, known as Freddie Mac, to do for the savings and loan associations what Fannie Mae did for other lenders, and both companies were soon packaging ordinary uninsured mortgages into securities carrying their own guarantee. A loan to a family in Ohio could now be owned, in effect, by a pension fund in Tokyo or an insurance company in Frankfurt.

Wall Street took up the technique in the following decade. Traders at the firm of Salomon Brothers, led by Lewis Ranieri, created a market in mortgage securities issued without any government guarantee and persuaded legislators to remove the legal obstacles to their sale. In 1983 came a refinement that divided the payments from a pool of mortgages into layers. The top layer had first claim on the money coming in and was therefore very safe. The lower layers were paid only after those above them and bore the first losses, in exchange for a higher yield. By this means a collection of loans of middling quality could be turned into a large quantity of securities rated as secure as government bonds, together with a small quantity of risky ones. Mortgage backed securities became one of the largest classes of financial asset in the world. The business of making a loan was separated from the business of holding it, and the originator, who was paid a fee and sold the loan on, no longer bore the consequences if the borrower failed to pay.

The old system meanwhile destroyed itself. The savings and loan associations held long term mortgages at fixed rates of five or six per cent and financed them with deposits that savers could withdraw at will. When inflation and interest rates rose into double figures at the end of the 1970s, they had to pay more for their funds than their loans earned, and by 1982 the industry as a whole was insolvent. Congress responded by freeing the associations to pay any rate on deposits and to invest in commercial property, land development and almost anything else, while continuing to insure their depositors. Institutions with nothing left to lose gambled with guaranteed money, and some were looted by their owners. Over a thousand failed in the following decade, and the cost to the American taxpayer exceeded a hundred billion dollars. The episode ended the era of the specialised local mortgage lender in the United States and left the field to commercial banks, mortgage companies and the securities markets.

Britain followed a parallel course. Until 1980 the government limited the growth of bank lending through a device known as the corset, and banks made few home loans. Its abolition, together with the removal of exchange controls, brought the banks into the mortgage market in force. The building societies' cartel collapsed in 1983 under the competition, and an act of 1986 allowed the societies to borrow on the wholesale money markets, to offer the full range of banking services and to convert themselves into companies owned by shareholders. Most of the large societies did so in the following years, their members voting for conversion in return for a distribution of shares. Queues for mortgages vanished. Lenders competed to offer larger loans relative to income and smaller deposits, and it became common to borrow the whole price of a house. A boom followed in the late 1980s, in which house prices nearly doubled in four years, and then a crash. When interest rates were raised to fifteen per cent, more than a million and a half households found that they owed more than their homes were worth, and some three hundred and fifty thousand homes were repossessed in the first half of the 1990s.

The pattern was repeated across the developed world. Sweden, Norway and Finland lifted their controls on bank lending in the middle of the 1980s, experienced a surge of credit and property prices, and suffered banking crises between 1990 and 1993 so severe that the Swedish state took over two of the country's largest banks. Australia and New Zealand deregulated in the same years. France, Spain and Italy liberalised later and more gradually. By the end of the century the typical loan in most of these countries covered a higher proportion of a higher price, over a longer term, than would have been thought prudent a generation earlier.

The economists Òscar Jordà, Moritz Schularick and Alan Taylor have measured the scale of the transformation in a study of seventeen advanced economies since 1870. For most of that period, they found, the main business of banks was lending to companies, and mortgages on residential property accounted for roughly a third of bank lending. From about 1960 the proportion began to climb, and by the early twenty-first century it had reached close to three fifths. The growth of the financial sector in the second half of the twentieth century, which is often described as an expansion of credit to business, was in fact overwhelmingly an expansion of lending to households against houses. The authors called it the great mortgaging. The modern bank, on their evidence, is primarily a real estate lender.

What all this credit bought is the critical question. If a relaxation of lending terms allows people to build more or better homes, its effect is beneficial. If the number of dwellings in the places people wish to live is more or less fixed, additional credit simply enables buyers to bid more for the same houses, and the result is higher prices and larger debts. A companion study by a group of German economic historians traced the price of housing in fourteen countries over a century and a half. Real prices, it found, were roughly flat from 1870 until the middle of the twentieth century, and then rose steeply, tripling on average by 2012. About four fifths of the increase was attributable to the rising price of land and very little to the cost of construction. The period of the rise coincides with the period in which mortgage credit expanded. The coincidence does not by itself prove that credit drove prices, since rising incomes and tighter planning also played their part, but it is consistent with the view that the liberalisation of lending did more to make existing homes expensive than to make new ones plentiful.

#### What finance did to the idea of home

The spread of the long mortgage changed what a house meant to those who lived in it. A tenant experiences housing as a cost. An owner with a mortgage experiences it as a cost and also as an investment, made with borrowed money, whose value rises or falls with the market. As ownership extended from a minority to a majority of households, and as prices began their long climb, a set of attitudes took hold that earlier generations would have found strange and that now seem like common sense.

The first was the idea of the housing ladder. A young couple would buy a small flat or house with the largest loan they could obtain. Rising prices would increase their equity, the share of the property not owed to the lender, and after a few years they would sell and use the gain as the deposit on something larger. The process would be repeated until, in late middle age, they owned a substantial house outright, which they might then sell in order to finance retirement. Borrowing magnifies the return. A buyer who puts down a tenth of the price and sees the house rise in value by a tenth has doubled his stake. No other form of investment available to ordinary people allowed them to borrow nine times their own capital, and during the inflation of the 1970s, when prices and wages rose rapidly while the debt stayed fixed in money terms, those who had bought at the right moment made gains that dwarfed anything they could have saved from earnings. The lesson was learned thoroughly. Renting came to be described as throwing money away, and the purpose of buying a home shifted from having somewhere secure to live towards getting onto the ladder before prices rose further.

The second was the treatment of the home as a store of wealth to be drawn upon. Lenders developed products that allowed owners to borrow against the increased value of their houses without moving: second mortgages, lines of credit secured on the home, refinancing for a larger sum. In the United States and Britain in the early 2000s households were extracting equity at a rate equivalent to several per cent of their income each year and spending it on cars, holidays, school fees and home improvements. The house had become, in a phrase of the time, a cash machine. Economists noticed that consumer spending now moved with house prices, and governments discovered that a rising housing market made voters feel prosperous even when their wages were stagnant. The sociologist Colin Crouch described the arrangement as privatised Keynesianism. Where governments had once sustained demand by borrowing themselves, they now relied on households to do the borrowing, against assets whose value depended on the continued flow of credit.

The third concerned the relation between housing and the welfare state. The sociologist Jim Kemeny observed as early as 1980 that countries with high rates of home ownership tended to have less generous public pensions and social insurance, and countries with extensive welfare states tended to have more renting. He suggested that the two were alternatives. A household that must find a deposit and meet mortgage payments in early adult life is hard pressed in exactly the years when it would otherwise be asked to pay high taxes, and so resists them. In old age the same household, living free of housing costs in a property it owns, can manage on a smaller pension. The political scientist Francis Castles later tested the relationship and called it the really big trade-off. From the 1990s governments in Britain, Australia and elsewhere began to speak openly of asset based welfare: citizens were to be encouraged to accumulate housing wealth during their working lives and to use it to pay for their own retirement and care. Housing was to do the work that social insurance had done.

The strategy had a condition that was not always stated. It functions only if house prices continue to rise at least as fast as incomes, and it functions well only for those who bought early. A generation that purchased cheaply and saw its homes multiply in value has indeed been provided for. The generation that follows must pay those multiplied prices, with larger debts relative to income, and cannot expect the same appreciation unless prices detach still further from earnings. Asset based welfare, in other words, is financed by a transfer from the buyers of the future to the owners of the present. As long as most voters are in the second group the arrangement is politically stable, and as the first group grows it becomes less so.

The fourth change was in the position of governments themselves. When most households own property and most household wealth consists of property, a fall in house prices is a national calamity. It erodes the savings of the majority, cuts consumer spending, leaves recent buyers owing more than they own and threatens the banks whose loans are secured on houses. Finance ministers and central bankers therefore acquire an overriding interest in preventing such a fall. Whatever a government may say about wanting housing to be affordable, it cannot afford to make it so quickly, and markets know this. The knowledge that the authorities will act to support prices is itself a reason to pay more. In this way the financial system, the electorate and the state became jointly committed to the proposition that the price of homes should go on rising.

None of this was planned as such. The New Deal reformers wanted to stop foreclosures and put builders back to work. The deregulators of the 1980s wanted to end the rationing of credit and give borrowers a choice. Each measure was reasonable taken singly. Their cumulative effect was to alter the nature of the thing being financed. A house had always been a durable possession and a mark of standing. By the end of the twentieth century it was, for most families in the English speaking world and a growing number elsewhere, a leveraged financial position on which their solvency, their retirement and their children's prospects depended. The view of housing as a financial product, with which this book began, was not primarily an ideology advocated by anyone. It was the outlook that naturally followed from the way homes had come to be paid for.

### Chapter 11: Bubbles and Crashes

An asset bought with borrowed money in the expectation that its price will rise is liable to a particular kind of instability. Rising prices attract buyers, their purchases raise prices further, lenders relax their terms because the security appears to be improving, and the process feeds on itself until something interrupts it. Then it runs in reverse. Land and housing are especially prone to such episodes, because supply responds slowly, because purchases are financed with debt, and because so many people have a stake in believing that prices can only go up. This chapter describes the main property bubbles of the past century, considers what they have in common, and asks what has been learned.

#### Florida, Japan and the anatomy of a land boom

The Florida boom of the 1920s is the classic American example, and it displays the mechanism with unusual clarity because so little of what was traded was real. The state had sunshine, newly built roads and railways, and a population of northerners with money to spend and cars in which to travel south. Developers bought tracts of scrub and swamp, drew street plans on them, and sold plots to buyers who never saw them. The transactions were made in the form of binders, receipts for a deposit of a tenth of the price that gave the holder the right to complete the purchase a month later. Binders were themselves bought and sold, changing hands several times a day in the streets of Miami in the summer of 1925, each buyer intending to sell at a profit before the balance fell due. The city had some twenty-five thousand estate agents. Plots described as waterfront were miles from the sea, and one promoter, Charles Ponzi, already notorious for an earlier fraud, sold land near Jacksonville that was under water.

The end came without any single cause. The railways, overwhelmed by shipments of building materials, declared an embargo on freight. Northern newspapers began to print warnings. Buyers who had counted on reselling their binders found no takers and defaulted on the balance. A hurricane struck Miami in September 1926 and killed several hundred people. Prices collapsed, banks across Florida and Georgia failed, and subdivisions were left as grids of pavement running through the palmetto, with ornamental gateways leading nowhere. The economist John Kenneth Galbraith later remarked of the episode that the Florida boom was the first indication of the mood of the decade and of the conviction that God intended the American middle class to be rich.

The Japanese bubble of the late 1980s was the largest property boom the world had seen, and its aftermath lasted a generation. Land had a special place in the Japanese economy. In a mountainous country with little level ground, urban land had risen in price almost without interruption since the war, and it was widely believed, in a phrase that became famous, that land prices never fall. Banks lent to companies on the security of their landholdings and seldom troubled to examine their business plans. The tax system favoured holding land over selling it. After an agreement among the leading industrial countries in 1985 to push up the value of the yen, the Bank of Japan cut interest rates to cushion exporters and kept them at a record low for more than two years. Money flooded into shares and property.

Between 1985 and 1990 the price of commercial land in the six largest cities roughly quadrupled, and residential land rose almost as much. At the peak the total value of land in Japan was reckoned at about four times that of the United States, a country twenty-five times larger, and the grounds of the Imperial Palace in central Tokyo were said, by a calculation much repeated at the time, to be worth as much as all the real estate in California. A flat of modest size within commuting distance of central Tokyo cost ten or more times the annual income of an ordinary salaried employee. Banks devised loans to be repaid over two generations, with the borrower's children inheriting the debt. Companies with land on their balance sheets borrowed against it to buy more land and shares, and the rising value of both justified further loans.

The authorities eventually acted. The central bank raised interest rates sharply from 1989, and in the spring of 1990 the finance ministry instructed banks to restrict the growth of their lending to property companies. The stock market fell first. Land followed from 1991 and went on falling, year after year, for a decade and a half. By the mid 2000s commercial land in the big cities had lost about four fifths of its peak value, and residential land more than half. Families who had bought flats at the top found themselves paying for thirty years on properties worth a fraction of the debt. The banks, whose loans were secured on land now worth far less than the sums advanced, concealed their losses for years and lent to insolvent borrowers to keep them alive, and several large institutions finally collapsed in 1997 and 1998. The economy, which had been the envy of the world, stagnated through what came to be called the lost decades.

The lasting effect on Japanese attitudes was profound. A generation learned that property can lose value and keep losing it. Housing ceased to be regarded as a route to wealth. The government, anxious to revive construction and no longer afraid of fuelling speculation, relaxed building controls, with the results for the affordability of Tokyo described in Part Three. Japan today is the one large rich country in which a house is expected to depreciate, and in which the young are not excluded from the capital by its price. The cure, if it can be called that, was fifteen years of falling land values and a banking system on public life support, and no other country has shown any desire to undergo it.

From these and earlier cases, such as the Melbourne boom described in Part Two, the economic historian Charles Kindleberger and others have drawn a general anatomy. A boom begins with some real change that improves prospects: a new railway, a new industry, a change in policy, a fall in interest rates. Prices rise for good reasons. The rise attracts buyers whose interest is in the rise itself, and credit expands to accommodate them, since lenders judge their security by current prices. New kinds of contract appear that allow people to buy with less money down, like the Florida binder or the hundred year mortgage. A story takes hold that explains why the old rules no longer apply: land is scarce, the population is growing, they are not making any more of it. Those who stand aside are made to look foolish for years. At some point the supply of new buyers and new credit falters, often when the authorities raise interest rates, and prices stop rising. Since many holders can meet their payments only by selling at a profit, the halt forces sales, and the decline begins.

Two features of property make its bubbles more damaging than those in shares. The first is debt. Shares are mostly bought with the buyer's own money, and when they fall the loss stops with the owner. Property is bought with loans, and when it falls the loss passes to the lender, and through the lender to the financial system. The second is duration. A share price can halve in a week and find its level. Owners of houses are reluctant to sell for less than they paid, so that sales dry up and prices decline slowly over years, during which construction ceases, builders and their suppliers are thrown out of work, and households burdened with debt cut their spending. A study by the economists Carmen Reinhart and Kenneth Rogoff of financial crises across eight centuries found that those associated with collapsing property prices were followed by deeper and longer recessions than any other kind.

#### The American subprime crisis

The crisis that began in the American housing market in 2006 and brought down a large part of the world's financial system two years later was the product of the machinery described in the last chapter operating without restraint. Its origins have been investigated more thoroughly than those of any comparable event, and although the allocation of blame remains disputed, the sequence is well established.

House prices in the United States had been rising since the mid 1990s. After the collapse of the technology share boom in 2000 and the attacks of September 2001, the Federal Reserve reduced short term interest rates to one per cent and held them there until 2004. At the same time large surpluses of savings from Asia and the oil exporting countries were seeking safe investments in dollars. Investors everywhere wanted securities that yielded a little more than government bonds and carried a top credit rating, and the mortgage market was organised to supply them.

The traditional mortgages guaranteed by Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac went to borrowers with good credit records, documented incomes and deposits. That market was finite. To produce more securities, lenders turned to borrowers who did not meet those standards, in a sector known as subprime. Mortgage brokers, paid by commission on each loan and bearing no risk once it was sold, signed up customers with poor credit histories, with no deposit, and increasingly with no evidence of income at all. The loans were designed to appear affordable. A typical product charged a low fixed rate for the first two years and then reset to a much higher variable rate. Others required payment of interest only, or allowed the borrower to pay less than the interest due, the shortfall being added to the debt. All depended on the assumption that the borrower would refinance or sell before the higher payments began, which in turn depended on the price of the house continuing to rise. Subprime and other non-standard loans grew from a small fraction of new mortgages at the start of the decade to about a third in 2006.

Investment banks bought these loans by the thousand, pooled them and issued securities in layers, as described earlier. The rating agencies, which were paid by the issuers, awarded their highest grade to the upper layers on the reasoning that defaults in different parts of the country would not all occur together. The lower layers, which were hard to sell, were themselves pooled and divided again to create a second generation of securities, most of which were also rated as top quality. Banks held large amounts of these instruments in subsidiaries kept off their balance sheets and financed them with short term borrowing. Insurance companies sold protection against their default. Because house prices had not fallen across the whole of the United States in any year since the 1930s, the statistical models in use assigned almost no probability to their doing so.

Prices rose by more than eighty per cent in real terms between 1997 and 2006, and far more in the markets where speculation was most intense, among them Las Vegas, Phoenix, inland California and Florida. Home ownership reached a record sixty-nine per cent of households in 2004. Builders started more than two million homes in 2005. Then the mechanism stopped. Interest rates had been rising since 2004, borrowers whose initial rates expired could not meet the new payments, and with prices no longer increasing they could not refinance. Defaults on loans made in 2006 began almost at once. As foreclosed houses came onto the market, prices fell, which pushed more borrowers into owing more than their homes were worth, which produced more defaults.

The losses surfaced in the financial system in the summer of 2007, when banks discovered that they could not value the mortgage securities they held and ceased to trust one another. In March 2008 the investment bank Bear Stearns was rescued with support from the Federal Reserve. In September the government took control of Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac, which between them owned or guaranteed about half of all American mortgages. A week later Lehman Brothers filed for bankruptcy, the insurer American International Group was nationalised in all but name, and credit markets around the world froze. Congress authorised seven hundred billion dollars to recapitalise the banks. The recession that followed was the deepest since the 1930s. Nearly nine million jobs were lost in the United States, and world trade fell faster than at the onset of the Great Depression.

For homeowners the consequences were prolonged. National house prices fell by about a third from peak to trough, and by more than half in the worst affected cities. At the low point around a quarter of all borrowers owed more than the value of their homes. Between 2007 and 2016 lenders completed something close to eight million foreclosures. The process itself was marked by scandal, as it emerged that banks had processed repossessions with documents signed in bulk by employees who had not read them, and in some cases could not show that they owned the loans on which they were foreclosing. Government programmes to modify loans reached far fewer households than promised. The banks were rescued in full and the borrowers, for the most part, were not, a contrast that left a deep and lasting resentment.

The losses fell very unequally. Subprime lending had been concentrated in black and Hispanic neighbourhoods, including among borrowers who would have qualified for ordinary loans and were steered into more expensive ones. Several large lenders later paid substantial settlements over the practice. For black and Hispanic families, whose wealth consisted almost entirely of the equity in their homes, the collapse was devastating. One widely cited study found that the median wealth of Hispanic households fell by about two thirds between 2005 and 2009 and that of black households by more than half, against a sixth for white households. The districts that had been refused credit under the redlining of the mid century had been flooded with credit of the worst kind, and were stripped of wealth a second time.

The causes have been argued over ever since. One school emphasises the supply of credit. The economists Atif Mian and Amir Sufi showed that mortgage lending had expanded fastest in areas where incomes were actually falling, which is hard to explain except by a loosening of standards on the part of lenders, and that the subsequent collapse in spending was greatest where household debt had risen most. Another blames monetary policy for holding interest rates too low for too long. A third points to the flow of foreign savings, a fourth to the failure of regulators to use the powers they had, and a fifth to the incentives of an industry in which everyone was paid for volume and no one held the risk. Some conservatives argued that the root lay in government policies to promote home ownership among the poor, through the lending targets set for Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac and the law requiring banks to serve their local communities. The official commission of inquiry rejected that explanation by a majority, noting that the worst loans were made by private lenders outside the scope of those rules, though a minority of its members dissented.

The reforms that followed were considerable. Legislation in 2010 required lenders to verify that a borrower could repay, restricted the most hazardous loan designs, obliged those who packaged mortgages into securities to retain a share of the risk, and created a federal agency to protect consumers of financial products. Subprime lending of the earlier kind disappeared. Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac remained under government control and came to stand behind an even larger share of the market than before. The thirty year fixed rate mortgage and the tax advantages of ownership were left untouched. The system that emerged was safer, and it rested more heavily than ever on the guarantee of the state.

#### Ireland, Spain and the European periphery

The American crisis is usually told as a story of exotic securities. The property bubbles that burst in Europe at the same moment involved almost none. They were financed by ordinary banks making ordinary loans, and they were in proportion to the size of the economies concerned a good deal larger. Their common cause was the arrival of the euro. Countries that had lived for decades with high inflation and high interest rates found themselves, from 1999, able to borrow at German rates. In Ireland and Spain the real cost of a mortgage, after allowing for inflation, was close to zero or negative for several years, and banks in both countries could fund themselves without limit from lenders in the rest of the currency union.

Ireland in the 1990s had earned the name of the Celtic Tiger through a genuine economic transformation, as foreign investment and a young educated workforce turned one of the poorer countries of western Europe into one of the richest. The population grew, emigrants returned, and house prices rose for sound reasons. After the turn of the century the boom changed character. Building and property lending became the engine of the economy in their own right. At the peak in 2006 some ninety thousand dwellings were completed in a country of little more than four million people, a rate per head several times that of Britain, and construction employed about one worker in eight. Prices roughly quadrupled between the mid 1990s and 2007. Banks lent the full price of a house, and sometimes more, over thirty-five or forty years.
