A handful of developers borrowed billions on the security of sites bought at prices that assumed the boom would continue indefinitely, and one bank in particular, Anglo Irish, expanded its loan book many times over on such business. Tax incentives encouraged building in rural counties where there was no demand. The government's revenues came to depend on the taxes generated by property transactions.

The halt in 2007 was followed by one of the steepest collapses on record. House prices fell by about half, and by more in Dublin flats. Construction virtually ceased, unemployment tripled to fifteen per cent, and emigration resumed. In September 2008, facing a run on the banks, the government guaranteed all their liabilities, a commitment that eventually cost the state some sixty-four billion euros, equivalent to about forty per cent of national income, and forced Ireland to accept a rescue from the European Union and the International Monetary Fund in 2010. A state agency took over the banks' loans to developers at a discount and became for a time one of the largest property owners in the world. Across the country stood more than two thousand unfinished housing developments, known as ghost estates, some with a few occupied houses among rows of empty shells. A few were eventually demolished.

The sequel is as instructive as the crash. Having built far too much, Ireland then built almost nothing for most of a decade. The construction industry had been destroyed, the banks would not lend and the state had no money. When the economy recovered and the population resumed its growth, the supply of homes fell far short of demand, and by the late 2010s rents in Dublin exceeded their boom time peak and homelessness had reached record levels. Within fifteen years the country passed from a glut to an acute shortage, and housing replaced unemployment as the dominant political issue.

Spain's boom was similar in kind and greater in absolute scale. Between 1998 and 2007 the country started something like five million dwellings, in some years more than Germany, France and Italy combined, and prices nevertheless rose two and a half to three times over. The lenders at the centre were the regional savings banks, institutions controlled in practice by local politicians, which financed the developers and then financed the buyers of what the developers built. Whole new districts were laid out on the edges of towns and along the Mediterranean coast, some of them planned for tens of thousands of residents who never came. When the market turned, prices fell by more than a third, unemployment reached twenty-six per cent, and the savings banks proved to be insolvent. Several were merged into a new entity that itself required rescue in 2012, and Spain obtained a European loan to recapitalise its banking system. The social consequences, including several hundred thousand repossessions under a law that left the evicted family still owing the debt, are described in the final part of this book.

Other countries on the edges of Europe had their own variations. In Iceland three banks grew to ten times the size of the national economy and failed in a single week of October 2008. In Hungary, Poland, Romania and Croatia, households had taken out mortgages denominated in Swiss francs, attracted by interest rates far below those on loans in their own currencies. When the franc soared during the crisis, their monthly payments and their outstanding debts rose by half or more while the value of their homes fell. Hungary eventually compelled its banks to convert the loans into the local currency at a rate favourable to the borrowers, at the banks' expense, and other governments took similar steps. In the Baltic states, where Scandinavian banks had financed a property boom after accession to the European Union, house prices fell by half or more and national income by a fifth.

Several lessons were drawn from the European experience. The first was that a housing bubble does not require financial engineering. Plain lending by plain banks is sufficient if credit is cheap and plentiful enough. The second concerned supply. Those who attribute high house prices chiefly to restrictions on building have to reckon with the fact that Ireland and Spain built at extraordinary rates during their booms and saw prices multiply regardless. A defender of the supply argument can reply that prices would have risen still further had less been built, and that a good part of the construction was in places where nobody wished to live. But the episode shows at the least that when credit is expanding fast enough, speculation can absorb any quantity of new building for several years. The third lesson concerned who pays. In both countries the losses of private banks and their creditors were assumed by the state and repaid by taxpayers through years of austerity, while those who had lent to the banks from abroad were made whole. The fourth was the simplest. In the space of a decade the price of an Irish or Spanish home doubled, halved and, in the big cities, doubled again. Whatever was being priced, it was not the usefulness of the dwelling, which did not change.

#### Lessons, lending limits and the return of the boom

Not every country with a housing boom in the 2000s suffered a crash, and the exceptions shaped the response. Canada's banks came through 2008 without a failure or a rescue. Canadian law required any mortgage exceeding eighty per cent of the value of the property to be insured, in practice by a public corporation, on terms the government set, and a small number of large and closely supervised banks held their loans to maturity instead of selling them on. Australia also escaped, helped by a mining boom and by regulators who had tightened lending standards earlier in the decade. Germany had experienced no boom at all. The inference drawn was that the damage depended less on how far prices rose than on how recklessly the purchases had been financed.

Regulators accordingly turned to direct limits on mortgage lending, a group of measures given the name macroprudential. The idea was not new. Hong Kong had capped the size of a loan relative to the value of the property since 1991, and South Korea had introduced limits on loans relative to both value and income in 2002. After the crisis such rules spread widely. Ireland's central bank, determined that the country should not repeat its experience, ruled in 2015 that most buyers could borrow no more than three and a half times their income and must provide a deposit of at least a tenth. The Bank of England instructed lenders that only a small proportion of new mortgages could exceed four and a half times income. New Zealand, Sweden, Norway, the Netherlands and others adopted versions of their own, and borrowers were tested for their ability to keep paying if interest rates rose by several points.

These rules have very probably made banking systems safer. They also raise a question of fairness that bears on the argument of this book. A cap on borrowing relative to income restrains those who depend on their earnings and leaves untouched those who can draw on family money. In a market where prices are already many times average incomes, the households shut out by a lending limit are the young and the unpropertied, while buyers with inherited deposits and investors with existing equity proceed. The effect is to make access to ownership depend still more on the wealth of one's parents. Defenders of the limits reply that the alternative, allowing people to take on debts they cannot carry, is worse for the same households, and that the true problem is the level of prices, which lending rules can only dampen.

The larger response to the crisis worked in the opposite direction. To prevent a depression, central banks cut their interest rates to zero or below and bought government bonds and mortgage securities in quantities running into trillions, with the deliberate aim of lowering long term borrowing costs and raising the prices of assets. The policy was maintained in one form or another for more than a decade. Its effect on housing was what might have been expected. A buyer who can afford a given monthly payment can borrow far more when the rate of interest is two per cent than when it is six, and prices adjusted accordingly. By the mid 2010s real house prices in most of the countries that had crashed were rising again, and in those that had not crashed, including Canada, Australia, New Zealand, Sweden, Germany and Switzerland, they climbed to levels far beyond the previous peak. In Auckland, Vancouver, Sydney, Munich and Stockholm the ratio of prices to incomes roughly doubled. The measures taken to repair the damage of one housing boom thus set off another, more widely distributed and less dependent on bad loans.

The pandemic of 2020 added a final surge. With interest rates cut again, households confined to their homes and governments supporting incomes, prices rose in almost every developed country simultaneously, by a fifth or more in two years in the United States and by a third in New Zealand. When inflation returned in 2022 and central banks raised rates at the fastest pace in forty years, the result was not a general collapse but a freeze. Owners who had fixed their mortgages at very low rates had no wish to sell and take a new loan at double the cost, so few homes came onto the market, prices dipped modestly and then recovered, and those who did not yet own faced high prices and high borrowing costs together. Affordability for a first time buyer in the United States, measured by the share of a typical income needed to meet the payments on a typical house, fell to its worst level since the early 1980s.

The experience reopened an old dispute about the responsibility of central banks. The orthodox view before 2008 was that monetary policy should aim at stable consumer prices and should not attempt to restrain the prices of houses or shares, since nobody can identify a bubble with confidence while it is inflating, and it is better to clean up after a bust than to prevent a boom. That view lost much of its authority in the crisis. Critics now argue that house prices, or at least the cost of housing services, should carry more weight in the measure of inflation that central banks target, and that a decade of near zero interest rates amounted to an enormous transfer of wealth to the owners of property from those who owned none. Central bankers reply that their instrument is a blunt one, that without their actions unemployment would have been far higher, and that the affordability of housing is properly the business of governments, which control taxation, planning and building.

A second dispute concerns who should bear the loss when prices fall. In most countries the law places it squarely on the borrower. The debt is fixed in money, the value of the house is not, and a family whose home loses a third of its value has lost its entire deposit and more while the lender's claim is undiminished. Several American states are an exception, in that a lender there can take the house but cannot pursue the borrower for any shortfall. In Spain the demand that surrender of the property should cancel the debt became the central claim of the movement of those affected by mortgages. Mian and Sufi proposed a form of mortgage in which the payments would fall automatically if local house prices declined, the lender receiving in exchange a share of any gain on sale. Such contracts would spread the risk more evenly and would make crashes less destructive. They have not been adopted anywhere on a significant scale, partly because the tax and regulatory systems are built around conventional debt.

The most sobering lesson is how little the experience changed. Fifteen years after a housing collapse that nearly destroyed the world's financial system, households in many rich countries carried more mortgage debt than before it, prices stood higher relative to incomes, and the proportion of young adults able to buy a home had fallen. The safeguards added since make a repetition of 2008 in the same form unlikely. They have not altered the underlying arrangement, in which the price of homes is set by the availability of credit and the bulk of the population has its savings invested in the proposition that the price will not fall.

### Chapter 12: Global Capital, Landlords and China

The previous two chapters concerned the homeowner and the lender. This one concerns investors who buy housing not to live in but to hold, and the international flows of money that seek out residential property as a place of safety or profit. It then turns to China, where the conversion of housing into the main vehicle of household saving has gone further than anywhere else, and closes with the debate over what is called financialisation.

#### The rise of the institutional landlord

For most of the twentieth century the letting of homes was a small scale business. Outside public and non-profit housing, the typical landlord in Britain, France, the United States or Spain was an individual or a family owning a handful of properties, often inherited. Insurance companies and pension funds held offices and shopping centres but generally avoided dwellings, which were troublesome to manage, politically exposed and, where rents were controlled, unprofitable. Germany was a partial exception, with its large companies owned by municipalities, trade unions, employers and churches, but these were not run for maximum return. The large commercial owner of rented homes, answerable to shareholders, is mainly a creation of the past thirty years.

It arose first in Germany, through the sale of what had been social property. German cities in the early 2000s were heavily indebted, the old tax privileges of non-profit housing companies had been abolished in 1990, and international private equity funds were looking for assets with reliable income. A wave of disposals followed. The federal government sold the housing of the railway workers and of the national pension insurance fund. Berlin sold a company with some sixty-five thousand flats in 2004. Dresden sold its entire municipal stock, about forty-eight thousand dwellings, to an American fund in 2006 and thereby paid off all its debts at a stroke. In all, more than half a million publicly owned dwellings changed hands in a few years. The purchasers were firms such as Fortress, Cerberus and Terra Firma, which typically resold after a period or floated their holdings on the stock exchange. Out of successive mergers emerged a few very large listed landlords. The biggest, Vonovia, owned more than half a million flats in Germany, Sweden and Austria after absorbing its principal rival in 2021.

In the United States the opening came with the foreclosure crisis. By 2011 banks and the government sponsored mortgage companies held hundreds of thousands of repossessed houses that they could not sell, in markets where prices had fallen by half. Renting out detached houses had always been a business for small local owners, since scattered properties were thought impossible to manage efficiently at scale. Blackstone, the largest private equity firm in the world, judged that software and cheap finance had changed the calculation. Beginning in 2012 it bought tens of thousands of houses at auction in Phoenix, Atlanta, Tampa, Las Vegas and other cities of the south and west, at one stage spending more than a hundred million dollars a week, and assembled them into a company called Invitation Homes. Other investors followed. Within a few years a new asset class, the single family rental, had been established, with rents collected, maintenance scheduled and annual increases set by centralised systems. The firms later moved from buying existing houses to commissioning whole estates built for rent.

Similar buyers appeared wherever distressed property was for sale. In Spain the banks and the public bad bank disposed of portfolios of repossessed homes and loans to Blackstone, Cerberus, Lone Star and others, and a special tax status for listed property companies was introduced to attract them. Blackstone became the country's largest private owner of rental housing. In Ireland the state agency holding the developers' loans sold large blocks of apartments to international funds, and after 2015 institutional investors bought a high proportion of all new flats built in Dublin, where they were nicknamed cuckoo funds for taking over homes that might otherwise have gone to buyers. In Sweden and Denmark foreign funds acquired older regulated flats with the intention of renovating them and raising the rents.

The case made by and for these investors is straightforward. They bring capital to a sector that needs it. In the United States they bought houses that nobody else would buy at the bottom of the market, repaired them and put them back into use, and helped to end the fall in prices. In countries with a shortage of rental housing, pension and insurance funds are among the few sources of the long term finance needed to build apartments in quantity, and the British and Irish governments have actively courted them for that purpose. A professional landlord with a reputation to protect, it is argued, offers more consistent standards and more secure tenancies than an amateur who may sell at any time. The money invested ultimately belongs to ordinary savers and pensioners. And the scale of the phenomenon is modest. Institutional owners hold only a few per cent of the rented houses in the United States, and even in Germany the large listed companies own a small fraction of the total stock.

The case against has several strands. Tenants and their organisations report that the business model of many such firms depends on extracting more from each property: raising rents as far as regulations allow, adding fees for services formerly included, economising on repairs and pursuing arrears and evictions with an automated rigour that an individual landlord would seldom apply. In continental Europe a common strategy was to buy regulated flats, carry out improvements whose cost could legally be passed on, and so lift the rent far above its previous level, a practice tenants in Sweden and Germany call renovation eviction. Research on American cities has found that the large owners of houses filed for eviction at markedly higher rates than small landlords in the same neighbourhoods. National averages, moreover, conceal local concentration. In certain districts of Atlanta, Charlotte and Jacksonville a few companies own a substantial share of all the houses of the type a first time buyer would seek, and compete directly with such buyers using cash. In Berlin the largest landlords owned enough of the rental stock for their pricing decisions to affect the whole market.

There is also an objection of principle. A fund that buys existing homes adds nothing to the supply of housing. It changes who receives the rent and, if it succeeds in its aims, increases the amount. In 2019 the United Nations special rapporteur on the right to housing, Leilani Farha, wrote formally to Blackstone and to several governments, charging that the firm's practices were inconsistent with the human right to housing, an accusation the company rejected. Her general argument was that when dwellings are treated primarily as instruments for generating returns to distant investors, the interests of those who live in them are systematically subordinated.

Governments have begun to respond. Denmark legislated in 2020 to prevent investors from raising rents sharply after acquiring and renovating older buildings, in a measure popularly named after the firm that had prompted it. The Netherlands allowed municipalities to prohibit the purchase of cheaper homes by buyers who do not intend to live in them. Ireland imposed a higher transaction tax on the bulk purchase of houses. In the United States bills to limit the ownership of single family homes by large investors have been introduced in Congress and in several states. And in Berlin, as described elsewhere in this book, a majority of voters in 2021 supported taking the large companies' housing into public ownership. The institutional landlord, a creature of privatisation and financial crisis, has become one of the principal targets of housing politics.

#### Safe-deposit cities, foreign buyers and golden visas

A second kind of investor wants neither rent nor, necessarily, a quick profit. For the wealthy of countries where property rights are insecure, currencies unstable or fortunes of doubtful origin, a flat in London, New York or Vancouver is a store of value held under a reliable legal system, comparable to gold in a vault. The global growth of private wealth since the 1990s, the opening of Russia and China, and the ease of moving money across borders produced a demand of this kind on a scale without precedent, and it concentrated on a short list of cities.

London was the first and remains the archetype. Its attractions included the English language and legal system, a time zone between Asia and America, elite schools, light taxation of foreigners and, until recently, the possibility of owning property anonymously through a company registered in an offshore jurisdiction. Something like a hundred thousand properties in England and Wales came to be held in that way, a large share of them in a few central boroughs. In Kensington, Chelsea, Mayfair and Belgravia whole streets were bought by owners from the Gulf, Russia, the former Soviet republics, Nigeria, Malaysia and China. Prices in these districts rose out of all relation to British incomes, and the effect spread outward as those displaced by foreign money bought in the next ring of neighbourhoods. A development of luxury apartments overlooking Hyde Park, completed in 2011, became a symbol of the phenomenon when journalists observed that at night almost none of its windows were lit. Many of the new towers along the Thames were sold in Hong Kong and Singapore before they were offered in London.

New York acquired a row of extremely slender and extremely tall residential towers south of Central Park, in which apartments sold for tens of millions of dollars to buyers frequently concealed behind limited liability companies, and which stood largely unoccupied. Miami served a similar function for Latin American wealth, Dubai for money from Russia, South Asia and Africa. The critics' phrase for such buildings was safe deposit boxes in the sky. The harm alleged was partly direct, in that land and construction capacity were devoted to dwellings nobody lived in, and partly a matter of example, since developers everywhere reoriented their output toward the product that foreign investors would buy.

Vancouver became the most studied case. The city had long attracted migrants from Hong Kong, and from the 2000s it drew a great deal of capital from mainland China, where newly rich families were anxious to move assets abroad. Detached houses on the west side of the city sold for several million Canadian dollars, and the ratio of house prices to local incomes rose to one of the highest in the world, exceeded only by Hong Kong itself. Residents on ordinary professional salaries found that they could not buy within an hour's travel of their work. For some years the authorities declined to collect data on the nationality or residence of buyers, on the grounds that the question was divisive, and the debate was conducted with accusations of racism on one side and of denial on the other. A public inquiry later established that the property market of British Columbia had also been used to launder the proceeds of crime on a considerable scale.

The province finally acted in 2016, imposing a tax of fifteen per cent, later raised to twenty, on purchases of homes in the Vancouver region by foreign nationals. The city followed with an annual tax on dwellings left empty for most of the year, initially one per cent of their value and later three, and the province added a further tax aimed at owners who paid little income tax in Canada. Sales to foreign buyers fell sharply and several thousand vacant units were let or sold. Prices paused and then resumed their rise, which suggested that foreign demand had been one cause among several. Toronto, Sydney, Melbourne and Auckland, which had experienced the same pressures, adopted similar measures. New Zealand went further in 2018 and prohibited most non-residents from buying existing homes at all. Canada enacted a nationwide ban in 2023, and Australia announced a temporary one two years later.

In the same period a number of European governments were doing the reverse, selling the right of residence in exchange for the purchase of property. After the financial crisis, with their property markets prostrate, Portugal, Spain, Greece, Cyprus and Malta introduced what were called golden visas. A foreigner from outside the European Union who bought real estate above a set value, half a million euros in Portugal and Spain and less in Greece, received a residence permit for himself and his family, with the freedom to travel throughout most of Europe, and in some cases a path to citizenship. The schemes were explicitly intended to bring in capital and revive construction. They did so. Portugal's attracted several billion euros, most of it from Chinese, Brazilian and other buyers, and the greater part of it went into flats in Lisbon and Porto.

By the end of the decade the mood had changed. Lisbon had become one of the least affordable cities in Europe relative to local wages, as the visa programme, a favourable tax regime for foreign residents and the growth of tourist lettings converged on a small historic centre. The European Commission warned of the risks of corruption and money laundering. Portugal ended the property route to its visa in 2023, Ireland and Cyprus closed their schemes, and Spain abolished its programme in 2025. Greece raised its thresholds steeply in the areas under most pressure. A policy designed to rescue a collapsed property market had come to be regarded, within ten years, as an obstacle to housing the country's own citizens.

Economists who have examined the evidence generally conclude that foreign buyers and empty investment properties account for a small share of transactions in most cities, and that their effect on prices across a whole metropolitan market is real but limited, a matter of a few percentage points. Taxes and bans directed at them are popular because they impose no cost on voters, and they can serve as a substitute for more difficult reforms. That assessment is probably correct as far as it goes. It misses part of what troubles people. The objection to the safe deposit flat is not only that it raises prices. It is that a dwelling in a city where people struggle to find homes is being used for a purpose that has nothing to do with being lived in, and that the market regards this as a perfectly satisfactory use. The reaction against it expresses, in a rough way, the conviction that housing ought to be for residents first, which is the conviction on which the opposing view of housing has always rested.

#### China: from welfare housing to the largest property market on earth

In 1978, when China began its economic reforms, there was no housing market in its cities. Urban land belonged to the state. Dwellings were built by state enterprises and government bodies and assigned to their employees at rents so low that they did not cover maintenance. Housing was treated as a cost of production, investment in it had been neglected for decades, and the average city dweller had less than four square metres of living space. Forty years later the same cities contained the largest stock of privately owned housing in the world, built and sold by commercial developers, and its value was the principal form of wealth of several hundred million households. No transformation described in this book was so large or so fast, and none illustrates so vividly the consequences of making housing an investment.

The legal foundation was laid in the late 1980s. The constitution continued to declare that urban land was owned by the state, but an amendment of 1988 permitted the right to use land to be transferred for payment. The first auction of such a right was held in Shenzhen in December 1987. Under the system that developed, a municipal government sells a developer the right to use a plot for a fixed term, seventy years in the case of housing, and the developer builds and sells flats whose buyers hold the remainder of the term. The arrangement resembles the leasehold systems of Hong Kong and Singapore, on which it was modelled, and in principle it reserves the ownership of land to the public.

The decisive step was taken in 1998. With the Asian financial crisis threatening growth, the government of Zhu Rongji announced that the allocation of housing by employers would cease. Existing flats were sold to their occupants at a fraction of their value, an act that made most urban families property owners overnight. From then on, housing was to be bought on the market, with the help of mortgages from the state banks. The measure was intended to stimulate construction and consumer spending, and it succeeded beyond any expectation. Developers, many of them newly formed private companies, built on a scale never before attempted. In the two decades after 2000, China's urban population rose by more than four hundred million, and its cities added housing equivalent to the entire stock of western Europe. Average living space per person in urban areas rose to about forty square metres, comparable with that of much richer countries.

Three features of the Chinese system gave property a role it has had nowhere else. The first was the finance of local government. A fiscal reform of 1994 assigned most tax revenue to the centre while leaving local authorities responsible for most public spending. Cities found the answer in land. They requisitioned farmland from villagers at prices based on its agricultural yield, serviced it with roads and utilities, and sold the use rights to developers for many times the cost. The proceeds, and loans raised against future land sales, paid for the metros, highways and industrial parks of the new China. In many cities land revenue came to supply a third or more of the local budget. Municipal governments thus acquired a direct interest in high and rising land prices, and controlled the supply of land in a manner that sustained them.

The second feature was the lack of alternatives for savers. Chinese households save a very large share of their income. Bank deposits paid interest below the rate of inflation, the stock market was volatile and distrusted, and controls prevented investment abroad. Property was the one asset that seemed both tangible and certain to appreciate, and families bought second and third flats as a store of value, often leaving them unlet and unfurnished. By the late 2010s around seven tenths of household wealth was held in housing, a far higher proportion than in the United States, and surveys suggested that a fifth or more of urban dwellings stood empty.

The third was social. Home ownership became a condition of marriage. It was widely expected that a man would own a flat before proposing, and parents and grandparents pooled their savings to buy one for a son. Access to the better state schools depended on owning property in the catchment area. A household registration system tied entitlement to public services to one's officially recorded place of residence, and in several large cities buying property was a route to obtaining it. The price of housing in Beijing, Shanghai and Shenzhen rose to thirty or forty times average annual income, among the highest ratios ever recorded anywhere, and young people in those cities spoke of themselves as house slaves.

The method of sale added a financial hazard. Developers sold flats before they were built and used the buyers' payments, together with heavy borrowing, to acquire more land and start more projects. The largest firms expanded at headlong speed on this basis, and the biggest of them, Evergrande, accumulated liabilities of some three hundred billion dollars. Property and the industries that depend on it, including steel, cement, furniture and household appliances, came by several estimates to account for a quarter or more of Chinese economic output, a larger share than in Ireland or Spain at the height of their booms. Whole districts were built ahead of any demand, and foreign journalists reported on what they called ghost cities.

The leadership was aware of the danger. In December 2016 President Xi Jinping told a meeting on economic policy that houses are for living in, not for speculation, and the phrase was repeated in official documents for years afterwards. Cities restricted the number of flats a household could buy, raised the deposits required for second homes and capped prices on new developments. The measures slowed the market from time to time without altering its direction. In August 2020 the regulators set limits on developers' borrowing, known as the three red lines, which barred the most indebted firms from taking on new loans.

The effect was greater than intended. Cut off from credit, Evergrande defaulted on its bonds in 2021, and was followed by dozens of other developers, including in 2023 the firm that had been considered the safest in the industry. Construction stopped on sites across the country, leaving buyers who had paid in full for flats that might never be finished, and in the summer of 2022 purchasers in scores of cities announced that they would stop paying their mortgages on unbuilt homes. A court in Hong Kong ordered the liquidation of Evergrande in January 2024. Sales of new housing fell by roughly half from their peak, prices in most cities declined for several years in succession, and the revenues of local governments from land collapsed. With their main asset losing value, households cut their spending, and the slump in property became the chief drag on the Chinese economy. The government responded with successive packages of support, lowering interest rates, easing purchase restrictions and encouraging local authorities to buy unsold flats for use as subsidised housing.

The Chinese case contains several ironies. A state that calls itself socialist and owns all the urban land presided over the most intense commodification of housing in history. The public ownership of land, which Georgists and others have recommended as a means of capturing rising values for the community, became the mechanism through which local governments promoted speculation. And a government with greater powers of control than any in the democratic world found that once the bulk of its citizens' savings were invested in housing, it could neither allow prices to go on rising nor bear the consequences of letting them fall. The slogan that houses are for living in was sincere. It came about twenty years too late.

#### The financialisation debate and an assessment

The developments traced in this part have been given a collective name. Financialisation, in the usage of the scholars who introduced the term, means the growing dominance of financial actors, markets, practices and measurements in a field formerly governed by other considerations. Applied to housing, it refers to the process by which the dwelling has come to be valued, managed and regulated chiefly as a financial asset: as collateral for debt, as a holding in an investment portfolio, as a household's principal store of wealth. The Dutch geographer Manuel Aalbers, among others, has argued that this is the defining feature of housing in the present age, more important than the old division between owning and renting or between market and state.

The critics' argument can be summarised in a few propositions. Housing has absorbed a vast quantity of the world's capital, described by some as a wall of money in search of safe returns, and most of that capital has gone into bidding up the price of existing buildings and land instead of producing new homes. The result has been a large increase in the wealth of those who already owned property and a corresponding increase in the cost of housing for everyone else. Residential real estate is now the largest asset class in the world, worth by one estimate well over two hundred trillion dollars, several times the value of all listed shares. The return on it consists substantially of the capital gain on land, which is unearned in exactly the sense that Mill and Ricardo described. And a society organised around this form of wealth divides increasingly into those who have it, and can pass it on, and those who do not. A group of sociologists has called the outcome the asset economy, in which a person's position in life depends less on what he earns than on what he, or his family, owns.

The economist Thomas Piketty's well known finding that the ratio of private wealth to national income has risen sharply in rich countries since the 1970s gives the argument statistical support. When his figures are broken down, the greater part of the increase turns out to consist of housing, and specifically of the rising value of the land beneath it. The return of what Piketty called patrimonial capitalism, in which inheritance once again matters as much as effort, is to a large degree a story about houses.

Defenders of the financial system reject much of this account. Finance, they point out, is what made mass home ownership possible in the first place. Without the long mortgage, families of ordinary means would still be tenants, and the alternative to borrowing against a house is not a costless home but no home of one's own. Capital markets that channel pension savings into apartment buildings are doing what capital markets should do. Nobody is harmed when a house rises in value, and millions of people of modest income have accumulated through their homes a degree of security that no other institution has given them. If prices are too high, the cause lies in the restrictions on supply discussed in Part Three, not in the availability of finance, and the remedy is to build. The term financialisation, on this view, attaches a sinister label to the ordinary and beneficial workings of credit and investment.

Between these positions there is perhaps more common ground than the participants admit. Both sides can accept that credit is necessary to housing and that its terms affect prices. Both can accept that the same expansion of credit has different effects in different places: where homes can readily be built, more lending yields more homes, and where they cannot, it yields dearer ones. The financial critique and the supply critique are then not rivals but descriptions of the two blades of a pair of scissors. Abundant money meeting a restricted supply of well located land is what has produced the extraordinary rise in prices, and neither element would have done so alone.

What the financial perspective adds, and what the supply argument tends to neglect, is an account of the interests created along the way. Once a majority of households hold most of their wealth in housing, once pension provision assumes it, once banks are predominantly mortgage lenders and once public finances lean on property transactions and land sales, a whole society is committed to the continued appreciation of homes. The commitment is not the result of a conspiracy by investors. It is shared by tens of millions of ordinary owners, and it is entirely rational from the point of view of each. It is also the main reason why housing has become steadily less affordable to those who do not yet own, and why governments that profess to want cheaper housing recoil from any measure that might actually lower its price.

The experience of the three countries that have seen prolonged falls illustrates the difficulty. Japan after 1991 and China after 2021 show what happens to an economy built on property when property declines: years of stagnation, damaged banks and a population made poorer and more cautious. Ireland after 2008 shows how quickly a collapse of prices destroys the capacity to build and prepares the next shortage. A gradual decline in the real price of housing over many years, with nominal prices held roughly flat while incomes grow, would avoid these outcomes and is what most economists would recommend. It has rarely been achieved for long, and it requires voters to accept that their largest asset should cease to enrich them.

This part of the book has taken the view of the house as a financial product seriously and followed it to its consequences. The view is not foolish. A home is in fact the largest asset most families will own, and to ignore its financial character would be to ignore something of great importance to them. But the history suggests three cautions. An asset bought with heavy borrowing is a source of risk as well as of wealth, and the risk falls hardest on those who entered last and could least afford it. The gains from rising prices are for the most part transfers from later buyers to earlier ones, and so from the young to the old, and cannot continue indefinitely without excluding the next generation. And a society that has staked its savings on the price of housing loses the freedom to make housing cheap.

The promotion of home ownership was not, however, only or mainly a financial project. Governments encouraged it for reasons of politics and morals: because they believed that people who own their homes make steadier citizens, better parents and more reliable supporters of the existing order. The conviction that a nation of proprietors is a sound nation is one of the oldest ideas in this book, and in the twentieth century it became the governing principle of housing policy in the English speaking world and well beyond. It is the subject of Part Five.

## Part Five: The Owner's Democracy

### Chapter 13: Britain and the Property-Owning Democracy

The conservative tradition differs from the market liberal one in a way that is easy to miss, since the two are usually allies. For the liberal, the point of a free market in housing is that people get what they choose, and it is a matter of indifference whether they choose to own or to rent. For the conservative, ownership has a value of its own. A person who owns his home has a stake in the order of things, an interest in his neighbourhood, a motive for thrift and something to hand on to his children. Property makes citizens. It follows that the state may properly use its power and its money to turn tenants into owners, and for most of the twentieth century governments of the right did exactly that. This part of the book follows the idea through Britain, the United States and the conservative traditions of continental Europe and the settler countries, and Britain comes first because it was there that the doctrine was given its name.

#### Conservative ideas of property and the interwar suburb

The philosophical source is Edmund Burke. Writing against the French Revolution in 1790, Burke described society as a partnership between the living, the dead and those yet to be born, and he saw in inherited property the institution that gives the partnership substance. The power of perpetuating property in families, he wrote, is one of the most valuable and interesting circumstances belonging to it, and that which tends most to the perpetuation of society itself. Property provides ballast. Those who hold it have reason to be cautious about innovation and to think in generations instead of years. Burke had in mind the landed estates of the aristocracy, and he would have been surprised to see his argument applied to a semi detached house. But the logic transfers readily. If property makes its holders prudent and attached to the existing order, then the more people who hold some, the more stable the order will be.

Nineteenth century Conservatives approached housing mainly as a question of social peace. Benjamin Disraeli, who had written in a novel of the two nations of rich and poor between whom there was no intercourse and no sympathy, presided as prime minister over the slum clearance act of 1875. His successor Lord Salisbury surprised his party in 1883 with an article arguing that the overcrowding of the poor was a public danger that the state had a duty to address, and it was Salisbury who set up the royal commission of the following year. These were measures of paternal responsibility, however, not of ownership. A first step in the latter direction came in 1899 with an act, sponsored by Joseph Chamberlain, that allowed local authorities to lend working men most of the price of a small house. Little use was made of it, but the principle that public credit might be employed to make owners of the people had been stated.

The decisive change in thinking followed the First World War and the extension of the vote. In 1918 the franchise was given to all men and to most women over thirty, tripling the electorate, and the Labour Party, committed to common ownership of the means of production, became the main opposition. Conservatives had to consider how a party of property could win elections in a democracy where most voters had none. Noel Skelton, a young Scottish member of parliament, gave the answer in a series of articles in the Spectator in 1923, published the next year as Constructive Conservatism. The mass of the people, he wrote, had been given political status and educational status, but their economic status had not kept pace. A democracy in which the many held votes and the few held property was unstable and would be drawn to socialism. The remedy was not to resist democracy but to complete it, by enabling the wage earner to become an owner. He called the goal a property-owning democracy. Skelton was thinking chiefly of profit sharing in industry and smallholdings on the land, but the phrase was soon attached to housing, where the opportunity proved to be greatest.

The opportunity arose from the peculiar conditions of the interwar years. The wartime controls had made building houses to let unprofitable, and private investors did not return to it. But interest rates fell to historically low levels after 1932, building costs and land prices were low, real wages for those in work were rising, and the building societies were awash with funds. A new three bedroom semi detached house in the outer suburbs of London could be bought for five or six hundred pounds, with a deposit of twenty-five or thirty pounds and weekly repayments no greater than the rent of a controlled tenancy in the inner city. Speculative builders, of whom there were thousands, laid out estates along every arterial road and beside every new station on the electrified railways. Nearly three million houses were built for sale in England and Wales between the wars. The proportion of households owning their homes rose from about a tenth in 1914 to about a third in 1938.

Government encouraged the process. Neville Chamberlain, as minister of health in the 1920s and later as chancellor, was the principal architect of Conservative housing policy. His act of 1923 offered a subsidy to private builders of small houses, in preference to local authorities, and he was explicit about his reasons. Every person who became the owner of his house, he argued, acquired a new interest in the stability of the country and was the less likely to be attracted by revolutionary doctrines. The building societies shared the view and promoted it. The head of one of the largest wrote that the man who has something to protect and improve, a stake of some sort in the country, naturally turns his thoughts in the direction of sound, ordered and responsible citizenship. Owner occupation was commonly described in these years as a bulwark against Bolshevism.

The new suburbs had a distinct culture, which contemporaries observed with a mixture of amusement and disdain. Each house had a name as well as a number, a front garden with a privet hedge and a gate, bay windows, a patch of lawn and often some mock Tudor timbering on the gable to distinguish it from the plain council houses being built at the same time. Owners on a private estate at Oxford went so far as to build a wall across the road, topped with spikes, to separate themselves from the tenants of the adjoining council estate, and it stood until 1959. The intellectuals of the period despised the suburbs as monotonous and petty. George Orwell described the long rows of little semi detached houses as prison cells, each with its inmate in terror of the boss and the building society. Yet those who lived in them had obtained, for the first time, a bathroom, a garden, electric light and a home that nobody could take away so long as the instalments were paid, and they showed no inclination to give these up.

The political result appeared to bear out Skelton's reasoning. The suburban constituencies created by the building boom returned Conservative members with large majorities throughout the 1930s, and the party held office, alone or as the dominant partner in coalition, for all but three of the years between the wars. Labour's strength remained in the districts of rented terraces and council estates. A relationship between tenure and voting had been established that would shape the calculations of both parties for the rest of the century: owners voted Conservative, council tenants voted Labour, and whoever could alter the balance between the two forms of housing might expect to alter the balance of politics.

#### From Macmillan to consensus

The Second World War interrupted the advance of ownership and seemed for a time to have reversed it. Nearly half a million homes had been destroyed or made uninhabitable by bombing, building had ceased for six years, and the Labour government elected in 1945 gave priority to council housing for rent, allowing private builders only a small quota of licences. Conservatives in opposition revived Skelton's slogan as a statement of what distinguished them. Anthony Eden told the party conference in 1946 that the Conservative objective was a nationwide property-owning democracy, in contrast to the socialist purpose of concentrating ownership in the hands of the state. The party's delegates, at the conference of 1950, forced on a reluctant leadership a pledge to build three hundred thousand houses a year.

Harold Macmillan, appointed minister of housing when the party returned to office in 1951, treated the pledge as a military operation and met it within two years. It is often forgotten that he did so largely by building council houses, more of them in fact than Labour had managed, since local authorities were the only organisations capable of producing at such speed. But the direction of policy was soon changed. Licensing of private building was abolished in 1954, subsidies for general council building were reduced and then confined to slum clearance and housing for the old, and by the end of the decade private builders were again producing the majority of new homes. Macmillan's own summary of the Conservative philosophy was that local authorities and local authorities alone could clear and rehouse the slums, while the general housing need of the nation should be met by private enterprise, and that of all forms of ownership the ownership of the home was the most satisfying to the individual and the most beneficial to the nation.

The tax system was arranged accordingly. Homeowners had long been able to deduct the interest on their mortgages from their taxable income, and as income tax came to be paid by the majority of wage earners the concession grew in value. Until 1963 it was partly offset by a tax on the notional rent that an owner enjoyed by living in his own house. The Conservative government abolished that tax in the year before a general election, leaving the relief on interest in place. Two years later, when a capital gains tax was introduced, the family home was exempted from it. An owner occupier thus paid no tax on the benefit of living in his house, received tax relief on the cost of borrowing to buy it, and kept the whole of any increase in its value. A landlord enjoyed none of these advantages and a tenant none either. From this point the financial case for buying over renting, for anyone who could raise a deposit, was overwhelming, and it became more so as inflation accelerated.

What turned a Conservative policy into a national consensus was the conversion of the Labour Party. Labour had been the party of council housing, and Aneurin Bevan had hoped that renting from the local authority would become the normal condition of people of all classes. By the 1960s that ambition had faded. Labour's own voters were buying houses in growing numbers, and the party's revisionist thinkers, notably Anthony Crosland, argued that socialism was about equality and not about who held the title to a dwelling. A white paper issued by Harold Wilson's government in 1965 described the expansion of building for owner occupation as normal, reflecting a long term social advance, and the expansion of public housing as meeting exceptional needs. The government introduced a scheme to give buyers on incomes too low to benefit from tax relief an equivalent subsidy on their mortgage interest. A later Labour government's review of housing policy, published in 1977, stated that for most people owning one's own home was a basic and natural desire.

That sentence marks a turning point in the history of ideas about housing in Britain. A preference that had been manufactured over half a century by cheap credit, tax concessions, the decay of private renting under control and the association of council estates with lower status was now described by the party of the left as a fact of human nature. From then on the two main parties competed over which could better satisfy the desire, and neither questioned it. The proportion of households owning their homes passed one half at the beginning of the 1970s. Council tenants were about a third, and private tenants, who had been nine tenths of the nation in 1914, were a shrinking remainder.

The Conservative attachment to ownership also acquired, in these years, a sharper edge of hostility to the alternative. Council housing had been accepted by the party in the 1950s as a necessary public service. By the 1970s many Conservatives had come to see it as a system of political dependency, in which Labour councils built estates, let them at subsidised rents to people who then voted Labour, and administered them with a petty authoritarianism that told tenants what colour to paint their front doors. The criticism was not without foundation. Many estates of the 1960s, system built in concrete and already deteriorating, were unpopular with their residents, and housing departments were often remote and slow. The proposal that tenants should be allowed, indeed entitled, to buy the houses they lived in offered a way of dissolving what the right regarded as the municipal fiefdoms of its opponents while extending the property-owning democracy to the one large group it had not reached.

Sales of council houses were not a new idea. Local authorities had possessed the power to sell, with ministerial consent, since the 1920s, and Conservative controlled councils in Birmingham and London sold several thousand homes to tenants in the late 1960s and 1970s. Labour ministers restricted the practice and Labour councils refused to engage in it. The Conservative manifesto for the election of October 1974, on which Margaret Thatcher as shadow housing spokesman had worked, promised that tenants of three years' standing would have a right to buy at a third below market value. She was at first uneasy about the scale of the discount, fearing resentment among those who had scrimped to buy homes on private estates at full price. By the time she led the party to victory in 1979 her doubts had gone, and the right to buy stood at the centre of the programme.

#### The Right to Buy

The Housing Act of 1980, taken through Parliament by the environment secretary Michael Heseltine, gave almost every council tenant in England and Wales who had held a tenancy for three years a statutory right to purchase the home he or she lived in. A parallel act applied to Scotland. The council could not refuse. The price was the market value less a discount, which began at thirty-three per cent and rose by one point for each additional year of tenancy to a maximum of fifty. Tenants were also given the right to a mortgage from the local authority for the whole of the reduced price. Later legislation shortened the qualifying period to two years and raised the maximum discount to sixty per cent on houses and seventy per cent on flats, which had proved harder to sell. A family that had rented a house for thirty years could buy it for less than half of what it would fetch on the open market.

Heseltine presented the measure in terms that went beyond housing. It would, he told the House of Commons, lay the foundations for one of the most important social revolutions of the century, transferring wealth from the state to the people and giving ordinary families independence from the municipal landlord. No single piece of legislation, he said, had enabled the transfer of so much capital wealth to the people. The description was accurate. Over the following years the public sector sold assets worth tens of billions of pounds to their occupants at a discount averaging nearly half.

The response was immediate. Some two hundred thousand dwellings were sold in 1982 alone, and by the end of the decade the total exceeded a million. Over the whole life of the policy about two million homes have been bought in Great Britain, something like a third of the council stock as it stood in 1980. Together with continued private building, the sales lifted the rate of home ownership from fifty-five per cent of households in 1979 to sixty-seven per cent in 1990. For the purchasers the experience was commonly one of release. They replaced the uniform council door, built porches and extensions, installed new kitchens, and spoke to interviewers of pride, of having something to leave their children and of no longer needing permission. Many made substantial capital gains. The policy was, and has remained, popular with those who benefited from it, and studies of opinion suggest it was popular even among tenants who did not buy.

A second provision of the legislation, less noticed at the time, determined the long term consequences. Councils were permitted to spend only a fraction of the money they received from sales, initially half and soon a fifth, and were required to apply the remainder to reducing their debts. They were at the same time prevented by controls on their borrowing from building replacements. Council house building, which had exceeded a hundred thousand dwellings a year in the mid 1970s, fell to a few thousand by the end of the 1980s and to a few hundred in the 1990s. Housing associations, the non-profit bodies to which the government transferred the task of providing new social housing, built far fewer homes than had been sold. The stock of social rented housing in Britain therefore shrank by more than a million and a half dwellings in two decades. The Right to Buy was not an exchange of one form of secure housing for another. It was a transfer of homes out of the public sector, without replacement.

The sales were not evenly spread. Tenants bought houses, especially houses with gardens on the more attractive suburban estates, and they were the tenants in work, in middle age and with some savings. Flats in tower blocks and on the poorer inner city estates were seldom bought, and the unemployed, the elderly and single parents were seldom buyers. The effect was to remove from the council sector its better property and its more prosperous residents together. What remained was increasingly the housing of those with no other option. In 1979 nearly half of council tenants had household incomes in the upper half of the national distribution. Within fifteen years the proportion had fallen to under a fifth, and two thirds of households in social housing had no member in employment. The term coined for the process was residualisation. Council housing, which Bevan had conceived as accommodation for the whole community, became a tenure of the poor, and its declining reputation was then cited as further evidence that renting from the state was a mark of failure.

The subsequent history of the houses sold added a twist that neither the supporters nor the opponents of the policy had anticipated. The original buyers were free to resell after a few years, and when they or their heirs did so, a great many of the properties were acquired by private landlords. Surveys in the 2010s found that around four in ten of the flats sold under the Right to Buy in London were being let privately, and in some boroughs the proportion was higher. The tenants were in many cases households receiving housing benefit, so that the state was paying a market rent, several times the former council rent, on dwellings it had built and sold at half price. Some councils, obliged by law to accommodate homeless families and short of housing in which to do so, found themselves leasing back their own former flats from investors. Those who had bought flats, meanwhile, discovered that as leaseholders they were liable for a share of the cost of major repairs to their blocks, and were on occasion presented with bills for tens of thousands of pounds.

The electoral effects were less clear cut than legend suggests. The Conservatives won the elections of 1983 and 1987 with strong support among skilled workers, and people who had bought their council houses were more likely to vote Conservative than those who had not. Careful studies have found, however, that the buyers had mostly been inclined to the Conservatives before they bought, and that purchase itself made little difference to their vote. What is beyond dispute is the effect on the Labour Party. Having opposed the Right to Buy in 1980 and promised to repeal it, Labour found the policy so popular among its own supporters that it abandoned its opposition before the election of 1987, and when it returned to office ten years later it left the right intact, merely trimming the discounts.

Judgments on the Right to Buy divide along the lines that separate the two views of housing described at the start of this book. Considered as the creation of owners, it was the most successful act of redistribution of wealth to working class households in British history, and it gave more than a million and a half families an asset and an independence they valued. Considered as housing policy for the nation, it sold a public resource built up over sixty years, at a heavy discount, to the generation that happened to occupy it, and left their successors with a smaller and poorer social sector and higher rents. Both descriptions are true. Scotland ended the right in 2016 and Wales in 2019, on the grounds that the loss of social housing could no longer be afforded, and in England the maximum discounts were sharply reduced in 2024.

#### After Thatcher: from the ownership peak to Generation Rent

The property-owning democracy reached its furthest extent in Britain soon after the turn of the century. In 2003 about seventy-one per cent of households in England owned their homes, with or without a mortgage. The figure had risen in every decade since the First World War, and commentators took it for granted that it would continue to do so until owner occupation was all but universal. Instead it began to fall. By 2016 it stood at about sixty-three per cent, and although it has since stabilised, the composition of the owning population has changed in a way the average conceals.

The decline was concentrated among the young. In the early 1990s roughly two thirds of people aged twenty-five to thirty-four in England lived in a home that they or their partner owned. Twenty-five years later the proportion was little more than a third. Among people on middle incomes in that age group, the fall was steeper still. Ownership among those over sixty-five, by contrast, continued to rise and approached four fifths. The generation that had bought in the 1970s, 1980s and 1990s, some of them under the Right to Buy, had kept its houses and seen them multiply in value. The generation born after about 1980 faced prices that had roughly tripled in real terms since their parents were the same age, while earnings had risen by perhaps a third. A deposit that a couple in the 1980s could save in two or three years now took ten or more, unless parents or grandparents provided it. The press coined the label Generation Rent.

The young who could not buy did not, as an earlier generation might have, become council tenants, since the council stock had been sold and not replaced. They rented from private landlords, under the short insecure tenancies introduced in 1988, and many of those landlords were members of the older generation who had invested their housing gains in a second property. The same people thus appeared in the story twice, as the owners whose homes had appreciated and as the landlords to whom the young now paid rent. Critics on the left and right alike began to speak of a transfer of income from the young to the old conducted through the housing market.

Conservative governments after 2010 saw the danger to their own position and tried to restore the ladder. Their chosen instruments were subsidies to buyers. The largest, a scheme called Help to Buy introduced in 2013, offered purchasers of newly built homes a government loan of a fifth of the price, or two fifths in London, interest free for five years, so that they needed a deposit of only one twentieth. Some three hundred and fifty thousand homes were bought with its help over a decade. Other measures included exemptions from the tax on property purchases for first time buyers, a savings account to which the government added a bonus of a quarter, and a short lived plan to oblige housing associations to sell to their tenants at a discount. Each was presented as the heir of the Right to Buy.

The results illustrated the limits of the approach. An official evaluation and several independent studies concluded that Help to Buy had raised the prices of new homes in the areas where supply was most constrained, notably London, by an amount comparable to the value of the subsidy, and had increased the quantity built mainly in places where housing was already affordable. A substantial proportion of those assisted could have bought without it. The clearest beneficiaries were the large housebuilding companies, whose profit margins widened conspicuously. One of them awarded its chief executive a bonus of more than a hundred million pounds, later reduced after public outcry, linked to a share price that the scheme had helped to lift. The episode gave rise to the remark that the policy had been help to sell as much as help to buy. Economists of all persuasions pointed out that giving purchasers more money to spend on a commodity in fixed supply raises its price, and that the party of the market might have been expected to know it.

The underlying dilemma was candidly set out by a Conservative minister, David Willetts, in a book published in 2010 with the title The Pinch and the subtitle how the baby boomers took their children's future. Willetts argued that his own generation, born between 1945 and 1965, had by weight of numbers and good fortune accumulated a disproportionate share of the nation's wealth, above all through housing, and had failed in the obligation that Burke placed at the centre of conservatism, to hand on to the next generation what it had received from the last. A property-owning democracy that the young could not enter was a contradiction in terms.

The contradiction had electoral consequences. For most of the twentieth century the main predictor of how a person voted in Britain was social class. By the general election of 2017 it was age, and behind age, tenure. Homeowners of every class leaned Conservative and renters of every class leaned Labour, and the age at which a voter became more likely to support the Conservatives than Labour, once put at thirty-four, had risen to forty-seven and continued upward. A party that had prospered for a century by making owners found that it was no longer making enough of them. Its natural supporters, the owners it had already made, were at the same time the people most opposed to the building of new homes near their own, so that every attempt to increase supply provoked a revolt in the constituencies on which it depended. At the election of 2024 the Conservatives suffered the heaviest defeat in their history, and among voters under forty their support fell to a small fraction of the electorate.

The British experience over a century thus traces a complete arc. An idea conceived in the 1920s as a defence of the social order against socialism succeeded beyond the hopes of its authors. It raised the ownership of homes from a tenth of the population to seven tenths, gave millions of families security and wealth they would not otherwise have had, and converted the party of the left to its premises. It did so through a combination of cheap credit, tax privileges and the sale of public assets, each of which raised the price of housing or reduced the alternatives to buying. The process could continue only so long as each new generation could afford to enter, and the measures that had helped earlier generations to buy were among the reasons the later one could not. Whether the property-owning democracy can be renewed, by building on a scale that would stabilise prices or by other means, or whether it was a phenomenon of one fortunate cohort, is the question that now divides British conservatism.

### Chapter 14: The American Dream

In no country has the ownership of a home been so closely identified with national purpose as in the United States. The detached house on its own plot of ground is the setting of American domestic life as it is imagined in films, advertising and political speeches, and the phrase that names the national aspiration is now understood by most people to mean, first of all, a house of one's own. The identification has deep roots in the country's political thought. It was also deliberately cultivated by government and business in the twentieth century, and it was realised through public policies that extended ownership to most white Americans while withholding it from most black ones.

#### From Jefferson's farmer to Hoover's homeowner

The founding statement is Thomas Jefferson's. Writing in the 1780s, Jefferson argued that the security of a republic rests on a population of independent farmers who own the land they till. Those who labour in the earth, he wrote, are the chosen people of God, and the corruption of morals in the mass of cultivators is a phenomenon of which no age or nation has furnished an example. A man who owns his farm depends on no employer and no landlord, cannot be coerced in his vote and has a tangible interest in the laws. Dependence, by contrast, begets subservience and prepares fit tools for the designs of ambition. Jefferson feared cities and the propertyless crowds he had seen in Europe, and he believed that the vast lands of the American interior would allow the republic to remain a society of small proprietors for a thousand generations.

This vision shaped the disposal of the public lands. The survey of the West into uniform sections, described in Part One, and the laws that offered those sections for sale in small parcels were designed to put land within the reach of ordinary settlers. The Homestead Act of 1862, passed by a Republican Congress during the Civil War after the Southern states that had long blocked it had seceded, made the offer free. Abraham Lincoln, who signed it, had said that he favoured cutting up the wild lands into parcels so that every poor man might have a home. The association between owning land, personal independence and citizenship was thereby fixed in American political culture well before the country became urban, and when it did become urban the ideal was transferred from the farm to the house.

The transfer was neither automatic nor quick. In 1900 fewer than half of American households owned their homes, and in the large cities most families rented. Organised promotion of home ownership as a national goal began around the First World War, when labour unrest and the Russian Revolution alarmed employers and officials. In 1917 the national association of real estate agents launched a campaign under the slogan Own Your Own Home, which the federal Department of Labor took over the following year. Its posters and pamphlets told working men that the homeowner was a better citizen, a steadier worker and a happier husband, and that rent receipts were worthless paper. The reasoning was the same as that of the British Conservatives of the period. A man with a mortgage, it was said, does not strike.

The chief patron of the cause in the 1920s was Herbert Hoover, secretary of commerce from 1921 and president from 1929. Hoover was an engineer by profession and a believer in cooperation between government and business, and he regarded the spread of home ownership as a foundation of what he called American individualism. His department set up a division of building and housing, which worked to standardise building materials, promoted model zoning laws and building codes, and published a manual for prospective buyers that went through many editions. He served as president of a voluntary organisation, Better Homes in America, which by the end of the decade had committees in several thousand communities staging annual demonstrations of model houses. In 1931, with the Depression deepening and foreclosures mounting, he convened a great national conference on home building and home ownership. In opening it he observed that the immortal ballads of home had not been written about tenements or apartments, and he declared that the sentiment for home ownership was so embedded in the American heart that millions of people who dwelt in rented housing longed for the day when they might own.

It was in that same year that a historian, James Truslow Adams, introduced the expression the American dream, in a book on the national character. Adams meant by it something broader than material success: a social order in which every person could attain the fullest stature of which he or she was capable, regardless of the circumstances of birth. He did not mention houses. The narrowing of the phrase to mean the ownership of a single family home in the suburbs was the work of the following decades, and of the builders, lenders and estate agents who adopted it.

Hoover's methods were those of exhortation and voluntary action. He did establish, in 1932, a system of federal home loan banks to lend to savings institutions, but it came too late and did too little to stem the tide of foreclosure. The institutions that actually made mass ownership possible were created by his successor, as described in Part Four: the corporation that refinanced defaulted mortgages, the administration that insured long term loans with low deposits, and the association that bought those loans from lenders. The philosophy, however, passed unchanged from the Republican engineer to the Democratic reformer. Franklin Roosevelt said that a nation of homeowners, of people who own a real share in their own land, is unconquerable. The New Deal has often been described as an American version of social democracy. In housing it was something else. With the exception of a small public housing programme, it committed the resources and the credit of the federal government to the Jeffersonian end of making the citizen a proprietor.

#### Levittown and the suburban republic

When the Second World War ended, the United States faced the most severe housing shortage in its history. Little had been built during fifteen years of depression and war. Some sixteen million servicemen and women were returning, marriages and births were at record levels, and it was estimated that several million families were living doubled up with relatives or in makeshift quarters such as converted barracks, trailers, garages and, in one much reported case, a pair of disused streetcars. The Servicemen's Readjustment Act of 1944, known as the GI Bill, had promised veterans, among other benefits, a government guarantee on loans to buy homes. The demand existed and the finance existed. What was needed was a way of building houses as fast as the country had built ships and aircraft.

The man who supplied it was William Levitt, whose family firm had built housing for war workers and had learned in the process how to organise construction as a sequence of standardised tasks. In 1947 the firm began work on a tract of potato fields on Long Island, some forty kilometres east of Manhattan. Levitt reversed the logic of the assembly line. Instead of the product moving past stationary workers, teams of workers, each trained in a single operation, moved from one house site to the next. There were twenty-six operations in all, beginning with the pouring of a concrete slab, since the houses had no basements. Timber arrived cut to size from the firm's own mill, and nails from its own factory. The company employed non-union labour and bought appliances directly from the manufacturers. At the peak it completed more than thirty houses a day.

The product was a small house of about seventy square metres with two bedrooms, a living room, a kitchen and a bathroom, and an unfinished attic that the owner could convert. It stood on its own plot of about five hundred and fifty square metres, and it came equipped with a refrigerator, a cooker and a washing machine. The price was just under eight thousand dollars. A veteran could buy it with no deposit and a thirty year guaranteed mortgage, and his monthly payment, at about sixty dollars, was less than the rent of a comparable apartment in the city. By 1951 the development, renamed Levittown, contained more than seventeen thousand houses and some eighty thousand people. The firm went on to build a second Levittown in Pennsylvania and a third in New Jersey.

Levitt was imitated across the country. Housing starts, which had averaged fewer than three hundred thousand a year in the 1930s, exceeded a million in 1946 and reached almost two million in 1950. In the two decades after the war some thirty million dwellings were built in the United States, the great majority of them detached single family houses in new suburbs, sold with mortgages insured or guaranteed by the federal government. The proportion of households owning their homes rose from forty-four per cent in 1940 to sixty-two per cent in 1960, a larger increase in twenty years than in the preceding century and a half. For the first time, a majority of American families, including the families of factory workers, clerks and salesmen, owned property of substantial value.

Other arms of government reinforced the pattern.
