The interest paid on a mortgage could be deducted from taxable income, as could local property taxes, and with the great extension of income tax during the war these deductions became valuable to the middle class. The Interstate Highway Act of 1956 committed the federal government to pay nine tenths of the cost of a national network of motorways, which opened vast areas of cheap land on the edge of every city to commuters. Public housing for rent, authorised on a small scale in 1937 and again in 1949, was confined by law to the poor and opposed at every step by the property industry, which denounced it as creeping socialism. The combined effect of federal policy was to make buying a new house in the suburbs the cheapest and easiest way for a white family of moderate income to be well housed, and to leave the cities, where most rental housing stood, to decline.

The promoters of the suburban house were clear about its political meaning. Levitt remarked that no man who owns his own house and lot can be a communist, because he has too much to do. The magazines of the period presented the homeowner tending his lawn and repairing his gutters as the embodiment of the national virtues of self reliance and family responsibility. The most celebrated statement came in Moscow in July 1959, at an American exhibition whose centrepiece was a full scale model of a six room suburban house, furnished and equipped, of the type said to be affordable to an ordinary American worker. Vice President Richard Nixon led the Soviet premier, Nikita Khrushchev, through it, and in the kitchen the two men fell into an impromptu argument about the merits of their systems. Nixon pointed to the washing machine and told Khrushchev that this house could be bought for fourteen thousand dollars, that most veterans of the war had bought houses in that range, and that any steelworker could buy one, paying a hundred dollars a month over twenty-five or thirty years. Khrushchev replied that in the Soviet Union one had only to be born to be entitled to housing. The exchange, which became known as the kitchen debate, placed the mortgaged family home at the centre of the ideological contest of the Cold War.

The new suburbs had their critics from the outset. The writer Lewis Mumford described them as a multitude of uniform, unidentifiable houses, lined up inflexibly at uniform distances on uniform roads, inhabited by people of the same class, the same income and the same age group. Sociologists worried about conformity and the isolation of women at home. Later critics added the costs of dependence on the car, the consumption of land and energy, and the draining of people and taxes from the cities. Much of this criticism underrated what the residents themselves felt they had gained, which was space, privacy, a garden for the children, good schools and an asset. The sociologist Herbert Gans, who lived in the New Jersey Levittown for two years in order to study it, reported that most of his neighbours were content, that they had formed an active community life, and that the uniformity deplored by outsiders mattered little to people who had come from crowded flats.

What the postwar suburb demonstrated was that the conservative ideal of a nation of small proprietors could be realised in an urban industrial society, given two conditions. One was abundant cheap land made accessible by public investment in roads. The other was long term credit on easy terms underwritten by the state. The achievement was presented as a triumph of free enterprise, and in its organisation of production it was. In its finance it was one of the largest government programmes in American history, and like all such programmes it distributed its benefits according to rules. The most consequential of those rules concerned race.

#### The colour line

The houses of Levittown were sold under a contract containing a clause that the premises could not be used or occupied by any person other than members of the Caucasian race, domestic servants excepted. In 1960 the Long Island Levittown had some eighty-two thousand residents, and not one of them was black. Levitt, himself the grandson of a rabbi and excluded as a Jew from some neighbourhoods, explained that he could solve a housing problem or try to solve a racial problem, but could not combine the two, and that if he sold to one black family ninety per cent of his white customers would not buy. His position was not exceptional. It was the standard practice of the industry, and it was the policy of the federal agencies that made the industry possible.

The mechanism was the appraisal of risk. When the Home Owners' Loan Corporation refinanced mortgages in the 1930s, it commissioned maps of more than two hundred cities on which every neighbourhood was assigned one of four grades. The best, coloured green, were new, uniform and occupied by what the surveyors called American business and professional men. The worst, coloured red, were described as hazardous. Age and condition of buildings affected the grade, but so did the character of the inhabitants, and the presence of black residents, however prosperous, generally sufficed to place a district in the lowest category. The surveyors' notes are frank. A neighbourhood might be marked down for an infiltration of Jews, or of Italians, or of what the forms called undesirable elements. From the colour of the lowest grade came the term redlining.

The Federal Housing Administration, which insured the mortgages that financed the postwar suburbs, embodied the same assumptions in its rules. Its underwriting manual instructed valuers that a neighbourhood, to retain stability, should continue to be occupied by the same social and racial classes, warned of the adverse influence of inharmonious racial or nationality groups, and recommended that deeds contain covenants prohibiting occupancy except by the race for which the properties were intended. It favoured new construction on open land over existing houses, and single family houses over apartments. In practice the agency would not insure loans in black neighbourhoods, in racially mixed neighbourhoods or in white neighbourhoods near to black ones, and it required developers seeking its support for a new subdivision to guarantee that the houses would be sold only to whites. Of the mortgages it insured between 1934 and 1962, it has been estimated that about two per cent went to non-white families.

The racial covenant was the private counterpart of these public rules. A clause in the deed bound the buyer and all subsequent owners not to sell or let to members of specified groups, most commonly black people but also, in various regions, Jews, Asians and Mexicans. By the 1940s such covenants covered a large part of the housing in many northern and western cities. The Supreme Court ruled in 1948 that courts could not enforce them, but they remained in deeds, and the federal housing agencies continued for some years to treat their presence as a point in a property's favour. Custom, the practices of estate agents and the threat of violence did the rest. When a black family, William and Daisy Myers and their children, bought a house in the Pennsylvania Levittown from a departing owner in 1957, crowds gathered outside it every night for weeks, stones were thrown through the windows and a cross was burned on the lawn of a neighbour who had befriended them.

Black families who could afford to buy were therefore confined to a restricted supply of older housing in the cities, for which conventional mortgages were unavailable. The shortage of both houses and credit created an opening for a predatory trade. In Chicago and other cities speculators bought houses in districts on the edge of the black areas, frequently from white owners whom they had frightened into selling cheaply with warnings that the neighbourhood was about to change. They then resold to black buyers at a large mark up under what was called a contract for deed. The buyer made a deposit and monthly payments but acquired no title and no equity until the last instalment was paid, and if he missed a single payment the seller could evict him, keep everything paid to date and sell the house again. A study of Chicago estimated that in the 1950s and 1960s some eighty-five per cent of the homes bought by black families in the city were sold on such terms, and that the practice extracted several billion dollars, in present values, from the black community. Families worked two jobs and took in lodgers to keep up the payments, the houses deteriorated for lack of money for repairs, and the resulting overcrowding and decay were then cited as proof that black occupancy lowered property values.

Public policy completed the pattern. The public housing that was built for the poor was sited, at the insistence of white neighbourhoods and their elected representatives, almost entirely within existing black districts, so that it reinforced segregation. The urban renewal programme launched in 1949 cleared so many black neighbourhoods to make way for motorways, civic centres, hospitals and middle class apartments that the writer James Baldwin said urban renewal meant Negro removal. Those displaced crowded into the remaining black areas, which expanded block by block into adjacent white districts as their residents fled to the suburbs with the help of federally insured loans.

The legal structure was dismantled in the 1960s. An executive order in 1962 prohibited discrimination in housing that received federal assistance. The Fair Housing Act, passed in April 1968 in the week after the assassination of Martin Luther King, outlawed discrimination in the sale and rental of most housing. Further laws in the 1970s required lenders to disclose where they made loans and to serve the whole of the communities from which they took deposits. Outright refusal to sell or lend on grounds of race became illegal, and over the following decades many black families did move to the suburbs.

But the opportunity that had been denied could not be restored by a change in the rules. The white families who bought houses in the 1940s and 1950s for eight or ten thousand dollars saw them rise in value many times over, borrowed against them to send their children to college, and passed them on. The black families who had been excluded from those purchases had no such asset to appreciate or bequeath, and by the time the law opened the suburbs to them the houses cost several times as much. The historian Richard Rothstein, among others, has argued that this is the principal source of the racial gap in wealth in the United States today, which is far wider than the gap in incomes. The median white household has something like eight times the net wealth of the median black household. About three quarters of white households own their homes and well under half of black households do, a gap larger than it was when the Fair Housing Act was passed. And the subprime lending of the early 2000s, described in Part Four, fell most heavily on the same neighbourhoods that had been redlined seventy years before.

The American case carries a lesson for the conservative tradition as a whole. A policy of creating owners is a policy of distributing wealth, since those who are helped to buy receive whatever gain follows. It therefore matters a great deal who is included, and the effects of exclusion are transmitted down the generations through inheritance, long after the exclusion itself has ended. The property-owning democracy, wherever it has been built, has had a boundary, and those outside it at the moment of its creation have found it very difficult to enter later.

#### The ownership society and its limits

By the 1960s the rate of home ownership in the United States had reached a plateau a little under two thirds, where it remained for thirty years. Those who could buy on the terms available had done so. To raise the figure further meant reaching households with lower incomes, less savings and weaker credit, and from the 1990s administrations of both parties set out to do that. Their efforts ended in the crisis of 2008, and the episode tested the conservative proposition that ownership is good for people and for society more severely than anything before it.

The support the federal government gives to owners is very large and mostly invisible, because it takes the form of taxes not collected. The deduction of mortgage interest, the deduction of property taxes, the exemption of most capital gains on the sale of a home and the absence of any tax on the benefit of living in one's own house together cost the Treasury well over a hundred billion dollars a year in the early 2000s, several times the whole budget for housing assistance to the poor. The distribution of this support follows from its design. A deduction is worth more to a taxpayer in a higher bracket, with a larger mortgage on a more expensive house, and it is worth nothing to the majority of lower income owners who do not itemise their deductions at all. Analyses by the congressional budget office and others consistently found that most of the benefit of the mortgage interest deduction went to households in the top fifth of the income distribution. Economists of every school regarded it as an expensive way of encouraging people who would have bought anyway to buy larger houses, and as tending to raise prices in places where supply was tight. It survived every attempt at reform until 2017, when a change in the general structure of deductions, made for other reasons, greatly reduced the number of taxpayers who claimed it.

In 1995 the administration of Bill Clinton announced a national homeownership strategy with the aim of raising the rate to a record level by the end of the decade. The plan relied on persuading lenders to accept smaller deposits and more flexible evidence of income, and on increasing the share of loans to low and moderate income borrowers that Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac were required to buy. George W. Bush made the theme his own. In a series of speeches from 2002 he set a target of five and a half million additional minority homeowners by the end of the decade, and he described his broader programme as the creation of an ownership society. If you own something, he said, you have a vital stake in the future of the country, and the more ownership there is in America, the more vitality there is. Legislation in 2003 offered grants towards deposits, and the president called on the industry to reduce the barriers of paperwork and down payment that kept families from buying. The rhetoric was Jefferson's and Hoover's, applied to the mortgage market of the twenty-first century.

The ownership rate did rise, to a peak of just over sixty-nine per cent in 2004. The collapse that followed has been described already. By 2016 the rate had fallen below sixty-three per cent, its lowest in half a century, and the gains among black and Hispanic households had been more than wiped out. Several million families who had been encouraged to buy lost their homes, their savings and their credit standing, and would have been better off had they remained tenants. Whatever view is taken of where blame for the crisis lies, it established one point beyond argument. Ownership acquired with little equity and unstable finance at the top of a market does not confer the security and independence that the conservative tradition attributes to property. It confers risk, concentrated on those least able to bear it.

The crisis prompted a closer look at the claims made for ownership in general. That owners differ from tenants is not in doubt. They vote more, join more local organisations, maintain their property better, move less often, and their children do somewhat better at school. The difficulty lies in knowing how far ownership causes these things. People who buy homes are on average older, richer, more settled and more likely to be married than those who rent, and would probably behave differently whatever their tenure. The economists Denise DiPasquale and Edward Glaeser, in a careful study, concluded that some effect remained after allowing for such differences, and that much of it arose simply because owners stay longer in one place and so have more reason to invest in it. That finding points to a qualification. If the civic benefits come from stability of residence, they could be obtained equally by giving tenants secure and lasting tenancies, as is done in Germany and Switzerland, where rates of ownership are low and civic life is not noticeably weaker.

Other research identified costs. The British economist Andrew Oswald observed that across countries and regions, higher rates of home ownership were associated with higher unemployment, and proposed that owners, being costly to move, are slower to follow jobs, so that a nation of owners has a less adaptable labour force. Later studies have partly confirmed and partly qualified the claim. Fischel's account of the homevoter, discussed in Part Three, identified a further cost: owners use local politics to obstruct new building. And the experience of owners trapped in declining industrial cities, unable to sell houses that had lost most of their value, showed that tying a family's wealth to a single immovable asset in a single place is a hazardous form of saving.

It is worth noting, finally, a form of American home ownership that receives little attention and almost no subsidy. Some twenty million people in the United States, most of them on low incomes, live in factory built dwellings known as mobile or manufactured homes. Such a home can be bought for a fraction of the cost of a conventional house, and it is the largest source of unsubsidised affordable housing in the country. It also illustrates in a pure form the distinction on which this book has repeatedly insisted. The resident typically owns the structure and rents the plot on which it stands, in a park belonging to someone else. The structure depreciates like a vehicle. The land appreciates, and its owner can raise the ground rent or sell the site for redevelopment, in which case the supposedly mobile home, which in practice cannot be moved without great expense, is lost. In recent years investment funds have bought such parks in large numbers, attracted by tenants who cannot leave. The owner of a manufactured home has the responsibilities of ownership without the asset. The owner of a suburban house has an asset because he owns the ground.

The American record therefore gives the conservative tradition both its greatest success and its sharpest warnings. The postwar expansion of ownership created a broad propertied middle class and gave tens of millions of families a security and a stake that they valued and that contributed to the stability of the country. It was accomplished by public credit, public roads and tax privileges, not by the unaided market. It excluded a tenth of the population on grounds of race, with consequences that persist. And when the attempt was made, half a century later, to extend it to those who had been left out, the means chosen were those of easy credit alone, without the cheap land, rising wages and safe finance of the earlier period, and the result was catastrophe for many of the intended beneficiaries.

### Chapter 15: Other Conservatisms

The British and American versions of the ownership ideal are the best known, but they are not the only ones, nor in some respects the most successful. On the European continent the same goal was pursued by parties inspired by Catholic social doctrine, for whom the family home was a moral institution before it was an economic one. It was pursued with fewer scruples by the authoritarian regimes of the Mediterranean, which saw in the small proprietor a guarantee of political quiescence. And in the British settler countries and Japan it became part of the national self-image. This chapter surveys these variants and ends with an assessment of the tradition as a whole.

#### Catholic social teaching and Christian democracy

The doctrine laid down in the encyclical Rerum Novarum in 1891, described in Part Two, gave Catholic politicians a housing programme distinct from both liberalism and socialism. Against the socialists it affirmed that private property is a natural right and that the family, not the state, is the primary unit of society. Against the liberals it held that the market left to itself degrades the worker, that wages must be sufficient for a family to live decently and to save, and that the law should actively help as many as possible to become owners. Pius XI restated the teaching in 1931, adding the principle of subsidiarity: a larger body should not take over what a smaller one can do for itself. The ideal that followed was a detached house with a garden, owned by the family that lived in it, in which a mother could raise children away from the moral dangers of the tenement, and in which the father, as a proprietor, would be immune to revolutionary agitation. Collective blocks of rented flats, especially those built by socialist municipalities with communal laundries and meeting halls, were viewed with suspicion as machines for dissolving the family into the mass.

Belgium shows the doctrine in its purest application. The Catholic party governed the country without interruption from 1884 to 1914, and its housing law of 1889, the first of its kind in Europe, was designed to make workers into owners. It allowed the public savings bank to lend at low interest, through local credit societies, to workers who wished to buy or build a small house, and it reduced the taxes and fees on such transactions. Cheap workmen's season tickets on the dense national railway network allowed industrial workers to go on living in their villages, where land was cheap and the influence of the parish priest was strong, and to travel daily to the factories and mines. The policy was explicit in its purposes. It aimed to keep the working class dispersed, rural, propertied and Catholic, and to prevent the formation of the large urban concentrations in which socialism flourished.

The approach was renewed after the Second World War by a law of 1948 named after the Christian democratic minister Alfred De Taeye. It granted a cash premium to any family of modest means that built or bought a new house for its own occupation, together with a state guarantee on the mortgage. Several hundred thousand houses were built under it in the following decades, most of them commissioned individually by their future owners on plots along existing roads. The socialist alternative, a law of the following year that funded rental housing by public societies, received fewer resources and produced a small social sector. The outcome is visible in the Belgian landscape and statistics alike. About seven households in ten own their homes, social rented housing amounts to only some six or seven per cent of the stock, and the country is covered by a continuous scatter of detached houses strung along its roads, a pattern that planners deplore and that Belgians, who have a proverb to the effect that they are born with a brick in the stomach, have shown no wish to abandon.

In the Netherlands the confessional parties took a different route to a related end. Dutch society was organised into separate Catholic, Protestant and socialist communities, each with its own schools, newspapers, trade unions and broadcasting, and the housing associations created under the act of 1901 were formed along the same lines. Catholic and Protestant associations built rented housing for their own people, with public loans. The result was a very large non-profit rental sector, of which the religious parties were as much the authors as the socialists were. It illustrates that Christian democracy was not committed to owner occupation as such. Its commitment was to housing provided by the institutions of civil society, close to the family and the church, in preference to the state.

Italy's Christian Democrats, who dominated every government from 1946 to the early 1990s, combined both approaches. A plan launched in 1949 on the initiative of the labour minister Amintore Fanfani, and known by the name of the insurance institute that administered it, financed the construction of some three hundred and fifty thousand dwellings over fourteen years through a levy on wages paid by workers and employers together. Its stated purpose was as much to relieve unemployment as to provide homes. The estates it built, designed by many of the leading Italian architects of the day in a deliberately modest and traditional idiom, are among the most admired public housing of the period. A large proportion of the dwellings were allocated on terms that led to ownership, and in later decades most of the remainder were sold to their tenants. Italian policy thereafter favoured purchase through tax concessions and tolerated a great deal of unauthorised building, periodically legalised by amnesty. Rent regulation introduced in 1978 made letting unattractive, and the private rental sector shrank. Italy today has a rate of home ownership above seven tenths and one of the smallest stocks of social housing in western Europe.

West Germany under Konrad Adenauer faced the most extreme shortage on the continent, with a fifth of all dwellings destroyed and some twelve million refugees and expellees from the east to accommodate. The first housing act of 1950 necessarily concentrated on quantity, subsidising whoever would build, and most of the output was rented flats. The Christian Democrats made clear that this was a matter of necessity and not of preference. The second housing act of 1956, shaped by the housing minister Paul Lücke, a devout Catholic, declared that the promotion of housing should give priority to the family home with a garden, and that its aim was to bind broad sections of the people to the soil through property. Tax relief and premiums for those saving with the building savings banks supported the policy. Lücke was also responsible for the gradual removal of rent controls from 1960. Germany nevertheless remained a country of tenants. The rented flats built in the emergency years were of good quality and securely let, the lending system required large deposits, and buying never became the financial necessity that it was in Britain. The Christian democratic preference for the owner occupied house shaped the German suburbs without displacing the rental apartment as the home of the majority.

France, where Catholic social thought influenced parties of the centre and right, followed a similar course at a distance. A law of 1928 sponsored by the minister Louis Loucheur provided state loans for the building of small suburban houses, and gave rise to the belts of modest detached dwellings that surround French towns. After the war the urgency of the shortage led to the mass construction of rented estates. A reform of 1977 under President Giscard d'Estaing redirected support towards individual buyers, with subsidised loans for households of modest income, and the president spoke of the French preference for the individual house. Thirty years later Nicolas Sarkozy campaigned for the presidency on a promise to create a France of owners and set a target of seventy per cent ownership. It was not reached. The rate has remained a little under three fifths, and Sarkozy's proposal that France should adopt American style mortgages secured on rising house values was quietly dropped after 2008.

#### The authoritarian right

The dictatorships of the right that governed much of southern and central Europe between the 1920s and the 1970s shared the Christian democratic preference for the propertied family and added to it a frank concern with control. A regime that had suppressed trade unions and opposition parties still had to reckon with an urban working class, and housing offered a means of winning its acquiescence or at least dividing it. The small owner, absorbed in paying for his home and anxious to keep it, was the model subject.

The fascist and National Socialist regimes were ideologically hostile to the big city, which they associated with socialism, cosmopolitanism and falling birth rates. Mussolini proclaimed a policy of ruralisation and sought to discourage migration to the towns. His government did build public housing through local institutes, and it rehoused the poor displaced by its monumental clearances in the centre of Rome in isolated settlements on the far outskirts, called borgate, some of which lacked water and transport for years. In Germany the Nazi regime promoted the small suburban homestead, a modest house with a large garden plot on which a worker's family could grow vegetables and keep small livestock, as the form of housing proper to a people rooted in blood and soil. Eligibility depended on political reliability and racial fitness. In practice rearmament took priority and the regime built less housing than the Weimar republic had done. Its most consequential act in the field was of a different kind. From 1938 Jewish owners were compelled to sell their houses and businesses at a fraction of their value, and Jewish tenants were stripped of legal protection and concentrated in designated buildings before deportation. In Berlin, Vienna and other cities tens of thousands of dwellings were thereby made available to others, a redistribution of housing by persecution whose beneficiaries were numerous.

Portugal under António Salazar offered the most coherent statement of the authoritarian Catholic ideal. A programme of economical houses begun in 1933 built small neighbourhoods of single family dwellings in a traditional national style, each with its garden, allotted to members of the state controlled unions and to public employees. The occupant paid a monthly sum for twenty-five years and then became the owner, provided that his conduct had been satisfactory. Salazar set out the philosophy in terms that could have been taken from the encyclicals. The intimacy of family life, he said, demands shelter, isolation and, in a word, the house, the independent house, our own house. He rejected the large collective block as destructive of the family. The programme was small in relation to need, and the majority of the poor in Lisbon and Porto lived in rented rooms or in shanty settlements, but it defined what the regime considered proper housing.

Spain under Franco pursued the same end on a far larger scale, and the results have shaped the country to the present. The civil war had destroyed or damaged a quarter of a million dwellings, and in the two decades that followed several million people left the countryside for Madrid, Barcelona, Bilbao and Valencia. The regime's housing institute, created in 1939, and the housing arm of its official trade union built estates for rent and for sale, but output in the 1940s was meagre. Rents on existing dwellings had been frozen, so that private capital would not build to let. The newcomers put up shacks on the edges of the cities, and by the mid 1950s the shanty towns of Madrid alone held well over a hundred thousand people.

The response came in 1957 with the creation of a ministry of housing under José Luis Arrese, a veteran of the Falange. Arrese stated the policy in a phrase that is still quoted: the regime wanted a country of owners, not of proletarians. On another occasion he said that the man without a home takes over the street and, driven by his ill humour, becomes subversive, bitter and violent. The instrument was a system of officially protected housing. Private developers who built dwellings within set limits of size and price received subsidies, cheap loans, tax exemptions and priority in the supply of scarce materials. They were free to sell, and with few exceptions they did. A law of 1960 on horizontal property gave a secure legal form to the ownership of individual flats within a building, which had previously been awkward, and it made possible the sale of apartment blocks unit by unit. A national plan for the years 1961 to 1976 set a target of nearly four million dwellings, and it was met.

The quantity was achieved at a cost in quality and in urban order. Speculative builders, often with close connections to the authorities, erected tall blocks at very high densities on the outskirts of the cities, frequently before streets, sewers, schools or transport had been provided, and some estates developed structural defects within a few years. But the transformation of tenure was complete. In 1950 fewer than half of Spanish households owned their homes, and in the large cities perhaps one in twenty. By 1970 the national figure was close to two thirds, and by 1991 it exceeded three quarters. Because the freeze on old rents remained in force, the rental sector withered as owners sold to sitting tenants or left buildings to decay. And because the protected dwellings lost their protected status after a period of years and could then be resold at market prices, the public money invested in them created private assets and left no lasting stock of social housing. The regime had succeeded in making Spain a nation of proprietors. The consequences for those who came later are taken up in the final part of this book.

Greece arrived at an even higher rate of ownership by a method that required neither public subsidy nor mortgage banks, under conservative governments that deliberately stood aside. The system, known as antiparochi, or exchange in kind, rested on a law of 1929 that allowed the separate ownership of floors and apartments. The owner of a plot, typically occupied by an old single family house, would hand it to a small builder. The builder demolished the house and erected an apartment block of five or six storeys at his own expense, and in payment the landowner received a share of the finished flats, usually between a third and a half. The builder sold the rest, often before completion, to finance the work. No money needed to change hands for the land, no bank loan was required, and the state encouraged the process by exempting such transactions from tax and by permitting generous building heights.

Between the 1950s and the 1980s most of Athens and Thessaloniki was rebuilt in this way. The outcome was a dense and architecturally uniform city of concrete blocks with little green space. It was also a city in which a very large proportion of families owned property, in which different social classes lived in the same building, the better off on the upper floors and the poorer on the lower, and in which there were no shanty towns and almost no public housing. Family plots were turned into flats for sons and daughters, and housing became the chief form of saving and of provision for the next generation. Similar practices developed in Turkey and elsewhere in the eastern Mediterranean. The Greek case shows that mass ownership can arise from small scale private initiative on fragmented land holdings where the state permits it, and that a government may regard the result, a population of small property holders with every reason to fear disorder, as ample reward for its forbearance.

#### The settler societies, Ireland and Japan

In Australia, New Zealand and Canada the ownership of a detached house on a generous plot became part of the national identity earlier and more thoroughly than in Britain. Land was plentiful, wages were high, the cities were new and low built, and the immigrants who peopled them had come precisely to escape the landlord. Colonial governments had assisted settlers to acquire land from the beginning, and when the housing question arose in the twentieth century the instinctive answer was to help the family to buy.

Australia is the leading case. As noted in Part Two, something like four households in ten already owned their homes in the 1880s. The ideal was given its classic political expression by Robert Menzies, the founder of the modern Liberal Party, which in Australia is the party of the right. In a radio address of 1942 entitled The Forgotten People he appealed to the middle class of salary earners, shopkeepers, skilled artisans and farmers, whom he distinguished from both the rich and the organised manual workers. The real life of the nation, he said, is to be found in the homes of people who are nameless and unadvertised, and he praised what he called homes material, homes human and homes spiritual. The instinct to be with one's wife and children in a house and garden that are one's own, to which one can withdraw and in which one can be among friends, was in his account the foundation of sanity and sobriety, and its possession made a man a responsible citizen. One of the best instincts in us, he added, is that which induces us to have one little piece of earth with a house and a garden which is ours.

Menzies governed from 1949 to 1966, and his governments acted on the creed. The wartime Labor government had negotiated an agreement under which the Commonwealth lent money to the states to build public housing for rent. When the agreement was renewed in 1956, a substantial share of the funds was redirected to building societies for lending to buyers, and the states were encouraged to sell the houses they had built to the tenants. Returned servicemen received loans on favourable terms. The proportion of Australian households owning their homes rose from fifty-three per cent in 1947 to more than seventy per cent in 1966, at that date probably the highest in the developed world. The expression the great Australian dream came into use to describe the detached house on its quarter acre block, and public housing remained a small residual sector of around five per cent.

The later history parallels the British. From the 1990s Australian tax policy came to favour not only the owner occupier but the small investor. A provision known as negative gearing allows a landlord whose rental income falls short of his interest and other costs to deduct the loss from his other taxable income, and a reform of 1999 halved the tax on capital gains. Together these made it attractive to borrow heavily to buy a property that lost money each year, in expectation of a lightly taxed gain on resale. The number of households owning investment properties multiplied, house prices in Sydney and Melbourne rose to around ten times average incomes, and ownership among Australians in their late twenties and thirties fell steeply. Grants to first time buyers, introduced in 2000 and periodically increased, were judged by most economists to have been absorbed into prices. A proposal by the Labor opposition to restrict negative gearing contributed to its unexpected defeat at the election of 2019, an indication of how many voters had come to hold a stake in the arrangement.

New Zealand followed a comparable path with a significant difference of emphasis. The first Labour government, elected in 1935, built state houses for rent of a quality deliberately equal to private housing, and the prime minister personally carried furniture into the first of them in 1937. The conservative National Party, which took office in 1949, offered the tenants the right to buy and expanded cheap government loans for the building of private homes, later allowing families to draw their children's welfare allowances in advance as a lump sum for a deposit. Ownership rose to about three quarters of households by the 1980s. Canada established a federal mortgage corporation in 1946 that insured loans on the American model and sustained a similar rate of suburban building.

Ireland is an instructive case because the conservative preference for ownership was applied there to public housing far earlier than in Britain. The land acts of the late nineteenth and early twentieth centuries, described in Part One, had turned tenant farmers into owners with state finance, and the same principle was soon extended. Local authorities had built cottages for rural labourers from the 1880s, and legislation in the 1930s gave the occupants the right to purchase them by annuity. Tenant purchase was extended to urban council housing in 1966. Over the decades the Irish state built a large number of dwellings and sold about two thirds of them to the people living in them, at substantial discounts. Combined with grants and tax relief for private purchasers, the policy produced a rate of home ownership of about eighty per cent by the early 1990s. The dominant party of independent Ireland, Fianna Fáil, was populist and nationalist in character, and its attachment to the small proprietor drew on both Catholic social teaching and the memory of the landlord. The country's later experience of boom, collapse and shortage has been described in Part Four.

Japan presents the ideal in a different cultural setting. Before the Second World War the great majority of urban Japanese rented. Wartime rent control, the destruction of the cities by bombing and the occupation's land reform, which transferred farmland to its cultivators, altered the picture. The conservative Liberal Democratic Party, which governed almost continuously from 1955, built its housing policy on three institutions created in the early 1950s: a public loan corporation for those who could buy, public rental housing for the poor, and a housing corporation that built estates of apartments for salaried workers in the large cities. The first was much the most important. The typical career of a Japanese employee was imagined as a progression from a company dormitory to a rented apartment, then to a condominium, and finally to a detached house in the suburbs, and the attitude of devotion to this private goal was known by a phrase borrowed from English, my home ism. The government regarded it with favour as a stabilising force after the political turbulence of 1960. Ownership settled at around three fifths of households. What distinguishes Japan, as noted elsewhere in this book, is that the house itself is not expected to hold its value, so that the home is a goal of family life without being a vehicle of accumulation in the Western manner, a difference that became decisive after the bubble burst.

#### An assessment of the conservative tradition

The policy of making owners has been tried for a century, in democracies and dictatorships, in rich countries and poor ones, by parties of the right and, once converted, by parties of the left. Its record can be judged against the claims made for it, which were of three kinds: that ownership is good for the families who attain it, that it is good for the political order, and that it can be extended to more or less everyone.

On the first claim the tradition has much to its credit. Families who came to own their homes in the middle decades of the twentieth century gained things they valued and that tenants of the time generally lacked: security against eviction, freedom to alter and improve, protection against rising rents, low housing costs in old age and an asset to leave to their children. In countries where renting was insecure or stigmatised, the difference between the two conditions was large, and it is not surprising that people wanted to cross from one to the other. The satisfaction that owners report is real, and the conservative insistence that a home is more than a unit of accommodation, that it is bound up with family, memory and independence, captures something that purely economic accounts of housing miss.

Some qualifications are needed even here. Much of what owners enjoy is security and control, and these can be provided to tenants by law, as they are in Germany, Switzerland, Austria and the Netherlands, where long tenancies at regulated rents allow families to remain in the same home for decades and to treat it as their own. The benefits are also conditional on the terms of purchase. Ownership with a manageable debt, bought at a price that later proves reasonable, is a source of security. Ownership bought at the peak with a loan of the whole price is a source of anxiety and sometimes of ruin, as millions of American, Irish and Spanish families discovered. And the wealth that ownership confers is, for the most part, the rise in the value of land. It accrues only where and when prices rise, it is paid for by the next buyer, and it was large for the generations that bought before the long boom and is much smaller or absent for those who buy after it.

On the second claim, concerning political stability, the tradition's founders were broadly right about the effect and perhaps mistaken about its value. Societies in which most households own property have proved resistant to revolutionary politics. Owners do vote more conservatively on questions that touch their assets. The regimes that made owners in order to secure quiescence, from Franco's Spain to the England of the 1930s, obtained it. But the caution that property induces is not selective. The owner who will not support a revolution will not support a housing estate at the end of his road either, or a change in the tax treatment of his home, or anything else that might affect its value. A property-owning democracy turns out to be a democracy in which the propertied majority uses its votes to protect what it holds, and the principal victims of that protection are the people who have not yet acquired property, including the owners' own children. Burke valued property because it bound the generations together. In its modern form it has frequently set them at odds.

The third claim, that ownership can be made universal, is the one that the record most clearly contradicts. Every country that has pursued the policy has seen ownership rise to a ceiling somewhere between three fifths and four fifths of households and then stall or decline. The reason is contained in the methods used. Tax relief for buyers, subsidised loans, grants for deposits, relaxed lending standards and the discounted sale of public housing all increase what purchasers are able to pay. Where the supply of homes in desirable places does not increase in step, the additional purchasing power is absorbed by higher prices. The first cohort to receive the help benefits twice, from the subsidy and from the rise in value that follows. Later cohorts receive the subsidy and pay the higher price, and are no better off, or worse. Meanwhile the alternatives to buying are run down, because rental housing is taxed more heavily, regulated more tightly or sold off. The policy therefore makes ownership at once more necessary and less attainable.

A comparison of national rates of ownership is revealing in this connection. The highest in Europe, above nine tenths, are found in Romania and other formerly communist countries where state flats were given away. Next come the countries of the south, including Spain, Italy and Greece, at about three quarters, where ownership was promoted by authoritarian or clientelist governments and where the young now live with their parents into their thirties because they can neither buy nor rent. Britain, the United States, Canada and Australia stand at about two thirds. France is a little lower. The lowest rates are those of Germany, at about half, and Switzerland, at about two fifths, which are among the richest and most stable societies in the world. There is no sign in these figures that a higher rate of ownership makes a nation more prosperous, more secure or better housed. If anything the relationship runs the other way. Ownership is highest where the alternatives are worst.

The distributional record also needs to be stated plainly. Support for owner occupation has in most countries been the largest housing subsidy of all, exceeding by a wide margin what is spent on housing for the poor, and it has gone disproportionately to households in the upper half of the income scale, since they buy more expensive homes and pay higher rates of tax. Because housing wealth is passed on, the pattern of who owned in one generation shapes who can buy in the next. In Britain, the United States and Australia the chance that a young adult owns a home now depends to a marked degree on whether his or her parents did. A policy conceived as a way of diffusing property has become, in its mature phase, a mechanism for concentrating it within families, and the American history of racial exclusion shows how durable the effects of an initial boundary can be.

Conservatives themselves have begun to draw conclusions from this, and they have not all drawn the same ones. One response holds to the original ideal and argues that the fault lies in the failure to build. If enough homes were constructed where people wish to live, prices would stabilise and each generation could buy as the last one did. Those who take this view have allied themselves with the supply side reformers described in Part Three, against the instincts of their own property owning voters. A second response continues to offer assistance to buyers, on the grounds that the aspiration is legitimate and must be seen to be supported, whatever economists say about the effect on prices. A third, more radical, questions whether owner occupation should be treated as the only respectable tenure at all, and looks with new interest at secure renting on the German pattern as a foundation for family life that does not depend on entering the market at the right moment.

The conservative tradition was right that people want a place that is securely theirs, and right that a society benefits when most of its members have a stake in it. Where it went wrong was in equating that stake with a tradable asset whose value was expected to rise without limit, and in supposing that subsidy could spread such an asset to everyone. The traditions examined in the next two parts of this book began from a different premise. They held that security in one's home should not depend on buying it, and that the community, through the state, the municipality or the association, should provide directly what the market and the mortgage could not. Part Six takes up the first and more moderate of them.

## Part Six: The Social State

### Chapter 16: Red Vienna and Central Europe

The social democratic tradition accepts the market economy and private property and denies that either can be trusted to house a whole population decently. Its characteristic belief is that a home is a social right, to be secured by collective action in the way that schooling and medical care are secured, and its characteristic method is to place beside the private market a large sector of housing that is built with public support, let at rents related to cost, and managed by a municipality or a non-profit body for the long term. Where the conservative makes the tenant an owner, the social democrat makes renting a condition in which a family can live securely for life. This part of the book examines the tradition in the places where it achieved most, beginning with the city whose experiment between the wars remains its emblem.

#### The city in 1918, the settlers and the Breitner taxes

Vienna at the end of the First World War was a capital that had lost its empire. For centuries it had been the administrative and financial centre of a state of more than fifty million people. In November 1918 it found itself the oversized head of a small Alpine republic of six and a half million, cut off by new frontiers from the coal of Bohemia and the grain of Hungary. Its population of about two million was cold, hungry and swept by influenza and tuberculosis, which was known across Europe as the Viennese disease. Housing conditions before the war had been among the worst on the continent, as described in Part Two. Three quarters of dwellings consisted of one room and a kitchen or less, most had neither running water nor a lavatory of their own, and many households sublet sleeping space to strangers.

In one respect the war had transformed the position of tenants. The imperial government had frozen rents and prohibited evictions in 1917, and the inflation that followed, which by 1922 had reduced the Austrian crown to a fifteen thousandth of its former value, made the frozen rents trivial. A family that had paid a quarter of its income for a dwelling in 1914 paid, by the early 1920s, the equivalent of a few tram tickets. This was an enormous gain for those who had a tenancy. It also meant that no private investor would build housing to let, and that owners could not afford to repair what they had. If anything was to be built, it would have to be built by public authority.

The first response came from below. In the winter of 1918 and the years that followed, tens of thousands of Viennese occupied land on the fringes of the city, in the woods and on former military parade grounds, cleared it and began to grow food and to build huts and small houses. What started as a struggle against starvation became an organised movement of settlers, formed into cooperatives, which demanded land, materials and legal recognition from the city and in 1921 marched on the town hall in great numbers to obtain them. The movement attracted some of the most original minds in the city. The architect Adolf Loos served as chief architect of the municipal settlement office and designed terraced houses that their occupants could help to build. The philosopher and economist Otto Neurath organised the settlers' association. Members contributed a fixed number of hours of labour, commonly two thousand or more, and the houses were owned collectively by the cooperative.

The Social Democratic Workers' Party won an absolute majority on the Vienna city council in May 1919, in the first election held under universal suffrage for both sexes, and it held the city until 1934. Its leaders belonged to the school known as Austro-Marxism, which sought a path between the reformism of the German socialists and the dictatorship of the Bolsheviks. Unable to take power in the republic as a whole, where the conservative Christian Social party governed with the support of the Catholic countryside, they resolved to make the capital a demonstration of what socialism could accomplish by democratic means. A constitutional change in 1922 gave them the instrument. Vienna was separated from the surrounding province and became a federal state in its own right, with the power to levy its own taxes.

The man who used that power was the city councillor for finance, Hugo Breitner, a former bank director. Breitner faced a problem that defined the whole experiment. The city could not borrow, since the capital markets were closed to a socialist municipality and he did not wish to burden housing with interest. He therefore resolved to pay for construction out of current revenue, and to raise that revenue from those most able to pay. In 1923 he introduced a tax earmarked for housing, levied on the pre-war rental value of all rented property and payable by the tenant. Its scale was steeply progressive. The occupant of an ordinary working class dwelling paid a negligible sum. The occupants of the most valuable half of one per cent of properties, the great apartments and commercial premises of the inner city, contributed close to half the yield. Alongside it Breitner imposed a series of taxes on luxury: on domestic servants beyond the first, on motor cars, riding horses and racehorses, on champagne and on tables in the grander restaurants and night clubs.

The taxes made Breitner the most hated man in bourgeois Vienna. The conservative press depicted him as a vampire, and a leader of the right wing militias declared at a rally that there would be no peace until the head of this Asiatic rolled in the sand, a remark that referred to Breitner's Jewish origin. The city's posters answered with pictures of the new housing and the legend that it had been built from the proceeds of the housing tax. The financial result was remarkable. Because construction was paid for outright, the completed buildings carried no debt, and rents needed to cover only maintenance and administration. The city was thereby able to let new dwellings, equipped to a standard that few working class Viennese had ever known, for a sum amounting to about four per cent of a worker's wage.

The party's approach to the settlers' movement was ambivalent. Its leaders admired the settlers' energy but distrusted their independence, and they doubted that low houses with gardens could be built in sufficient numbers within the limits of the city. There was also a difference of outlook. The settlers wished to become, in effect, small collective proprietors growing their own food. The party's theorists wanted workers concentrated, organised and educated in a common culture. From 1923 the city directed its resources increasingly towards large blocks of flats under its own ownership and management, and the settlement movement, though it continued, was reduced to a secondary role. The choice between helping people to house themselves and housing them through a public authority was to recur throughout the history of social housing, and Vienna decided it in favour of the authority.

#### The building programme, the Karl-Marx-Hof and the civil war

In September 1923 the city council voted to build twenty-five thousand dwellings in five years. The target was reached ahead of time, a second programme followed, and by the end of 1933 the municipality had completed some sixty-four thousand dwellings in nearly four hundred developments. About two hundred thousand people, a tenth of the population, lived in them. No other city in the world had housed so large a share of its inhabitants in new public dwellings in so short a period, and none had done so without borrowing.

The typical form was the Hof, a large block built around the perimeter of its site and enclosing a landscaped courtyard. The existing building regulations allowed private developers to cover as much as eighty-five per cent of a plot. The city set itself a limit of half and frequently built on less, so that the courtyards became gardens with trees, lawns, paddling pools and playgrounds, overlooked by the flats and closed to traffic. Stairways were entered from the courtyard and served only a few dwellings on each floor, doing away with the long corridors of the old tenements, on which doors and the single shared tap had opened.

The dwellings themselves were small, most of them between thirty-eight and forty-eight square metres, a point on which the programme was criticised at the time by visiting housing experts from Germany and Britain. But each had what the old housing lacked. Every flat had its own running water and its own lavatory inside the front door, a kitchen with a gas cooker, an entrance lobby, and windows giving direct light and air to every room. Many had balconies. Bathrooms were not provided in the individual flats. Instead each estate had a communal bathhouse and a steam powered central laundry, where a week's washing could be done in a few hours.

The communal facilities were the feature that most impressed observers and that expressed the wider aims of the builders. The larger estates contained kindergartens, mother and baby clinics, dental surgeries, tuberculosis dispensaries, libraries, lecture halls, workshops, cooperative shops and meeting rooms for the party's many organisations. The city councillor for welfare, the anatomist Julius Tandler, built up alongside the housing a system of public health and child care that issued every newborn in the city with a parcel of clothing, on the principle that no Viennese child should be wrapped in newspaper. The intention was to form what the party's writers called new human beings: healthy, educated, sober, class conscious citizens of a future socialist society. The estates were named after heroes of the movement and of progressive thought, among them Marx, Engels, Lassalle, Bebel, Goethe and George Washington.

The most famous is the Karl-Marx-Hof, designed by the city architect Karl Ehn and opened in 1930. It extends for more than a kilometre along a railway line in the northern district of Döbling, a largely middle class area chosen partly for that reason. It contained 1,382 dwellings for about five thousand people on a site of which less than a fifth was built upon. Its central section, painted in red and ochre and pierced by great arches leading to a public square, with flagpoles mounted on towers above, was designed to be seen, and it announced the presence and confidence of the working class in a manner that no housing had done before. Most of the architects employed by the city had trained under Otto Wagner, the leading figure of Viennese architecture before the war, and their style was monumental and somewhat conservative, quite unlike the white flat roofed modernism of contemporary German housing. Critics on the left of the profession found it heavy. The inhabitants called the estates people's palaces.

Dwellings were allocated by a points system that took account of the applicant's existing housing conditions, the size of the family, illness, disability, war injury and length of residence in Vienna. Tenancies were secure and could pass to the next generation. Opponents alleged that party membership counted in practice, and the charge was not baseless, since the party's organisation penetrated every aspect of life on the estates. They also made a more theoretical criticism. Economists of the Austrian school argued that the experiment rested on the expropriation of private landlords through rent control and taxation, that it had destroyed the private building industry, and that the city, by letting at rents far below cost, was distributing a privilege to those lucky enough to obtain a flat at the expense of everyone else. Supporters replied that the private market had been given a century in which to house the workers of Vienna and that the results were on view in the old districts.

The political significance of the programme was understood on both sides. The estates were strongholds of the Social Democratic vote, and their solid walls, arched gateways and commanding positions gave rise to the accusation, repeated in the conservative press, that they had been built as fortresses for a future civil war, sited to control the railways and bridges. No evidence has been found that military considerations influenced their design. But the party did maintain a paramilitary organisation, as its opponents did, and when the confrontation came the estates were where it was fought.

Austrian democracy was destroyed in stages. In March 1933 the Christian Social chancellor Engelbert Dollfuss suspended parliament and began to govern by decree, with the backing of Mussolini. He cut the city's share of federal tax revenue, which ended the building programme. On 12 February 1934 a police search for weapons at a party building in Linz met armed resistance, and fighting spread to Vienna, where members of the socialist militia barricaded themselves in the municipal estates. The government brought up the army. Field guns and howitzers were fired at the Karl-Marx-Hof, the Goethe-Hof and other blocks while families sheltered inside. Resistance collapsed within four days. Several hundred people had been killed, nine of the socialist leaders were hanged, one of them carried to the gallows on a stretcher, and the party and the trade unions were outlawed. Breitner was imprisoned and later emigrated. The mayor was arrested at his desk.

The new authoritarian regime removed the names of Marx and Engels from the estates and installed its own administrators, but it did not sell the dwellings or evict the tenants in general, and neither did the Nazis after the annexation of 1938, though they expelled Jewish residents, who were then deported. The buildings were too useful and too solidly popular to undo. That durability is perhaps the most telling fact about them. A programme lasting barely ten years, carried out by a single city government in a hostile state and ended by artillery, created a stock of housing and a set of expectations that survived dictatorship, war and occupation and that remain the basis of the city's housing system a century later.

#### Vienna after 1945 and the model today

When the Social Democrats returned to the town hall in 1945 a fifth of the city's housing had been destroyed or damaged, and they resumed building at once. They have governed Vienna without interruption since, and the municipal housing stock has grown to about 220,000 dwellings, which makes the city the largest residential landlord in Europe. The system that surrounds this stock today is, however, different in important ways from that of the 1920s, and it is the present system, not the heroic original, that delegations from other countries now come to study.

The main change is the rise of a second sector alongside the city's own housing. Austria has a category of housing providers known as limited-profit associations, whose origins go back to a fund established under the emperor in 1910 and whose rules are laid down in federal law. Some are cooperatives owned by their members and others are companies owned by municipalities, trade unions, churches or charitable bodies. They are exempt from corporation tax, and in return they accept a set of binding obligations. They must charge rents that cover their costs and no more. Any surplus must be reinvested in housing. The return paid to their owners is capped at a low rate. Their activities are confined to building, renovating and managing dwellings, and they are subject to regular audit. In Vienna these associations own about 200,000 dwellings, almost as many as the city, and since the municipality suspended construction on its own account in 2004, resuming it on a small scale a decade later, they have built nearly all the new subsidised housing.

The finance comes from several sources that reinforce one another. Every employee in Austria pays a contribution of half a per cent of wages towards housing, matched by the employer, and the proceeds are distributed to the federal states. Vienna spends several hundred million euros a year on housing, the greater part of it in the form of long term loans at very low interest to associations that build to approved standards. As the loans are repaid the money is lent again, so that the system functions as a revolving fund and is less exposed than a system of annual grants to the fluctuations of the budget. Tenants of new association flats usually make a down payment towards the cost of land and construction, which is returned with a small deduction when they leave. Because support goes to the building and not to the occupant, the Austrian state spends relatively little on personal housing allowances, and its total outlay on housing, at around half a per cent of national income, is lower than that of countries such as Britain and France that subsidise tenants in the market.

Land policy is the second pillar. A municipal fund established in 1984 buys and holds land for future housing and owns several million square metres of it. When a site is to be developed, the fund invites associations to compete, with teams of architects, for the right to build. Entries are judged by an expert jury on four criteria: architecture, cost, environmental performance and social sustainability, a term that covers such matters as the mix of dwelling sizes, shared spaces and provision for particular groups. The winner buys the land at a fixed price that is well below the market level. Since 2018 the city has also used its planning powers to hold down land costs on private sites. A new zoning category provides that where land is rezoned for housing on any substantial scale, two thirds of the floor space must be subsidised housing, which effectively caps what a developer can pay the landowner.

The third feature is the breadth of eligibility. The income limit for municipal and association housing is set high enough that around three quarters of Viennese households fall below it. Once a tenancy is granted it is permanent. Income is not checked again, and the tenancy can be passed to a close relative who has lived in the dwelling. The policy is deliberate. A teacher, a bus driver and an unemployed labourer may live on the same staircase, and the estates are therefore not identified with poverty. Around half the population of the city lives in one or other form of subsidised rental housing, and because so much of the market is occupied by landlords who charge only their costs, private rents are restrained by the competition. Older private buildings are in addition subject to a national system of regulated rents. Vienna's average rents are well below those of Munich, Zurich, Paris or London, and the city has regularly been placed at or near the top of international rankings of the quality of urban life.

The model has costs and critics, and they should be recorded. The first criticism is that it favours insiders. A household that obtained a municipal flat thirty years ago continues to pay a low rent whatever its present income, and may pass the flat to its children, while a young family or a recent arrival waits. Economists have suggested charging higher rents to better off tenants. The city has refused, arguing that to do so would drive them out and destroy the social mix on which the system depends. The second criticism concerns newcomers more directly. Applicants must have lived at one address in Vienna for two years, and those with longer residence are advanced in the queue. Until 2006 foreign citizens were excluded from municipal housing altogether. In a city where over a third of residents now hold a foreign passport, many of the poorest households are to be found not in the subsidised sector but in the less regulated parts of the private market, paying considerably more for worse accommodation on short contracts.

A third line of criticism points to pressures on the system. The population of Vienna has grown by more than a quarter since the 1990s, land prices have multiplied, and rents for new private lettings have risen much faster than incomes. The subsidised sector builds several thousand dwellings a year, which is a great deal by international standards, but not enough to accommodate all who want one. Some economists of the market school add that the low rents are possible only because of a century of accumulated public investment and continuing subsidy, and that the true cost to the taxpayer, including the income forgone by charging less than the market would bear, is larger than the published figures suggest. Defenders answer that this is the point: the community has chosen to hold a large stock of housing permanently outside the market and to take its return in the form of affordable rents instead of money.

Three lessons are commonly drawn from Vienna by those who admire it. The first is the value of continuity. The city has pursued essentially the same policy, under the same party, for the better part of a century, never sold its stock, and built steadily in good times and bad. The second is that social housing remains popular and well maintained where it houses a broad cross section of the population and is of a quality that people with choices are glad to accept. The third is that control of land is decisive. The system depends at every point on the city's ability to obtain sites at prices unrelated to their speculative value. A government that wished to imitate Vienna would have to begin not with architecture but with land and with patience, and it would need to be prepared to wait several decades for the result.

#### Weimar Germany and Amsterdam

Vienna was the most concentrated experiment of the 1920s, but it was not the largest or the most influential in its architecture. In Germany and the Netherlands the same years saw non-profit housing built on a great scale under laws and arrangements that differed from the Austrian, and it was there that the visual language of modern housing was invented.

The German republic founded at Weimar in 1919 wrote housing into its constitution. Article 155 declared that the distribution and use of land would be supervised by the state in order to prevent abuse and to secure for every German a healthy dwelling. The means to act on the promise came from the great inflation. When the mark collapsed in 1923, debts fixed in money were wiped out, and owners of mortgaged property found themselves in possession of buildings free of charges, while their rents remained controlled. The government judged that this windfall should not be left with them. From 1924 it imposed a tax on existing buildings, calculated on the pre-war rent, and directed a large part of the yield to the construction of new housing. The house rent tax, as it was called, was a levy on the unearned gain that inflation had bestowed on proprietors, and in its justification it recalls the arguments of Mill and Henry George.

The proceeds were lent at very low interest to those who would build to approved standards, and the principal recipients were non-profit building societies and cooperatives, many of them founded by the trade unions, the civil service associations and the municipalities. Between 1924 and 1931 Germany built about two and a half million dwellings, housing something like nine million people, and public funds were involved in the majority of them. The national government set the framework and the cities carried out the work, each under its own director of building, and several of these officials were architects of the first rank who had been converted to a new conception of their task.

Frankfurt was the outstanding case. Under a liberal mayor and the city architect Ernst May, appointed in 1925, the municipality built some fifteen thousand dwellings in five years in a ring of new settlements on its outskirts, enough to house a tenth of the population. May had worked in England with Raymond Unwin and brought from him the garden city principle of low buildings among greenery. He combined it with a radically simplified architecture of flat roofs, plain white and coloured walls and long horizontal windows, and with industrialised methods of construction using precast concrete slabs made in a municipal factory. Every element of the dwelling was redesigned for economy and convenience. The best known result is the Frankfurt kitchen, devised in 1926 by the Austrian architect Margarete Schütte-Lihotzky, a compact fitted room planned on the principles of a railway dining car so that every task could be done with the fewest steps. About ten thousand were installed. It is the ancestor of the modern fitted kitchen.

In Berlin the city planning director Martin Wagner worked with the architect Bruno Taut and a housing company owned by the socialist trade unions to build large estates on the edge of the capital. The Horseshoe Estate at Britz, begun in 1925 and named for the curved block that encloses a pond at its centre, and the forest settlement known as Uncle Tom's Cabin at Zehlendorf were distinguished by Taut's bold use of colour and by the generous planting between the rows. The estates were intended as a deliberate reply to the rental barracks. Their motto was light, air and sun, and their flats, though small, each had a bathroom, a balcony or loggia and windows on two sides. Six of the Berlin estates of this period are now listed as world heritage sites.

The movement had its internal debates. Architects gathered at Frankfurt in 1929 for an international congress devoted to what was called the dwelling for the minimum of existence. The question was how small and cheap a decent home could be made, given that even subsidised rents in the new estates were beyond the reach of unskilled workers. Critics on the left observed that the white settlements were in practice occupied by clerks, teachers and skilled artisans, while the poor remained in the tenements, the same failing that had been found in the philanthropic dwellings of Victorian London. Critics on the right denounced the flat roofs as un-German and the whole enterprise as cultural Bolshevism.

The programme ended abruptly. In the financial crisis of 1931 the house rent tax was diverted to the general budget, and building all but stopped. When the Nazis took power two years later they dissolved the trade union housing companies or placed them under party control, dismissed Wagner and Taut, and closed the Bauhaus, where many of the architects had taught. May had already left for the Soviet Union with a team of colleagues to plan new industrial cities, a venture that ended in disillusion. The estates themselves were kept and are for the most part still lived in. The organisational legacy, a large sector of non-profit housing enterprises working with public loans, was revived in West Germany after 1945 and carried the reconstruction.

The Netherlands produced the third great body of work of the period, under the Housing Act of 1901 described in Part Two. In Amsterdam the socialist alderman Floor Wibaut and the director of the municipal housing department Arie Keppler used the act to finance building by housing associations representing the different sections of Dutch society, and they insisted on architecture of high quality. The architects of what became known as the Amsterdam School, among them Michel de Klerk and Piet Kramer, treated workers' housing as an occasion for craftsmanship. Their blocks are of richly detailed brick, with curving walls, towers, sculpted corners and windows of unexpected shapes. The most celebrated, a block built by de Klerk for a socialist housing association and completed in 1921, is known as the Ship. A resident is reported to have said after the architect's death that it was as if every brick called out that he had built it for workers who came home tired. Much of the new housing was laid out in the southern extension of the city planned by the architect Hendrik Berlage, with its broad avenues and enclosed squares. By the 1930s a substantial part of the population of Amsterdam lived in association housing, and the foundations had been laid for the largest non-profit rental sector, in proportion to population, in the world.

These experiments of the 1920s shared certain convictions that defined social democratic housing for the rest of the century. Housing for working people should be of a quality equal or superior to what the market provided for the middle class. It should be designed by the best architects and should embody the most advanced ideas about health and domestic life. It should be owned by bodies with no interest in profit and let at rents related to cost. And it should be financed by taxing, in one form or another, the unearned gains of property. Where they differed was in the agent. Vienna built through the municipality, Germany and the Netherlands through associations rooted in the labour movement and the churches. That distinction, between the state as landlord and the non-profit association as landlord, would prove important when public housing later came under attack.

### Chapter 17: The Nordic and Continental Models

After 1945 the ideas tried out in a few cities between the wars became national policy across northern and western Europe. Governments faced with bombed cities, a decade of arrears in building and a baby boom accepted that the state must take responsibility for housing the whole population, and for thirty years they built at a rate never seen before or since.
