The systems they created differed considerably from one country to another, in who owned the housing, how it was financed and for whom it was intended, and the differences determined how well each survived the change in political climate after the 1970s. This chapter examines the principal variants.

#### Sweden: the people's home and the Million Programme

Sweden came to be regarded for much of the twentieth century as the model social democratic society, and housing was at the centre of its claim. The Social Democratic Party governed from 1932 to 1976 with only a brief interruption. Its guiding image was supplied by its leader Per Albin Hansson in a speech of 1928. The good society, he said, should be like a good home, in which there are no privileged and no neglected members, no favourites and no stepchildren, and in which equality, consideration and cooperation prevail. He called it the people's home.

The country had far to go. In the 1930s Swedish housing was among the most overcrowded in northern Europe, with the typical urban working class family living in a single room and a kitchen. The economists Gunnar and Alva Myrdal drew attention to the consequences in a book of 1934 on the crisis in the population question. Sweden's birth rate had fallen to the lowest in the world, and the Myrdals argued that young couples were not having children because they could not afford to house them. The answer was not exhortation but a systematic reduction of the costs of family life, beginning with housing. The argument appealed to conservatives worried about national decline as well as to socialists, and it led to the first state programmes: loans for the improvement of rural housing and subsidised blocks reserved for large families of limited means. The latter soon acquired a stigma, and the lesson that the Social Democrats drew from them shaped everything that followed. Housing built specially for the poor would mark out its occupants. The policy must be general.

The postwar system rested on that principle. The state offered loans on favourable terms for the construction of housing of all types, by all kinds of builder, provided standards were met and costs controlled. The most favourable terms went to housing companies owned by the municipalities, which were to operate without profit and let to anyone, with no test of income. Their name in Swedish means, roughly, housing for the benefit of everyone, and that was the intention. A place in the municipal sector was to be as normal for a doctor as for a docker. Close behind in the ranking of support came the cooperatives. The largest, a national organisation founded by the Stockholm tenants' association in 1923, built blocks whose residents bought a share that gave them the right to occupy a flat and a vote in the running of the building. Private landlords continued to exist and could also obtain state loans, on somewhat less generous terms.

Rents throughout the system were kept in line by a distinctive arrangement. Wartime rent control was eventually replaced, in stages completed in the 1970s, by a system under which the rents of the municipal companies were negotiated each year with the tenants' union, a mass organisation with several hundred thousand members, on the basis of the companies' costs. Private landlords were then bound by law to charge no more than the rent of a comparable municipal flat. The principle was that rent should reflect the standard and size of a dwelling, its use value, and not the scarcity of its location. A flat in the centre of Stockholm therefore cost little more than a similar flat in a distant suburb, a result that was egalitarian in intent and that had the consequences one would expect for the length of the queue.

By the early 1960s the country was prosperous, people were moving rapidly from the countryside to the cities, and the shortage of housing had become the government's chief political embarrassment. A young family might wait ten years for a flat in Stockholm. In 1965 the parliament resolved that a million dwellings should be built in the following ten years, in a country that then had fewer than eight million inhabitants and about three million homes. The target was met. Between 1965 and 1974 Sweden completed just over a million dwellings, the highest rate of housing construction per head ever achieved in a market economy. About a third were detached and terraced houses. The rest were flats, a large proportion of them in big estates on the outskirts of the cities, built with prefabricated concrete panels by a few large contractors, with cranes running on rails between the rows.

The quantitative achievement was complete, and overcrowding in the old sense disappeared. Every dwelling had a bathroom, central heating and a fitted kitchen, and the flats were spacious by European standards. The reception of the large estates was nonetheless cool almost from the first. They were criticised as monotonous, bleak and badly served by shops and transport. Just as they were finished, demand shifted. Rising incomes and tax relief on mortgage interest made it possible for many families to buy houses, and those who could do so moved out. By the mid 1970s there were tens of thousands of empty flats in the newest estates. The vacancies were filled over the following decades largely by refugees and immigrants, who arrived in growing numbers and had no place in the queue for anything better. Districts such as Rinkeby and Tensta in Stockholm and Rosengård in Malmö became, in the public mind, synonymous with segregation, unemployment and, more recently, gang violence. The country that had set out to abolish the distinction between housing for the poor and housing for everyone had produced, by an unintended route, some of the most ethnically segregated suburbs in Europe.

The system was reshaped after 1990. As described in Part Three, a centre right government in the early 1990s removed most of the subsidies for construction during a severe financial crisis, and building fell to a fraction of its former level. In Stockholm, conservative led councils allowed tenants to buy their municipal blocks collectively and convert them into cooperatives, whose shares could then be sold at market prices, and tens of thousands of flats in the inner city left the rental stock in this way, to the considerable enrichment of those who happened to be living in them. Following a complaint by private landlords to the European Commission that the municipal companies enjoyed unfair state support, a law of 2011 required those companies to operate on business principles and to seek a market rate of return, which ended their special role as price leaders working at cost.

The negotiated rent system remains, and so does the queue. The average wait for a rental flat through the Stockholm housing agency is around nine or ten years and can exceed twenty for the most sought after districts. Those without time in the queue, including the young, newcomers from other parts of Sweden and immigrants, depend on a secondary market of sublets at much higher prices, or buy cooperative flats with large mortgages, and Swedish households have become among the most heavily indebted in Europe. Swedish economists have argued for decades, with little effect, that rents should be allowed to reflect location. The tenants' union replies that this would simply transfer money from tenants to landlords without building anything, and that the real failure has been the withdrawal of the state from construction.

The Swedish story is in some ways the reverse of the Viennese. Both systems were universal in principle, of high quality and based on cost rents. Vienna built steadily and never stopped. Sweden built its million homes in a single decade, in a form that soon fell from favour, and then largely ceased. A system designed for a homogeneous society in which everyone waited their turn adapted poorly to rapid immigration and to a generation unwilling or unable to wait. It remains, even so, a country in which very few people are badly housed in the physical sense, and that fact, which would have astonished the Myrdals, is the programme's lasting monument.

#### Denmark, the Netherlands and Finland

Three smaller countries of northern Europe developed systems that are in some respects more instructive than the Swedish, because each solved a problem that defeated larger neighbours and each has lately run into a difficulty of its own.

Denmark's non-profit sector houses about a fifth of the population, some one million people, in dwellings owned by several hundred independent housing organisations. The sector has two features that distinguish it. The first is what Danes call tenants' democracy. Since reforms of the 1970s and 1980s, each estate has formed a self governing section with its own budget. The residents meet at least once a year, elect a board from among themselves, approve the budget, and thereby set their own rents, since rent must cover the costs of the section and nothing else. They decide on maintenance, improvements and house rules. Residents also hold the majority of seats on the governing body of the parent organisation. A Danish tenant in this sector is thus neither a customer of a company nor a client of a municipal department but something closer to a member of a cooperative, and the arrangement gives occupants a degree of control over their housing that is unusual for people who do not own it.

The second feature is financial. New estates are built with a small contribution of capital from the municipality, a small deposit from the tenants and a large mortgage loan, on which the state pays part of the interest. Rents are calculated to service the loan. When the loan is finally paid off, after thirty years or so, the rent is not reduced. The tenants go on paying the same amount, and the sum formerly paid to the lender flows instead into a national fund owned collectively by the housing organisations, established in 1967. The fund finances the renovation of older estates, social programmes in troubled ones and part of the cost of new building. The sector thus accumulates its own capital from the rents of buildings whose debts have been cleared, and it has become progressively less dependent on the public budget. Advocates of social housing elsewhere regard the mechanism with envy, since it shows how a non-profit sector, once mature, can renew itself. It is the opposite of the British and Spanish practice of selling off the asset once it is paid for.

Denmark has also supplied the most controversial housing law of recent years. A number of large estates built in the 1960s and 1970s came to have high proportions of residents who were unemployed, on low incomes and of immigrant origin. In 2018 the government adopted a plan directed at areas it officially designated as ghettos, a term later replaced by parallel societies. One of the criteria for designation was that more than half the residents had origins outside Western countries. In the areas classed as most severely affected, the housing organisations were required to reduce the share of family social housing to no more than two fifths by 2030, by selling blocks to private investors, converting them to other uses or demolishing them. Thousands of tenants have had to move. The government presented the policy as a means of breaking up concentrations of disadvantage and promoting integration. Residents, housing experts and human rights bodies have attacked it as discrimination on ethnic grounds, and it has been challenged before the Danish and European courts. The episode shows how a universal housing system can be turned, under the pressure of anxieties about immigration, into an instrument for dispersing a particular population.

The Netherlands has the largest social rented sector in the world relative to its size. Housing associations own around 2.3 million dwellings, close to three in ten of all homes in the country and nearer half in Amsterdam and Rotterdam. For most of the twentieth century they were financed by government loans and annual subsidies and were closely supervised. In 1995, in an operation known as the grossing up, the state cancelled its outstanding loans to the associations against its commitments of future subsidy and set them free as independent social enterprises with a combined property portfolio worth tens of billions. They were to finance themselves thereafter, borrowing on the capital market with the backing of a mutual guarantee fund and cross subsidising new social housing from sales and from commercial development.

Independence brought both dynamism and scandal. Some associations built ambitiously, and their executives awarded themselves salaries on a corporate scale. One bought an ocean liner with the intention of converting it into a training centre and hotel, at a loss of more than two hundred million euros. In 2012 the largest association in the country, Vestia, was found to have built up a portfolio of interest rate derivatives so large and so badly judged that it lost about two billion euros and had to be rescued by the rest of the sector. A parliamentary inquiry followed, and a new housing act in 2015 confined the associations strictly to their core task.

A second constraint came from Brussels. Dutch social housing had traditionally been open to a broad range of households. Commercial landlords complained to the European Commission that the associations, with their state guarantees and cheap land, were competing unfairly in the middle of the market. The Commission took the view that state aid for housing could be justified only as a service for disadvantaged citizens, and under a settlement that took effect in 2011 the associations were obliged to allocate nine tenths of their vacancies to households below a set income, a little over thirty thousand euros a year at the time. A sector designed on the universal principle was thus pushed by European competition law towards the residual model, housing the poor alone. Households just above the limit, too well off for an association flat and unable to afford to buy, found themselves with nowhere to go, and the Netherlands now has an acknowledged shortage of several hundred thousand homes. The case is often cited as evidence that a broad social housing sector is difficult to sustain within rules written to protect commercial competition.

Finland is of interest for a narrower achievement. It has a substantial stock of state subsidised rental housing, owned by municipal companies and non-profit bodies, and the city of Helsinki, which owns most of the land within its boundaries, requires each new district to contain a fixed mixture of subsidised rental, price controlled and market housing. But the country's international reputation rests on its approach to homelessness. In the 1980s Finland had close to twenty thousand homeless people in a population of five million, many of them single men with problems of alcohol who lived in large dormitory shelters.

In 2008 the government adopted a national programme founded on a principle developed in the United States and known as Housing First. The traditional method, sometimes described as a staircase, required a homeless person to progress through shelters and transitional hostels, and to demonstrate sobriety and stability, before qualifying for a home. The new principle reverses the order. A person is given a permanent dwelling with an ordinary tenancy at the outset, without conditions as to treatment, and support with addiction, mental illness and money is then provided there. The reasoning is that it is very difficult to address any other problem while living on the street. To supply the dwellings, a foundation bought flats scattered through ordinary buildings, and the old shelters were themselves converted into blocks of self contained apartments with staff on the premises. The best known, a hostel in Helsinki run by the Salvation Army, was rebuilt from two hundred and fifty dormitory beds into eighty flats.

The number of homeless people in Finland has fallen in almost every year since, to well under four thousand, and long term homelessness has declined by more than two thirds. Finland is the only member of the European Union in which homelessness has decreased over a sustained period. Studies commissioned by the government estimate that housing a formerly homeless person saves the public purse some fifteen thousand euros a year in emergency health care, policing and the courts. Those who have studied the Finnish case stress that the method worked there because there was housing to offer. The principle of giving a home first is of little use where there are no homes to give, which is why attempts to copy it in countries with a shortage of social housing have had more modest results.

#### France: from the abbé Pierre to the grands ensembles and the SRU law

France entered the postwar period worse housed than any other large country in western Europe. Rent control since 1914 had discouraged building and maintenance for forty years. Two wars had been fought on French soil. A census in 1954 found that nearly half of all dwellings had no running water, three quarters no lavatory inside, and nine tenths no bath or shower. In the cities hundreds of thousands of families lived in furnished hotel rooms, and around Paris and other cities there were shanty towns, called bidonvilles after the flattened oil drums of which the shacks were made, inhabited by migrants from the countryside and by workers from Algeria and Portugal. The early governments of the Fourth Republic gave priority to industry and reconstruction, and housing waited.

The turning point was a broadcast. The winter of 1954 was exceptionally cold, and on the first of February a Catholic priest and former resistance fighter known as the abbé Pierre, who ran a community for the homeless, went on the radio. A woman, he told listeners, had frozen to death in the night on a Paris pavement, clutching the eviction notice she had been served the day before. He appealed for blankets, tents and stoves and for premises to be opened that same night. The response, which he called an insurrection of kindness, was overwhelming. Donations poured in, railway stations were opened as shelters, and parliament, which had recently refused a credit for emergency housing, voted ten times the sum. The episode entered national memory, and the abbé remained for half a century the conscience of the country on the question. More practically, it committed the state to building on a scale that would end the crisis.

The instrument was the system of moderate rent housing, known by its French initials as HLM. Its origins lay in the legislation of the 1890s on cheap dwellings, but it became a mass producer only in the 1950s. HLM bodies are of several kinds, some attached to municipalities and departments and others constituted as non-profit companies, often sponsored by employers. Their finance has a peculiarity that deserves notice. The most popular savings account in France, held by the great majority of households, pays a rate of interest fixed by the government and is free of tax. The deposits are gathered by a public financial institution dating from 1816 and lent to the HLM bodies for terms of forty years or more to build social housing. The small savings of the French people are thereby transformed into long term loans for housing, without passing through the capital market. A second source was created in 1953, when firms above a certain size were required to devote one per cent of their wage bill to the housing of employees, a contribution still levied in reduced form.

With these resources and the methods of heavy prefabrication in which French engineers led the world, the state launched the construction of what were called the grands ensembles, great estates of slabs and towers placed in open land on the edges of cities, in zones designated for priority development. Annual completions of all kinds rose from under a hundred thousand in the early 1950s to more than half a million in the early 1970s. At Sarcelles, north of Paris, an estate for forty thousand people rose from the fields. At La Courneuve a single slab block four hundred metres long contained some four thousand dwellings. To families arriving from a hotel room or a shanty, the flats, with their central heating, bathrooms and views, represented modern life, and the first residents were a mixture of skilled workers, clerks, junior managers and repatriates from Algeria.

Disenchantment followed quickly. The estates had been built without shops, cafés, workplaces or adequate transport, and the press invented a word for the depression said to afflict the women marooned in them. In 1973 a ministerial circular ended the building of very large estates and of long slab blocks. As in Sweden, the better off tenants departed, here encouraged by the reform of 1977 that shifted subsidies towards individual home buyers. Their places were taken by poorer households and by immigrant families from North and West Africa, who had been living in the shanty towns and transit camps and whom the authorities now rehoused in the estates that others were leaving. Factory closures from the mid 1970s removed the jobs that had justified the estates' locations. By the 1980s the word banlieue, which means simply suburb, had acquired in France the connotations that inner city has in English.

The estates became the scene of recurrent unrest, beginning with disturbances near Lyon in 1981 and culminating in three weeks of rioting across the country in the autumn of 2005, after two teenagers died while hiding from the police in an electricity substation in a suburb east of Paris. Successive governments responded with what was termed the policy for the city, a succession of programmes of social support, tax free enterprise zones and physical renewal. From 2003 a national agency undertook the demolition of the worst blocks and their replacement with smaller buildings of mixed tenure, at a cost of tens of billions of euros. Well over a hundred thousand dwellings have been demolished. The physical environment of many estates has been transformed. Unemployment and poverty in them remain two to three times the national average.

French policy has nevertheless retained a commitment to the broad conception of social housing. The sector contains more than five million dwellings and houses about one household in six. Income ceilings are set so that some two thirds of the population are in principle eligible, and there are several categories of dwelling at different rent levels for different groups. The defining measure of recent decades reflects this outlook. In December 2000 the socialist led government passed a law on urban solidarity and renewal, known by its initials as the SRU law. Its article 55 requires every commune above a certain size within an urban area to have at least a fifth of its housing stock in the social sector, a proportion raised to a quarter in 2013. A commune that falls short must pay an annual levy and agree to a schedule of construction, and if it fails to meet the schedule the levy can be multiplied and the prefect can take over the granting of building permits.

The target of the law was the affluent suburb that used its control of planning to keep social housing, and the people who lived in it, out. The best known example is Neuilly-sur-Seine, on the western edge of Paris, of which Nicolas Sarkozy was mayor for nearly twenty years, and which had a social housing share of a few per cent. Some wealthy communes have preferred to pay the penalties, and conservative governments have several times tried to weaken the obligation. It has survived, and it has had a measurable effect. Around half of the new social housing built in France since 2000 has been in communes subject to the law, including in Paris itself, where the share of social housing has risen from about an eighth to nearly a quarter through new building and the purchase of existing blocks. The principle is the reverse of the Danish. Instead of dispersing the poor from the estates where they are concentrated, it obliges the prosperous districts to make room.

The French system thus displays the social democratic tradition at both its weakest and its most resolute. The grands ensembles are a standing warning of what happens when housing is produced as a quantity, in isolated locations, for a population that is then abandoned by the economy. The savings circuit and the SRU law show a state that continues, more than a century after its first housing act, to treat the provision and the fair distribution of non-market housing as a normal function of government.

#### Germany and Switzerland: nations of protected tenants

The German speaking countries are the standing refutation of the belief that a modern prosperous society must be a society of homeowners. In Germany roughly half of all households rent their homes, in Switzerland about three fifths, and in the large cities of both countries the proportion is far higher, exceeding eight tenths in Berlin and Zurich. Tenants there include doctors, lawyers and managers as well as workers, and many live in the same flat for decades. The explanation lies less in public housing, of which Germany now has rather little, than in the law of tenancy and in a set of institutions that make renting from a private landlord a secure and respectable way to live.

West Germany's reconstruction was carried out under a system that blurred the line between public and private. The housing act of 1950 offered public loans at low interest to anyone who would build dwellings of a defined size and standard: municipal companies, cooperatives, trade union and church enterprises, insurance companies and private individuals alike. In exchange the owner accepted a controlled rent and a duty to let to households below an income limit, for as long as the public loan was outstanding. When the loan was repaid, typically after thirty or forty years, the obligations lapsed and the dwelling became an ordinary private rental. Social housing in the German sense was therefore not a category of landlord or a permanent stock. It was a temporary condition attached to a subsidy. The method produced housing at great speed, more than five million dwellings in the first decade, and it avoided the creation of separate estates for the poor, since subsidised and unsubsidised flats stood side by side and were indistinguishable.

Its weakness appeared a generation later. As the loans were paid off, dwellings left the social category by the hundred thousand each year, and new subsidised construction did not replace them. The number of social dwellings in Germany fell from about four million in the 1980s to little more than one million today. The non-profit enterprises that had owned a large part of the stock suffered a blow of their own in 1982, when a news magazine revealed that the directors of the largest of them, a housing concern owned by the trade union federation with some four hundred thousand flats, had enriched themselves at its expense. The company collapsed in debt and was sold, at one stage for the symbolic price of one mark, and the scandal gave the government of Helmut Kohl the occasion to abolish, from 1990, the legal status and tax privileges of non-profit housing altogether. Many of the former non-profit and municipal dwellings were later sold to investment funds, as described in Part Four.

What remained, and what protects German tenants today, is the civil law. A residential tenancy in Germany is normally of unlimited duration. The landlord can end it only for a reason recognised by statute, the principal ones being serious breach by the tenant and the owner's genuine need of the dwelling for himself or a close relative, and the courts examine such claims with scepticism. A sale of the building does not affect the tenancy, in accordance with a maxim that purchase does not break a lease. The rent can be raised during a tenancy only up to the level customarily paid for comparable dwellings in the locality, as recorded in an official table compiled by the municipality from recent lettings, and by no more than a fifth over three years, or less in areas of shortage. The cost of improvements can be passed on to a limited extent. Disputes are heard by local courts, and several million households belong to tenants' associations that provide legal advice and insurance against the costs of litigation.

The system gives the tenant most of what the conservative tradition claims for the owner. He cannot be put out. His rent can rise only gradually. He may decorate and in practice often installs his own kitchen. He knows that he can remain as long as he wishes, bring up children in the flat and grow old in it. For the landlord, the arrangement offers a steady and predictable return over a long horizon in place of capital gain, which suited the insurance companies, pension funds and prudent private individuals who traditionally owned German rental housing. Renting is also treated even handedly by the tax system. An owner occupier in Germany receives no relief on mortgage interest, and lenders require substantial deposits. With little financial pressure to buy and little insecurity in renting, half the population has seen no reason to do so, and for several decades German house prices did not rise in real terms at all.

That equilibrium has been disturbed since about 2010. Low interest rates, the appeal of German property to international investors and a return of population growth in the large cities drove up both prices and the rents asked of new tenants, which were unregulated. In Berlin, long one of the cheapest capitals in Europe, rents for new lettings roughly doubled in a decade. The gap between what sitting tenants paid and what a newcomer was asked widened, creating the familiar division between insiders and outsiders and giving landlords a motive to be rid of long standing tenants. The federal government responded in 2015 with what it called a rent brake, under which the rent on a new letting in an area of shortage may not exceed the local reference level by more than a tenth. Its effect was limited by exceptions for new and modernised buildings and by the fact that enforcement depended on the tenant challenging his own landlord. The more drastic measures attempted in Berlin, a five year freeze and a referendum on expropriation, are discussed in Part Seven.

Switzerland shows the same basic pattern with its own mechanisms. Swiss tenancy law permits a landlord to earn a fair return on his investment and no more, and links permissible rent changes to a national reference rate of mortgage interest published by the federal authorities. When the reference rate falls, tenants are entitled to ask for a reduction, and when it rises landlords may increase the rent by a defined amount. A tenant can contest an initial rent or an increase that he considers abusive before a conciliation board. Because Switzerland decides many questions by popular vote, tenants, who are the majority of the electorate, have repeatedly used the ballot to defend and extend their protections, and proposals to weaken them have generally been defeated. The cities add a non-profit sector of their own. In Zurich, as noted elsewhere in this book, cooperatives charging cost rents own about a fifth of all dwellings.

The sociologist Jim Kemeny, whose ideas have appeared several times in these pages, drew a general conclusion from such cases. He distinguished two kinds of rental system. In the dualist kind, typical of the English speaking countries, the state confines non-profit housing to a residual sector for the poor, shields the private rental market from its competition, and leaves private tenants with little protection, so that anyone who can buy does. In the unitary or integrated kind, found in Germany, Switzerland, Austria, the Netherlands and Scandinavia, non-profit and private landlords operate under similar rules and compete for the same tenants, renting is regulated to provide security, and no form of tenure is privileged by taxation. The principle is sometimes called tenure neutrality. On this account the low rate of home ownership in central Europe is not a sign of backwardness or a peculiarity of national character. It is the result of a policy that has made it unnecessary to own in order to be secure.

### Chapter 18: Britain and the United States

The two leading English speaking countries built public housing on very different scales and with very different outcomes. Britain constructed more than five million council homes in the sixty years after 1919, and at the peak nearly a third of the population lived in one. The United States built little more than a million public housing units in total, for under two per cent of its households. Yet the two histories have something in common that sets them apart from those of the continent. In both, public housing came in time to be regarded as accommodation for the poor, and in both it was largely repudiated. This chapter asks why, and then offers an assessment of the social democratic tradition as a whole.

#### The rise of British council housing

The First World War created the British council house. In 1917, with recruits having been found unfit for service in alarming numbers and with unrest growing in the munitions districts, the government appointed a committee under the Liberal member of parliament Sir John Tudor Walters to consider what kind of homes should be built for the working classes after the war. Its report, published within weeks of the armistice and largely written by Raymond Unwin, the planner of Letchworth, set a standard far above anything previously provided. Houses were to be built at no more than twelve to the acre in towns, in short terraces or pairs, each with a garden, a living room, a scullery, a larder, a bathroom and three bedrooms, and wherever possible a parlour. The layout was to follow the principles of the garden suburb.

The prime minister, David Lloyd George, had promised the returning troops a country fit for heroes to live in, and ministers were frank among themselves about the alternative. The parliamentary secretary to the Local Government Board told the House of Commons that the money to be spent on housing was an insurance against Bolshevism and revolution. The Housing and Town Planning Act of 1919, known after the minister Christopher Addison, required every local authority to survey the housing needs of its district and to submit plans to meet them, and it provided that any loss incurred beyond the product of a penny on the local rates would be borne by the Treasury. For the first time the state undertook to subsidise the housing of ordinary working people as a general policy, with an open ended commitment. The houses built under the act were the best council houses ever erected in Britain. They were also expensive, since costs soared in the postwar boom, and in 1921, with the economy in recession and the threat of revolution receding, the programme was cut off at 213,000 dwellings and Addison resigned.

The principle survived the retrenchment. The first Labour government, in office for nine months of 1924, passed an act devised by the Clydeside socialist John Wheatley that offered local authorities a fixed annual subsidy for each house built to let, over forty years, and reached an agreement with the building trades to expand the workforce. Half a million houses were built under it before it was ended in 1933. Another Labour government in 1930 added subsidies for the clearance of slums, calculated on the number of people rehoused, and the governments of the 1930s, although Conservative dominated, confined council building to that purpose and pursued it with some energy. By 1939 local authorities in Britain had built more than a million dwellings, one in ten of the entire stock.

The typical product was the cottage estate on the edge of a city. The largest, built by the London County Council at Becontree in Essex between 1921 and 1935, contained some twenty-six thousand houses for more than a hundred thousand people and was reckoned the biggest public housing estate in the world. Its residents, moved from the East End, gained gardens, bathrooms and fresh air and lost the pubs, markets, workplaces and relatives they had lived among, and many found the fares and rents hard to meet. In the cities themselves a few authorities built flats on continental lines. Leeds, under a housing committee led by a socialist clergyman, cleared its worst slums and erected at Quarry Hill a vast estate of nearly a thousand flats modelled openly on the Karl-Marx-Hof, with a mechanical system of refuse disposal imported from France.

A pattern noticed at the time was that the council houses of the 1920s were let, at rents that reflected their quality, to the better paid and more regularly employed workers, and that the poor stayed in the slums. Slum clearance in the 1930s rehoused the poor in cheaper dwellings, and some authorities segregated the two groups on different estates. The distinction between housing for general needs and housing for the poorest was thus present in British council housing from early on, and with it a question that was never resolved: whether the council house was to be a normal home for a broad range of working people or a service for those who could not manage otherwise.

The Labour government of 1945 gave the first answer without hesitation. Responsibility for housing lay with the minister of health, Aneurin Bevan, a former miner from South Wales and the leading figure of the party's left. Six years of war had destroyed or badly damaged half a million homes and stopped all building, and there were long waiting lists in every town. Bevan decided that the scarce labour and materials available should be directed overwhelmingly to local authorities building for rent, and he restricted private building to a fifth of the total by a system of licences. The speculative builder, he said, was by his nature not a plannable instrument.

On quality he refused to compromise. Against advice to build smaller and faster, he raised the standard three bedroom council house from about seventy-five square metres to more than eighty-three, and required an upstairs and a downstairs lavatory. We shall be judged for a year or two by the number of houses we have built, he said, and in ten years' time by the type of houses we have built. He removed from the law the stipulation that council housing was for the working classes, so that anyone might apply. His intention was that the council estate should recreate what he called the lovely feature of the English and Welsh village, where the doctor, the grocer, the butcher and the farm labourer all lived in the same street, and he described the segregation of different income groups into different districts as a wholly evil thing. He envisaged, in short, the universal model of public housing that the Swedes and the Viennese adopted, in which renting from the community would be an ordinary condition for people of every class.

Bevan's government built about a million homes in six years, four fifths of them council houses, together with more than a hundred and fifty thousand temporary prefabricated bungalows, made in former aircraft factories, some of which were still occupied half a century later. It passed the New Towns Act, under which public corporations built complete communities beyond the green belts. It was attacked throughout for building too slowly, and the Conservatives' promise to do better helped them to win the election of 1951. The houses of the Bevan years have stood the test he set. They remain among the most solidly built and most sought after of all council dwellings, and it was these, above all, that their tenants were to buy thirty years later.

#### The decline of council housing

British council housing went on growing for thirty years after Bevan left office, and by 1979 it sheltered about a third of all households, some six and a half million homes. Within the following generation it was sold, transferred, demolished or allowed to decay until it housed fewer than one household in ten directly. The reasons were partly political, as the last part described. They also lay within the programme itself, in decisions about design, allocation and management taken during its years of greatest expansion.

The first was a retreat from quality in favour of numbers. Macmillan achieved his three hundred thousand houses a year in part by cutting the size of the standard council house by about a tenth and removing the second lavatory and other features on which Bevan had insisted. From the mid 1950s, as general needs subsidies were withdrawn, council building was redirected to slum clearance, and the task was conceived on an industrial scale. Between 1955 and the mid 1970s local authorities demolished about one and a half million older houses and displaced nearly four million people. Much of what was cleared was beyond saving. A good deal was structurally sound terraced housing that a later generation would have renovated, and the neighbourhoods destroyed with it, with their street life and networks of kin, were mourned in a classic study of the East End of London which found that families moved to a new estate saw their relatives a fraction as often as before.

The second decision concerned what was built in their place. In 1956 the government introduced a subsidy that increased with the height of the building, so that a flat on the fifteenth floor attracted roughly three times the grant of a house. Land in the cities was scarce, the green belts prevented outward growth, architects were captivated by the modernist vision of towers standing in parkland, and a small number of large contractors promoted systems of prefabricated concrete panels, licensed from France and Scandinavia, that promised speed. For a decade local authorities competed to build high. At the peak in 1966 flats in blocks of five storeys or more made up a quarter of all council dwellings approved. Many were erected by unskilled labour, badly assembled and poorly insulated, and they were allocated without much thought to families with young children.

The turning point came at a quarter to six on the morning of 16 May 1968. A tenant on the eighteenth floor of a newly completed tower in east London called Ronan Point lit her gas cooker to make a cup of tea. A small explosion from a leaking connection blew out the load bearing wall panels of her kitchen, and one whole corner of the twenty-two storey block collapsed, floor upon floor, like a pack of cards. Four people were killed. The inquiry found that the panels had been held in place by little more than their own weight. Public confidence in system built towers ended that day, the height subsidy had already been withdrawn, and building of high flats declined rapidly. But several hundred thousand such dwellings had been completed, and councils had borrowed over sixty years to pay for them.

Some estates of the period became notorious within a few years of opening. At Hulme in Manchester, four huge curved blocks of deck access flats completed in 1972, and named with unintended irony after the architects of Georgian Bath, suffered from damp, vermin and heating that tenants could not afford to run. Families were moved out within two years after a child fell from a balcony, and the complex was demolished in the 1990s. Similar stories could be told of estates in Liverpool, Glasgow, Sheffield and London. The sociologist Alice Coleman, in a study of 1985, attributed vandalism and crime to specific design features such as overhead walkways and anonymous entrances, drawing on the American theory of defensible space, and her work influenced the Thatcher government. Others replied that the same designs functioned well where they were properly maintained and housed a stable mix of residents, and that the real causes of decline were poverty and neglect.

The third change was in who lived in council housing. Until the 1970s local authorities allotted tenancies mainly by time on the waiting list and by local connection, and they often preferred applicants judged respectable. The system could be discriminatory, particularly against black and Asian families, and it was widely criticised. Legislation in 1977 placed on councils a duty to secure accommodation for homeless families and others in priority need, and allocation shifted steadily from waiting time to need. The reform was humane in purpose and logical in a period of shortage. Its effect, coinciding with the sale of the better houses to the better off tenants and the collapse of industrial employment in the cities, was to concentrate the poorest and most troubled households in the least popular estates. Economic and social decline then reinforced each other. The council estate, which in 1950 had been a place to which working people aspired, became in the public mind by 1990 a place from which they hoped to escape.

Central government meanwhile restricted the money available for upkeep. With new building halted and rents required to rise, councils fell behind with repairs, and by the late 1990s the backlog was put at nineteen billion pounds. The Labour government elected in 1997 set a standard of decency that every social home was to meet within ten years, and financed a large programme of new kitchens, bathrooms, windows and heating. It made the funding easier to obtain for authorities that handed over their housing to other bodies. Under arrangements begun by the Conservatives in 1988 and continued by Labour, more than a million council dwellings were transferred, after ballots of the tenants, to housing associations, which could borrow privately outside the limits on public spending. Housing associations now own more social housing in England than local authorities do.

The condition of what remained was brought before the country in the early hours of 14 June 2017, when a fire that started in a refrigerator on the fourth floor of Grenfell Tower, a council owned block of twenty-four storeys in one of the richest boroughs of London, spread up the outside of the building through cladding panels with a combustible plastic core that had been fitted during a recent refurbishment. Seventy-two people died. The public inquiry found that the cladding had been chosen in part to save money, that manufacturers had deliberately concealed the fire risks of their products, that the regulatory system had failed, and that the organisation managing the block had for years treated residents who raised concerns about safety as troublemakers. For many observers the fire summed up the status to which council tenants had been reduced.

Social housing of all kinds now accommodates about one household in six in England. More than a million households are on waiting lists, and the number of homeless families placed by councils in temporary accommodation, much of it in bed and breakfast hotels and converted offices, has reached the highest level ever recorded. There are signs of a revival of interest. A terrace of council houses in Norwich, built to exacting standards of energy efficiency along traditional streets, won the country's principal architecture prize in 2019, the first council scheme to do so, and several cities have resumed building on their own account. Scotland, which abolished the right to buy, has maintained a larger social sector and a higher rate of construction than England.

The British experience suggests that the fate of public housing is not settled by the principle. Bevan's programme and the tower blocks of the 1960s were both council housing. The first aimed at a universal service of high quality and largely achieved it for a time. The second treated housing as the rapid disposal of a problem population in the cheapest form that subsidy rules allowed. When the political attack came, it was the reputation of the second that made the first so easy to dismantle.

#### American public housing

The United States came to public housing late and reluctantly, and the terms on which it was admitted go far to explain what became of it. A small group of reformers in the early 1930s, of whom the most influential was a young writer named Catherine Bauer, had travelled in Europe and seen the estates of Frankfurt, Berlin and Vienna. Bauer's book Modern Housing, published in 1934, argued that the American approach of regulating slums and hoping that private enterprise would do better had failed, and that good housing for working people would come only as it had in Europe, through non-profit construction on a large scale, demanded by an organised labour movement and designed as complete neighbourhoods. She helped the trade unions to draft the legislation that became the Housing Act of 1937.

The act that emerged from Congress differed from her conception in ways that proved decisive. The property industry, represented by the associations of estate agents, builders and savings institutions, fought it as socialism and as unfair competition, and exacted conditions as the price of its passage. Public housing was to be available only to families of very low income, well below the level that could afford any decent private dwelling, so that it would not compete with the market. Tenants whose earnings rose above the limit were to be evicted. For every public unit built, a slum dwelling had to be demolished or closed, so that the total supply of housing did not increase. The cost per room was capped at a low figure. And the decision to build at all was left to local authorities, which meant that any town or suburb that did not want public housing need not have it. The universal model that Bauer had admired was thus excluded by law. American public housing was to be a segregated service for the poor, built as cheaply as possible, in whatever localities would accept it.

The early projects, built by the federal Public Works Administration and then by local authorities, were modest low rise developments, carefully managed, with tenants selected for steady employment and good conduct. They were, like all housing of the period, racially segregated, and the first of them, in Atlanta, cleared a mixed neighbourhood to build homes for white families only. After the war the Housing Act of 1949 declared the goal of a decent home for every American family and authorised more than eight hundred thousand public housing units in six years. Opposition was unrelenting. Congress repeatedly cut the appropriations, the target was not reached for two decades, and in cities across the country referendums and council votes kept projects out of white districts. What was built went where land was cheap and resistance weak, which meant in or beside existing black ghettos, often on sites cleared of slums.

Because sites were few and the need was great, and because the architectural fashion of the time favoured it, housing authorities in the large cities built high. In St Louis the Pruitt-Igoe project, completed in 1956 to the design of Minoru Yamasaki, consisted of thirty-three identical slab blocks of eleven storeys containing nearly three thousand apartments. It had been planned as two segregated sections, one for each race, but after the courts prohibited segregation in public housing white families left and it became wholly black. Economies imposed during construction left it with lifts that stopped only at every third floor, exposed steam pipes, and no public lavatories or play areas at ground level. The city was losing population and industry, and occupancy never reached the level on which the budget depended. Since federal law provided money for construction but required running costs to be met from rents, and the tenants were poor, maintenance collapsed. Lifts failed, windows went unrepaired, pipes burst, and the open galleries and stairwells became dangerous. By the late 1960s the project was two thirds empty. In March 1972 the authorities dynamited the first of the blocks, and the film of the building subsiding into its own dust was shown around the world. The architectural critic Charles Jencks later declared that modern architecture had died at that moment.

The image was used for decades as proof that public housing could not work, and the usual explanation offered was design. Later study has been less kind to that view. Pruitt-Igoe failed for reasons that were mostly not architectural: a declining city, a financial structure that guaranteed neglect, the concentration of several thousand very poor families, many of them headed by lone mothers who were forbidden by welfare rules to have a man in the house, and official racism. The same history, with variations, unfolded in Chicago, where the housing authority built a continuous corridor of towers along a motorway on the South Side, including the Robert Taylor Homes with more than four thousand apartments in twenty-eight blocks, which became the largest concentration of poverty in the United States. A federal court found in 1969 that the Chicago authority had deliberately selected sites and tenants so as to keep black families out of white neighbourhoods. An amendment of the same year, intended to help tenants by limiting their rent to a quarter of income, had the side effect of cutting the authorities' revenue further and driving out the remaining working families.

There was one large exception, and it qualifies every generalisation about American public housing. The New York City Housing Authority, the first and by far the largest in the country, owns some one hundred and seventy-seven thousand apartments in more than three hundred developments, most of them tall brick towers of exactly the kind that failed elsewhere, and houses well over four hundred thousand people, a population greater than that of many American cities. It never suffered wholesale abandonment. For decades it selected tenants carefully, kept a substantial proportion of working households, employed resident caretakers and maintained its own police force, in a city where the demand for cheap housing never slackened. Its buildings today need repairs estimated at tens of billions of dollars after years of inadequate federal funding, and conditions in many are poor. But the waiting list runs to several hundred thousand names, and no one seriously proposes demolition.

New York also possessed the nearest American equivalent to European non-profit housing. From the 1920s the garment workers' unions sponsored cooperative apartment blocks for their members, and a state programme of 1955 offered cheap loans and tax exemptions to limited profit developers and cooperatives housing middle income families. Its largest product, Co-op City in the Bronx, completed in the early 1970s, contains more than fifteen thousand apartments and is the largest housing cooperative in the world. These developments have remained stable and popular for half a century.

National policy, as recounted in Part Three, turned away from public housing after 1973. Many of the high rise projects, including all the Chicago towers, were demolished from the 1990s, and their residents were given vouchers or rehoused in smaller mixed income developments. A law of 1998 forbids any net increase in the number of public housing units. In very recent years the idea has resurfaced under a different name. Housing reformers in several states and cities, pointing to Vienna and to a county housing agency in Maryland that finances mixed income buildings from a revolving public fund and retains ownership of them, have begun to campaign for what they call social housing, meaning permanently affordable homes in public or non-profit ownership open to a range of incomes. Voters in Seattle approved the creation of a public developer on this model in 2023 and a tax to fund it in 2025. Whether the United States will build such housing in any quantity remains to be seen. That the argument is being made at all, eighty years after Catherine Bauer made it, suggests that the verdict of Pruitt-Igoe was less final than it seemed.

#### An assessment of the social democratic tradition

The social democratic tradition set itself a clear test. It proposed that a society could, by collective action within a market economy, ensure that every household had a decent and secure home at a cost it could bear. The record of the countries surveyed in this part shows that the test can be passed, that it has been passed in some places for long periods, and that it has also been failed in ways that discredited the attempt.

The successes should be stated first, because in the English speaking world they are often forgotten. Between about 1920 and 1975 the countries of northern and western Europe abolished the housing conditions described in Part Two. The one room dwelling with a shared tap, the cellar, the bed rented by the hour and the family of eight in a single unventilated room ceased to exist, within the lifetime of people who had been born in them. This was done very largely by public and non-profit building financed by the state. The private market had been given a century to house the industrial working class and had not done it. In Vienna, Zurich, Copenhagen, Helsinki and many Dutch and German cities, a large stock of non-market housing continues to provide good homes at moderate rents to a broad section of the population, and to restrain rents in the private sector by its presence. The German and Swiss tenancy systems demonstrate that renting can be made as secure as owning. Finland has shown that homelessness can be reduced nearly to nothing.

The failures are as plain. The tower blocks of Glasgow and Chicago, the grands ensembles of the Paris suburbs and the concrete estates of the Swedish million programme became places of concentrated poverty and, in many cases, of physical decay, and some were demolished within a generation of being built. Waiting lists in the most successful systems run to many years. Housing designed for a settled working class has adapted badly to immigration, and the estates now house a disproportionate share of ethnic minorities in conditions of segregation. In Britain, the United States and several other countries the public sector was run down or sold, and its remains are stigmatised.

Comparing the two sets of cases, it is possible to identify the conditions that separate them. The first and most important is breadth. Where public or non-profit housing was open to most of the population and was of a quality that people with choices were glad to accept, as in Vienna, Denmark and the Netherlands before the European ruling, it retained a mixed body of tenants, political support and adequate rents. Where it was restricted to the poor from the outset, as in the United States, or became so through sales and needs based allocation, as in Britain, it lost all three. A service for the poor alone, the British social policy writer Richard Titmuss observed, tends to become a poor service. The paradox is that the fairest seeming principle, that scarce subsidised housing should go to those in greatest need, is the one that leads most reliably to the ghetto.

The second condition is continuity of building. Systems that built steadily over many decades, adjusting their output and designs to changing demand, fared better than those that built enormous quantities in a few years by industrial methods and then stopped. The crash programmes of the 1960s were responses to real emergencies, but they produced a monoculture of dwellings in the style of a single moment, located wherever large sites could be found, and when tastes and needs changed there was nothing else on offer. Steady building also keeps the queue moving, which matters for the legitimacy of a system that allocates by waiting.

The third condition concerns finance and ownership. The systems that have endured are those in which the housing is owned by bodies that cannot easily sell it and in which the rents of older buildings, once their debts are paid, are used to finance new ones. The Danish national fund and the Austrian revolving loans are the clearest examples. Where social housing was a temporary status attached to a subsidy, as in Germany, or could be bought by the sitting tenant at a discount, as in Britain, the stock dissolved. A non-market sector has to be protected from the temptation that every generation feels to cash it in.

The fourth is land. Vienna, Helsinki, Amsterdam and Stockholm built their social housing largely on land that the municipality already owned or could acquire at a price unrelated to its development value. Where public builders had to buy sites on the open market, in competition with private developers, they were pushed to the cheapest and most isolated locations, with the results seen at Sarcelles and Pruitt-Igoe.

Critics from the market tradition would accept much of this description and draw a different moral. The successes, they would say, are expensive, depend on unusual political stability and on taxing or regulating private property in ways that deter investment, and benefit insiders. A family holding a cheap secure tenancy in Stockholm or Vienna is privileged in relation to the young couple or the immigrant who cannot get one, and has little reason to move when its needs change. Allocation by queue or by official assessment is no more just than allocation by price, and it is slower. The failures, on this view, are not accidents. They are what tends to happen when housing is produced by bureaucracies answerable to politicians instead of to customers. The objection has force, and the experience of the Swedish queue and of some municipal housing departments bears it out. It has less force against systems in which non-profit landlords compete with one another and with private landlords for tenants, as in Austria and the Netherlands, or in which tenants govern their own estates, as in Denmark.

Critics from the left have a different complaint. Social democracy, they argue, left the private ownership of land and the private rental market in place, and so remained exposed to their pressures. Its housing was built on land whose price it could not ultimately control, financed by loans whose interest it did not set, and it was undone as soon as governments short of money or hostile in principle chose to sell. The universal systems were also, from the start, better at housing the organised and regularly employed than the very poor, the unemployed and migrants. A lasting solution, on this view, requires going further and removing housing from the market altogether.

The decades since 1980 have been hard on the tradition. Governments of the left as well as the right withdrew from building, converted subsidies into allowances and sold public stock, and European competition law has pressed against the universal model. The results of that withdrawal, in the form of rising rents, lengthening queues and the return of homelessness to cities that had almost forgotten it, have lately produced a reaction. Delegations from London, Dublin, Barcelona, New York and Seattle travel to Vienna to learn how it was done, and the term social housing has reappeared in the programmes of parties that had dropped it. What they find there is that the method is no secret. It requires public land, patient finance, institutions that hold housing for the long term and a willingness to house the middle as well as the bottom. The difficulty is that it takes decades to bear fruit, and that it must be defended in every one of those decades against those who would rather have the money.

Those who believed that even this was not enough, and that housing must be taken out of the market entirely, had their opportunity in a large part of the world for much of the twentieth century. Their record is the subject of Part Seven.

## Part Seven: Beyond the Market

### Chapter 19: The Soviet Experiment

The socialist tradition holds that the housing question cannot be solved while dwellings are bought and sold for profit. Regulation and public building, on this view, treat symptoms. The cause is the private ownership of land and housing, which allows one class to live on the necessity of another, and the remedy is to abolish it. For most of the twentieth century this was not a speculation. In the Soviet Union from 1917, in Eastern Europe and China from the late 1940s, and in Cuba, Vietnam and elsewhere, governments took urban land and much or all of the housing stock into public ownership, forbade or restricted private renting and allocated dwellings administratively at nominal rents. At its greatest extent the system housed a third of the human race. This part of the book examines what it did, what became of it when the regimes fell, and the smaller scale forms of common ownership that carry the idea today.

#### Revolution, the divided flat and the house-commune

The Bolsheviks came to power in November 1917 in a country whose cities were among the worst housed in Europe. In Petrograd and Moscow the majority of workers lived in factory barracks, in cellars, or in rented corners of rooms divided by curtains, while the wealthy occupied apartments of eight or ten rooms with quarters for servants. Lenin had read Engels on the housing question and had quoted with approval, a few weeks before the seizure of power, the passage in which Engels observed that a victorious proletariat could relieve the shortage at once by expropriating part of the dwellings of the rich and quartering the homeless in them. That is what the new government did.

A decree of August 1918 abolished private ownership of real estate in towns, transferring to the local soviets all buildings above a value fixed by each city. Smaller houses occupied by their owners were generally left alone. The soviets then carried out what was called the compaction of the bourgeoisie. A norm of living space was set, at first about ten square metres for each adult, and any household occupying more was required to give up the surplus. Commissions toured the better districts measuring rooms. A professor, lawyer or merchant who had lived with his family in a large flat was confined to one or two rooms of it, and the remaining rooms were assigned, one to a family, to workers moved in from the barracks and basements. The former owner now shared his kitchen, bathroom, lavatory and corridor with strangers of a class that had every reason to resent him. In Moscow alone some half a million workers and their families were rehoused in this way within a few years.

The policy was popular with those who gained by it, and it expressed in the most direct form the principle that housing should be distributed according to need. It added nothing to the supply. Civil war, famine and the collapse of the economy meant that almost nothing was built or repaired for several years, pipes froze and burst, and residents burned doors and floorboards for fuel. The population of Petrograd fell by two thirds. With rents abolished altogether in 1921 there was no revenue for maintenance, and the nationalised housing stock deteriorated at an alarming rate.

The retreat known as the New Economic Policy brought a partial restoration of private property in housing. From 1921 smaller buildings were returned to their former owners or leased to cooperatives of their tenants, rents were reintroduced on a scale varying with the occupant's income and class, and private individuals were again permitted to build and to let. Throughout the 1920s a large part of urban housing, and nearly all rural housing, remained in private hands. The state itself built little. The writer Mikhail Bulgakov, who lived in a communal flat in Moscow in these years, made the struggle for rooms the subject of some of his sharpest satire. In one story a house committee attempts to take rooms from an eminent surgeon who has seven and insists he needs an eighth, and in his great novel the devil, visiting Moscow and inspecting its citizens, concludes that they are much as people have always been, except that the housing question has spoiled them.

The same decade produced the most radical thinking about housing that the socialist movement ever attempted. If private property was to disappear, the private household that went with it might disappear as well. Architects and theorists associated with the avant-garde argued that the individual kitchen enslaved women, that the family as an economic unit was a relic of capitalism, and that the dwelling of the future should be a house-commune. Cooking, eating, laundry and the rearing of children would be carried on collectively in shared facilities, and the individual would need only a small private cell for sleep. Experimental buildings were erected on these lines, with communal dining halls and nurseries. Some student communes pooled all earnings and possessions and regulated the day by timetable. The most thoughtful of the architects, Moisei Ginzburg, designed for the employees of the finance ministry in Moscow a building completed in 1930 that he described as transitional. It offered compact apartments on two levels, ingeniously planned to give each a high living room, linked by a bridge to a block containing a canteen, a gymnasium and a library, so that residents might be drawn gradually towards collective life without being forced into it. The building influenced Le Corbusier and through him a great deal of postwar housing in the West.

A wider debate concerned the city itself. One school, the urbanists, proposed that the population be housed in great communal blocks of two or three thousand people. Another, the disurbanists, held that the socialist future lay in abolishing the distinction between town and country by spreading light individual dwellings in ribbons across the whole territory, along lines of transport and power. Both drew on Engels, who had written that the housing question would be solved only with the abolition of the antithesis between town and country.

The debate was closed from above. In May 1930 the central committee of the Communist Party issued a resolution condemning what it called highly unsound, semi-fantastic and therefore extremely harmful attempts to leap in a single bound over the obstacles to a socialist reconstruction of the way of life. Projects for the immediate collectivisation of domestic life and the removal of children from their parents were denounced as utopian. The regime had embarked on forced industrialisation, it needed every rouble for steel mills and dams, and it had no intention of spending on communal palaces. What it offered instead, and what became the normal Soviet dwelling for the next thirty years, was the communal apartment produced by the compaction of 1918, now extended to new buildings as well: a flat designed for one family and occupied by several, not from any theory of collective living but because there was nothing else.

#### Stalin's cities: the communal apartment, the residence permit and the barracks

The industrialisation that Stalin launched at the end of the 1920s produced the fastest urbanisation that had occurred anywhere up to that time. The collectivisation of agriculture drove millions of peasants off the land, and the new factories drew them in. The urban population of the Soviet Union rose from twenty-six million in 1926 to fifty-six million in 1939. Investment was concentrated on heavy industry and armaments, and housing received what was left. The result was a deterioration in housing conditions without parallel in a country at peace. Average living space in the towns, which had been somewhat under six square metres a head in the mid 1920s, fell to about four by 1940, well below the official sanitary minimum of nine and comparable with the worst slums of Victorian England.

The new industrial cities showed the system at its harshest. At Magnitogorsk, the great steel centre built from nothing on the steppe beyond the Urals, a German architect, the same Ernst May who had planned the new settlements of Frankfurt, was engaged to design a model socialist town. Little of it was built. The workers who erected the blast furnaces lived in tents, in dugouts roofed with turf, and in long wooden barracks without partitions, where a family's territory was a bed and the space beneath it. An American welder who worked there in the 1930s recorded that the barracks were heated by a single stove and that in winter the water froze in the buckets. Seven years after the founding of the city, the majority of its population still lived in barracks or in huts they had put together themselves.

In the older cities the communal apartment became the universal form of life. Each family had one room, in which it slept, ate, studied and stored everything it owned, and shared the kitchen, the lavatory and the telephone in the corridor with five, ten or more other families. Life was organised by an elaborate etiquette of rotas for cleaning, separate electricity meters, labelled shelves and individual light bulbs in the lavatory, each with its own switch in the owner's room. Neighbours knew everything about one another. In the years of the terror this had a sinister aspect, since a denunciation could free a room, and there are documented cases of people informing on their neighbours for that purpose. The communal flat also produced forms of solidarity, and many Russians who grew up in one remember it with a mixture of horror and nostalgia. For better or worse, it shaped the habits of three generations.

The state used housing as an instrument of control over the movement of labour. In December 1932 it introduced an internal passport, without which no one could legally live in a town, and attached to it a residence permit registered by the police for a specific address. Peasants on the collective farms were not issued passports and so were bound to the land. To live in Moscow, Leningrad or any other major city required a permit, and a permit required an entitlement to living space there. Much of the new housing, moreover, was built and owned not by the municipal soviets but by ministries and enterprises for their own workforces. A worker who left his job, or was dismissed, could be evicted from the factory's housing, a provision made explicit by decree in 1932. The arrangement that trade unions in the West had fought as the tied cottage and the company town was thus reproduced by the workers' state on a continental scale, and for the same purpose of holding labour in place.

Not all Soviet citizens were housed alike. Engineers, senior officials, favoured writers and scientists, and outstanding workers received separate flats in new buildings as a reward and an incentive, once the leadership had denounced the levelling of wages as a petty bourgeois deviation. In Moscow a huge grey complex was completed in 1931 on the river opposite the Kremlin for the families of the governing elite, with five hundred spacious apartments, its own cinema, shops, clinic and laundry. It became known as the House on the Embankment, and during the purges of 1937 and 1938 about a third of its residents were arrested. The monumental apartment buildings that line the main avenues of Soviet cities, with their columns, high ceilings and sculptured cornices, date from the later Stalin years. They were built at great expense for a small privileged stratum and displayed as evidence of what socialism was providing for the people.

The war made everything worse. The German invasion destroyed or damaged something like a third of the urban housing in the occupied territories, along with seventy thousand villages, and left about twenty-five million people without shelter. Cities such as Stalingrad, Minsk and Kiev had to be rebuilt almost entirely. In the postwar years families lived in cellars, in the ruins and in dugouts, and reconstruction again gave precedence to industry. One response of the government, which is often overlooked, was to encourage people to build for themselves. A decree of 1948 confirmed the right of citizens to build and own a house of one or two storeys, on a plot allocated by the local soviet, and offered small loans for the purpose. Self built wooden houses on the edges of towns accounted for a substantial share of all the urban housing constructed in the following decade. Even at the height of Stalinism, in other words, a significant part of the Soviet population lived in dwellings that were their personal property, though the land beneath them belonged to the state.

By the time of Stalin's death in March 1953 the housing situation had become a danger to the regime. An urban population that had more than doubled again since 1939 was crowded into a stock that had barely grown. Surveys carried out in secret for the leadership showed that complaints about housing exceeded all others in letters from citizens to the authorities. The country that had abolished the landlord had, after thirty-five years, a housing shortage more acute than that of any other industrial state, and its new leaders knew that something would have to be done.

#### Khrushchev and the separate flat

Nikita Khrushchev, who emerged as leader within two years of Stalin's death, had been the party chief of Moscow and knew its housing at first hand. He made the question his own. In December 1954 he addressed a national conference of builders and architects, and in a speech lasting several hours he attacked the profession for designing façades instead of homes. Architects, he said, were building monuments to themselves, with towers, spires and colonnades, while people waited for somewhere to live. What was needed was industrial production of standard buildings from factory made components. A decree of the following year condemned what it termed excesses in design, and the ornate style of the Stalin period ended overnight. A further decree of July 1957 set the goal of eliminating the housing shortage within ten to twelve years.

The instrument was a standardised block of flats, assembled on site from large concrete panels cast in specially built factories. The first mass produced type was a plain rectangular building of five storeys, the maximum that regulations permitted without a lift. It contained small flats of one, two or three rooms, the two room version measuring about forty-four square metres in all. Ceilings were two and a half metres high. The kitchen was some six square metres, the bath and the lavatory shared one tiny room, and one living room commonly served as the passage to another. Walls were thin and transmitted every sound. The engineer who designed the most widely used series is said to have studied French prefabrication methods and to have reduced the number of distinct components to a minimum. A block could be erected in a fortnight. Russians named these buildings after their sponsor, and later, punning on the Russian word for slum, gave them a less flattering nickname.

Their significance lay in a single feature. Each flat was intended for one family and had its own front door. The party programme adopted in 1961 promised that within twenty years every family, including newlyweds, would have a separate, well appointed dwelling. After forty years in which the individual household had been an object of ideological suspicion and practical neglect, the Soviet state committed itself to the family flat as the normal unit of life.
