The contrast with the house-commune of the 1920s could not have been more complete.

The scale of what followed has few equals. Housing construction in the Soviet Union roughly doubled between 1955 and 1960 and remained at more than two million dwellings a year for the next three decades, a rate per head of population higher than that of any Western country at the time. In the first ten years of the programme tens of millions of families moved into new flats, and by one official count more than a hundred million people, close to half the population, had been rehoused by 1970. Whole districts rose on the outskirts of every city, beginning with an experimental quarter in the south west of Moscow whose name became a byword for the new housing and the title of an operetta by Shostakovich, in which young couples sing of their joy at receiving a flat.

The effect on daily life was profound. A family that had lived for twenty years in one room of a communal apartment, with a queue for the stove and a neighbour listening at the wall, acquired a door it could close. Historians of the period have connected the separate flat with the modest liberalisation of Soviet society that followed. In the privacy of their own kitchens people could speak more freely than had been possible for a generation, and the kitchen conversation became the characteristic institution of the Soviet intelligentsia, the place where forbidden books were passed from hand to hand and songs were sung to a guitar. The new housing also created a demand for furniture, refrigerators and television sets, and with it the beginnings of a consumer society.

The uniformity of the product became a national joke. The most popular Soviet film comedy, first shown on New Year's Day 1976 and broadcast every year since, turns on a man from Moscow who, after drinking with friends, is put on an aeroplane to Leningrad by mistake. He gives a taxi driver his home address, arrives at a street with the same name, enters an identical building, opens an identical door with his own key and falls asleep in a flat furnished exactly like his own. The film is affectionate, and audiences recognised in it the truth of their lives.

The economic basis of the system remained what it had been. Rents had been fixed in 1928 at a few kopecks per square metre and were never raised. Together with heating, water and electricity, which were also heavily subsidised, housing cost the average Soviet family about three per cent of its income, against a fifth or a quarter in the West. Tenancy was secure for life and could be passed to family members registered at the address. Eviction was virtually unknown. These were real benefits, and they are the reason many citizens of the former Soviet Union look back on the system with regret. Their cost was that rents covered only a fraction of the expense of maintaining the buildings, let alone of constructing them, so that the whole burden fell on a state budget with many other calls upon it. Maintenance was chronically neglected, and stairwells, lifts and courtyards belonged in practice to nobody.

Flats were allocated through waiting lists kept by local soviets and by enterprises. To be placed on the list a family had to show that it had less than a set amount of space per person, usually five to seven square metres, and it then waited for a period that averaged about a decade and could be much longer. Powerful ministries and factories built more and housed their employees sooner, and a job with a good enterprise was valued above all for the flat that came with it. Officials, officers and members of the creative unions were entitled to extra space. Those with connections or with something to trade moved up the list. Because flats could not be bought or sold, an entire informal economy grew up around exchanging them. Notices offering to swap two rooms in one district for one room in each of two others covered the walls near every exchange bureau, and chains of five or six households would move simultaneously. Marriages and divorces were contracted for the sake of registration at an address. From 1962 the government allowed cooperatives, in which members who could put down forty per cent of the cost obtained a flat without waiting, and these supplied a small share of new housing, chiefly to professionals.

Khrushchev's deadline passed and the shortage remained. The population of the cities kept growing, households became smaller and more numerous, and the dwellings built in the 1950s began to wear out. In 1986 Mikhail Gorbachev renewed the promise in a new form, pledging a separate flat or house for every family by the year 2000. By the end of the Soviet Union in 1991 roughly a fifth of urban households were still on waiting lists, and well over a million people in Moscow and Leningrad still lived in communal apartments. The Soviet state had built more housing, for more people, than any government in history up to that time. It had not succeeded, in seventy-four years, in giving every family a home of its own, which was the one thing in this field it had promised.

#### Eastern Europe

The countries that came under communist rule after 1945 adopted the Soviet model with variations that reflected their own traditions and the differing degrees of control their governments were able or willing to exercise. All of them nationalised large apartment buildings, froze rents at nominal levels, and from the late 1950s built panel block estates on the Soviet pattern. They differed widely in how much private ownership and private building they tolerated, and the differences throw light on what the socialist housing system actually was.

East Germany applied the model most completely. The regime inherited the tenements of Berlin, Leipzig and Dresden, which remained largely in private hands in law but were subject to rents frozen at the level of 1936. Since such rents did not cover even minimal repairs, owners abandoned their buildings or surrendered them to the municipal housing administration, and the older districts were left to decay for forty years, their façades blackened and their roofs leaking. A bitter joke of the time adapted the slogan of the peace movement to describe the result: creating ruins without weapons. The state concentrated its resources on new construction. In 1973 the party leader Erich Honecker announced a housing programme intended to solve the housing question as a social problem by 1990, with a target of nearly three million dwellings built or modernised. It was presented as the centrepiece of what he called the unity of economic and social policy.

The programme was carried out with a single standardised system of concrete panels, and it produced estates of enormous size on the edges of the cities. Marzahn, in the east of Berlin, was built from 1977 for more than a hundred thousand residents. The flats, with central heating, a bathroom and a balcony, were greatly preferred to the unmodernised old buildings, in which a quarter of dwellings still had no lavatory of their own, and they were allocated with priority to young families and to workers in favoured industries. Rents amounted to three or four per cent of household income. Honecker personally handed over what was announced as the three millionth dwelling in 1988. It later emerged that the figures had been inflated and that the cost of the programme, financed in part by borrowing from the West, had contributed to the bankruptcy of the state. When the wall came down, several hundred thousand East Germans were still on waiting lists.

Poland entrusted most new urban housing to cooperatives. These were cooperatives in name more than in substance, being large bureaucratic organisations that built with state credit and to state plans, but membership required a deposit and regular contributions, and it gave the member a transferable right to a flat. Waiting times were among the longest in the bloc. By the 1980s a young couple joining a cooperative in Warsaw could expect to wait fifteen or twenty years, and children were enrolled by their parents at birth. The shortage was one of the grievances behind the strikes of 1980 that gave rise to the Solidarity movement. Czechoslovakia built panel estates so extensively that about a third of the population of the present Czech Republic lives in them.

Hungary and Bulgaria illustrate a fact that sits awkwardly with the usual picture of socialist housing. In both countries the majority of dwellings were privately owned throughout the communist period. The state nationalised the apartment houses of the cities and built public rental estates, but in the villages and small towns, and increasingly on the edges of the cities, families built their own houses on their own plots with the help of relatives and neighbours, and the authorities, recognising that they could not meet the need themselves, provided loans and materials. In Hungary in the 1980s about two thirds of all new dwellings were built privately. A market in these houses existed, at prices far above the official ones. Yugoslavia, which had broken with Moscow in 1948, followed a path of its own that is considered in the next chapter.

The Hungarian sociologist Iván Szelényi made, on the basis of surveys he conducted in the late 1960s, a discovery that was politically explosive and that cost him his position. He set out to learn who actually received the new state flats, which were of good quality and let at trivial rents and therefore carried a large hidden subsidy. He found that they went disproportionately to officials, professionals, managers and other white collar employees. Manual workers, who were poorer, were far more likely to have built their own houses at their own expense, without subsidy, or to be living in old unmodernised dwellings. The allocation of housing by administrative decision, which was supposed to correct the inequalities of the market, was in practice regressive. It rewarded those whom the system valued and those who knew how to deal with it. The better off received the larger subsidy, and the worse off paid full price. Szelényi concluded that under state socialism the administrative allocation of scarce goods creates inequalities of its own, and that in such a society the market, far from being the source of injustice, might act as a corrective. Later research in the Soviet Union, Poland and Czechoslovakia found the same pattern.

Romania provided the grimmest illustration of what the power to plan housing can be used for. Nicolae Ceaușescu, impressed by a visit to North Korea, embarked in the 1970s on a programme he called systematisation. Its declared aim was to eliminate the difference between town and country by concentrating the rural population in new agro-industrial centres of apartment blocks. In its final form, announced in 1988, it envisaged the demolition of about half of the country's thirteen thousand villages. A number were in fact destroyed and their inhabitants moved into unfinished blocks without running water before the regime fell. In Bucharest a quarter of the historic centre, including churches, monasteries and some forty thousand homes, was razed to make way for a boulevard wider than the Champs Élysées and a palace that is among the largest buildings in the world.

Throughout the region the panel block estate became the common landscape of socialism, housing a third or more of the urban population from the Elbe to the Pacific. Western visitors found the estates oppressive. The verdict of those who lived in them was more mixed. They contained a broad range of society, since doctors, engineers and factory workers had all received flats through the same lists, and they did not carry the stigma attached to public housing in the West. They had nurseries, schools, clinics and shops within walking distance, and public transport to the centre. Surveys after 1989 found that most residents were reasonably satisfied. The fate of these estates since the fall of communism has in general been better than that of their Western counterparts, largely because they never became places where only the poor lived.

### Chapter 20: Other Socialisms and the Great Privatisation

The Soviet pattern was not the only form that socialist housing took. China built its system around the workplace and kept the countryside on a separate footing. Cuba abolished the landlord by turning tenants into owners who could not sell. Yugoslavia vested housing in self-managed enterprises, and several African governments nationalised land and rental property in the 1970s. This chapter surveys these variants and then turns to the event that ended most of them, the transfer of state housing to its occupants after 1989, before attempting a judgment on the whole experiment.

#### China under Mao: the work unit and the village

The Communist Party of China came to power in 1949 as a movement of peasants, and its first great act was a land reform in the countryside. Between 1950 and 1952 the fields of landlords and rich peasants were confiscated and distributed among the poor, in a campaign conducted through mass meetings at which landlords were denounced and in which, by most estimates, well over a million people were killed. The peasants' ownership of their plots was brief. From the mid 1950s agriculture was collectivised and the land passed to the communes. One thing remained with the household. Each rural family kept a house, built at its own expense on a homestead plot assigned by the collective, and the right to that plot has continued to the present day. Rural housing in China was thus never state housing. It was self built and privately owned, on land held collectively by the village, and it accounted for the homes of four fifths of the population.

In the cities the party moved more gradually than the Bolsheviks had. The property of those classed as enemies was confiscated at once, but ordinary private owners, who held most of the urban housing in 1949, were at first left in possession. The change came in the socialist transformation of 1956 to 1958. Owners who let more than a small amount of floor space were required to hand over the management of their rental property to the state, which collected the rents and paid them a fixed share, typically between a fifth and two fifths, as compensation. These payments ceased in 1966 at the start of the Cultural Revolution, when the properties passed fully to the state, and in the turmoil of the following years Red Guards seized many of the remaining owner occupied houses, driving their occupants into a single room or out altogether.

New urban housing was provided through the institution that organised every aspect of city life under Mao: the work unit. A work unit might be a factory, a government office, a university or a hospital. It did not merely employ its members. It housed them in dormitories and apartment blocks built on or beside its own premises, usually within a walled compound, and it supplied canteens, a clinic, a nursery, a school, bath houses, ration coupons and a pension. It kept a personal file on each member, and its permission was needed to marry, to have a child and to change jobs, which few ever did. Housing was allotted by the unit's own committee according to rank, seniority, family size and political standing. Rent was nominal, generally one or two per cent of household income and less than the cost of upkeep.

The arrangement had consequences that resembled those in the Soviet Union and went further. Since housing came with the job and could be obtained in no other way, an employee who left his unit lost his home, and labour was effectively immobile. Since the unit paid for housing out of funds that could otherwise be invested in production, and since the national leadership preached production first and livelihood second, very little was built. Urban housing received a smaller share of investment in China than in any other socialist country. While the urban population roughly doubled between 1949 and 1978, the average living space per person in the cities fell from about four and a half square metres to three and a half. Three generations commonly shared one or two rooms, young couples waited years after marriage for a room of their own, and families cooked in the corridors. Wealthy and well connected units housed their people better than poor ones, so that a person's dwelling depended on the fortunes of his employer, and those who belonged to no unit, or to a small collective enterprise, received nothing.

A further instrument kept the pressure on urban housing within bounds. From 1958 every citizen was registered as belonging either to the agricultural or the non-agricultural population at a specified place, and the status was inherited. A person with a rural registration could not obtain grain rations, employment or housing in a city. The system prevented the migration from countryside to town that accompanied industrialisation everywhere else, and during the 1960s and 1970s the government actually sent some seventeen million urban young people to live in the villages. China under Mao thus had no shanty towns. It achieved this not by housing its rural migrants but by forbidding them to move.

Deng Xiaoping, who took control after Mao's death, said as early as 1980 that urban residents should be allowed to buy their homes and that rents should be raised to a level that made buying worthwhile. Experiments in selling flats and raising rents were conducted in a few cities during the following decade, and the general reform came in 1998, as described in Part Four. Two legacies of the Maoist system have nevertheless continued to shape Chinese housing.

The first is the registration system, which has been relaxed without being abolished. Some three hundred million people now work in cities where they are not registered residents, and they are still largely excluded from subsidised housing, and in the biggest cities from the right to buy property and from local schools. The second is the dual ownership of land. Urban land belongs to the state and can be leased to developers. Rural land belongs to village collectives and in law cannot be sold for urban use except after requisition by the government. As cities expanded outward they surrounded hundreds of former farming villages. The villagers, who had lost their fields but retained their homestead plots, replaced their houses with crowded blocks of six, eight or ten storeys, built without permits and so close together that neighbours can touch hands across the alley, and let the rooms to migrant workers. These urban villages, of which Shenzhen and Guangzhou each contain several hundred, house a large proportion of the migrant population in conditions outside the formal system altogether. They are the Chinese equivalent of the informal settlement, and the villagers who own them have in many cases become rich landlords, collecting rents on collectively owned land that the state does not recognise as urban.

#### Cuba, Yugoslavia, Ethiopia and Tanzania

Cuba arrived at a solution to the housing question that had been proposed a century earlier by Proudhon and ridiculed by Engels. Before the revolution of 1959 some three quarters of the people of Havana were tenants, and urban landlords were a principal target of popular resentment. Within weeks of taking power the government of Fidel Castro halted evictions and cut rents by between thirty and fifty per cent, the larger reductions applying to the cheaper dwellings. In October 1960 it issued the Urban Reform Law, which declared housing to be a right and abolished the private letting of dwellings altogether.

The mechanism was distinctive. The state took over all rented housing, and each tenant became the purchaser of the dwelling he occupied. The rent he had been paying was converted into instalments of purchase, to be paid to the state for a period of between five and twenty years, depending on the age of the building, after which he would hold full title. Former landlords were compensated with a monthly pension up to a fixed limit, and those who had owned only a few properties received it for life. Families who already owned their homes kept them. No one was permitted to own more than one residence and a holiday house. Dwellings built by the state after the reform were let at a rent of ten per cent of household income, and the occupants of most of these were likewise made owners by a law of 1984. Cuba thus became, under a communist government, a country in which about eighty-five per cent of households own their homes, one of the highest proportions in the world.

The ownership conferred was of a peculiar kind. A Cuban owner could live in his dwelling, bequeath it to relatives who lived with him and, under strict conditions, exchange it. He could not sell it to another person for money, since the object of the reform had been to eliminate housing as a source of profit. For half a century the only legal way to move was the swap. Those wishing to exchange gathered at recognised spots, the best known being a tree lined promenade in central Havana, with handwritten notices describing what they offered and what they sought, and intermediaries assembled chains of exchanges among several households. Swaps of unequal dwellings were balanced by payments under the table, and a marriage might be arranged in order to register a new occupant. A popular Cuban film comedy of the 1980s took the practice as its subject and its title.

Construction never kept pace with need. The state gave priority to schools, hospitals and rural development, and from the early 1970s it relied for much urban building on what were called microbrigades. Groups of workers were released from their ordinary jobs, while continuing to draw wages, to build apartment blocks, and a share of the completed flats was allotted to members of the workplace that had supplied the labour, by vote of their colleagues. The method built some large estates and involved people directly in housing themselves and their fellow workers, at the cost of low productivity and poor finish. The collapse of Soviet support after 1991 brought building nearly to a halt. Materials for repair became unobtainable, the elegant older districts of Havana, starved of maintenance for decades, decayed to the point where partial collapses of buildings became a regular occurrence in the rainy season, and families subdivided high ceilinged rooms with improvised mezzanines. The official shortage is put at well over half a million dwellings. In 2011, as part of a wider economic opening, the government legalised the purchase and sale of homes between residents. A market sprang up at once, financed largely by money sent from relatives abroad, and with it a new division between those who have access to foreign currency and those who do not.

Yugoslavia, expelled from the Soviet bloc in 1948, developed a system that it described as social ownership, to distinguish it from ownership by the state. Enterprises were formally managed by councils of their workers, and each was required to pay a percentage of its wage bill into a housing fund. With this money the enterprise built or bought flats and allocated them to employees according to a points system drawn up by the workers' council. The recipient acquired what was called an occupancy right, which was permanent, could be inherited by members of the household and could be exchanged, and for which he paid a low rent. Flats so held belonged to society, not to the occupant or the firm.

As in Hungary, the system proved to favour the better placed. Profitable enterprises in the richer republics built more than struggling ones, and within each enterprise managers and specialists accumulated points faster than unskilled workers. By the 1980s socially owned flats made up about a third of urban housing and were occupied disproportionately by the educated and the well connected. Everyone else did what Yugoslavs had always done and built for themselves. Family houses, often constructed over many years with the earnings of relatives working in Germany and frequently without planning permission, ringed every town. The authorities tolerated them, since they relieved the pressure on the housing funds, and periodically legalised them. Socialist Yugoslavia thus had two housing systems side by side: a subsidised one for the more privileged and an unsubsidised private one for workers and peasants.

In Africa the socialist approach was applied in the 1970s by governments that had neither the administrative capacity nor the resources of the European states. Ethiopia offers the fullest case. The military regime that overthrew the emperor in 1974 nationalised all rural land the following year and distributed its use to those who farmed it. A second proclamation in July 1975 nationalised all urban land and all houses that their owners did not themselves occupy, which were known as extra houses. No compensation was paid. The cheaper dwellings were placed under neighbourhood associations and the more valuable under a state agency, and rents on the cheaper ones were cut by up to half. The measure ended a system in which a small number of aristocratic families had owned most of Addis Ababa. It also ended private investment in rental housing. The neighbourhood associations collected rents too low to pay for repairs, and the dwellings in their charge, mostly single storey structures of mud and timber, deteriorated for forty years. Much of the old centre of Addis Ababa still consists of them, and all land in Ethiopia remains the property of the state, leased to users.

Tanzania under Julius Nyerere nationalised rental buildings above a certain value in 1971 and then attempted something more ambitious in the countryside. Nyerere believed that African socialism should be built on the traditional values of the extended family, a principle he called ujamaa, and that the scattered rural population should come together in villages where schools, clinics and water could be provided and land farmed in common. Persuasion produced few villages, and from 1973 the policy was made compulsory. Within three years some five million people or more were moved, in many cases by force and with their former homes burned to prevent return, to sites selected by officials who often knew little of local soils and water. Collective farming failed and was abandoned. The services did reach many of the new villages, and literacy and health improved, but the episode is generally cited as an example of the harm that can be done when a state reorders where people live according to a plan.

These cases have a feature in common that recurs throughout the history of socialist housing. The act of expropriation was popular and was carried out quickly. Existing tenants gained security and low rents at once. What followed was harder. With private building discouraged and rents too low to finance upkeep, the entire burden of constructing and maintaining housing fell on states with limited means, and the stock inherited from the old order was consumed without being replaced.

#### The great privatisation

The fall of the communist governments of Europe between 1989 and 1991 was followed by the largest transfer of real property in history. Within a few years the housing that states, municipalities and enterprises had owned across a territory stretching from the Elbe to Vladivostok passed, for the most part, into the private ownership of the people who happened to be living in it. China carried out a similar transfer in its cities at the end of the decade. The countries that had gone furthest in abolishing private property in housing emerged as the most thoroughly owner occupied societies in the world.

The new governments had several reasons to dispose of the stock. Maintaining it was a heavy charge on budgets that were collapsing. Reformers and their Western advisers believed that private ownership was the foundation of a market economy and that owners would care for buildings as tenants and state agencies had not. Giving people their flats cost the treasury nothing in the short term and softened the hardships of the transition, at a time when savings were being destroyed by inflation and employment by the closure of factories. It was also difficult to imagine any other course. Tenants already possessed rights of occupancy that were permanent, heritable and exchangeable, and regarded their flats as their own in all but name.

The terms varied. Russia adopted a law in 1991 allowing tenants to take ownership of their dwellings, and from the following year the transfer was free of charge whatever the size or value of the flat. The right was repeatedly extended and finally made permanent, and more than four fifths of the eligible housing has been privatised. Lithuania, Estonia, Bulgaria, Hungary, Slovenia, Croatia and Serbia sold flats to their occupants at prices that amounted to a small fraction of market value, sometimes payable in vouchers or in inflated currency that made them close to free. Romania sold almost its entire state stock within a few years of the revolution. Albania simply gave it away. Only Poland, the Czech Republic and the eastern part of Germany moved more slowly, retaining substantial municipal or cooperative rental sectors.

The outcome can be read in the statistics. In Romania about ninety-five per cent of households own their home, in Hungary, Lithuania, Croatia and Slovakia about nine tenths, and in Russia and China the proportions are similar. Mortgages played almost no part. The typical owner in these countries holds his flat free of debt, and public or social rental housing has all but vanished, amounting to two or three per cent of the stock in most of them.

A second process ran alongside the first in some countries. Restitution returned property confiscated by the communists to its former owners or their heirs. The Czech Republic, Estonia, Latvia and eastern Germany restored the actual buildings wherever possible. In Germany the unification treaty laid down the principle of return before compensation, and more than two million claims were lodged, many by people or their descendants who had left for the West decades earlier, and some by the heirs of Jewish owners dispossessed before 1945. In Prague and Riga the residents of restituted buildings found themselves tenants of a private landlord they had never heard of, with their rents regulated for a transitional period and then freed. Hungary and Poland chose not to return urban property in kind, although in Warsaw a chaotic and frequently fraudulent process of restitution by court order went on for years, leading to the eviction of long standing tenants and a major scandal in 2016.

The distribution of the gains reflected the distribution of housing under the old regime, and so reproduced its inequalities in a new and permanent form. A family that had been allocated a large flat in the centre of Moscow, Budapest or Belgrade, which usually meant a family with standing in the old system, received an asset that within a few years was worth a great deal. A family in a small flat in a declining industrial town received something of little value, and those who had been on the waiting list, or living with relatives, or housed in a workers' hostel, received nothing at all. The privileges that Szelényi had identified were converted into wealth. The privatisation resembled the British Right to Buy in its mechanism, and in the absence of any price it was more extreme in its effects.

Ownership brought responsibilities for which neither the new owners nor the law were prepared. A panel block of two hundred flats now belonged to two hundred separate households, many of them pensioners or unemployed, who were collectively liable for a roof, lifts, pipes and insulation that had been neglected for thirty years. For a long time no workable legal form existed for managing the common parts or compelling contributions, and housing researchers coined the term poor owners for the result. Governments in Slovakia, Poland, the Czech Republic and the Baltic states eventually organised subsidised loan programmes for the renovation and insulation of panel buildings, and many estates have been transformed in appearance. In Russia a compulsory monthly levy for capital repairs was introduced in 2014. In Moscow the city government decided in 2017 on a more drastic course, announcing the demolition of several thousand five storey blocks of the Khrushchev era and the rehousing of more than a million residents in new towers, a project that provoked the largest street protests the capital had seen in years from people who feared being moved to worse locations.

Eastern Germany experienced a different problem. After unification about a million and a half people moved to the west, the birth rate collapsed, and cities such as Leipzig, Halle and Chemnitz found themselves with tens of thousands of empty flats. A federal programme begun in 2002 paid for the demolition of more than three hundred thousand dwellings, most of them in panel estates completed only a decade or two earlier, in order to stabilise the housing market. It was one of the few occasions in modern history when a government has destroyed sound housing on a large scale because there was too much of it.

The long term consequences of universal ownership are still unfolding. On the positive side, people in the former communist countries have a security of tenure and a freedom from housing costs that few in the West enjoy, and this cushioned them through the upheavals of the 1990s. On the negative side, societies with almost no rental sector have great difficulty accommodating people who need to move. A young person seeking work in the capital must buy, at prices that have risen steeply relative to wages, or rent informally from a private owner with no legal protection. Throughout the region young adults remain in the parental home longer than anywhere else in Europe, commonly into their early thirties, and the flat inherited from grandparents has become the main route to independence. The generation that received its housing free now holds it, and their children must wait for it or pay the market price. In this respect the heirs of state socialism have arrived at the same predicament as the property-owning democracies of the West, by the opposite road and in a single step.

#### An assessment of state socialism in housing

The housing systems of the communist states were the most complete attempt ever made to act on the proposition that a home should be a right and not a commodity. They lasted between forty and seventy years, long enough for their effects to be judged, and both their defenders and their critics can find support in the record.

What they achieved is not negligible, and it is worth stating without irony. They eliminated the private landlord and with him the fear of eviction, which had been the central insecurity of working class life in the cities of the old regimes. They reduced the cost of housing to a level at which it ceased to be a burden on any household, however poor. They built, after a long delay, an immense quantity of dwellings, and moved populations numbered in hundreds of millions from barracks, cellars and shared rooms into self contained flats with heating and plumbing. They did so in countries that were, for the most part, much poorer than those of western Europe and that had suffered far worse destruction in war. They avoided, on the whole, the segregation of rich and poor into separate districts, and the estates they built did not become ghettos. Open homelessness of the kind visible in Western cities was rare.

Each of these achievements has a qualification attached. Security of tenure was real for those who had a tenancy. It was accompanied by a system of residence permits that determined where a citizen was allowed to live at all, and that bound peasants to the countryside in the Soviet Union until the 1970s and in China to the present. A person without a permit and a registered address was not housed by the state. He was liable to prosecution, in the Soviet Union under laws against vagrancy and what was termed parasitism, and the absence of visible homelessness owed something to the police. Low rents were a benefit to the occupant and a fatal weakness of the system, since they provided no funds for maintenance and made housing a pure cost to the state, to be minimised whenever other priorities pressed. The equality of the estates was genuine in comparison with the West, but the allocation of the better flats to those with rank and connections created a hierarchy of housing that was less visible and no less real.

The central failure was shortage, and it was not incidental. Every socialist country, without exception, had a waiting list measured in years or decades throughout its existence. The reason follows from the design. Where housing is nearly free, the demand for it is limited only by the rules of entitlement, and every household that qualifies will want as much as the rules allow. Where the supply is determined by the amount the state chooses to invest, and the state has factories, armies and prestige projects competing for the same resources, housing will tend to come last, because unlike a steel mill it produces no revenue. The citizen has no means of expressing the urgency of his need except by petition and patience. The economist János Kornai, who analysed the phenomenon in his own country of Hungary, described socialist systems in general as economies of shortage, and housing was his leading example.

Shortage in turn generated the features of the system that its inhabitants found most demeaning. Since the official channel was slow, people resorted to unofficial ones: the exchange, the bribe, the favour, the fictitious marriage, the cultivation of anyone with influence over the list. Since housing came through the workplace, the employer held a power over the employee far greater than that of a capitalist firm. Since a room in a communal flat might be obtained by the removal of a neighbour, the housing shortage became entangled in the Stalin years with the machinery of denunciation. A system intended to free people from dependence on landlords made them dependent on officials.

The quality and variety of what was built also suffered from the absence of any signal from the occupants. Planners were rewarded for the number of square metres completed, and so they produced a small range of standard types in the largest possible numbers. The dwellings were of uniform design across eleven time zones, whatever the climate or local custom, and finishing was notoriously poor. New residents commonly spent their first months repairing the defects of a flat that was officially complete. None of this was peculiar to socialism, as the system built estates of the West in the same years show, but in the West the dissatisfied could eventually go elsewhere.

It is instructive that in every socialist country the gap left by the state was filled by private effort that the ideology did not acknowledge. Soviet families built their own houses on the edges of towns. Hungarian and Yugoslav workers built at weekends with the help of kin. Chinese peasants housed themselves throughout. Polish parents paid into cooperatives for decades on behalf of their children. A very large share of the population under socialism, a majority in several countries, lived in housing that was in practice privately produced and privately held. The pure model of state provision was never realised anywhere.

The manner of the system's ending supplies a last piece of evidence. When the choice was offered, the people who had lived for two generations as tenants of the state chose, almost universally, to become owners, and no government that offered the alternative of remaining a public tenant found many takers. Supporters of socialism can point out that the choice was hardly a free one, since the flats were offered for nothing in a time of economic collapse, and that the consequences of universal ownership have been troubling. That is true. It remains the case that the attachment of ordinary people to the idea of a home that is securely and transferably theirs proved stronger than seventy years of official teaching to the contrary.

The verdict that seems warranted is a divided one. State socialism demonstrated that a society can remove housing from the market and still house its people, after a fashion, and that doing so confers a real security that market societies have not matched. It also demonstrated that when prices are abolished something else must do their work, and that the something else was administrative discretion, with its queues, its privileges and its indifference to what people wanted. Those who still hold that housing should not be a commodity have for the most part drawn the lesson. They no longer propose that the state should own and allocate everything. They look instead to forms of common ownership on a human scale, controlled by the residents themselves, and it is to these that the final chapter of this part turns.

### Chapter 21: Common Ownership Today

The collapse of state socialism did not end the search for ways of housing people outside the market. It redirected it. Since the 1970s, and with renewed energy since the financial crisis of 2008, those who reject the treatment of housing as a commodity have turned to institutions that are neither private property in the ordinary sense nor property of the state: the cooperative, in which the residents collectively own the building they live in, and the community land trust, in which a local body holds the land permanently and removes it from speculation. Alongside these stand more confrontational practices, the occupation of empty buildings, the rent strike and the demand for expropriation. This chapter examines each in turn.

#### Housing cooperatives

The origins of the housing cooperative in nineteenth century Denmark and Germany were described in Part Two. The essential idea is that the dwellings belong to an association of which the residents are the members. Each member holds a share, has one vote in the general meeting whatever the size of his flat, and occupies a dwelling by virtue of membership. Nobody stands in the position of landlord, and no profit is taken out. Within this definition there is a distinction on which everything else turns, between cooperatives in which the member's share can be sold for whatever the market will pay and those in which its price is restricted.

Zurich is the leading contemporary example of the restricted kind. The city, the largest in Switzerland and one of the most expensive in the world, adopted in 1907 a policy of promoting non-profit housing, and it has pursued it ever since by leasing municipal land to cooperatives on long terms, guaranteeing their loans and taking a small share in their capital. Roughly a fifth of all dwellings in the city now belong to cooperatives, and a further share to the municipality and to foundations. In 2011 the voters approved, by a majority of three to one, an amendment to the city constitution requiring that non-profit housing should make up a third of all rental dwellings by 2050.

A Zurich cooperative charges what is called cost rent, a figure calculated from the interest on its loans, the ground rent, maintenance, administration and a reserve for renewal, and nothing more. In a new building this may be only a little below the market rent. The difference emerges with time. As the loans are paid down and market rents around it rise with the price of land, the cooperative's rent stays tied to its historical costs, and after twenty or thirty years it is typically a third or more below that of comparable private flats, and in older buildings less than half. A member who joins pays for a share, usually a modest sum related to the size of the flat, and receives the same amount back on leaving, without interest or gain. There is consequently nothing to speculate with. Most cooperatives apply an occupancy rule, under which the number of rooms may exceed the number of residents by no more than one, and a household that shrinks is expected to move to a smaller flat within the cooperative. The rule keeps large flats available for families and is the kind of provision that a private landlord could not impose and a private owner would not accept.

The newer Zurich cooperatives have attracted international attention for their architecture and their social ambition. One, opened in 2014 on a site above a tram depot, combines flats of many sizes with shops, a cinema, offices and shared rooms, and its residents undertake not to own cars. Another, built by a consortium of some fifty existing cooperatives on a former industrial site at the edge of the city, houses about twelve hundred people in thirteen buildings and includes cluster apartments in which several small private units share a large kitchen and living room. These projects are in the direct line of the house-commune and the Familistère, with the difference that nobody is obliged to live in them.

Uruguay offers a model adapted to a much poorer society. A housing law of 1968 gave legal recognition to cooperatives of users and provided state loans for them. Two types were established. In one the members contribute savings. In the other, which became the more important, they contribute labour, each household working about twenty hours a week on the building site for the two or three years of construction, under the direction of a technical assistance institute staffed by architects and social workers. The work is organised so that nobody knows which dwelling will be his until all are finished and they are assigned by lot, which ensures that every house is built with equal care. On completion the cooperative owns the buildings and the land as a single undivided property, and each family holds a right of use that is unlimited in time and can be inherited but cannot be sold on the market. A member who leaves receives back the value of his contributions.

The mutual aid cooperatives formed a national federation in 1970, which survived the military dictatorship of 1973 to 1985 as one of the few organisations able to mobilise opposition. In 1984 it collected hundreds of thousands of signatures in a single day against a government attempt to convert the cooperatives into individual private property, and the attempt was abandoned. The federation today comprises several hundred cooperatives housing some thirty thousand families, and since the 1990s it has built in the old centre of Montevideo as well as on the periphery. The model has been taken up, with Uruguayan advice, in Central America, Bolivia and Paraguay. It demonstrates that people with very low incomes can produce good housing collectively if they have access to land, credit and technical help, and that collective ownership can be defended by those who live under it.

The history of the Scandinavian cooperatives supplies the cautionary counterpart. In Sweden the tenant owner cooperative was for decades a non-speculative form of housing, with the price at which a member could sell his share controlled by law. The control was lifted at the end of the 1960s. From that point a share in a cooperative flat in Stockholm could be sold for whatever a buyer would pay, and such shares now change hands for sums equal to the price of a freehold flat elsewhere, financed by mortgages. The form is still called a cooperative. In substance it has become owner occupation, and it has been the vehicle for the conversion of municipal rental blocks described in Part Six. Norway deregulated its large cooperative sector in the 1980s with the same result, and Denmark loosened the price rules for its own cooperatives in ways that produced sharp increases in values in Copenhagen. A cooperative whose members can capture the rise in the value of their homes will sooner or later be treated by them as an investment. The restriction on resale is what makes the difference, and it is the feature that members, once they have benefited from low costs for some years, have the strongest incentive to remove.

Newer movements have accordingly sought legal structures that put the restriction beyond the reach of the residents themselves. In Germany an organisation founded in Freiburg in 1992 links nearly two hundred self managed housing projects in a network. Each building is owned by a company with two shareholders, the association of its residents and the central organisation, and the latter holds a veto over any sale, so that a house once acquired can never return to the market even if all its occupants should wish it. In Catalonia a model introduced from Denmark and Uruguay has taken root under the name of cession of use. The best known example, a timber framed building of twenty-eight flats in the Sants district of Barcelona completed in 2018, stands on land that remains the property of the city and is leased to the cooperative for seventy-five years. The members collectively own the building, pay a monthly charge based on cost, and cannot sell their homes. The city has since made further sites available on the same terms.

Cooperatives of the non-speculative type have clear merits. They give residents the security and control of owners without making them speculators, they keep housing affordable in perpetuity, and they foster a degree of neighbourly cooperation that is rare in other forms of tenure. Their limitations are equally clear. They require founders with time, organisational skill and some capital, which tends to favour the educated middle class. They can be exclusive in choosing their members. And they have grown to a significant share of the housing stock only where public authorities have supported them over many decades with land and credit, which is to say that they flourish as partners of the state and not as substitutes for it.

#### The community land trust

The community land trust is an American invention with an unusual pedigree. It draws on Henry George's doctrine that land values belong to the community, on the garden city of Ebenezer Howard, on the village land gift movement of Gandhi's disciple Vinoba Bhave in India, and on the leasehold system under which a national fund holds land for Jewish settlement in Israel. Its immediate origin lies in the civil rights movement of the American South.

In the rural counties of south western Georgia in the early 1960s, black tenant farmers and sharecroppers who registered to vote or took part in protests were routinely evicted from the land they worked. Activists concluded that political rights would mean little without an economic base that white landowners could not take away. A group that included Slater King, a cousin of Martin Luther King, the organiser Charles Sherrod and the pacifist Robert Swann travelled to Israel in 1968 to study its cooperative agricultural settlements, which stand on land leased from the national fund. On their return they founded New Communities, a non-profit corporation that in 1969 bought nearly six thousand acres of farmland and woodland near the town of Albany, at that time the largest tract of land owned by black Americans in the United States. The plan was for families to lease homesteads from the corporation and to farm the remainder cooperatively. The venture endured hostility from neighbours and from the state government, was repeatedly denied the emergency loans that white farmers received from the federal agriculture department during years of drought, and lost its land to foreclosure in 1985. Twenty-four years later it was awarded twelve million dollars in a settlement of claims of racial discrimination by that department, and used the money to buy a former plantation.

The model that Swann and his colleagues formulated from this experience has three elements. The first is the separation of ownership. A non-profit corporation acquires land and holds it permanently, never to be sold. Buildings on the land are owned by those who use them, whether individual households, cooperatives or businesses, under a ground lease that typically runs for ninety-nine years and can be renewed and inherited. The second is a limit on resale. A homeowner who decides to leave must sell the house either back to the trust or to another household of limited income, at a price fixed by a formula in the lease. Formulas vary, but a common one allows the seller to recover what he paid, plus the value of improvements and a quarter of any increase in the market value of the house. The seller thus builds up some equity, and the home remains affordable to the next buyer without any further subsidy. The third is the structure of control. The board of a classic trust is divided into three equal parts, representing the people who live on its land, residents of the surrounding area, and public officials or others with relevant expertise, so that no single interest can dominate and, in particular, the leaseholders cannot vote to lift the restrictions and sell at full price.

The idea moved from the countryside to the city in the 1980s. Its best known application was in Burlington, the largest town in the state of Vermont, where a socialist mayor, Bernie Sanders, elected in 1981, faced rising house prices and the prospect of displacement in a working class district near the waterfront. His administration provided two hundred thousand dollars in 1984 to establish a land trust. The organisation that grew from it, after a merger, now holds some three thousand homes, about a quarter of them owner occupied houses and flats on leased land and the rest rented apartments and cooperatives, and houses a significant fraction of the town's population. A study of the first two decades of its resales found that homes had become more affordable with each change of owner, that the public subsidy originally invested had been preserved, and that most sellers had left with enough equity to buy on the open market.

A notable case arose in Boston. In a district of the Roxbury area that had been devastated by arson, abandonment and illegal dumping, with a fifth of its land lying vacant, residents formed an organisation in 1984 to plan its recovery. Four years later the city took the exceptional step of delegating to the group's land trust its own legal power to acquire vacant private plots compulsorily. The trust assembled some sixty acres and has developed several hundred permanently affordable homes together with parks, a greenhouse and commercial premises. During the foreclosure crisis after 2008, when surrounding streets saw scores of repossessions, almost none occurred on the trust's land. That experience was repeated nationally. A survey found that owners of homes on community land trusts were several times less likely to be in serious arrears or foreclosure than owners with conventional mortgages, although their incomes were much lower. The trusts had vetted their loans, refused predatory ones and intervened when a household ran into difficulty.

The model has spread abroad. England and Wales have several hundred community land trusts, many of them in villages where local people had been priced out by second home buyers, and a trust in east London has sold flats on a former hospital site at prices linked to local earnings, roughly a third of market value. Brussels established a trust in 2012 with regional funding, the first on the European continent, and others have followed in France, where a legal form introduced in 2014 allows public bodies to hold land and sell long leases on dwellings. The most striking adaptation is in San Juan, Puerto Rico. Along a polluted tidal channel in the centre of the city, some twenty thousand people lived in informal settlements built on mangrove swamp over the course of a century, without title. A plan to dredge the channel would have made their land valuable and their displacement likely. Instead of receiving individual titles, which many would have sold, the residents chose in 2004 to have the land vested in a collective trust that they control, with each family holding a right to its house. The arrangement has been studied by groups in informal settlements from Brazil to Kenya as a way of obtaining security without exposing a community to the market.

The community land trust is, in effect, the garden city reduced to the scale of a neighbourhood and the Georgist principle applied by private agreement instead of by taxation. It allows people of modest means to own a home and to accumulate some savings while ensuring that the rise in the value of the land is retained for the community and the home stays affordable indefinitely. Its supporters see in it a third form of tenure, between owning and renting, that reconciles the individual's desire for a place of his own with the public interest in keeping housing out of speculation.

The limitation is one of scale. After half a century there are about three hundred community land trusts in the United States, holding in total some tens of thousands of homes, in a country with a hundred and forty million dwellings. The trusts must buy land at market prices, which requires grants or donations, and each new home depends on a subsidy. Critics on the right point out that the buyer of a trust home forgoes most of the appreciation that has been the main source of wealth for other American owners, and ask whether it is fair to offer the poor a diluted form of ownership. Critics on the left doubt that an institution reliant on philanthropy and public grants can alter the housing system as a whole. Its advocates reply that the trust was never meant to stand alone, and that where governments have given land and money to it in earnest, as in Burlington, it has shown what a permanently affordable sector could look like.

#### Squatting and occupation

The most direct way of asserting that housing is for use and not for profit is to move into an empty building without asking. Squatting is as old as property. As a deliberate political practice in the cities of the rich world it dates from the years after the Second World War, and it has recurred whenever dwellings stand vacant in the presence of people who need them.

Britain in the summer of 1946 saw the first modern wave. Servicemen had returned to an acute shortage of homes, and the country was dotted with military camps that the forces no longer required. Beginning at a disused anti-aircraft site in Lincolnshire, families simply moved into the huts. Within weeks some forty-five thousand people were occupying more than a thousand camps. The Labour government, embarrassed, decided not to resist, and instructed local authorities to connect water and electricity and to collect rent. It took a different view when, in September, a group organised by Communist Party members led several hundred families into an empty block of luxury flats in Kensington and other buildings in central London. The organisers were prosecuted for conspiracy, the occupiers were persuaded to leave, and the episode ended. The line the government drew, between tolerating the use of abandoned public property and defending private property in the West End, was one that authorities elsewhere would draw again.

The movement revived in the late 1960s, in a period of slum clearance when councils and speculators held thousands of houses empty for years awaiting demolition or redevelopment. Activists in London installed homeless families in vacant council houses, and after the bailiffs hired by one borough were shown on television wrecking the interiors with crowbars to make them uninhabitable, public sympathy forced the councils to negotiate. Many agreed to license the use of empty property for short periods. English law at that time treated squatting as a civil matter between owner and occupier, not a crime, provided entry was made without damage, and by the mid 1970s there were an estimated thirty thousand squatters in London. Some of the housing cooperatives and short life housing associations of the city trace their origins to these occupations.

Amsterdam became the capital of the European movement. The city had a severe shortage, a long waiting list for regulated housing and a great many buildings kept empty by owners speculating on redevelopment. Dutch courts ruled in 1971 that a person who had established a home in a building, as shown by the presence of a bed, a table and a chair, enjoyed the legal protection of the domestic peace, and could not be removed without a court order. Squatters, known in Dutch as krakers, organised themselves by neighbourhood, with consultation hours for the newly homeless, teams skilled in opening doors and telephone trees to summon defenders. They occupied not only houses but warehouses, offices and a newspaper building. When the city set out in 1975 to demolish an old quarter for a metro line and a motorway, their resistance, together with that of residents, led to the abandonment of the motorway and a change in planning policy towards the conservation of old neighbourhoods.

The climax came in 1980. In February the police cleared a squatted house on one of the main canals and the squatters retook it and built barricades, which the authorities removed with armoured vehicles. On the thirtieth of April, the day of the investiture of a new queen, thousands of demonstrators fought the police across the city centre under a slogan that translates as no home, no coronation. The following years brought a degree of accommodation. The municipality bought some two hundred of the occupied buildings and handed them to housing associations to be let to their occupants, and many of the cultural venues of present day Amsterdam began as squats. The legal tolerance was gradually narrowed and ended in 2010, when squatting was made a criminal offence throughout the Netherlands. England and Wales followed in 2012 with respect to residential buildings.

Similar movements arose in Copenhagen, where in 1971 a disused military barracks was occupied and declared a free town, which survived every attempt at closure and was eventually bought by a foundation of its own residents, and in West Berlin, where around 1980 some hundred and sixty tenement buildings in the Kreuzberg district, emptied by a public redevelopment agency that intended to demolish them, were taken over by young people who set about repairing them. They called the practice rehabilitative squatting. After violent confrontations in which a young man was killed, the city legalised most of the occupied houses by granting leases or selling them to cooperatives formed by the occupiers, and abandoned its policy of wholesale demolition in favour of what it termed careful urban renewal. The outcome in Berlin and Amsterdam was thus not only the housing of some thousands of people but a change in the way cities treated their older districts.

In southern Europe and the developing world occupations have been carried out less by a youthful counterculture than by organised movements of the poor. In Spain, where the word for the practice is spelled with a k to signal its political character, the movement of those affected by mortgages, described in Part Ten, responded to the evictions that followed the crash of 2008 by rehousing dispossessed families in empty blocks belonging to banks that had been rescued with public money. It called the campaign its social work, an ironic borrowing of the name of the charitable foundations of the savings banks. The argument was that institutions kept alive by the taxpayer had no moral right to hold thousands of dwellings vacant while evicting the families who had lived in them. The question of occupation has since become one of the most charged in Spanish politics, with conservative parties and the security industry portraying it as a threat to every owner of a holiday flat, and housing groups pointing out that occupation of a person's own home is rare and that the great majority of cases involve empty property owned by banks and funds.

In Brazil a movement of homeless workers has organised since 1997 the occupation of idle land on the edges of the large cities, setting up encampments of thousands of families in a single night and pressing the authorities to expropriate the site and fund housing on it. Other movements have occupied vacant office towers and hotels in the centre of São Paulo, one of which, a former textile company headquarters of twenty-two storeys, housed several hundred families for years. These groups rest their case on the constitution, which provides that property must fulfil a social function, and they argue that a building left empty for speculation has forfeited protection. In South Africa an organisation of shack dwellers formed in Durban in 2005 has resisted evictions and occupied land in defiance of both municipal authorities and the governing party.

Squatting raises in its sharpest form the question that underlies this whole book. The owner has a legal right and the occupier has a need, and the practice is defended or condemned according to which is thought to weigh more. Its opponents hold that respect for property is the foundation of a free and orderly society, that the owner's reasons for keeping a building empty are his own affair, and that to tolerate seizure is to invite it everywhere. Its defenders reply, with Winstanley, the Jubilee and the social function doctrine, that ownership of what one does not use, in the presence of want, is an abuse that the community is not bound to respect. As a means of housing people, squatting has always been marginal and precarious. Its importance has been as a demonstration. It makes visible the coexistence of empty buildings and homeless people, and on several occasions it has forced governments to change their policies towards both.

#### Rent strikes, expropriation and an assessment

Tenants have one collective weapon that corresponds to the strike of the industrial worker, and it has been used at intervals for more than a century. The Buenos Aires strike of 1907 and the Glasgow strike of 1915 have been described in earlier chapters. In New York, a wave of rent strikes between 1917 and 1920, organised by socialist tenant leagues among the Jewish immigrants of the Lower East Side, the Bronx and Brooklyn, led directly to the state's first rent laws. In the Mexican port of Veracruz in 1922 a strike that began among the prostitutes of the tenement district, who burned their mattresses in the street in protest at the charges levied on them, spread under the leadership of an anarchist tailor named Herón Proal to tens of thousands of tenants and lasted for months. In Barcelona in 1931, in the first summer of the Spanish republic, the anarchist trade union federation organised a strike demanding a reduction of rents by two fifths, in which perhaps a hundred thousand people took part before it was broken by arrests. In Harlem in the winter of 1963 the tenants of several hundred rat infested buildings withheld their rent under the leadership of Jesse Gray and won court orders for repairs.

The rent strike is difficult to sustain, since each participant risks eviction and the landlord can usually afford to wait longer than the tenant. It has succeeded mainly as a means of bringing government into a dispute. Almost every major system of rent regulation described in this book was enacted in response to organised refusal to pay, or the fear of it. Tenant unions modelled on labour unions have revived in recent years in Spain, Germany, Ireland, Britain and the United States, and during the pandemic of 2020 the demand to cancel rents was raised in several countries. In Catalonia a tenants' union founded in 2017 has organised collective bargaining with large landlords and, since 2024, strikes against particular funds.

The most ambitious recent attempt to push beyond regulation took place in Berlin. The city had been for decades a place of cheap and plentiful rented housing. In the early 2000s, deep in debt, its government sold a large part of the municipal stock, including in 2004 a company with some sixty-five thousand flats, to private equity funds at low prices. In the following decade the population grew, investors bought heavily, and rents for new lettings doubled. A coalition of Social Democrats, Greens and the Left party responded in February 2020 with a law that went further than anything attempted in western Europe since the 1940s. Known as the rent cap, it froze the rents of about one and a half million dwellings at their level of June 2019 for five years, set upper limits by the age and equipment of the building for new lettings, and from November 2020 obliged landlords to reduce any existing rent that exceeded the limit by more than a fifth. Buildings completed since 2014 were exempt.

The measure lasted fourteen months. In April 2021 the Federal Constitutional Court declared it void from the beginning, on the grounds that the federation had already legislated on rents and that a single state had no power to add its own rules. Tenants who had been paying reduced rents were required to make up the difference. Economists who studied the brief period of its operation found that rents in the regulated part of the market did fall, that the number of flats advertised for rent dropped by something like half as owners sold or held back, and that rents in the exempt newer buildings and in the surrounding towns rose faster. Each side read the results as confirming its expectations.

By then a more radical campaign was under way. A citizens' initiative had discovered in the German constitution an article that had never been used. Article 15 of the Basic Law of 1949, a concession to the socialist left of that time, permits land, natural resources and means of production to be transferred to public ownership for the purpose of socialisation, by a law that provides for compensation. The initiative proposed to apply it to every private company owning more than three thousand dwellings in Berlin, which would bring about two hundred and forty thousand flats into a public institution, with compensation set well below market value on the grounds that the constitution requires only a fair balance of interests. The campaign named itself after the largest of the landlords concerned. It collected the signatures needed to force a vote, and on 26 September 2021 the proposal was approved by fifty-nine per cent of those voting, a larger share than any party obtained in the city election held the same day.

The referendum was not binding. The city government appointed a commission of legal experts, which reported in 2023 that socialisation on the lines proposed would be constitutionally permissible and that compensation below market value could be justified. A new coalition led by the Christian Democrats undertook to prepare a framework law and has shown no haste. Opponents argue that the cost, estimated at anything between eight and thirty-six billion euros depending on the method, would exhaust the city's capacity to borrow without creating a single additional dwelling, and that the same money would build tens of thousands of new flats. Supporters reply that the debt could be serviced from the rents, as with any property purchase, that the object is to remove a large stock permanently from the market, and that the city's own sale of these flats twenty years earlier is what created the problem. Whatever the eventual outcome, a majority of the electorate of a European capital has voted to expropriate large landlords, and that fact has altered the limits of what is considered possible in housing politics.

Barcelona has pursued related aims by less dramatic means. A Catalan law of 2015, which originated in a popular legislative initiative drafted by the mortgage victims' movement, allows the authorities to compel banks and large owners to offer a social rent to occupants facing eviction and to take over the use of dwellings left empty for more than two years. The city administration elected that year, led by a former spokeswoman of the movement, fined banks and funds for holding vacant flats, exercised a right of first refusal to buy buildings coming onto the market, required from 2018 that thirty per cent of the floor space in any large new development or major renovation be reserved for protected housing, and leased municipal land to cooperatives. The measures have added to the public and cooperative stock at a modest rate. Developers say that the thirty per cent rule has discouraged building within the city, and the debate over its effects continues.

It remains to assess the tradition examined in this part as a whole. Its central conviction, that people should not be at the mercy of others' pursuit of profit for something as necessary as a home, has had a longer and wider influence than the fate of the communist states would suggest. The record shows that the conviction can be acted upon in two quite different ways. The first, comprehensive ownership and allocation by the state, housed vast numbers at negligible cost to the occupant and gave them great security. It did so at the price of chronic shortage, of dependence on officials and employers, and of a uniformity that ignored what people wanted, and when its citizens were given the choice they left it. The second, common ownership on a small scale by the residents themselves or by a body answerable to them, has produced some of the most attractive and durable housing described in this book, in Zurich, Montevideo, Copenhagen and Burlington. It has remained small almost everywhere, and it has grown large only where a sympathetic public authority supplied land and finance over generations.

Both experiences point to the same underlying issue. Every one of the successful examples depended on obtaining land at something other than its market price: by municipal lease in Zurich, by state loan in Uruguay, by gift or grant in Vermont, by decree in the socialist countries. And every attempt to extend non-market housing into an existing city has run up against the price of the ground. The thinkers who placed land at the centre of the housing question, and proposed to deal with it directly, are the subject of Part Eight.

## Part Eight: The Land Question

### Chapter 22: Henry George and the Single Tax

Every tradition examined so far has at some point collided with the same obstacle. The market liberal finds that freeing builders does little where landowners can withhold sites. The conservative finds that subsidies to buyers raise the price of plots. The social democrat and the cooperator find that they cannot build without land obtained below its market value. One school of thought made this obstacle its whole subject. It held that the housing question is at bottom a land question, that the value of land is created by the community and wrongly kept by individuals, and that if this one thing were put right most of the rest would follow. Its founder was an American journalist with no formal education in economics, whose book became one of the most widely read works of the nineteenth century, and this part of the book begins with him.

#### The life of a San Francisco printer

Henry George was born in Philadelphia in 1839, the son of a publisher of religious books, and left school before he was fourteen. He went to sea as a foremast boy on a voyage to Australia and India, learned the trade of typesetter, and in 1858 worked his passage to California, drawn by news of gold on the Fraser River. He found none. For the next ten years he lived in San Francisco and Sacramento as a printer, often out of work and at times destitute. On the day his second child was born, in 1865, there was no food in the house, and he later recalled that he went out into the street resolved to get money from the first man who looked as though he had it, and that he was desperate enough to have killed for it. A stranger gave him five dollars.

He turned himself into a writer by will and practice, and rose to become editor of a succession of small California newspapers. The place and the moment formed his thinking. California in the 1860s was a new society whose development could be watched like an experiment. When George arrived, wages were high and land was to be had for little. Within a decade the transcontinental railway was approaching, the population was growing fast, great fortunes were being made, and wages were falling while beggars appeared on the streets of San Francisco. The state's land, moreover, had passed into few hands with extraordinary speed. Enormous Mexican grants had been confirmed to speculators, the railway had received from Congress alternate square miles along its route amounting to millions of acres, and individual holdings of several hundred thousand acres were not unusual.

In 1868 George published an article asking what the railway would bring. His answer was that it would make the rich richer and the poor poorer: those who owned land would gain as its value rose, and those who had only their labour would find it harder to live. Shortly afterwards he visited New York and was struck, as he put it, by the shocking contrast between monstrous wealth and debasing want in the greatest city of the continent. He made a vow to find the cause. The answer, by his own account, came to him one afternoon while he was riding in the hills above Oakland. He stopped to rest his horse and asked a passing teamster, for want of anything better to say, what land was worth thereabouts. The man pointed to some cows grazing in the distance and said that he did not know exactly, but that there was a man over there who would sell some land for a thousand dollars an acre. George wrote that the reason of advancing poverty with advancing wealth came to him like a flash. With the growth of population, land grows in value, and the men who work it must pay more for the privilege.

He developed the idea in a pamphlet of 1871 and then, beginning in 1877 during a severe depression, in a full length book written at night after his day's work. Progress and Poverty was finished in March 1879. New York publishers declined it, and George, who could still set type, helped to produce the plates for a first edition of five hundred copies himself. A commercial publisher then took it on. Sales were slow for a year and then began to climb, helped by cheap editions and by serialisation in newspapers. Within a few years the book had been translated into most European languages and had sold, by the usual estimates, some three million copies, more than any other work on economics up to that time.

The author became a public figure of a kind that is hard to imagine today. He travelled to Ireland in 1881 as a newspaper correspondent during the agitation against the landlords, was twice arrested there by the police, and returned a hero to Irish America. Five lecture tours of Great Britain followed, in which he addressed packed halls in every large town. A young music critic named George Bernard Shaw wandered into one such meeting in London in 1882 and left it, as he said, a convert to the importance of economics. Many of the men who later founded the Independent Labour Party and the Fabian Society traced their political awakening to hearing George or reading him.

In 1886 the trade unions of New York invited him to stand for mayor.
