He ran as the candidate of a united labour party against a wealthy Democrat backed by the political machine and a young Republican named Theodore Roosevelt. The campaign alarmed the propertied classes of the city as nothing had before. George polled sixty-eight thousand votes and came second, ahead of Roosevelt, and his supporters always maintained that he had been counted out. Among his most effective allies was a Catholic priest, Edward McGlynn, who was excommunicated for refusing to cease his advocacy and was reinstated five years later after a review of his views in Rome.

George wrote several more books, among them a defence of free trade and a reply to the Pope, which is discussed below. In 1897, against the advice of his doctors, he agreed to run again for mayor of the newly enlarged city of New York. He died of a stroke four days before the election. His body lay in state while something like a hundred thousand people filed past, and the funeral procession through Manhattan and across the bridge to Brooklyn was compared by the newspapers to that of Lincoln. He had never held public office, taught at a university or possessed any wealth, and for two decades he had been the most influential economic writer in the English speaking world.

#### The argument of Progress and Poverty

The book opens with a puzzle. The nineteenth century, George observes, has seen a prodigious increase in the power to produce wealth. Steam and electricity, machinery, improved methods and the division of labour have multiplied the output of each worker many times over. A person of the previous century, shown these inventions in a vision, would have concluded that poverty was about to be abolished. Yet the opposite has occurred. It is in the oldest and richest communities, where material progress is furthest advanced, that the deepest poverty and the fiercest struggle for existence are found. The tramp comes with the locomotive. The almshouse and the prison are as surely the marks of progress as the costly dwelling and the magnificent church. This association of poverty with progress, George declares, is the great enigma of the times, the riddle that the Sphinx of fate puts to civilisation and that it must answer or be destroyed.

He proceeds by examining and rejecting the explanations then current. One held that wages are paid out of a fixed fund of capital, so that more workers must mean less for each. George replies that wages are not advanced from capital at all. They are drawn from the product of the labour for which they are paid, and the worker creates his wage before he receives it. The other explanation was that of Malthus, that population tends to outrun subsistence. George devotes several chapters to refuting it, arguing that a larger population, far from pressing on resources, makes each person more productive through cooperation and exchange. Twenty men working together where nature is niggardly will produce more than twenty times what one could produce alone where nature is bountiful. The densest populations are the richest.

If neither a shortage of capital nor a surplus of people is the cause, the explanation must lie in the way the product is divided. George adopts Ricardo's law of rent and extends it from farmland to all land. The product of a society is shared among three factors: land, labour and capital, receiving rent, wages and interest. Rent is determined by the advantage of a given site over the least productive site in use, which pays no rent. Wages and interest are determined by what labour and capital can earn on that marginal site. As population grows and the arts advance, inferior and more distant land is brought into use, the margin falls, and the whole of the difference is transferred to the owners of the better land. Material progress therefore raises rent, and since rent is a deduction from the total, it does so at the expense of wages and interest. The landowner, who contributes nothing to production, is the residual beneficiary of every improvement.

George illustrates the process with a parable that became famous. He asks the reader to imagine an unbounded prairie, and a first settler choosing a place on it. One spot is as good as another. He builds a cabin and lives in rude self sufficiency. Others arrive and settle near him, for the sake of company and mutual help. Soon there is a blacksmith, a store, a school, a church. What one family could not do, many do together. A town grows, then a city. The original settler's land has not changed. It is no more fertile than it was. But it now stands at the centre of a population whose combined activity has endowed it with an enormous productive power, and that power attaches not to any person's labour but to the place. Whoever owns the place can charge for access to it. The settler, or the speculator who bought him out, may sit and smoke his pipe, or travel abroad, and without doing a stroke of work will grow rich from what the whole community has made.

To this George added an account of why progress is interrupted by periodic depressions. Because land values are expected to rise, land is bought and held for the rise and not for use. Speculation pushes the price beyond what labour and capital can pay and still earn their normal return. Land that ought to be in use is withheld, workers and capital are shut out from the opportunities to produce, and industry stops, not because too much has been made but because the price of access to the earth has been bid too high. Idle land and idle men stand side by side. George claimed to see the mechanism at work in the vacant lots held by speculators in every growing American city while families crowded into tenements on the plots next door.

The remedy follows from the diagnosis. We must, George writes in the sentence that gave his opponents their text, make land common property. He immediately explains that he does not mean what the words suggest. It is not necessary to confiscate land or to disturb anyone's possession. Private possession is needed to secure to the improver the fruits of his improvement, and it should be left exactly as it is. It is necessary only to confiscate rent. Let individuals keep what they call their land, he writes. Let them continue to buy, sell, bequeath and devise it. We may safely leave them the shell if we take the kernel. The state should collect by taxation the annual rental value of every site, exclusive of the buildings and improvements upon it, and with that revenue it could abolish all other taxes.

This was the proposal that his followers named the single tax, a term George accepted with some reluctance. He claimed for it several advantages, on which the economists of his own and later times have largely agreed. A tax on the value of land cannot be shifted to tenants or consumers, because the supply of land is fixed and its owner is already charging all the market will bear. It cannot reduce the quantity of land. It does not penalise any productive activity, whereas taxes on buildings discourage building, taxes on wages discourage work and taxes on trade discourage exchange. It is cheap to collect and impossible to evade, since land cannot be hidden or moved abroad. And it would make the holding of idle land unprofitable, so that sites would be offered to those who would use them, speculation would cease, wages would rise and building would be stimulated.

It matters for an understanding of George's place among the ideologies that he was not a socialist and regarded himself as completing the liberal programme. He opposed the public ownership of capital, defended competition and free trade, and wished to shrink the functions of government in most respects. What a person makes by his own exertion, including any building he erects, is his by the most sacred of titles, and the state has no right to tax it. What no person made, and what gains its value from the presence of everyone, belongs equally to all. The moral foundation is Locke's theory of property applied with a consistency that Locke had avoided. George closed the book with a passage of religious eloquence in which he foresaw, once the injustice in the ownership of land was removed, a civilisation without poverty in which human powers could at last unfold. He believed he had found not one reform among many but the reform that made the others unnecessary.

#### Disciples and critics

Few books have attracted so distinguished and so varied a following. Leo Tolstoy read Progress and Poverty in the 1880s and spent the last twenty-five years of his life urging its doctrine on his countrymen. He wrote that people do not argue with the teaching of George, they simply do not know it, and that those who become acquainted with it cannot but agree. The hero of his last novel, a landowner seized by remorse, resolves to hand his estates to the peasants on George's plan, with the rent to be paid into a common fund for their own use. Tolstoy pressed the scheme on the tsar's ministers as the only alternative to revolution, without result.

In China, Sun Yat-sen, the leader of the movement that overthrew the imperial dynasty in 1911, encountered George's ideas during his years of exile in London and the United States. He made what he called the equalisation of land rights one of his three principles of the people. Landowners would declare the value of their own land, the state would tax them on that figure and retain the right to buy at it, so that undervaluation would be dangerous, and all future increases in value would belong to the public. Sun said that the teachings of the American would be the basis of his programme of reform. The principle passed, in diluted form, into the constitution and tax law of the republic that his party later established in Taiwan.

In Britain the influence was more diffuse and more practical. The young Winston Churchill, then a Liberal minister, delivered in 1909 a series of speeches that remain among the most effective statements of the case. Roads are made, he said, streets are made, services are improved, electric light turns night into day, water is brought from reservoirs a hundred miles off in the mountains, and all the while the landlord sits still. Every one of those improvements is effected by the labour and cost of other people and the taxpayers. To not one of them does the land monopolist contribute, and yet by every one of them the value of his land is enhanced. Land monopoly, he declared, is not the only monopoly, but it is by far the greatest, a perpetual monopoly and the mother of all other forms. Lloyd George, Asquith and most of the leaders of the early Labour Party accepted some version of the doctrine.

The movement produced its own institutions. Followers in the United States and elsewhere founded single tax clubs by the hundred and elected a number of mayors and members of Congress. Some established colonies to demonstrate the principle. At Fairhope, on the eastern shore of Mobile Bay in Alabama, a group from Iowa bought land in 1894 and leased plots to settlers at an annual rent reassessed according to value, using the proceeds to pay the residents' taxes and provide public services. The colony's corporation still owns several thousand acres on that basis. A similar village was founded at Arden in Delaware in 1900. A Quaker stenographer and single tax enthusiast, Elizabeth Magie, patented in 1904 a board game intended to teach how landlords enrich themselves at the expense of tenants, with a second set of rules under which land rent went into a common pot and all players prospered. A later adaptation that kept only the first set of rules was sold to a games company in the 1930s as Monopoly.

The critics were equally notable, and they came from every direction. Karl Marx read the book soon after it appeared and dismissed it in a letter as the capitalists' last ditch. George, he wrote, understood nothing of surplus value. To leave capital and wage labour in place while handing rent to the state would merely relieve the capitalists of taxation and so strengthen their rule. The proposal had in fact been listed among the transitional measures of the Communist Manifesto, but George presented as a cure for all social ills what was at most one step. George returned the compliment, calling Marx a most superficial thinker and the prince of muddleheads. The socialist objection has remained that landowners are only one species of the possessing class, that in a modern economy the returns to capital are far larger than the rent of land, and that a reform which leaves the employer untouched cannot abolish poverty.

From the opposite side, defenders of property saw in George a confiscator. His doctrine was among the targets of the encyclical Rerum Novarum in 1891. Pope Leo XIII, without naming him, condemned the notion that the land should be treated as common and its fruits alone as private. A man who has spent his labour on a piece of the earth, the Pope argued, has impressed his personality upon it and made it his, and the just rewards of his thrift, if invested in land, are his wages in another form. George answered in an open letter of some length, courteous and firm, in which he argued that the encyclical confused the product of labour, which he agreed was sacred, with the element that no labour had produced, and that the Church in defending private property in land was defending the cause of the very poverty it deplored.

The academic economists raised objections of a more technical kind, and several have never been fully answered. The first concerned justice to existing owners. A person who bought a plot the previous year paid a price that already included the capitalised value of all its expected future rents. To tax those rents away would be to confiscate his savings, though he had done nothing but make a lawful purchase, while the earlier owner who had reaped the unearned gain and sold out would go free. George's reply, that the buyer of stolen goods acquires no title against the true owner and that the slaveholders too had bought in good faith, did not reassure those with property. Mill, as noted earlier, had proposed to take only future increases, and many who sympathised with George preferred that more moderate course.

The second objection concerned practicality. Could assessors reliably separate the value of a site from the value of what stood on it, in the built up centre of a city where no bare land was ever sold? The third concerned adequacy. The American economist Edwin Seligman and others argued that the rent of land, though considerable, would not suffice to meet the growing expenditures of modern governments, so that the tax could not be single. A fourth was theoretical. The economist John Bates Clark, who developed the theory that each factor of production is paid according to its marginal contribution, maintained that land was simply one form of capital and that its return was no more unearned than interest. The assimilation of land into capital became standard in the economics taught in universities through the twentieth century, and some historians of the subject have suggested that it was adopted in part to dispose of George.

The movement declined after the First World War. The introduction of income taxes gave governments another source of revenue aimed at the rich. The motor car and the suburban railway opened vast quantities of cheap land around cities and eased the pressure of urban rents. Most important, the spread of home ownership changed the politics of the question. In George's day the landlord was a visible class and most voters paid rent. By the middle of the twentieth century a majority of households in the English speaking countries owned the ground they lived on and had no wish to see its value taxed. The doctrine that had filled the largest halls in Britain and America passed, within a generation, into the keeping of small societies of the faithful.

#### What modern economics makes of the land tax

The political eclipse of George's movement was not matched by an intellectual one. Economists of the twentieth and twenty-first centuries, examining his central proposal with tools he did not possess, have for the most part endorsed it, and they have done so across the whole range of political opinion.

The reasoning is the one George gave, stated more formally. Almost every tax changes behaviour in ways that reduce output. A tax on wages makes work less rewarding, a tax on profits discourages investment, a tax on a commodity reduces its sale, and the loss to society exceeds the revenue raised. A tax on the unimproved value of land is the exception, because nothing the owner can do alters the amount of land in existence or its location. He cannot produce less of it or move it elsewhere. Nor can he pass the tax on to a tenant, since the rent is already set by what tenants will pay and that does not rise because the landlord's costs have risen. The tax falls on the owner and reduces the price at which the land will sell, by roughly the capitalised value of the future payments. It is, in the language of the subject, a tax without a deadweight loss.

Milton Friedman, the leading advocate of free markets in the second half of the twentieth century, said in an interview in 1978 that in his opinion the least bad tax was the property tax on the unimproved value of land, the Henry George argument of many years ago. William Vickrey, who won the Nobel prize in 1996, called it the best of all taxes on economic grounds and spent much of his career urging cities to adopt it. Robert Solow, Franco Modigliani and James Tobin, all Nobel laureates of a more interventionist cast, joined some thirty other economists in 1990 in an open letter to Mikhail Gorbachev. As the Soviet Union prepared to move to a market economy, they wrote, it should avoid the error of the West and retain land rent as a source of public revenue instead of allowing it to pass into private hands. The advice was not taken.

A result published in the 1970s gave George's intuition an unexpectedly exact form. Joseph Stiglitz, working with Richard Arnott, showed that under certain conditions the total rent of land in a city will equal the total amount spent on the public goods that the city provides. The roads, parks, schools, policing and transport that make a location desirable are reflected in what people will pay to live there, and when a city is of the most efficient size the two sums match. A tax taking the whole of land rent would then be exactly sufficient to pay for local public services, and no other tax would be needed for that purpose. Stiglitz named the proposition the Henry George theorem. It supplies a principle of some elegance: public spending creates land value, and land value can pay for public spending.

Official inquiries have reached similar conclusions. A comprehensive review of the British tax system chaired by the Nobel laureate James Mirrlees reported in 2011 that the economic case for a land value tax was simple and almost undeniable, and asked why it had not been adopted. A review of Australian taxation under the head of the Treasury in 2010 recommended replacing the taxes on property transactions with a broad tax on land. The Organisation for Economic Cooperation and Development has repeatedly found recurrent taxes on immovable property to be the least harmful to growth of all major taxes, and taxes on land the best of these.

Some of the older objections have weakened. The difficulty of valuing land separately from buildings, which seemed formidable in 1900, has been much reduced by computerised records of sales and by statistical methods, and in countries such as Denmark, Australia and New Zealand assessors have produced site valuations routinely for a century. The claim that land rent is too small to matter has been undermined by the rise in land values described in Part Four. In the expensive cities of the rich world, land now accounts for half or more of the value of residential property, and in countries such as Britain it is the largest single component of national wealth. Estimates of total land rent in advanced economies range upward from a twentieth of national income, a sum comparable to the yield of a major tax.

Other objections retain their force. A land tax of any size, introduced without warning, would impose a capital loss on current owners equal to the present value of the tax, and those owners include not only large landlords but tens of millions of households with mortgages secured on the values that would fall. The proposition that the tax would lower house prices, which is its chief attraction for those who do not own, is exactly what makes it alarming to those who do, and to the banks that have lent to them. Economists generally propose to deal with the problem by introducing the tax gradually over several decades, by using the revenue to reduce other taxes that the same households pay, and by allowing owners with low incomes, especially the elderly, to defer payment until the property is sold or inherited. Such arrangements meet the economic objection. Whether they meet the political one is a different matter, taken up in the next chapter.

A further point has been made by economists otherwise sympathetic to George. He believed that land rent was the root of poverty and that its collection would by itself transform society. Modern analysis suggests something more modest. A land tax would raise substantial revenue efficiently, would discourage the holding of vacant and underused sites, would dampen speculative booms by removing part of the expected gain, and would return to the public a share of the value that public investment creates. It would not abolish inequality arising from differences in earnings, education or the ownership of capital, and it would not make housing cheap where the planning system forbids building. It is a good tax, not a panacea.

In recent years the doctrine has enjoyed a modest revival among people far removed from the single tax clubs of the 1890s. Advocates of more building in expensive cities have noticed that George's analysis complements their own. Restrictive zoning creates scarcity, and the gain from scarcity accrues to those who own land in the right places, so that a tax on land value both captures the gain and gives owners a reason to welcome development. Writers and investors in the technology industry have popularised the argument, and a number of American cities have examined the idea. In 2023 the mayor of Detroit proposed to cut the tax on buildings sharply and to raise the tax on land, with the aim of penalising the speculators who hold tens of thousands of vacant lots in that city and rewarding those who build and maintain. The proposal required a change in state law, which the legislature did not pass. The episode is typical of the modern history of the land tax, an idea that nearly every economist commends and that nearly every legislature declines.

### Chapter 23: The Politics of Land Taxation

If the taxation of land values is as sound as economists say, its rarity needs explaining. The explanation is political, and it is best understood through the history of the attempts. This chapter follows the long British struggle over the issue, surveys the countries and cities that have actually taxed land, considers why the idea has so often failed and why ordinary property taxes provoke such resistance, and ends with the indirect methods by which governments have tried to recover for the public some of the value that public decisions create.

#### Britain: from the People's Budget to the betterment levies

The Liberal government that took office in Britain at the end of 1905 depended on an alliance of free traders, nonconformists, trade unionists and Irish nationalists, and almost the only cause on which all of them agreed was hostility to the landlord. Britain was then the most concentrated landed society in Europe. A survey compiled in the 1870s had shown that fewer than seven thousand individuals owned four fifths of the land of the United Kingdom, and the ground beneath the growing cities belonged in large part to a few aristocratic estates. The taxation of land values was demanded by resolutions of hundreds of municipal councils, which wished to levy their local rates on site values, and by a parliamentary group that counted a majority of Liberal members among its supporters.

The occasion came in 1909. The chancellor of the exchequer, David Lloyd George, needed a large additional revenue to pay for old age pensions, introduced the year before, and for new battleships. His budget, presented in April, raised income tax and death duties and added a group of land taxes. There was to be a duty of twenty per cent on the unearned increase in the value of land, payable when it was sold or passed at death, an annual tax of a halfpenny in the pound on the capital value of undeveloped land and minerals, and a duty on the benefit accruing to a landlord at the end of a lease. To make them possible, every parcel of land in the kingdom was to be valued, with the site assessed separately from the buildings, an undertaking that its opponents called a new Domesday.

The sums involved were small. What alarmed the landowners was the valuation, which they correctly saw as the foundation for heavier taxes to come. Lloyd George, the son of a Welsh schoolmaster raised by a shoemaker uncle, took the argument to the country in speeches of calculated insolence. At Limehouse in the East End of London in July he described how land near the docks, once a marsh let for a couple of pounds an acre, now sold for thousands because of the trade of the port and the labour of the people, and asked who had made the difference. He observed that a fully equipped duke cost as much to keep up as two Dreadnoughts, was just as great a terror, and lasted longer. Churchill, then president of the Board of Trade, made the speeches quoted in the last chapter. Liberal meetings sang a hymn written for the campaign, with a chorus declaring that God gave the land to the people.

In November 1909 the House of Lords, in which the hereditary landowners sat, rejected the budget, breaking a convention of two and a half centuries that the upper house did not interfere with finance. Two general elections followed in 1910, fought on the question of peers against people. The Liberals retained office with the support of Labour and the Irish members, the budget was passed, and the Parliament Act of 1911 removed the Lords' power to veto legislation, replacing it with a power of delay. The land question thus produced the most important constitutional change in Britain in the twentieth century.

The land taxes themselves were a failure. The valuation proved enormously laborious, was challenged in the courts and had not been completed when war broke out in 1914. The duties yielded less than the cost of collecting them, and the coalition government repealed them in 1920 and refunded what had been paid. A second attempt by the Labour chancellor Philip Snowden in 1931, a straightforward annual tax of a penny in the pound on site values, was suspended before it took effect and repealed in 1934. No general tax on land values has been enacted in Britain since.

After 1945 the question returned in a different form. The introduction of comprehensive planning control, described in Part Three, meant that the right to develop land was now granted by public authority, and that the grant of permission could multiply the value of a field a hundredfold. The gain, known as betterment, was created by a decision of the community in the most direct sense imaginable, and Labour governments made three attempts to recover it. The Town and Country Planning Act of 1947 imposed a development charge equal to the whole of the increase in value. Owners, left with no incentive to sell, held their land off the market, and the Conservatives abolished the charge in 1953. The Land Commission Act of 1967 levied forty per cent of development value and created a public body to buy and assemble land. It was abolished in 1970. The Community Land Act of 1975 and a development land tax of the following year, at rates up to eighty per cent, aimed to route all development land through local authorities. They were repealed after 1979.

The pattern was the same on each occasion. Landowners and developers knew that the opposition was pledged to repeal, and they had only to wait a few years for a change of government. They withheld land, building slowed, and the measure was then condemned as unworkable. A reform that takes a generation to mature could not survive in a system where the parties alternated every five or six years and disagreed fundamentally about its principle. A further change deepened the problem. An act of 1959 provided that when a public authority bought land compulsorily, it must pay the full market price including the value attributable to the prospect of planning permission. The new towns and council estates of the previous decade had been built on land acquired at close to its existing use value. From then on the public sector had to pay landowners for the value that its own decisions would create.

What exists in Britain today is a negotiated substitute. Since 1990 local authorities have been able to require a developer, as a condition of planning permission, to enter into an agreement to provide affordable housing, roads, schools or cash, and since 2010 they have been able to levy a fixed charge per square metre on new development. These obligations recover a portion of the uplift in land value, estimated at something like a third to a half in favourable cases and much less elsewhere. They are bargained site by site, with developers employing consultants to demonstrate that a scheme cannot bear the contribution, and the amount secured varies greatly with the skill and resolve of the council. Nearly half of all new affordable housing in England in recent years has been obtained through such agreements, which means that the supply of social housing has become dependent on the profitability of private development.

The ordinary British tax on housing is meanwhile further from George's ideal than anything he could have imagined. The council tax, introduced in 1993 after the collapse of the poll tax, is based in England on bands of property value fixed in 1991 and never revised. The highest band pays three times the lowest, although the most expensive homes are worth hundreds of times the cheapest, so that the tax takes a far larger proportion of the value of a modest house in the north than of a mansion in London. No government has dared to carry out a revaluation, for fear of the households that would lose. A country that fought a constitutional crisis over the taxation of land values has ended, a century later, with a property tax that bears almost no relation to them.

#### The places that taxed land

While the mother country of the single tax movement failed to enact it, a number of other jurisdictions did, in forms that range from the nearly pure to the heavily diluted. Their experience is the only practical evidence there is, and it is more extensive than is generally realised.

The earliest and in some ways the most interesting case arose under colonial rule in China. When Germany obtained a lease of the territory around the bay of Kiaochow in 1898 and began to build the port of Qingdao, the naval administration was determined to avoid the frenzy of land speculation that had accompanied the founding of Hong Kong and Shanghai. A young official named Wilhelm Schrameier, who was acquainted with the ideas of the German land reformers, drew up an ordinance. The government bought all the land in the area of the future city from the Chinese villagers at the price prevailing before the occupation, and sold building plots at auction on conditions. Every owner paid an annual tax of six per cent on the assessed value of the land, exclusive of buildings. When a plot was resold, or after twenty-five years without a sale, a third of any increase in its value went to the government. Plots not built upon within a set time were taxed at a rising rate. The city that grew up under these rules was orderly and solvent, and its land revenue paid for a large part of its public works. German administration ended in 1914, but Sun Yat-sen visited Qingdao, invited Schrameier to advise him in Canton, and took the system as a model.

The British settler colonies adopted land taxes for a purpose particular to themselves. In Australia and New Zealand the best land had been taken up in the early years of settlement by a small number of pastoralists holding immense sheep runs, and later arrivals found the country locked against them. Progressive taxes on the unimproved value of land, with an exemption for small holdings, were introduced to break up the great estates, first in South Australia in 1884, in New Zealand in 1891, and by the Commonwealth of Australia in 1910. Local governments in Queensland, New South Wales and New Zealand were authorised or required to levy their rates on site values alone, exempting buildings, and many still do. Australian cities built up with the low density and high rate of owner occupation described elsewhere in this book, and advocates have claimed some credit for the land tax in encouraging the prompt development of suburban plots.

The subsequent history shows how such a tax erodes. The federal land tax was abolished in 1952. The Australian states retain land taxes, but each exempts the owner's principal residence, which removes most residential land from the base and confines the tax to investors and businesses. New Zealand abolished its national land tax in 1992. One Australian jurisdiction has moved in the opposite direction. The Australian Capital Territory began in 2012 to phase out its tax on property transactions over twenty years and to replace it with higher annual rates on land values, the only example in the developed world of a government carrying out the reform that economists recommend, and it has persisted through several elections.

Denmark has the longest continuous experience in Europe. Danish smallholders, influenced by George through the folk high schools, pressed for land value taxation in the early years of the century, and after an experimental valuation the state introduced a national tax on land values in 1922, with municipal land taxes following. A small party founded on Georgist principles entered a coalition government in 1957 and secured a tax on increases in land value, and its supporters point out that the three years of that government saw low inflation, a balance of payments surplus and a halt to land speculation. The party lost its seats in 1960 and the measure was repealed. Denmark still levies a municipal tax on land values, separately assessed from buildings, at rates of between one and a half and three per cent, alongside a tax on property values, although both have been subject to freezes and ceilings since the early 2000s that have much reduced their effect.

In the United States the single tax took hold in one state. Pennsylvania permitted its cities from 1913 to tax land at a higher rate than buildings, and Pittsburgh and Scranton did so, setting the rate on land at twice that on improvements. In 1979 and 1980 Pittsburgh, faced with the collapse of its steel industry and a need for revenue, raised the land rate to more than five times the rate on buildings. A study by the economists Wallace Oates and Robert Schwab found that in the following decade the city experienced a boom in commercial construction that was not matched in comparable cities of the region, and concluded that the tax structure, while not the cause of the boom, had allowed the city to raise the money it needed without discouraging building. The system was abandoned in 2001 after a botched reassessment of property produced large and unpredictable changes in tax bills and a revolt of homeowners. About fifteen smaller Pennsylvania municipalities continue to use the split rate. The state capital, Harrisburg, which adopted it in 1975 when a large part of its centre stood vacant, attributes to it a share of the subsequent recovery.

In Canada the cities of the western provinces went further for a time. Vancouver, in 1910, under a mayor elected on a single tax platform, exempted buildings from taxation altogether, and Edmonton, Calgary and other prairie towns followed. A building boom ensued, which enthusiasts credited to the policy. When the boom collapsed in 1913 and land values fell, the cities found themselves with insufficient revenue and thousands of plots forfeited for unpaid taxes, and they gradually restored the taxation of buildings. The experience illustrated a weakness of reliance on land values alone: revenue rises and falls with a speculative market.

Other examples are scattered across the world. Johannesburg and several South African cities rated site values exclusively from the early twentieth century until a national law of 2004 required the inclusion of buildings. Jamaica moved its property tax to an unimproved value basis from the 1950s, and Kenya and other former British territories in Africa inherited site value rating. Taiwan levies a progressive annual tax on land value and a separate tax on the increment at sale, in accordance with Sun's principles, although assessed values lag far behind market prices. Japan introduced a national land value tax in 1992 in reaction to the bubble and suspended it six years later as prices fell. South Korea legislated in 1989 a set of measures based on what was called the public concept of land, including a tax on excessive holdings of residential land in the big cities, parts of which were later struck down by its constitutional court.

Estonia is the purest contemporary case. When the country recovered its independence in 1991, land had no private owners, having been nationalised under Soviet rule, and there was therefore no vested interest to oppose a tax upon it. A land tax was introduced in 1993 as the only recurrent tax on property. It is levied on the assessed value of land alone, at rates set by each municipality within a range that now runs from a fraction of one per cent to two and a half per cent, and all the revenue goes to local government. Buildings are not taxed at all. The system is cheap to administer and has attracted little controversy, though the land under an owner's home was exempted up to a generous limit in 2013 and valuations were left unrevised for two decades. It is perhaps significant that the cleanest example of George's tax was adopted in the one place where, at the moment of decision, nobody yet owned the land.

#### Why the land tax is rare

The record just reviewed displays a regularity. Land taxes have been enacted where landowners were a small and unpopular minority, as in the settler colonies of the 1890s, or where there were no private owners yet, as in Qingdao and Estonia. They have been diluted or repealed as ownership spread. Where a majority of voters own the land under their homes, no pure tax on land values has ever been introduced and survived. To understand why, it helps to look at the politics of the ordinary property tax, of which the land tax would be a more rigorous version.

Taxes on property are, by the common finding of opinion surveys in many countries, the most disliked of all taxes, more so than income or sales taxes that take far more money. Several features account for this. The tax is highly visible. It arrives as a bill for a large sum, to be paid directly by the householder, whereas income tax is deducted before wages are received and sales tax is hidden in prices. It is levied on an asset that yields no cash, so that a household whose home has risen in value owes more without having any more money with which to pay. The elderly owner on a fixed pension, living in a house bought decades ago in a district that has since become fashionable, is the figure invoked in every campaign against property taxation, and her predicament is real. The tax also depends on valuations made by officials, which are easy to dispute, and a general revaluation after a long interval produces abrupt changes that create losers who are angrier than the winners are grateful.

The classic revolt occurred in California. During the inflation of the 1970s house prices in the state doubled and tripled within a few years, assessments followed, and tax bills rose correspondingly while the state government sat on a large surplus. A retired manufacturer of household appliances named Howard Jarvis, who had campaigned against taxes for fifteen years without success, gathered more than a million signatures for a ballot measure. Proposition 13, approved by nearly two thirds of voters in June 1978, limited the tax on any property to one per cent of its value, rolled assessments back to the level of 1975, and provided that the assessed value could thereafter rise by no more than two per cent a year for as long as the property remained in the same hands. Only on a sale would it be reassessed at the current market price. A later amendment allowed parents to pass their low assessment to their children.

The consequences, which have been studied for more than four decades, run directly counter to everything George proposed. The longer an owner holds a property, the lower his tax in relation to its value, so that two identical neighbouring houses may carry tax bills differing by a factor of ten or more according to when each was bought. Holding land is rewarded and selling it is penalised. Owners of vacant plots and underused commercial sites acquired long ago pay almost nothing and are under no pressure to develop. Elderly couples remain in large houses because moving to a smaller one would multiply their tax. Young buyers subsidise their established neighbours. Local governments, deprived of their main independent source of revenue, turned to fees on new development and came to favour shopping centres, which generate sales tax, over housing, which generates costs. Many economists regard the measure as one cause of the state's housing shortage. An attempt in 2020 to remove its protection from large commercial properties alone was narrowly defeated at the polls. The measure remains, in the phrase used of it, the third rail of California politics.

Britain supplied a different lesson. Local government there had been financed since the seventeenth century by rates, a tax on the rental value of property. Margaret Thatcher disliked the rates, which fell on householders in proportion to the size of their homes regardless of how many adults lived in them, and in 1990 she replaced them in England and Wales with a flat charge payable by every adult resident, the same for a duke as for a dustman. The community charge, universally known as the poll tax, provoked widespread refusal to pay and a riot in central London, contributed to her removal from office within the year, and was abolished by her successor. The council tax that took its place, with its bands fixed at the values of 1991, was designed above all to avoid giving offence, and its defects, described earlier, are the price of that caution.

Elsewhere the retreat has been general. Ireland abolished domestic rates in 1977 after a competition of promises between the parties at a general election, and reintroduced a modest property tax only in 2013 under pressure from its international creditors. Italy abolished the property tax on primary residences in 2008, restored it in the fiscal emergency of 2012 and abolished it again shortly afterwards. France completed in 2023 the abolition of its residence tax on main homes. In Germany the valuations on which the property tax was based dated in the western states from 1964 and in the eastern from 1935, until the constitutional court declared them unlawful in 2018 and forced a reform, in which one state, Baden-Württemberg, chose to tax land values alone. In Spain the municipal property tax is levied at low rates on cadastral values well below market prices, and a separate municipal tax on the increase in the value of urban land at the time of sale, a distant relative of the levies of 1909, was struck down in its then form by the constitutional court in 2021 for taxing gains that had not in fact occurred.

The political arithmetic is simple enough. A land or property tax imposes a large, visible and certain cost on a majority of voters, who own homes, in return for benefits that are diffuse, uncertain and spread over the future, accruing chiefly to those who do not yet own and to the economy at large. The losers know who they are and will vote on the issue. The gainers, many of them young, mobile or not yet born, are harder to mobilise. Owners of valuable land are also disproportionately represented among those who finance parties and control the press. George and Lloyd George could set the people against the dukes because the people paid rent to the dukes. Once the people had bought the land, the same proposal set the government against the people.

Reformers have proposed ways around the obstacle. One is to make the change revenue neutral and visibly so, by abolishing an even more unpopular tax in exchange, such as the tax on property transactions, as the Australian Capital Territory has done. Another is to allow any owner to defer the tax, with interest, as a charge against the property to be settled on sale or death, which disposes of the case of the pensioner without cash. A third is to start with categories that command little sympathy, such as vacant building land, derelict sites and second homes, and several countries, including Ireland and France, now tax these at penal rates. A fourth is to introduce the tax at a very low rate and raise it by small steps over decades. None of these devices alters the underlying fact. In a society of homeowners, the proposition that land values belong to the community is a proposition that most voters' savings belong to the community, and it will be received accordingly.

#### Other ways of capturing land value

Where a general tax on land has proved impossible, governments have fallen back on narrower instruments that recover part of the increase in value at the moment and place where a specific public action creates it. The principle is the same. A new railway line, a road, a sewer or a decision to permit building makes particular plots more valuable, and their owners can be asked to contribute. Such measures are known collectively as land value capture, and they are easier to enact than a land tax because they fall on owners who have just received a visible windfall, and usually on developers and not on households.

Latin America has been the most inventive region. Colombia has levied since 1921 a charge on properties that benefit from public works, apportioning the cost of a road or drainage scheme among the owners in the zone of benefit according to the gain each receives. Bogotá and Medellín have financed a large part of their street networks in this manner, and in some years the charge has yielded more than the ordinary property tax. A law of 1997, founded on a clause of the Colombian constitution of 1991 that guarantees the public a share in the increased land values generated by urban development, went further. It entitles municipalities to between thirty and fifty per cent of the rise in the price of land that results from a planning decision, such as the conversion of rural land to urban use or the authorisation of greater height. Collection has been uneven, but the law states the Georgist principle more plainly than any statute in Europe.

Brazil developed a related idea under the name of created land. Planners in São Paulo argued in the 1970s that the right to build should be distinguished from the ownership of a plot. Every owner is entitled to build up to a basic ratio of floor space to site area. Any right to build beyond that, up to the maximum the plan allows, does not arise from the land and is not the owner's by right. It is additional ground created in the air by the public decision to permit density, and the owner must buy it from the city. The principle was written into the national City Statute of 2001. São Paulo has applied it in two ways: by a charge on building rights above the basic ratio across the city, and, in districts designated for redevelopment, by auctioning certificates on the stock exchange, each of which entitles the holder to a fixed quantity of additional floor area. The certificates sold for the renewal of two business districts raised several billion dollars, which paid for roads, a bridge, a metro extension and the rehousing of some of the residents of adjacent favelas. Critics observe that the money has been spent largely within the same wealthy districts and that the mechanism gives the city an interest in the very property boom it is taxing.

East Asia has relied on combining transport with development. In Japan the private railway companies that built commuter lines out of Tokyo and Osaka in the early twentieth century bought farmland along their routes before construction, built housing estates, department stores and amusement parks around the stations, and earned more from property than from fares. The companies thus captured for themselves the land value their railways created, which is why Japanese suburban railways are profitable without subsidy. Hong Kong's metro corporation operates on the same model with public backing. The government grants it development rights above and around new stations at the price the land would have fetched without the railway, and the corporation, in partnership with developers, builds towers and shopping centres and keeps the uplift. The method has financed one of the best urban railways in the world at no cost to the taxpayer.

Japan and Germany also make extensive use of a technique for developing the urban fringe without buying land at all. Under land readjustment, the owners of irregular agricultural plots in an area scheduled for building pool their holdings. The authority lays out roads, parks and drains, and returns to each owner a serviced building plot smaller than his original holding but worth considerably more. The land deducted, typically about a third, provides the sites for public facilities and a reserve that is sold to cover the cost of the works. Roughly a third of the urban area of Japan has been developed in this way. Owners accept it because they gain, and the public obtains its infrastructure and its share of the increment without expropriation or expenditure.

Several European cities have used their planning powers to similar effect. Munich adopted in 1994 a set of rules under which a landowner who benefits from a new zoning plan must bear the costs it occasions, including roads, green space, nursery and school places, and must devote a fixed proportion of the housing to subsidised dwellings, up to the point where at least a third of the increase in the value of his land remains with him. The Vienna zoning rule of 2018, described in Part Six, operates on the price of land directly by fixing what the majority of a rezoned site may be used for. In the Netherlands municipalities traditionally bought agricultural land themselves ahead of development, serviced it and sold the plots, keeping the whole gain for the public account, and for much of the twentieth century this was the normal way in which Dutch towns expanded.

Spain has had since its first planning law of 1956 a system with some of these features. Land classified for development is grouped into units within which the owners share the benefits and burdens equally, however the plan distributes the buildings and the open spaces among their individual plots. The owners must cede without payment the land needed for streets, parks and public facilities, must pay for the infrastructure, and must hand over to the municipality a percentage of the permitted building rights, which has varied between ten and fifteen per cent, free of charge. The municipality is supposed to hold the land so obtained as a public patrimony for subsidised housing. In practice many town halls, short of revenue, have sold it to the highest bidder, and during the boom the system gave them a financial interest in classifying as much land as possible for development. The constitutional clause that the community shall share in the surplus value generated by planning, quoted earlier in this book, describes this arrangement.

American cities use a device called tax increment financing, under which the additional property tax expected from a redevelopment area is pledged in advance to repay bonds issued for the public works that make the redevelopment possible. They also levy impact fees on new construction to pay for schools and roads, and many require that a proportion of units in new residential buildings be let or sold below market price, a practice known as inclusionary zoning.

These instruments have real achievements to their credit, and they share certain limitations. They capture value only from new development, leaving untouched the far larger stock of land that has appreciated without any change of use, so that the owner of an existing house in a district enriched by a new metro line contributes nothing. They are levied on the act of building, which is the activity the tax system ought to encourage, and where they are set too high they can deter it or be passed on in the price of new homes. They depend on negotiation, which rewards the better advised party. And they tend to make public authorities partners in rising land values. Supporters accept these points and answer that an imperfect method that can be enacted is worth more than a perfect one that cannot. Land value capture is Georgism adapted to a world of homeowners: the unearned increment is collected at the edges, from developers and at moments of change, because it cannot be collected at the centre.

### Chapter 24: Public Land

There is an alternative to taxing the value of land, which is for the community to own it. If a city, a trust or the state holds the freehold and lets sites on lease, the ground rent comes to the public as of right, and the increase in value that accompanies growth belongs to the public without any need for assessment or collection. George himself thought the two methods equivalent in principle and preferred the tax only because it avoided disturbing existing titles. A number of places have taken the other course, some from conviction and others from the accident of their founding. This chapter considers how they have fared.

#### Garden cities and new towns

Ebenezer Howard's proposal, described in Part Two, was to build new towns on cheap agricultural land held in trust, so that the rising ground rents would repay the cost of construction and thereafter finance the community's services. Letchworth, begun in 1903, was the demonstration. Its later history tested the idea in ways Howard had not foreseen.

The company that built Letchworth was a commercial enterprise with a limited dividend. Its shareholders were to receive no more than five per cent, and any surplus was to be applied for the benefit of the town. The early decades were difficult, capital was short, and no dividend at all was paid for many years. By the 1950s, however, the town was complete and the estate, comprising the freehold of nearly every factory, shop and house plot, had become very valuable. The limitation on dividends was a provision of the company's articles, which the shareholders could change. In 1960 a property company began buying the shares with a view to removing the restriction and realising the assets. Howard's scheme was about to end in the capture of the accumulated land value by a speculator, the very outcome it had been designed to prevent.

The residents and the local council appealed to Parliament, which passed an act in 1962 transferring the estate to a public corporation. That body was in turn replaced in 1995 by a charitable foundation, governed by elected and appointed representatives of the town, which today owns property worth several hundred million pounds. Its income of several million pounds a year, after expenses, is spent in Letchworth on a cinema, a museum, a hospital day unit, a community transport service, grants to local groups and the maintenance of the surrounding farmland. The town of some thirty-five thousand people thus enjoys a private endowment derived from the rise in the value of its own ground, exactly as Howard intended. The episode shows that his mechanism works. It also shows how exposed such an arrangement is unless the ownership is placed beyond the reach of those who might profit by dissolving it.

The British new towns programme applied the method on a national scale under state ownership. The New Towns Act of 1946 empowered the government to designate an area and to establish for it a development corporation with powers to acquire all the land, compulsorily if need be, at a price that reflected its existing use, generally as farmland, and not the value it would have once the town was built. The corporation borrowed from the Treasury over sixty years, laid out the town, built most of the housing for rent and the factories and shops for lease, and became the freeholder of the whole. Thirty-two towns were founded between 1946 and 1970, from Stevenage and Harlow in the first group to Milton Keynes, planned for a quarter of a million people, in the last. They now house close to three million.

The finances bore out Howard's arithmetic. Because the land had been bought cheaply and the corporations retained the commercial centres and industrial estates, whose rents rose with the growth of the towns, the earlier new towns repaid their Treasury loans with interest and went on to produce a surplus. The programme, taken as a whole, was one of the few great public works to have paid for itself. The question was who should enjoy the surplus. Howard's answer was the town. The government's answer was the Treasury. From 1961 the assets of completed towns were vested in a national commission, and after 1979 that body was instructed to sell them. Shopping centres, office blocks, industrial estates and building land were disposed of to pension funds and property companies over the following two decades, and the rented housing was transferred to local councils, where much of it was then bought by its tenants. The communities that had created the value were left with the liabilities of ageing infrastructure and no endowment with which to meet them.

One exception indicates what might have been done. When the development corporation of Milton Keynes was wound up in 1992, it endowed a charitable trust with a portfolio of commercial property and transferred to it the ownership of the town's parks, lakes and landscaped road verges, some two thousand hectares in all. The income from the property pays for the upkeep of the green space in perpetuity, without recourse to local taxation, and Milton Keynes has parkland of a quality and extent unmatched in English towns of its size.

The garden city idea travelled widely, usually shedding its financial core on the way. Germany built a garden suburb at Hellerau near Dresden before the First World War, on land held by a cooperative society. In the United States a group of planners inspired by Howard built Radburn in New Jersey in 1929, famous for separating pedestrians from motor traffic, as an ordinary speculative development, and the Depression bankrupted it. The federal government built three greenbelt towns in the 1930s as a New Deal experiment, owning the land and letting the houses, and Congress ordered them sold after the war. In one of them, in Maryland, the residents formed a cooperative and bought their homes collectively, and it survives in that form. Finland's Tapiola, near Helsinki, built in the 1950s by a non-profit foundation of trade unions and welfare organisations on land bought as a single estate, came nearer to the original in spirit. Canberra and the towns of the Israeli national land fund, considered below, applied the leasehold principle on a larger scale.

The lessons of this history are fairly clear. Where a single body acquires the land for a new settlement at a price unrelated to its future use, the rise in value will pay for the infrastructure and leave something over. The method is financially sound, and it has been proved repeatedly. Its difficulties are political and arise after success. The accumulated value is a standing temptation, to shareholders, to national treasuries and to the residents themselves, who may prefer to own their freeholds outright. A community that wishes to keep its land value for common purposes must lock it up in a trust or foundation that none of these parties can open. Britain created that value on the largest scale of any country and then, with few exceptions, gave it away.

#### The leasehold cities

A handful of cities and states have kept the freehold of all or most of their land in public hands over a long period and granted only leases to private users. They offer the nearest thing available to an experiment in the common ownership of urban land within a market economy, and their results differ so widely that the comparison is instructive.

Canberra was founded on the principle deliberately. When the Australian colonies federated in 1901 and resolved to build a new capital, the influence of Henry George on the labour movement was at its height. The minister responsible, King O'Malley, an American born enthusiast for the single tax, insisted that no land in the federal territory should ever be sold. The Commonwealth acquired the whole site from 1911, and from 1924 it auctioned leases of ninety-nine years, the lessee paying an annual rent of five per cent of the unimproved value, to be reappraised every twenty years. The rising value of the capital's land was to finance the capital. The system operated as intended for some decades, but the periodic reappraisals of rent were resented by leaseholders, who had come to regard their plots as their property, and successive governments shrank from enforcing them. In 1971, shortly before an election, the federal government abolished land rent for residential leases altogether. Lessees were left with what amounted to freehold, acquired for the price of a lease, and the territory lost the revenue for which the system had been created. Canberra remains nominally leasehold, and the government still sells new sites, but the Georgist experiment at its centre lasted less than fifty years.

Hong Kong has maintained public ownership of land longer than anywhere. When Britain took possession of the island in 1841, the administrator ruled that land would be held from the Crown on lease, and with the single exception of the site of the Anglican cathedral no freehold has ever been granted. The government disposes of land by auction or tender to the highest bidder, who pays a large capital sum, known as the premium, for a lease that now runs for fifty years, together with a small annual rent. A change in the permitted use of a site requires a further premium. This income, with the rates and the taxes on property transactions, has at times supplied a third or more of the government's revenue, and it is the reason Hong Kong has been able to maintain some of the lowest rates of income and profits tax in the world, with no sales tax at all.

The arrangement has a consequence that George did not anticipate. A government that depends on selling leases for the best price has a direct financial interest in keeping that price high, and it controls the supply. Hong Kong has developed only about a quarter of its territory, and its administrations have released land sparingly, a practice widely described as a high land price policy. The premium system favours a few very large development companies with the capital to bid, and four or five family controlled groups dominate the market in new housing. The result is the most expensive housing in the world relative to incomes. A median priced flat costs something like twenty times the median annual household income, new private flats of fifteen or twenty square metres are sold as a matter of course, and at the bottom of the market more than two hundred thousand people live in subdivided units, including the notorious caged bed spaces. The same government, as described elsewhere in this book, houses nearly half the population in public rental and subsidised ownership flats. Public land ownership in Hong Kong has thus produced both a large public housing sector and an extreme private market, and its low taxes are in effect paid for by home buyers through the price of land.

Singapore began from a different starting point and reached a different result. At self government in 1959 the state owned less than half the island. The new government regarded the acquisition of land as the precondition of everything it intended to do. The Land Acquisition Act of 1966 empowered it to take any land required for a public purpose, which was defined broadly to include housing and industrial development, and from 1973 compensation was fixed at the market value as at a statutory date in the past or at the date of acquisition, whichever was lower, with no allowance for potential development. Lee Kuan Yew explained the principle without embarrassment. He saw no reason, he later wrote, why private landowners should profit from an increase in land value brought about by economic development and infrastructure paid for with public funds. Over the following two decades the state's share of the land rose to about nine tenths. Compensation was returned to market value in 2007, by which time the task was complete.

The land so acquired was used primarily to build, and the state housing agency obtained sites at a low transfer price. Most Singaporeans, as already described, own flats on ninety-nine year leases that the state built on its own land, and when a lease expires the flat returns to the state. Land for private development is sold by tender on leases of the same length, and the proceeds are paid by law into the national reserves and may not be spent by the government of the day, which removes the temptation that operates in Hong Kong. Singapore applies the logic of public land ownership more rigorously than any other state. It is able to do so because of the continuous rule of a single party with wide powers and little patience for opposition, a circumstance that limits the lesson for others.

Several northern European cities pursued municipal land ownership within democratic systems. Stockholm began buying farms and estates beyond its boundaries in 1904 and continued for sixty years, until it owned an area several times that of the built up city, including the sites of the satellite suburbs it later constructed along its underground railway. It let building land on long leases under a law of 1907, at rents revised periodically. The policy allowed the planned and orderly development for which the city became known, at land costs far below those of other capitals. In recent decades the city has sold many of its leaseholds to their occupiers. Amsterdam adopted leasehold for all municipal land in 1896, and about four fifths of the city stands on ground owned by the municipality, the rent being readjusted every fifty or seventy-five years in line with land values. Those readjustments, falling due on large numbers of homes at a time of soaring prices, produced such alarm among homeowners that the city in 2017 offered lessees the option of converting to a perpetual lease with the rent fixed or bought off for ever. Helsinki owns about two thirds of the land within its boundaries and uses the position, as noted earlier, to dictate the mix of tenures in new districts.

Israel represents the national version. The Jewish National Fund, established in 1901 to buy land in Palestine for Jewish settlement, laid down from the first that its land should be the inalienable possession of the Jewish people and should be leased, never sold, a rule that its founders derived from the passage of Leviticus quoted in the first chapter of this book. After 1948 the state took over the lands of the departed and expelled Arab population, and a basic law of 1960 provided that the lands of the state and the fund, together about ninety-three per cent of the country, should not be transferred in ownership. Urban plots were let on leases of forty-nine years. Here too the principle has been worn down by the wishes of the lessees. Reforms since the 1990s have extended leases automatically, capitalised the rents into single payments, and from 2009 allowed the transfer of full ownership to the holders of urban residential leases. The system also embodied from the outset an exclusion, since the land held in the name of the Jewish people was not available on equal terms to the Arab citizens of the state, a matter that has been litigated before its supreme court.

#### Does public ownership of land deliver?

The cases just described, together with that of China considered in Part Four, allow a judgment on a question that land reformers have often treated as settled by definition. If the community owns the land, it has been assumed, the community will benefit from its value and housing will be affordable. The evidence shows that the first does not guarantee the second.

Public ownership of land is found alongside the most affordable housing in the developed world and the least. Singapore, Vienna and Helsinki, where public bodies hold much or most of the land, house their populations well and at moderate cost. Hong Kong and the great cities of China, where the state owns all of it, have the highest ratios of house prices to incomes ever recorded. The explanation of the difference lies in what the public owner does with its position. A landowner, public or private, can pursue either of two objects. It can seek the greatest revenue, in which case it will release sites slowly, sell to the highest bidder and welcome rising prices. Or it can seek the greatest supply of good housing at low cost, in which case it will make land available in quantity at prices below what the market would pay and accept a smaller financial return. The governments of Hong Kong and of Chinese municipalities chose the first course, because their budgets depended on it. Those of Singapore and Vienna chose the second.

The fiscal structure largely determines the choice. Where the proceeds of land go into the current budget of the authority that controls the supply, as in Hong Kong and in China after the tax reform of 1994, the authority behaves as a monopolist and becomes dependent on a rising market. Local officials in China were for two decades the country's most active promoters of high land prices. Where the proceeds are placed out of reach, as in Singapore, or where the authority does not sell for profit at all but supplies sites at a set price to non-profit builders, as in Vienna, the incentive is absent. George supposed that public collection of land rent would end speculation. He did not consider the possibility that the public collector might become the chief speculator.

A second finding concerns the durability of leasehold. In Canberra, Amsterdam, Stockholm and Israel, the people who held leases of public land came in time to think of themselves as owners, organised as a political interest, and obtained the abolition of ground rent, the fixing of rents for ever or the transfer of the freehold. The pattern is the same as that of the tenants who bought their council houses in Britain and the residents who privatised their flats in eastern Europe. A lease held by a voter tends to turn into a freehold. The public owner faces a succession of moments, each time rents fall due for revision, at which the gain to lessees from a concession is concentrated and immediate and the loss to the public is dispersed over the future. Systems that survive are those in which the leaseholder is a company or association and not an individual household, or in which, as in Singapore, the government is strong enough to refuse.

A third point is that public land has frequently been treated as a store of cash. In Britain, by the estimate of the geographer Brett Christophers, public bodies have sold about two million hectares since 1979, close to a tenth of the land area of the country, including the sites of council estates, hospitals, military bases, railway yards and school playing fields, in what he describes as the largest privatisation of all and the least noticed. Governments instructed public agencies to obtain the best price, so that land which might have been used for social housing was sold for luxury flats in order to reduce a deficit for a single year. Spanish practice has been similar. Municipalities and state bodies holding former barracks, railway land and the plots ceded by developers have regularly auctioned them to the highest bidder, and one of the largest redevelopment schemes in Europe, on railway land in the north of Madrid, was planned for many years chiefly as a means of extracting value for the public railway company. The creation in 2025 of a state housing company charged with retaining public land and building rental homes on it marked an attempt to change course.

There are also dangers of a familiar kind in concentrating the control of land in official hands. Where permission and price are settled by administrative decision, those who want land have reason to cultivate those who dispose of it. The allocation of land by Chinese local governments has been a leading source of corruption prosecutions in that country, and the relationship between the Hong Kong administration and the major developers has long been criticised. Compulsory acquisition at below market value, the method by which Singapore and the Chinese cities assembled their holdings, is an exercise of state power against individuals, many of them farmers and small owners, that would be unacceptable in most democracies and has been the cause of widespread protest in rural China.

None of this refutes the case for public land. It qualifies it. The cities that have used land ownership well have certain features in common. They acquired land early, before development raised its price, and went on acquiring it steadily. They treated it as an instrument of housing and planning policy and not as a source of revenue for the ordinary budget. They leased or sold it on conditions, at prices related to the use they wished to see, to bodies that would hold the buildings for the long term. And they had the political continuity to maintain the policy for generations. Where these conditions are met, public ownership gives a city a degree of control over its development and over the cost of housing that no system of taxation or regulation can equal. Where they are not, it can reproduce every vice of private landlordism with the addition of the powers of the state.

#### An assessment of the land question

The tradition examined in this part is unusual among those in this book in that its diagnosis has been confirmed more thoroughly than its authors could have hoped and its remedy adopted less than almost any other.

On the diagnosis the evidence is now very strong. The long run studies of house prices cited in Part Four found that the cost of building, adjusted for inflation, has changed little over a century and a half, and that the great rise in the price of housing since the middle of the twentieth century is almost entirely a rise in the price of land. The same dwelling costs several times as much in one city as in another, and the difference lies in the ground. The gains have gone to those who held land in the places that became desirable, in proportion to what they held and when they acquired it, and without reference to any effort of theirs. Every statement in that description would have been recognised by Ricardo, Mill and George as a restatement of their theory of rent. When commentators today speak of an affordability crisis, of the wealth gap between generations or of the advantage of those who got on the ladder early, they are describing the distribution of land rent, usually without using the term.

The diagnosis also explains difficulties encountered by each of the other traditions. Subsidies to home buyers are capitalised into land prices, as the conservative experience showed. Housing allowances are capitalised into rents wherever sites are scarce. Low interest rates raise what a buyer can borrow and therefore what the landowner can ask. Public investment in transport enriches the owners along the route. Non-profit and public builders are driven to poor locations by the cost of sites. In each case a benefit intended for the occupiers of housing leaks away to the owners of land, and it does so by the mechanism that George described.

The remedies, by contrast, have made little headway. A general tax on land values exists in a few small countries and a scattering of municipalities, at rates far below what George envisaged. Public ownership of urban land has been maintained in a handful of cities and has been eroded in most of them. The reason, as this part has shown, is that the unearned increment is no longer the income of a small landlord class. It has been distributed, unequally but widely, among the majority of households in the rich countries, who received it in the form of rising house prices, have borrowed against it and are counting on it for their old age. To tax or socialise land value now is to take from that majority, and democratic governments do not readily do so. The spread of home ownership, which the conservative tradition promoted as a defence of the social order against socialism, has proved a still more effective defence against Georgism.

It is nevertheless possible to identify what has worked, and it falls into three kinds of policy that are best seen as complements. The first is to tax land value, even partially. Where annual taxes on property are substantial and kept in line with current values, as in parts of the United States outside California, they absorb some of the gain and moderate prices, and the more closely they fall on land instead of buildings the better they do so. Charges on development, levied at the moment a public decision creates value, recover a further share. The second is to own land. Cities that bought ahead of need and used their holdings to supply sites for housing at controlled prices have achieved more than any others. The third is to limit by regulation what can be paid for land, as Vienna and Munich do by attaching obligations to the grant of permission to build. All three have the same effect. They reduce the amount that a private owner can expect to collect from the growth of the community, and so lower the price at which land changes hands.

The relation between the land question and the argument about supply deserves a final comment, since the two are sometimes presented as rival explanations. They are closer than they appear. Those who blame high prices on restrictive planning are pointing to an artificial scarcity of permission to build, and the value of that scarcity accrues to those who own land where building is allowed, as a form of rent.
