To permit more building is to reduce that rent by enlarging the supply of well located floor space. To tax it or to hold land publicly is to redirect the rent to the community. A city that did both would have plentiful building and would collect for public purposes the value that its growth created. Tokyo approximates the first half of that combination and Singapore the second. Few places have attempted the two together, though some of the more thoughtful advocates of each have begun to recognise the other as an ally.

George believed that he had found the single cause of poverty amid progress. He was wrong about that, as his critics said at the time. Differences in earnings, in education and in the ownership of capital would remain if every square metre of land rent were collected for the public. But on the narrower question with which this book is concerned he was more right than wrong. The reason why a home in a prosperous city is beyond the reach of people who work there is not that bricks or labour have become dear. It is that the right to occupy a piece of ground in such a city has become extremely valuable, and that the value is private. The other ideologies argue about who should build, who should own the dwelling and who should be helped to pay for it. The land question asks who should own the location. Until that is answered, in one of the ways described here or in some other, the answers given to the rest will continue to be undone by the price of the ground.

The traditions examined so far arose in Europe and North America and were shaped by the experience of industrial cities there. The majority of the world's people live elsewhere, and their governments have faced the housing question under different conditions and, in several cases, with strikingly different results. Part Nine turns to them.

## Part Nine: Other Worlds

### Chapter 25: The Developmental States of East Asia

The ideologies described in the preceding parts were formed in Europe and North America and exported, with their vocabulary, to the rest of the world. In much of that world they do not fit. The governments of East Asia built some of the most extensive public housing systems in history while professing hostility to the welfare state. Latin American jurists developed a doctrine of property that is neither liberal nor socialist. In Africa and South Asia the greater part of the urban population lives in housing that no ideology planned and no authority recognises. This part of the book looks at these other worlds, beginning with the region whose results have been most admired.

#### Singapore in depth

Singapore has appeared several times in these pages, as a state that owns its land, builds most of its housing and has made nearly all its citizens property owners. The system deserves a fuller description, because it combines elements that elsewhere belong to opposed traditions and because its success is so often cited by people who would not accept the means by which it was obtained.

The colonial government had created an improvement trust in 1927, which in three decades built some twenty-three thousand flats. When the People's Action Party won the election of 1959 and formed the first government under internal self rule, about a quarter of a million people were living in badly degenerated shophouses in the central area, partitioned into windowless cubicles, and another third of a million in squatter settlements of timber and thatch on the fringes. The new government replaced the trust in February 1960 with a Housing and Development Board and gave it a simple instruction, to build as many flats as possible as quickly and cheaply as possible. An opportunity to prove itself came the following year. A fire at Bukit Ho Swee in May 1961 destroyed a squatter settlement and left sixteen thousand people homeless in an afternoon. The board rehoused all of them within a year, largely in blocks it erected on the cleared site, and the speed of the response did much to win public confidence. In its first five years it completed some fifty-four thousand dwellings, more than twice the output of the colonial trust in thirty.

The early flats were rented. In 1964 the government introduced a scheme under the title Home Ownership for the People, offering flats for sale on long leases at prices below cost with loans from the board. Take up was slow until 1968, when buyers were permitted to use their savings in the Central Provident Fund for the deposit and the monthly instalments. The fund is a compulsory savings scheme of colonial origin, into which every employee and employer pay a percentage of wages, a combined rate that has at times reached half of earnings and now stands at a little over a third. With the change, a worker could buy a flat without reducing his take home pay, since the money came from an account he could not otherwise touch before retirement. Purchases rose at once, and within two decades the great majority of households in public housing were owners.

Lee Kuan Yew set out his reasons in his memoirs. He had observed, he wrote, the contrast between the blocks of low cost rental flats, badly misused and poorly maintained, and those of house proud owners. He was convinced that if every family owned its home the country would be more stable. There was also a military consideration. Singapore had introduced conscription in 1967, and he believed that the parents of national servicemen would be more willing to see their sons fight for a country in which they had a stake than to defend the properties of the wealthy. The aim, as he put it, was to give all citizens a stake in the country and its future. Housing was from the start an instrument for building a nation out of a population of recent immigrants of different races who had no particular reason to feel loyal to a small island with no hinterland.

The same purpose governs the rules. A citizen may own only one subsidised flat at a time and must live in it for at least five years before selling. Those applying for a new flat are generally married or engaged couples, or parents with children, and priority and additional grants are given to those who choose to live near their parents. Single people were excluded altogether until 1991 and may now buy from the age of thirty-five. Since 1989 each block and each neighbourhood has been subject to maximum proportions of Chinese, Malay, and Indian and other residents, set a little above each group's share of the population, and a flat cannot be sold to a buyer whose purchase would breach the limit. The government adopted the rule after noticing that the communities were beginning to regroup through the resale market, and it regards the prevention of ethnic enclaves as essential to social peace. Sellers from minority groups, who have a smaller pool of eligible buyers, bear some of the cost.

The flats are built in new towns of between one and three hundred thousand people, each with a town centre, neighbourhood centres, schools, markets, hawker centres, parks and a station on the mass transit system, planned and constructed by the board as a whole. The ground floor of each block is left open as a shared space for weddings, funerals and casual meetings. The blocks are plain and repetitive, and they are also clean, safe and well maintained by town councils that the residents finance through monthly charges. Because four fifths of the population lives in them, including most professionals and civil servants, they carry no social stigma.

The political uses of the system have not been concealed. Applicants deal with a state agency for their home, their mortgage and their retirement savings at once. During the 1990s the government announced that when public estates were scheduled for a major programme of upgrading, with new lifts and extended rooms, wards that had voted for the ruling party would be served first, and ministers defended the policy openly at elections. A population of owners whose main asset is a state built flat, and whose flat's value depends on government policy, has given the party that created the arrangement an unbroken tenure of office since 1959.

The design contains a tension that has become more evident with time. From the 1970s owners were allowed to resell their flats on an open market, and from the 1990s the government spoke of public housing as an appreciating asset through which citizens would share in the nation's prosperity and provide for their retirement. Prices of resale flats multiplied. A flat was now both a home sold cheaply by the state as a social good and an investment expected to rise in value, and the two aims pull in opposite directions. If prices rise, the next generation finds entry harder and must be given larger grants. In recent years some flats in central locations have changed hands for more than a million Singapore dollars. The government has responded by classifying new flats in the best locations into categories that carry extra subsidies but must be held for ten years and are subject to a clawback of part of the gain on first resale.

A further difficulty is built into the lease. Every flat is held for ninety-nine years and will revert to the state without compensation. As the first generation of flats passed their fortieth and fiftieth years, buyers and lenders began to discount them, and in 2017 a minister felt obliged to warn the public against paying high prices for old flats on the assumption that the government would redevelop them. The announcement caused considerable anxiety among older owners who had been encouraged to think of the flat as their retirement fund. The government has since outlined a scheme under which residents of ageing precincts may vote to sell their flats back to the state before the lease expires.

It should finally be said who stands outside the system. Roughly three in ten of the people living in Singapore are not citizens or permanent residents. Among them are about a million low wage migrant workers from South Asia and China, employed in construction, shipyards and domestic service on temporary permits, who have no access to public housing and no path to settlement. Many live in purpose built dormitories of twelve to twenty men to a room, and the rapid spread of infection through those dormitories in 2020 drew international attention to conditions that Singaporeans themselves had seldom seen. The celebrated housing of the citizens is built by, and coexists with, a labour force housed on quite different principles.

#### Hong Kong's public housing and the Japanese system

Hong Kong built public housing on a comparable scale to Singapore from quite different motives, and the contrast between the two is one of the most revealing in the study of housing. The colonial government of the 1950s was devoted to low taxes and minimal administration and had no intention of housing anyone. Its hand was forced by numbers. The population of the territory rose from about six hundred thousand at the end of the Japanese occupation in 1945 to well over two million by the mid 1950s, as refugees crossed from the mainland after the communist victory, and several hundred thousand of them lived in squatter huts on the hillsides around the city.

On Christmas night 1953 a fire swept through the squatter area at Shek Kip Mei in Kowloon and left more than fifty thousand people homeless. The government responded with emergency resettlement blocks of six and seven storeys, built in the shape of the letter H. Each family was allotted a single room on the basis of about two and a quarter square metres for each adult and half that for a child. Access was by open balconies, water taps and latrines were communal, and cooking was done on the balcony outside the door. Historians have pointed out that the purpose was less welfare than land. Squatters occupied sites needed for development, the fire hazard threatened the colony, and resettlement in dense blocks released the ground. A million people were housed in such estates within fifteen years.

A change of approach followed the riots of 1966 and 1967, which persuaded the colonial authorities that they needed to win the consent of the population. The governor Murray MacLehose announced in 1972 a ten year programme to provide permanent self contained homes for one million eight hundred thousand people. A unified housing authority was created, new towns for several hundred thousand residents each were built in the rural hinterland, and standards rose. A scheme introduced in 1978 offered flats for sale at a discount to tenants and to families of moderate income. The public sector has continued to grow, and today some thirty per cent of the population rents from the housing authority at rents that average about a tenth of income, and a further fifteen per cent or so owns subsidised flats.

The difference from Singapore lies in what stands beside the public sector. Singapore drew nearly everyone into state built ownership and left a small private market for the wealthy and for foreigners. Hong Kong retained a large private market in which, for the reasons described in Part Eight, prices are the highest in the world. The public tenant is secure and pays little. The household just above the income limit, or still on a waiting list that averages five to six years, faces private rents that consume half its income for a subdivided room. The gap between the two conditions is so wide that families take care not to earn too much, and young people have been known to apply for public housing on reaching eighteen. Policy has also been erratic. The first chief executive after the return to Chinese sovereignty in 1997 pledged eighty-five thousand new flats a year, the Asian financial crisis then cut property prices by two thirds, homeowners protested, and the government halted its sales of subsidised flats for nearly a decade in order to support the market, with the result that a shortage developed again. After the mass protests of 2019 the central government in Beijing identified unaffordable housing as a root cause of discontent and pressed the local administration to eliminate subdivided flats and caged homes by the middle of the century.

Japan presents the developmental state in a more market oriented form. The country emerged from the war in 1945 with its cities burned out and a shortage estimated at more than four million dwellings, a fifth of the stock. As in West Germany, the first object was quantity, and as in the United States the principal method was finance for owners. The three institutions on which postwar policy rested were noted in Part Five: a government loan corporation that lent at low fixed rates to households building or buying a home, public rental housing provided by local authorities for those on low incomes, and a national housing corporation, founded in 1955, that built estates of rented and for sale apartments for the salaried middle class of the big cities.

The estates of the housing corporation, known as danchi, had an influence on Japanese life out of proportion to their number. They introduced the concrete apartment with a steel door that locked, a flush lavatory, a stainless steel sink and, most importantly, a combined dining room and kitchen with a table and chairs, which separated the place for eating from the place for sleeping in a society where the same matted room had served both purposes. The families who moved into them in the late 1950s were regarded as pioneers of a modern way of living and were given a name by the press. Large companies supplemented public provision by housing their own employees, first in dormitories for the unmarried and then in subsidised company flats for families, as part of the system of lifetime employment, and by offering loans for house purchase. A good deal of the remainder of the urban population, especially the young and the less well paid, rented rooms in small privately owned wooden apartment buildings of low standard.

The rapid economic growth of the 1960s and 1970s was accompanied by a rise in land prices far faster than the rise in wages, and Japanese cities spread outward along the railway lines as families sought plots they could afford, accepting commutes of ninety minutes or more each way. A confidential report of the European Commission in 1979 described the Japanese as a nation of workaholics living in what Westerners would regard as rabbit hutches, a phrase that was leaked, caused lasting offence and was taken up by the Japanese themselves as a spur to improvement. Average floor space has since increased to a level comparable with western Europe.

Two features of the Japanese system set it apart. The first is the protection of tenants. A wartime law of 1941, retained and strengthened by the courts afterwards, prevented a landlord from refusing to renew a lease or from recovering possession without what was called just cause, which the judges interpreted so narrowly that a tenancy once granted was in practice perpetual. Landlords responded by building only small units for single people and childless couples, who move frequently, and by demanding large non-returnable payments at the start. Family sized rental housing became scarce, which pushed families towards ownership. A reform at the end of the 1990s introduced fixed term tenancies for new lettings.

The second is the treatment of the building as a wasting asset. Japanese tax rules write down a wooden house over about twenty years, buyers strongly prefer new construction, in part because earthquake standards are regularly raised, and a second hand house commonly sells for little more than the value of its plot. Houses are demolished and replaced after thirty years on average. In consequence Japan builds far more dwellings each year in proportion to its population than Britain or the United States, has a large and efficient industry of factory made houses, and, since the collapse of the land bubble, has had no expectation that a home will increase in value. The government housing loan corporation was wound down in 2007 and the housing corporation converted into an agency that manages its existing estates. With the population now falling, the latest official survey counted some nine million empty dwellings, about one in seven. Japanese housing policy, which set out after the war to make a nation of owners, has ended by producing something no other rich country has: housing that is plentiful, of good quality, and not a financial asset.

#### South Korea, the apartment republic, and Taiwan

South Korea urbanised faster than any other large country in history. In 1960 about a quarter of its people lived in towns. Thirty years later the proportion was three quarters, and the population of Seoul had grown from two and a half million to more than ten. The state that directed the country's industrialisation under military rule treated housing at first as a distraction from investment in exports, and the migrants who poured into the capital built shanties on the hillsides and along the streams. By the late 1960s such settlements housed something like a third of the city.

The government's early response was removal. In 1971 the Seoul authorities moved more than a hundred thousand squatters to a bare site twenty-five kilometres south of the city, without water, sewers, jobs or transport, having promised them plots. When the residents discovered that they were to be charged for the land at prices they could not pay, they rioted, burning official vehicles and occupying the local offices, in one of the most serious disturbances of the period. The episode impressed on the regime that the urban poor could not simply be dumped.

The pattern that then emerged was distinctive. The state would not build housing in quantity itself. It would organise the conditions under which the country's large industrial conglomerates built apartment blocks for sale to the middle class. The model district was Gangnam, south of the Han River, laid out in the 1970s on farmland through land readjustment, with wide avenues and very large blocks reserved for apartment complexes, to which the government moved the best high schools in order to draw families across the river. The apartments were built by the construction arms of the conglomerates as complete compounds of a dozen or more identical towers, with their own shops and playgrounds, and were sold before construction to buyers who paid in instalments, so that the purchasers financed the building.

The prices of these new apartments were capped by the government below the market level, and since demand far exceeded supply, the right to buy was allocated by a lottery among people who had held a dedicated savings account for a qualifying period, with points for the years without owning a home and the number of dependants. Winning the lottery was equivalent to winning a large sum of money, since the flat could later be resold at the market price. The system channelled household savings into construction, rewarded the patient middle class and created an entire culture of calculation around the subscription account. Apartment living, which had been unfamiliar and disliked, became the mark of middle class status. Around three fifths of all dwellings in the country are now flats in such complexes, and a French geographer who studied the phenomenon entitled her book The Apartment Republic.

Korea's peculiar form of tenancy grew up alongside. Under the arrangement known as jeonse, a tenant pays no monthly rent. He deposits with the landlord a lump sum, traditionally between half and four fifths of the value of the dwelling, for a term of two years, and at the end of the term the landlord returns it in full. The landlord's return is the use of the money. In the decades of high interest rates and scarce bank credit, the deposit served owners as a source of finance, and many used the deposits of their tenants to buy further properties. For the tenant the system was a form of compulsory saving and a stepping stone to purchase. It depended on rising prices and high interest rates, and it concentrated risk on the tenant, whose entire savings were in the hands of a private individual. When interest rates rose and prices fell in 2022, landlords who had borrowed and bought on thin margins could not repay, and it emerged that some operators had acquired hundreds or thousands of small flats with no capital of their own. Thousands of tenants, most of them young, lost their deposits, and several took their own lives. Monthly renting has since been displacing the old system.

The transition to democracy in 1987 was followed by a far more active policy. The government of Roh Tae-woo, facing soaring prices and discontent, announced a plan to build two million dwellings in five years, in a country that then had about seven million. It was achieved by creating five new towns around Seoul, each for several hundred thousand people, on land that a public corporation bought compulsorily at agricultural value, serviced and sold to builders. Prices stabilised for a decade. The same government began the first permanent public rental housing for the poor, and later administrations of both left and right expanded the sector until it reached about eight per cent of the stock.

Redevelopment of the older districts has been the most contentious aspect. Under a procedure introduced in the 1980s, the owners of property in a run down neighbourhood form an association, contract with a construction company to demolish the area and replace it with apartment towers, and share the profit. Tenants, who were usually the majority of residents and the poorer part, received little or nothing and were removed by hired gangs. An international study estimated that some seven hundred thousand people were evicted in Seoul in the five years before the Olympic Games of 1988. In January 2009 a group of tenants and shopkeepers resisting eviction from a building in the Yongsan district barricaded themselves on the roof, and a police assault ended in a fire that killed five of them and an officer.

Housing has remained at the centre of Korean politics. Under the liberal president elected in 2017, apartment prices in Seoul roughly doubled in five years despite more than twenty successive packages of taxes and lending restrictions, and the issue contributed to his party's defeat. The poorest residents of the capital live in single rooms of a few square metres in buildings originally let to students cramming for examinations, or in semi basement flats, a type made known abroad by a celebrated film and in which several people drowned during floods in 2022. Korean commentators widely connect the cost of housing with the country's birth rate, which has fallen to the lowest ever recorded anywhere, well under one child per woman. Young people speak of themselves as a generation that has given up courtship, marriage and children, and home ownership is first on the list of things renounced.

Taiwan offers a variation in which the state did less. The nationalist government that retreated to the island in 1949 carried out a thorough reform of agricultural land but left urban housing largely to private builders and to families. The result is a rate of home ownership of about eighty-five per cent, among the highest in the capitalist world, together with very little social housing, well under one per cent of the stock until recently. Annual taxes on property are extremely low, since assessed values are a small fraction of market prices, and the taxes on land that Sun Yat-sen prescribed have been weakened in the same way. Owning a second or third flat therefore costs almost nothing, and property is the favoured form of saving. Roughly one dwelling in ten stands empty, while prices in Taipei have risen to about fifteen times average household income. In 1989 tens of thousands of people who called themselves snails without shells slept overnight on the most expensive street in Taipei in protest, and the demonstration was repeated twenty-five years later. Recent governments have promised large programmes of social rental housing and raised the tax on owners of multiple homes, without yet altering the basic pattern.

#### What the East Asian cases show

The five societies considered in this chapter and the mainland China described in Part Four share a region, a period of extremely rapid growth and a set of attitudes, and they have arrived at housing outcomes that range from the best in the world to the worst. That spread within a single group of otherwise similar cases makes them especially useful for separating what matters from what does not.

The common attitude can be stated first. None of these governments approached housing as a matter of individual right or as a field to be left to the market. They approached it as a component of a national strategy of development. Housing construction was a means of mobilising household savings, of employing labour, of stimulating the industries that supply steel, cement and appliances, and of settling a newly urban population in an orderly way. Home ownership was favoured because it gave citizens a stake in stability and because a household that owned its home outright in old age would need less from the state. Scholars have described the model as a productivist form of welfare, in which social policy is subordinated to economic growth, and as property based welfare, in which the family's own assets take the place of pensions and social insurance. Public spending on social security in these countries was for decades a fraction of the European level, and the home was expected to fill the gap.

Given that common purpose, what distinguishes the successes from the failures? The first factor is the treatment of land. Singapore took the land into public ownership at low cost and devoted it to housing. South Korea acquired the sites of its new towns compulsorily at farm value. Japan, after its bubble, freed the right to build so thoroughly that land ceased to be scarce. In each of these cases, by different routes, the landowner was prevented from capturing the gains of urban growth, and housing was plentiful in relation to demand. Hong Kong and the Chinese cities, where the public owner sold land for the highest price, and Taiwan, where private owners held it almost untaxed, saw prices escape from incomes.

The second factor is whether housing was allowed to become the main vehicle of household investment. Where it did, as in China, Taiwan, Korea and Hong Kong, a population with high savings and few other outlets bid prices to extraordinary multiples of income, and governments found themselves unable to restrain a market in which most of their citizens had a stake. Singapore contained the pressure for a long time through its rules on ownership and resale, though it now faces the same tension. Japan escaped it only after a crash that discredited property as an investment for a generation.

The third is the capacity and character of the state. The feats of construction described here, a million rehoused in fifteen years in Hong Kong, two million dwellings in five in Korea, four fifths of a nation in public flats in Singapore, were carried out by governments with strong bureaucracies, wide legal powers over land and, for much of the period, little need to answer to those displaced. Squatters were cleared, farmers were bought out at prices they did not choose and tenants were evicted by force. The results in terms of housing provided were remarkable, and democracies cannot easily copy the methods. It is notable that as these societies became more democratic, compulsory acquisition at low prices became harder and owners acquired a political voice, as they have elsewhere.

The region also displays, earlier and more starkly than Europe or America, a consequence that may prove the most important of all. The societies in which housing became most expensive relative to incomes are those with the lowest birth rates ever recorded. South Korea, Taiwan, Hong Kong, Singapore and urban China all have fertility far below the level needed to maintain their populations, in several cases under one child per woman. Many causes contribute, among them long working hours, the cost of education and changes in the position of women. But surveys in each of these places find that the cost of a home suitable for raising a family ranks at or near the top of the reasons young people give for delaying or forgoing marriage and children. A strategy that tied family formation to the purchase of a flat, and then allowed flats to become unaffordable to the young, has undermined the families it was meant to support. Japanese and Korean officials now speak of housing policy as a branch of population policy, as the Myrdals did in Sweden in the 1930s.

For the argument of this book the East Asian experience carries a particular lesson. It shows that the choice between treating housing as an asset and treating it as a social good does not map onto the choice between market and state. Singapore's flats are built and allocated by the state and are also the citizens' principal asset. China's land is publicly owned and its housing is the most speculative in the world. Japan has a largely private market in which homes are consumed like any other durable good. What determines the outcome is the set of rules governing land, credit and resale, and the purposes for which governments write them.

### Chapter 26: Latin America

Latin America is the most urbanised region of the developing world and has been so for half a century. In 1950 about two fifths of its people lived in towns and cities. By 1990 the figure was nearly three quarters, and it now exceeds four fifths. The migration from the countryside that Europe absorbed over a hundred and fifty years took place there in forty, in countries with a fraction of the wealth, and neither the market nor the state was able to house the newcomers. They housed themselves. The cities that resulted, the ideas that grew up to explain and defend them, and the attempts of governments to respond make the region a laboratory of housing thought second in importance only to Europe.

#### The self-built city

The basic pattern was established in the 1940s and 1950s and has changed little in its essentials. A family arriving in Lima, Caracas, Mexico City or São Paulo could not afford to rent or buy a dwelling built to legal standards. It obtained a piece of ground outside the formal market, put up a shelter of whatever materials were to hand, and improved it gradually over the following years as earnings permitted. The methods of obtaining ground varied from country to country, and the differences mattered.

In Peru and Venezuela the typical method was invasion. A group of families, often already organised and sometimes advised by a political party or a sympathetic official, would select a tract of vacant land on the desert or hillside fringe of the city, usually publicly owned or of uncertain title. On a chosen night, frequently the eve of a public holiday when the police would be slow to act, several hundred families would arrive together with poles, straw matting and national flags, mark out plots and streets in an orderly grid and raise their huts by dawn. The authorities then faced a settled community displaying patriotic symbols, and eviction was politically costly. The largest of the Lima settlements began in this way in 1971, when some two hundred families occupied land in the south of the city. Within days the number had grown to several thousand, a clash with the police left one man dead, and the military government of the day, which had its own reasons for courting the poor, offered the invaders a site on empty desert twenty kilometres from the centre. They named it Villa El Salvador. Its residents laid it out in residential groups each with a central space for communal facilities, governed it through an elected assembly, and built their own schools, market and industrial park. It is now a district of some four hundred thousand people.

In Mexico and Colombia the usual method was purchase from an illegal developer. An entrepreneur would acquire or simply lay claim to a tract of land not approved for housing, in Mexico often communally held farmland that could not lawfully be sold, divide it into small plots without drains, water or paving, and sell them cheaply on instalments. The buyers had paid, and held a receipt, but had no valid title and no services. On the bed of a drained lake east of Mexico City, such subdivisions grew in the 1950s and 1960s into a settlement of more than a million inhabitants, later recognised as the municipality of Nezahualcóyotl. In Bogotá the greater part of the city's expansion in the second half of the century took place through what Colombians call pirate subdivisions.

In Brazil the characteristic form was the favela, a settlement on land occupied without the consent of its owner, typically on a site that the formal market had left alone because it was too steep, too marshy or too exposed to flooding. The favelas of Rio de Janeiro cling to the granite hills that rise in the middle of the city, directly above its richest neighbourhoods, and their residents have always worked as the maids, porters, builders and waiters of the districts below. The military regime that took power in 1964 regarded them as a blight and a source of subversion. Between 1962 and the mid 1970s the authorities demolished some eighty favelas in the wealthy southern zone and removed about a hundred and forty thousand people to public housing estates on the distant periphery, of which the best known, called the City of God, later became a byword for violence. Many of those removed could not afford the fares to their former jobs, defaulted on the payments and drifted back to other favelas.

The official view everywhere in these years was that the settlements were a cancer, the product of rural migrants who had brought their backwardness to the city, and that the remedy was clearance. Two researchers working in Lima in the late 1950s and early 1960s, the American anthropologist William Mangin and the English architect John Turner, proposed the opposite interpretation, and it has shaped thinking about the housing of the poor ever since.

They observed, first, that the people of the barriadas were not the most recent or the most desperate migrants. They were families who had lived for some years in the rented tenements of the city centre, had steady if modest incomes, and had made a calculated decision to trade a rented room for a plot of their own. Second, the settlements improved with time. A straw hut became within a few years a room of brick, then several rooms, then a second storey, built by the family and by small local contractors as money allowed, and after fifteen or twenty years a typical barriada was a solid working class district of two and three storey houses with shops and workshops on the ground floors. Third, the residents were getting far more for their money than they could have obtained in any government housing project. They built what they needed, when they could pay for it, with no debt and no rent, and they could let rooms or run a business from the premises.

Turner drew the general conclusion in a book entitled Housing by People. What matters about housing, he argued, is not what it is but what it does for those who live in it. A shack on a secure plot near work, which its occupants can extend, may serve a poor family better than a finished modern flat with a mortgage it cannot meet in a place where there are no jobs. Large organisations, whether public or private, cannot know what each household needs and can afford, and they impose standards that the poor cannot pay for. Households and communities should control the main decisions about their own housing, and governments should confine themselves to what individuals cannot do alone: making land available, laying on water and sanitation, and guaranteeing that people will not be evicted. Turner's argument, made from an anarchist point of view, has an obvious kinship with that of Hayek, and it was taken up in the 1970s by the World Bank, which had its own reasons for favouring solutions that cost governments little.

Some qualifications have been added by later research. The settlements that consolidated so successfully in Lima did so on flat desert land that was free and in a period of rising real wages. Where sites are steep or liable to flood, where plots are tiny or where incomes are falling, improvement is slower or does not occur. The possession of a plot has often depended on the favour of politicians, who exchanged tolerance and services for votes in a relationship that kept the residents dependent. A large proportion of the people in mature settlements are not owner builders but tenants renting rooms from them, a fact long overlooked by admirers of self help. And as cities have grown, the free land has run out, so that newcomers today must buy or rent within existing settlements at prices that are far from negligible.

The central observation has nevertheless held. By the end of the twentieth century something between a third and a half of the population of most large Latin American cities lived in housing that had begun outside the law, and most of that housing had become, through the efforts of its occupants, reasonably solid and serviced. No public programme anywhere in the region has produced dwellings on a remotely comparable scale. The self-built city is the dominant fact of Latin American housing, and every policy and every ideology in the region has had to define itself in relation to it.

#### The state as builder

Latin American governments did not accept the verdict of Mangin and Turner without a struggle. Throughout the second half of the twentieth century they tried repeatedly to provide formal housing themselves, in programmes that followed the changing fashions of international policy and that failed, for the most part, in strikingly similar ways.

The first phase belonged to the modernist housing estate. Its most dramatic example was built in Caracas under the dictatorship of Marcos Pérez Jiménez, who governed Venezuela on its oil revenues in the 1950s and proposed to eliminate the hillside shanties of the capital at a stroke. Between 1954 and 1957 the state housing agency, under the direction of the architect Carlos Raúl Villanueva, erected on a cleared hillside west of the centre a complex of thirty-eight slab blocks of fifteen storeys and dozens of lower ones, painted in bold colours and containing some nine thousand apartments, into which it moved the residents of the demolished settlements. The complex was to be named after the date of the coup that had brought the regime to power. In January 1958 the dictator was overthrown. In the disorder of the following days several thousand families broke into the blocks that were still unfinished or unallocated and occupied them, and they could not afterwards be removed. The estate was renamed for the date of the dictator's fall. Rents went uncollected, squatters built shacks on the open spaces between the towers, and the district became and has remained a stronghold of the radical left, autonomous of every government. The attempt to abolish the informal city by decree had produced an informal city in concrete.

Mexico built its own showpiece in the capital. The Nonoalco-Tlatelolco complex, completed in 1964 to the designs of Mario Pani, comprised more than a hundred buildings with some twelve thousand apartments for about eighty thousand people, on the site of former railway yards and tenements, together with schools, clinics, shops and a square bounded by an Aztec ruin, a colonial church and a modern ministry. It was intended for the working class and was occupied in practice largely by civil servants and the lower middle class who could meet the payments. Its name is associated with two disasters. On 2 October 1968, ten days before the opening of the Olympic Games, soldiers fired on a student demonstration in its central square and killed a number that has never been established, probably several hundred. In the earthquake of September 1985 a thirteen storey block in the complex collapsed, killing hundreds of residents, and others had to be demolished. The response of the survivors, who organised themselves to demand reconstruction on the spot and refused relocation to the periphery, gave rise to a powerful urban popular movement.

A second phase relied on compulsory savings. Brazil's military government established a national housing bank in 1964, financed from 1966 by a fund into which employers paid eight per cent of every worker's wage. The stated purpose, in the words of an early official, was that a homeowner thinks twice before joining a riot or damaging another's property and becomes an ally of order. The bank financed more than four million dwellings before it was dissolved in 1986. Because the fund had to earn a return for the workers whose money it was, loans went to those who could repay, and it has been estimated that only about a third of the housing financed reached the poor for whom the system had been created. Mexico set up a comparable institution in 1972, financed by a levy of five per cent on payrolls, which for two decades built estates for unionised workers through contractors.

The third phase, from the 1990s, adopted the Chilean model described in Part Three, in which the state subsidises the buyer and private firms build. Mexico applied it on the grandest scale. After a reform in 1992 the national workers' housing fund ceased to build and became a mortgage lender, and under the governments of 2000 to 2012 it and other public agencies granted several million loans for the purchase of new houses. A small number of large developers, listed on the stock exchange, bought cheap land far beyond the edges of the cities and covered it with tens of thousands of identical dwellings of thirty or forty square metres. For a few years the policy was celebrated as a triumph. Then the defects emerged. The estates were hours from places of work, lacked schools, clinics, shops and reliable water, and were built so poorly that many deteriorated at once. Families who had bought in order to have a home of their own found that they could not live in it. The census of 2010 counted about five million uninhabited dwellings in the country, a seventh of the stock, and a large share were in these developments, abandoned to vandalism and to criminal gangs. The three biggest homebuilders became insolvent in 2013 and 2014.

Brazil launched its own version in 2009, during the presidency of Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva, as a measure against the world recession. The programme, whose name means my house, my life, contracted for more than four million dwellings within seven years, a rate unprecedented in the country. Families in the lowest income band received a home for a payment of a few per cent of income over ten years, effectively a gift. The programme greatly reduced the number of households living in precarious conditions or sharing with relatives, and it was the first in Brazilian history to reach the very poor in large numbers. It was designed, however, in consultation with the construction industry and gave builders the initiative in choosing sites. The dwellings for the poorest were placed overwhelmingly in large estates at the extreme periphery, where land cost least. Urban researchers who had worked for years for the reform of Brazilian cities, described in the next section, saw the principles they had fought for set aside in favour of sheer volume. In parts of Rio de Janeiro the new estates fell under the control of armed militias, who charged residents for security and services and expelled those who resisted.

The repetition across countries and decades is remarkable. Santiago in the 1990s, the outskirts of Mexican cities in the 2000s, Brazilian peripheries in the 2010s, and similar programmes in Colombia, Venezuela and elsewhere each produced several hundred thousand or several million finished dwellings for people of low income, and each placed them where land was cheapest, in single class estates remote from employment and services. The explanation is the one identified in Part Eight. A programme that counts success in units delivered at a fixed price per unit will economise on the one cost that varies, which is land, and cheap land is cheap because it is badly located. The self-builder in his illegal settlement had frequently chosen a better site than the state chose for him.

One architectural response has attracted international notice. The Chilean architect Alejandro Aravena and his firm were asked in 2003 to rehouse about a hundred families who had squatted for thirty years on a site in the centre of the northern city of Iquique, using only the standard public subsidy, which would normally have paid for a small house on the periphery. Their solution was to spend most of the money on the well located land and on the parts of a house that a family cannot easily build for itself, namely the structure, the stairs, the kitchen and the bathroom, and to build only half of each dwelling, leaving a gap of equal size beside it for the residents to fill in over time. Within a few years the families had doubled their floor space at their own expense and the houses had multiplied in value. The method, which Aravena calls incremental housing, accepts Turner's insight that the poor will complete their own homes and combines it with what only a public authority can provide. It earned him the leading international prize in architecture in 2016. It remains an exception.

#### The social function of property and the right to the city

Latin America's most original contribution to the argument about housing is a legal doctrine. It holds that ownership is not simply a right of the individual against everyone else. It is an office that the owner holds in the interest of society, and it is protected by the law only so far as it is exercised in accordance with that interest. Property, in the phrase used across the region, has a social function. The idea has European origins, was first given constitutional force in Mexico, and has been developed furthest in Brazil.

The formulation is due to a French jurist, Léon Duguit, professor of law at Bordeaux. In a series of lectures delivered at Buenos Aires in 1911, Duguit argued that the conception of property in the Napoleonic code, as an absolute and exclusive right, was a metaphysical fiction that no longer corresponded to social reality. In a society of interdependent members, each person has a function to perform, and the holder of wealth has the function of employing it productively. The owner of land has a duty to cultivate it and the owner of a house a duty to see that it is occupied. The law should protect him in the performance of this function and may intervene if he neglects it. Property, Duguit said, is not a right but a social function. The German constitution adopted at Weimar eight years later expressed a similar thought in two words that are still part of German law: property obliges.

The Mexican constitution of 1917, drafted in the midst of revolution, went further than any European text. Its article 27 declared that the nation holds original ownership of all land and waters within its territory, that private property is a right derived from the nation by grant, and that the nation shall at all times have the right to impose on private property such forms as the public interest may dictate. Colombia amended its constitution in 1936 to state that property is a social function that implies obligations, and enacted a rule that rural land left uncultivated for ten years would revert to the state. Brazil, Chile, Bolivia, Venezuela and others adopted comparable clauses. For several decades the doctrine was applied chiefly to agrarian reform, as a justification for the expropriation of idle estates.

Its application to cities was the work of a Brazilian movement. During the years of military rule, neighbourhood associations in the favelas and peripheries, supported by the progressive wing of the Catholic Church, by trade unions and by architects, lawyers and planners, came together around a demand they called urban reform. When a constituent assembly met in 1987 to write a democratic constitution, the rules allowed citizens to submit proposals backed by signatures, and the movement presented an amendment on urban reform with more than a hundred and thirty thousand. The constitution of 1988 incorporated its core in two articles. Urban policy, they provide, has the purpose of ordering the full development of the social functions of the city. Urban property fulfils its social function when it meets the requirements set out in the city's master plan. A municipality may require the owner of land that is unbuilt, underused or unused to make proper use of it, and if he does not, may impose in succession compulsory subdivision or construction, a property tax that rises progressively over time, and finally expropriation paid for in public bonds. And a person who has occupied as his home an urban plot of up to two hundred and fifty square metres for five years, without interruption or challenge, and owns no other property, acquires title to it.

The constitution needed a statute to give it effect, and Congress took thirteen years to pass one. The City Statute of 2001 is among the most ambitious pieces of urban legislation in the world. It defines the instruments just mentioned, setting the maximum rate of the progressive tax on vacant property at fifteen per cent of its value each year. It allows the residents of a settlement to claim title collectively where individual plots cannot be distinguished. It regulates the sale of additional building rights discussed in Part Eight. It authorises special zones of social interest, in which an existing informal settlement is recognised, exempted from the ordinary standards for plot size and street width so that it can be legalised as it stands, and reserved for housing for low income residents, which discourages purchase by outsiders. The first such zones had been created in Recife in the 1980s. And it makes master plans compulsory for all cities above a modest size and requires that they be drawn up with public participation. The city of Porto Alegre had already pioneered, from 1989, a procedure in which assemblies of residents in each district decided the priorities for municipal investment, a practice that came to be imitated around the world under the name of participatory budgeting.

Behind these provisions stands a concept borrowed from the French philosopher Henri Lefebvre, who published in 1968 a short book on the right to the city. Lefebvre argued that the city is a collective work, produced by all who inhabit it, and that the people who make and use it have a right to it that is distinct from, and superior to, the rights of those who merely own pieces of it. The right includes access to the centre and to its services, and a share in the decisions that shape urban space. It is a claim to use, against the logic of exchange. The geographer David Harvey later described it as the right to change ourselves by changing the city. Brazilian law defines the right to sustainable cities as a collective right, the constitution of Ecuador adopted in 2008 guarantees the right to the city by name, and Mexico City wrote it into its own constitution in 2017.

The gap between the text and the practice has been wide, and candid Brazilian commentators acknowledge it. The compulsory use of idle property requires a municipal law, a register of vacant buildings, notification of each owner and a wait of several years at each stage, and owners contest every step in the courts. Very few of the country's more than five thousand municipalities have applied the progressive tax at all. São Paulo began to do so in 2014 and notified the owners of well over a thousand properties, and later administrations let the process lapse. Regularisation of favelas has proceeded slowly, and the mass housing programme of 2009 was designed, as noted above, with little regard for the principles of the statute. Meanwhile the centres of São Paulo and Rio contain hundreds of buildings that have stood empty for decades, some of which have been occupied by organised movements citing the constitution as their warrant.

The doctrine has critics of principle as well. Liberals object that a property right contingent on an official's judgment of its social usefulness is no right at all, and that the uncertainty deters the investment cities need. Some on the left observe that the language of social function leaves private ownership in place and merely asks it to behave, and that courts staffed by the propertied class can be relied upon to interpret it narrowly.

Its significance lies elsewhere. The social function doctrine offers a third position between the two poles with which this book began. It does not treat a house or a plot as a commodity whose owner may do with it as he likes, and it does not abolish private ownership. It attaches to ownership a condition, that the thing owned be used, and it gives the community a claim when the condition is not met. In doing so it recovers, in modern legal form, the principle that governed land in most societies before the liberal revolution, as described in the first part of this book: that the right to hold depends on use, and that land left idle returns to the common stock.

#### Upgrading, segregation and an assessment

Between the self-built settlement and the peripheral housing estate, a third approach has gradually won acceptance in Latin America as the most effective use of public money. It is to leave the informal settlements where they are and to bring to them what their residents could not provide for themselves: drains, water, paved streets, electricity, schools, clinics, public spaces and legal recognition. The method is known as settlement upgrading, and its development marks the practical triumph of Turner's ideas over those of the planners he criticised.

Rio de Janeiro, which had spent the 1960s tearing favelas down, adopted the opposite policy in 1994 with a programme whose name means favela to neighbourhood. With loans from the Inter-American Development Bank, the city selected settlements of medium size and commissioned architects, chosen by competition, to design for each a plan of works: access roads wide enough for refuse lorries and ambulances, stairways and retaining walls on the slopes, sewers, crèches, sports grounds and small squares. Houses that stood in the path of a landslide or a new street were replaced nearby. The programme reached well over a hundred favelas and several hundred thousand people, and it established the principle that these districts were permanent parts of the city with a claim to the same services as any other.

Medellín in Colombia carried the idea further. In the early 1990s it was the most violent city in the world, the base of the largest cocaine cartel, and its hillside settlements were controlled by armed gangs. From 2004 a sequence of mayors pursued a policy they called social urbanism, concentrating the best architecture and the largest investments in the poorest and most dangerous districts. Cable cars linked the steep northern settlements to the metro in minutes where the journey on foot and by bus had taken an hour or more. At the upper stations the city built large public libraries surrounded by parks, new schools, and in one district a series of outdoor escalators climbing the hillside. The works were financed in large part from the profits of the municipally owned utility company, which by statute transfers a share of its surplus to the city each year, an arrangement of the kind Ebenezer Howard would have approved. The homicide rate fell by more than nine tenths from its peak, for reasons that include a great deal besides urban design, and Medellín became a place of pilgrimage for mayors from other continents.

Upgrading has limitations that its advocates recognise. It improves the public realm and leaves the houses as they are, including those that are overcrowded, damp or structurally unsound. It can raise values and rents and so push out the tenants who are the poorest residents. It does not create new housing, and so does nothing for the households formed each year who have nowhere to go, and if it is not accompanied by a supply of affordable land, new informal settlements arise further out. And in many districts of Rio, Caracas, San Salvador and other cities the presence of the state remains nominal. Territorial control is exercised by drug trafficking organisations or by militias, which regulate building, sell plots and levy charges on every transaction, so that a parallel property system operates under private armed authority.

At the other end of the social scale, the wealthy have withdrawn into enclosed developments. Beginning in the 1970s on the outskirts of São Paulo, and subsequently around Buenos Aires, Mexico City, Santiago and Bogotá, developers have built walled estates with guarded gates, private streets, schools, clubs and shopping, some of them the size of small towns. Their residents seldom need to enter the public city at all. The Brazilian anthropologist Teresa Caldeira described São Paulo as a city of walls. The fear of crime is the stated reason, and the effect is to reproduce in physical form the inequality of societies that are among the most unequal in the world. Latin American cities now commonly consist of three distinct territories with little contact among them: the gated enclaves of the rich, the consolidated self-built districts of the working majority, and the precarious settlements and distant subsidy estates of the poorest.

An assessment of the region's experience might begin with what it disproves. It disproves the belief, common to market liberals and to state planners in their different ways, that informal housing is a temporary disorder that will disappear with growth or can be replaced by formal provision. After seventy years it houses a third or more of the urban population and shows no sign of vanishing. It also disproves the romantic view that self help is sufficient. The settlements became decent places to live where governments eventually supplied infrastructure and security of tenure, and they remained miserable where governments did not.

The experience suggests several positive conclusions. Informality in housing is best understood as the rational response of people excluded from the formal market by its prices and from legality by its standards. Rules for plot sizes, street widths and construction that are appropriate to the middle class make legal housing unaffordable to the majority, and a city that enforces them strictly manufactures illegality. This is the point that de Soto made, and it parallels the argument about zoning in rich countries. The most valuable thing a government can give a poor household is not a house but a secure and serviced place on which to build one, in a location from which work can be reached. Mass programmes of finished housing have repeatedly sacrificed location to numbers. And the legal traditions of the region have produced, in the social function of property and the right to the city, a vocabulary for asserting the claims of occupants and of the community against owners who hold land idle, even if the practice lags far behind.

One large omission should be noted. Latin American policy, like the ideology of ownership that underlies it, has treated the tenant as if he did not exist. Programmes have aimed almost exclusively at making people owners, whether through titles, subsidies or mortgages. Yet in the big cities a fifth to two fifths of households rent, most of them from small landlords in the consolidated popular districts, without written contracts or protection. Only in the past decade have a few countries, Chile, Colombia and Uruguay among them, begun to experiment with subsidies for renting and with publicly owned rental housing. The region that did most to demonstrate that the poor can house themselves has only recently begun to ask how they are to be housed when they do not own.

### Chapter 27: Africa, South Asia and the Middle East

The regions considered in this chapter contain the fastest growing cities on earth and the largest numbers of people living in housing that falls below any accepted standard. They also contain most of the people who will be added to the world's urban population in the coming generation. The ideologies of housing have been applied there under conditions their authors never contemplated: states with small revenues and limited administrative reach, land held under several legal systems at once, incomes too low for any formal dwelling, and populations doubling in fifteen or twenty years. What has resulted bears on the larger argument of this book precisely because it tests each tradition at its limit.

#### African cities: the township, the rental shack and the subsidy house

The colonial city in Africa was built on the assumption that Africans did not belong in it. European administrators and settlers lived in planned districts with piped water and sewers, on land held under registered title. Africans were regarded as temporary sojourners whose real homes were in the rural areas, admitted to town for as long as their labour was required. Those in formal employment were housed by their employers or by the municipality in what were called locations or townships, in rows of single rooms for men without their families. The rest lived wherever they could find a place, in settlements beyond the municipal boundary or on land under the authority of local chiefs. Planning law, building standards and the registration of title applied to the European town and were used to keep the African town at a distance, frequently on the pretext of protecting public health.

Independence in the 1960s changed the occupants of the planned districts and left the structure in place. The new governments inherited building codes copied from Britain or France, which prescribed materials and plot sizes far beyond the means of most of their citizens, and they retained them as a mark of modernity. They established national housing corporations to build for the people, and these produced a few thousand houses and flats a year to a standard that only civil servants and the salaried middle class could afford, and that were in practice allocated to them. A large estate built by the Nigerian government in Lagos for an international festival of the arts in 1977, and afterwards distributed by ballot, became a middle class suburb. The mass of new arrivals in the cities, whose numbers were growing by five or six per cent a year, built outside the law.

In the 1970s the World Bank and other lenders, persuaded by the arguments of Turner, promoted an alternative that came to be known as sites and services. Instead of finished houses, the state would provide plots with a water connection, a latrine or a sewer and a road, and the occupants would build for themselves. A project at Dandora on the edge of Nairobi, begun in 1975, laid out six thousand such plots. Similar schemes were carried out in Lusaka, Dakar, Dar es Salaam and elsewhere. They reached people considerably poorer than those served by the housing corporations. They did not reach the poorest, since even a serviced plot had to be paid for, and many original allottees sold or sublet to better off households. By the 1980s the lenders had lost interest, and with the debt crises and structural adjustment programmes of that decade most African governments withdrew from housing altogether.

The outcome is that the informal settlement is the normal form of urban housing across most of the continent. In many African cities more than half the population lives in districts that have no legal status, and the proportion in some exceeds two thirds. It would be a mistake to imagine these as places where every family has built its own shelter. Research in Nairobi has shown that the great majority of the residents of its informal settlements, more than nine in ten in the largest, are tenants. They rent single rooms of about nine square metres, built of mud and wattle or corrugated iron, from landlords who mostly live elsewhere and who include civil servants, businessmen and politicians. The structures are cheap to put up, the land has usually been allocated informally by officials or chiefs at little cost, the rents are high in relation to the investment, and the returns have been estimated at several times those available from formal property. The landlords have no incentive to improve dwellings on land they do not securely hold, and they have considerable political influence to resist any change that would threaten the arrangement. The slum of Kibera, with perhaps a quarter of a million people, is in economic terms a very profitable rental housing business conducted without regulation. The market liberal's free market in housing and the socialist's exploitative landlord are found together in their purest forms in the African shack settlement.

Governments have alternated between neglect and demolition. The military governor of Lagos destroyed the settlement of Maroko in a few days in July 1990, displacing some three hundred thousand people, to release valuable land beside a wealthy district. In 2005 the government of Zimbabwe carried out a campaign under a name meaning clear out the rubbish, which demolished informal housing and markets across the country and deprived about seven hundred thousand people of their homes or livelihoods, according to a United Nations envoy sent to investigate. Evictions on a smaller scale are routine in Abuja, Luanda, Kigali and Addis Ababa, often to make way for developments intended to give the capital the appearance of Dubai or Singapore.

South Africa is a special case and the most instructive. Apartheid had allocated land and housing by race, removed some three and a half million people to segregated townships and distant reserves, and denied Africans the right to own property in the cities. The government elected in 1994 promised a million houses in five years. It adopted, on the advice of the World Bank and local business, a capital subsidy on the Chilean pattern, and through it the state has since delivered well over three million dwellings free of charge to households below an income limit, each a small detached house on its own plot with title. No other country in Africa, and few anywhere, have housed so many of their poor so quickly.

The achievement has come at a cost that South Africans discuss openly. To build so many freestanding houses within a fixed subsidy, the authorities used the cheapest land, which lay beyond the existing townships on the far edges of the cities. The new settlements thus extended the geography that apartheid had created, placing the black poor still further from employment, and their residents spend a large part of their income on transport. Many beneficiaries have sold their houses informally for a fraction of the cost, or have built shacks in their yards to let, and the number of households living in informal settlements and backyard dwellings is larger today than in 1994. The constitution's guarantee of access to adequate housing, and the judgment in the Grootboom case, are discussed in Part Ten. A law of 1998 forbids the eviction of any unlawful occupier without a court order that takes account of whether alternative accommodation is available, which gives South African squatters stronger legal protection than tenants enjoy in many rich countries.

A few governments have attempted mass construction of apartments. Ethiopia, where all urban land belongs to the state, launched in 2005 a programme of condominium blocks that has produced several hundred thousand flats in Addis Ababa, allocated by lottery among registered applicants who make a down payment and repay a bank loan. The buildings have transformed the skyline. The required payments are beyond the poorest households, many winners let their flats and remain in cheaper housing, and tens of thousands of residents of the old inner districts have been displaced to distant sites to clear land for redevelopment. Angola used oil backed loans from China to build an entire satellite city of several hundred towers outside Luanda, which stood nearly empty for years because almost nobody could afford the flats until the government cut the price by more than half. Morocco, after suicide bombings in Casablanca in 2003 carried out by young men from a shanty town, began a national programme to eliminate such settlements, and has rehoused several hundred thousand families in new peripheral towns, with the familiar complaints about distance and the absence of work.

#### India, Pakistan and Sri Lanka

Independent India inherited from the British a set of housing laws and added to them a commitment to planning and to what its leaders called a socialistic pattern of society. For four decades the state attempted to control urban land and rents more comprehensively than any other democracy, and the results are often cited by market liberals as a warning. The country has also produced some of the most effective organisations of the urban poor anywhere, and a jurisprudence of rights that has influenced courts on other continents.

Three interventions shaped Indian cities. The first was rent control. Wartime measures were made permanent after independence in state after state, the Bombay act of 1947 fixing rents at the level of 1940 being the best known. Rents frozen in money terms through decades of inflation fell to a few rupees a month for flats worth fortunes. Tenancies passed by inheritance. Owners ceased to maintain their buildings, and in the old island city of Bombay thousands of tenanted structures became so dilapidated that a public board had to be created to repair them with a tax on the owners, and several collapse each year in the monsoon. No one built to let. A system grew up by which an incoming tenant paid a very large unofficial premium to the outgoing tenant and the landlord, in effect buying the tenancy, so that the benefit of control was capitalised and sold. Flats were kept locked and empty by owners who feared that any tenant, once admitted, could never be removed.

The second was public monopoly of land development. Delhi was placed in 1957 under a development authority empowered to acquire all land needed for the expansion of the city, compulsorily and at low prices, and to plan, service and allot it. The intention was to prevent speculation and to secure orderly growth with provision for all classes. The authority never came near to supplying land at the rate the population required, and what it did supply went mostly to middle and upper income groups and to cooperative societies of officials. Everyone else was accommodated outside the plan. Farmers on the fringe sold their fields in small plots to private colonisers, who resold them to families without any legal sanction, and some seventeen hundred such unauthorised colonies now house several million people in the capital, alongside hundreds of squatter clusters on public land. The planned city became a minority of the actual city.

The third was the Urban Land Ceiling Act of 1976, passed during the period of emergency rule under Indira Gandhi. It set a limit on the amount of vacant land that any person could hold in a large city, as little as five hundred square metres in the biggest, and provided that the surplus would be taken by the state at a nominal price for housing the poor. In practice owners obtained exemptions, tied up the proceedings in litigation or concealed their holdings, and of the large area declared surplus only a small fraction was ever taken into possession. The main effect was to freeze a great deal of land in legal uncertainty and to drive up the price of the remainder. The act was repealed by the central government in 1999. In the same period of emergency rule the authorities in Delhi, under the direction of the prime minister's son, bulldozed the homes of some seven hundred thousand people and moved them to resettlement colonies on the outskirts, and when the residents of one old quarter resisted, the police opened fire.

The poor of Bombay, now Mumbai, housed themselves in the interstices. Roughly two fifths of the population of the city lives in slums that occupy less than a tenth of its land. The largest, Dharavi, in the geographical centre of the metropolis, holds several hundred thousand people on about two square kilometres, together with thousands of workshops producing leather goods, pottery, garments and recycled plastic for sale across the country and abroad. Tens of thousands more live on the pavements themselves, in lean-tos built against walls.

It was pavement dwellers who brought the case that defined the Indian law on the subject. In 1981 the state government announced that it would remove them and deport them to their places of origin. A journalist, Olga Tellis, and others petitioned the Supreme Court. In its judgment of 1985 the court accepted the fundamental contention. The constitution guarantees that no person shall be deprived of life except by procedure established by law. The right to life, the judges held, includes the right to a livelihood, because no one can live without the means of living, and the pavement dwellers had come to the city and chosen their place of shelter because it was near their work. To evict them was to deprive them of their livelihood and so of their life. Having stated the principle, the court held that the municipal law authorising the removal of obstructions from public footpaths was a reasonable procedure, and allowed the evictions to proceed after the monsoon. The decision has been criticised for giving with one hand and taking with the other. Its doctrine, that social and economic rights can be derived from the right to life, opened a path that Indian courts and others have followed since.

More practical gains came from organisation. In the mid 1980s a group of social workers began to work with women living on the pavements of central Bombay, who formed savings groups and a collective whose name means women together. They allied with a federation of slum dwellers led by Jockin Arputham, who had grown up in a Bombay slum and had organised resistance to demolitions since the 1960s. The alliance developed methods that have since been copied in dozens of countries. Residents conduct their own census and mapping of their settlements, so that they possess better information than the authorities and cannot be dismissed as uncounted. They save small sums daily in groups managed by women, which builds both capital and trust. They design and build model houses and community toilet blocks to demonstrate what they want, and negotiate with governments from a position of knowledge. When the railways required the removal of sixty thousand people living along the tracks in Mumbai, the alliance managed the resettlement itself, without force. Arputham helped to found an international network of such federations, and was nominated for the Nobel peace prize.

Government policy in Mumbai took a turn peculiar to a city of extreme land values. Under a scheme introduced in the 1990s, a private developer who obtains the consent of the residents of a slum may clear it, rehouse them free of charge in small flats in multi storey blocks on part of the site, and build apartments for sale on the rest, with additional building rights that can be used or sold elsewhere. The slum dwellers' occupation of valuable land is in effect exchanged for a flat. Several hundred thousand families have been rehoused, in towers that critics describe as vertical slums, and the method works only where land prices are high enough to finance it. A contract to redevelop Dharavi on this basis was awarded to one of the country's largest industrial groups in 2022.

Neighbouring countries have contributed their own approaches. In Karachi, the social scientist Akhtar Hameed Khan began in 1980 to work in Orangi, an unplanned settlement of about a million people. He found that residents could build their own lane sewers and household latrines at a fraction of the official cost if given technical guidance and freed from contractors' profits, and that they would pay for them, leaving the government to provide only the trunk drains. Nearly a hundred thousand households did so. His successor as director, Perween Rahman, who documented the seizure of land by criminal syndicates, was shot dead in 2013. Sri Lanka in the 1980s ran a national programme, under a prime minister who made housing his cause, that abandoned state construction in favour of small loans and technical help to families building their own homes, and that reached several hundred thousand households. It was one of the few occasions on which a government applied Turner's principle of supporting people's own building as a deliberate nationwide policy.

#### Turkey, Egypt, Iran and the Gulf
