The countries of the Middle East display, within a shared legal inheritance from the Ottoman empire and Islamic law, almost the whole range of approaches described in this book, from the tolerated self-built settlement to mass state construction and from the frozen rent to the distribution of free land.

Turkey supplies one of the best documented cases of informal housing turning into a system of mass property ownership. When migration from Anatolia to Istanbul, Ankara and Izmir began in earnest around 1950, the newcomers built on vacant land at the edges of the cities, most of it belonging to the treasury, a legacy of the Ottoman category of state land described in Part One. The Turkish word for such a dwelling means placed overnight. It reflects a widely held belief, with some basis in the law on the demolition of unlawful structures, that a house completed with a roof between dusk and dawn and inhabited by morning could not be pulled down without a court order. Relatives and fellow villagers would gather to build in a single night. Because the land was public and the builders were voters, successive governments found it more convenient to tolerate than to evict, and settlements were supplied with roads, water and electricity in the periods before elections.

The decisive step was a series of amnesty laws, of which the most far reaching were passed in the mid 1980s under the government of Turgut Özal. Occupants of unauthorised dwellings were given documents entitling them to eventual title, and, more importantly, their neighbourhoods were given plans permitting buildings of four storeys or more. A family that had squatted on a plot and put up a single storey house could now enter into an arrangement with a small contractor of the kind already described for Greece. The contractor demolished the house and built a block of flats, the family received several of them and the contractor sold the rest. Within a generation the squatters of the 1960s became the owners of apartment buildings, and the poor migrant quarters of Istanbul were transformed into dense districts of multi storey blocks. The process created a large class of small property owners with a strong attachment to the parties that had made it possible. It also produced a building stock erected without engineering supervision in one of the most seismically active regions of the world.

Under the governments led by Recep Tayyip Erdoğan since 2002, the state has become the principal developer. A public housing administration, given wide powers over treasury land and exempted from many planning rules, has built well over a million dwellings, mostly in large estates of uniform towers on the peripheries of cities, sold on long term instalments. A law of 2012 authorised the designation of any area as being at risk from disaster and its compulsory redevelopment, a power used to clear old inner city quarters, including a neighbourhood of Istanbul inhabited by Roma for several centuries. Construction became the engine of the economy and a source of patronage. Repeated amnesties legalised buildings that did not comply with the earthquake code, the latest in 2018 covering several million structures. When two great earthquakes struck the south east of the country in February 2023, more than fifty thousand people died in the collapse of buildings, many of them recently built, and the amnesties were widely blamed.

Egypt illustrates the long consequences of rent control combined with a ban on building where people wish to live. Rents were reduced and frozen in stages under Nasser in the 1950s and 1960s, and tenancies were made heritable. As in Bombay, private building for rent ceased, owners let their properties decay or kept them empty, and newcomers paid large sums of key money. At the same time the law forbade construction on agricultural land, which in a country confined to the narrow valley of the Nile is the only land adjacent to the cities. Egyptians built on it anyway. Farmers sold their fields in strips to small builders, who erected narrow apartment buildings of bare brick and concrete, five or ten storeys high, without permits or planning. These unplanned districts now house something like three fifths of the population of Greater Cairo. They are solidly built and mostly connected to services, and they are the work of an unregulated private market operating illegally.

The state's own effort has gone into new towns in the desert. Since the 1970s Egypt has founded more than twenty, and in 2015 it began a new administrative capital forty-five kilometres east of Cairo, planned for six million people. Most of these towns have attracted a small fraction of their intended population, because the housing built in them is priced for the middle and upper classes and they are far from the work available to the poor. Official counts show several million dwellings standing empty in the country as a whole, held as investments by owners who have no reliable alternative store of value and no wish to let under the old law. New leases were freed from control in 1996. The old contracts remained, and legislation passed in 2025 provided for their termination after a transitional period of some years, ending an arrangement that had lasted three quarters of a century.

Iran combined revolution with property. In the months after the overthrow of the shah in 1979, crowds occupied the empty houses and hotels of those who had fled, and the new authorities created a foundation to hold confiscated assets on behalf of the dispossessed. A law of the same year nationalised undeveloped urban land beyond a small permitted holding, and the state distributed plots to cooperatives and individuals during the following decade. The constitution of the Islamic republic declares it a duty of the government to provide every family with a dwelling suited to its needs. The most ambitious attempt to fulfil it came under President Mahmoud Ahmadinejad, whose government from 2007 offered public land free on ninety-nine year leases to builders of modest flats for first time buyers, financed by credit from the central bank. Some two million units were begun, a large part of them in new towns in the desert around Tehran. Many were completed years late and without water, sewerage, schools or transport, and economists hold the monetary financing of the programme responsible for a surge of inflation.

The oil monarchies of the Gulf have used housing as a channel for distributing wealth to their citizens. In Kuwait in the 1950s the ruler bought land in and around the old town from its inhabitants at prices far above its value, as a means of putting oil revenue into their hands, and resold or granted plots elsewhere. Citizens of Kuwait, Saudi Arabia and the Emirates are entitled, on marriage, to apply for a plot of land, a house or an interest free loan from the state, and they wait many years to receive it. The entitlement belongs to citizens alone. The migrant workers from South Asia and elsewhere who form the majority of the population in several of these states, and nearly nine tenths in Qatar and the Emirates, are housed by their employers in labour camps outside the cities, under a system of sponsorship that ties their right to remain to their job.

Saudi Arabia has provided an unexpected footnote to the argument of Part Eight. By the 2010s a large part of the land within the boundaries of Riyadh, Jeddah and other cities was held undeveloped by a small number of wealthy families and princes as a store of value, while young Saudis could not find plots at prices they could afford. In 2016 the kingdom introduced an annual tax of two and a half per cent on the value of vacant land within urban limits, known as the white land tax, with the declared aim of forcing it into use, and the rate was later raised. It is a land value tax of exactly the kind that Henry George proposed, enacted by an absolute monarchy that has no legislature to obstruct it and no electorate of homeowners to offend.

#### What the experience of the majority world shows

The housing debate in Europe and North America is conducted for the most part as if the cases described in this chapter and the last were exceptions. In terms of numbers they are the rule. The United Nations estimates that the world's urban population will grow by about two and a half billion by the middle of the century, and that nearly nine tenths of the increase will occur in Asia and Africa. The cities of Nigeria, India, Pakistan, Ethiopia, Congo and Bangladesh will add more inhabitants in thirty years than the whole of Europe contains today. How those people are housed is, in sheer human terms, the greater part of the housing question, and the experience so far allows some conclusions about what each of the traditions in this book has to offer them.

The first conclusion is that informal housing is a system and not the absence of one. From the gecekondu of Istanbul to the rental shacks of Nairobi, the unauthorised colonies of Delhi and the unplanned blocks of Cairo, the settlements that planners regard as disorder have their own developers, landlords, brokers, rules of tenure and methods of finance. They deliver shelter to hundreds of millions of people at prices they can pay, which formal systems have not done. They also deliver it without sanitation, without security and frequently on dangerous ground, and they are entirely capable of exploitation. To recognise that they function is not to admire them.

The second is that the market liberal tradition is partly vindicated and partly refuted by this evidence. It is vindicated in its criticism of regulation. Rent controls in Bombay and Cairo destroyed the supply of rental housing as thoroughly as theory predicts. Public monopolies of land in Delhi and ceilings on holdings produced scarcity. Building standards imported from Europe made legality unaffordable. Where governments permitted or legalised what people built for themselves, as in Turkey, housing was produced in great quantity. It is refuted in its expectation that a free market will suffice. The freest housing markets in the world are the unregulated settlements of African cities, and their product is a nine square metre room without a latrine, let at a high return to an absentee owner. A market can only supply what its customers can pay for, and where incomes are very low and public services absent, what they can pay for is very little.

The third concerns state provision. The governments that set out to build finished housing for the poor on a mass scale, in South Africa, Ethiopia, Turkey, Iran, Mexico and Brazil, achieved impressive numbers and repeated one another's mistake with striking regularity. They built on the cheapest land, far from work, in single purpose estates, and found that the beneficiaries sold, sublet or abandoned the dwellings in order to live nearer their livelihoods. The lesson of the European estates of the 1960s was relearned on three continents. Where the state built well, as in Singapore, it controlled the land and built complete towns with employment and transport.

The fourth is that the approaches that have worked best are modest and unglamorous. Security against eviction, whether or not accompanied by formal title, leads people to invest in their homes. Trunk infrastructure for water, sewerage, drainage and roads is the thing residents cannot provide for themselves and the thing that most improves health. Serviced land in reasonable locations, made available ahead of need and at standards the poor can meet, prevents the formation of the worst settlements. Organisations of residents, especially those built on women's savings groups, make better use of resources than agencies working from above. These are the lessons of Orangi, of the Mumbai federations, of the upgrading programmes of Latin America and of Sri Lanka's support for self builders. They correspond to no single ideology. They take from the market tradition its respect for what people do for themselves, from the social democratic tradition its insistence on public responsibility for common services, and from the land reformers the recognition that everything depends on access to a place.

The fifth is the invisibility of renting. In nearly every country discussed, policy has aimed at ownership through titles, plots, subsidised units or mortgages, while a third or more of the urban poor are tenants of small landlords with no written agreement and no rights. The conservative ideal of the proprietor has been exported to societies where a very large part of the population will not be proprietors for the foreseeable future, and almost nothing has been done to make renting decent or secure.

A last consideration casts a shadow over the rest. A large share of informal settlements stands on land that the formal market rejected for good reason: flood plains, tidal flats, unstable hillsides, dry river beds. As the climate changes, these are the places most exposed to storm surges, landslides, floods and extreme heat, and their inhabitants are those least able to move or rebuild. The coastal settlements of Lagos, Dhaka, Jakarta, Manila and Mumbai house tens of millions of people within a metre or two of high water. The question of who is entitled to occupy which ground, which has run through this book from the commons of medieval Europe onward, is about to be asked again in a harsher form.

With this survey the book's review of the traditions and of the regions is complete. The final part returns to the present and to the rich countries with which the argument began. It considers what it means to call housing a right, examines the particular issues over which the traditions now contend, looks closely at Spain, and attempts a conclusion.

## Part Ten: The Present Argument

### Chapter 28: Housing as a Right

This book began with two views of a house, as an asset and as something close to a public good to which every citizen has a claim. The second view is most often expressed today in the language of rights. Constitutions, treaties and political programmes declare that everyone has a right to housing, and movements of tenants and of the homeless invoke it. The phrase is used so freely that its meaning is seldom examined. This chapter asks where the idea came from, what courts and legislatures have made of it, and what the declaration of such a right can and cannot accomplish.

#### The idea of a social right

The rights proclaimed by the American and French revolutions were rights against the state. Freedom of speech and worship, security from arbitrary arrest, and the protection of property all required government chiefly to refrain. The idea that a citizen might also have a right to receive something, to education, to subsistence or to shelter, belongs to a later period and to a different conception of what freedom requires. It was advanced by socialists in the revolutions of 1848, appeared in the Mexican constitution of 1917 and the German constitution of 1919, and was proclaimed with emphasis by the Soviet Union, whose constitution of 1936 guaranteed rest, education and maintenance in old age, and whose constitution of 1977 added a right to housing.

In the Western democracies the decisive statement came from Franklin Roosevelt. In his message to Congress in January 1944, with victory in the war in prospect, he argued that the political rights on which the republic had been founded had proved inadequate to assure equality in the pursuit of happiness. True individual freedom, he said, cannot exist without economic security and independence, for necessitous men are not free men, and people who are hungry and out of a job are the stuff of which dictatorships are made. He proposed a second bill of rights, among whose articles was the right of every family to a decent home. Roosevelt died the following year and the proposal was never enacted, but his widow, Eleanor Roosevelt, chaired the commission of the newly founded United Nations that drafted the Universal Declaration of Human Rights.

The Declaration, adopted in December 1948, states in its twenty-fifth article that everyone has the right to a standard of living adequate for the health and well being of himself and his family, including food, clothing, housing and medical care. It was not a treaty and created no legal obligation. When the United Nations came to convert it into binding law, the divisions of the Cold War led to the drafting of two separate covenants, both concluded in 1966. One covered civil and political rights, which the Western states regarded as the genuine article. The other covered economic, social and cultural rights, championed by the Soviet bloc and the newly independent countries. Article 11 of the second recognises the right of everyone to an adequate standard of living, including adequate housing, and to the continuous improvement of living conditions. More than a hundred and seventy states have ratified it. The United States signed it in 1977 and has never ratified it.

The wording of the covenant differs significantly from that of its companion. A state that ratifies the covenant on civil rights undertakes to respect and ensure those rights immediately. A state that ratifies the covenant on social rights undertakes to take steps, to the maximum of its available resources, with a view to achieving progressively the full realisation of the rights. The obligation is one of effort and direction, not of result, and for many years it was regarded as too vague to mean anything. The committee of experts that supervises the covenant set out to give it content. In a statement of 1991 it identified seven elements of adequacy: legal security of tenure, the availability of services and infrastructure, affordability, habitability, accessibility for disadvantaged groups, a location that allows access to employment and services, and cultural adequacy. It declared that the right should not be understood narrowly as a roof over one's head but as the right to live somewhere in security, peace and dignity. A further statement in 1997 dealt with forced evictions. These, the committee said, are in principle incompatible with the covenant. They may be carried out only in exceptional circumstances, after consultation and adequate notice, with legal remedies available, and should not result in individuals being rendered homeless.

The committee was equally clear about what the right does not require. It does not oblige a state to build housing for the entire population, or to provide housing free of charge to all who ask. It obliges the state to have a strategy, to give priority to those in the worst conditions, to refrain from making things worse, and to be able to show that it is doing what its resources allow. Since 2000 the United Nations has also appointed an independent expert, known as the special rapporteur on adequate housing, who visits countries and reports on their performance, and whose reports on the financialisation of housing were mentioned in Part Four.

The philosophical objections to the whole enterprise have been stated most forcefully by liberals in the tradition examined in Part Three. Hayek called social justice a mirage and denied that claims to particular benefits could be rights. A right, on this view, must correspond to a duty that someone can be identified as owing. My right not to be assaulted imposes on every other person a duty to refrain, which all can perform at once and at no cost. A right to housing imposes a duty to provide, but on whom? If on the state, then on the taxpayers, who are thereby compelled to work for the benefit of others, and the amount owed depends on resources that may not exist. Such claims, the argument runs, are aspirations dressed in the language of entitlement. They debase the currency of rights and cannot be enforced by courts, which have no means of building houses.

The replies have come from several quarters. The philosopher Henry Shue argued that the distinction between negative and positive rights does not survive inspection. The right not to be assaulted is worthless without police, courts and prisons, which are expensive and are paid for by compulsory taxation. The right to property requires land registries and bailiffs. Every right, if it is to be more than words, demands that the state both refrain from certain acts and provide certain institutions. The difference between civil and social rights is one of degree. The economist Amartya Sen has argued that what matters is a person's real capability to live a life he has reason to value, and that the absence of shelter destroys that capability as surely as censorship or imprisonment.

The most pointed argument was made by the legal philosopher Jeremy Waldron in an essay of 1991 on homelessness and freedom. Everything a person does, Waldron observed, has to be done somewhere. In a society where all land is either private property, from which the owner may exclude anyone, or public property subject to regulations, a person with no home has no place where he is entitled to be. If the authorities then prohibit sleeping, washing, cooking and urinating in public places, as many cities do, the homeless person is forbidden to perform these acts anywhere at all. Since they are acts that every human being must perform, he is in effect forbidden to exist. This is not a deprivation of welfare. It is a deprivation of liberty in the most traditional and negative sense, the liberty to act without being prevented by others. A society that values freedom, Waldron concluded, cannot be indifferent to whether its members have a place in which the basic actions of life may lawfully be carried out. The argument is notable because it rests the case for housing not on need or equality but on the liberal's own first principle.

#### Constitutions and courts

More than eighty national constitutions now refer to housing in some form. The great majority do so in language that commits the state to a policy without giving the citizen a claim. A smaller number have attempted to make the right enforceable, and their experience shows what courts can contribute.

South Africa has gone furthest in constitutional adjudication. Section 26 of the constitution of 1996 provides that everyone has the right to have access to adequate housing, that the state must take reasonable legislative and other measures, within its available resources, to achieve the progressive realisation of this right, and that no one may be evicted from their home or have it demolished without an order of court made after considering all the relevant circumstances. The leading case arose from a settlement near Cape Town called Wallacedene. Its residents lived in shacks on waterlogged ground, without water, sewerage or refuse removal, and had been on the waiting list for subsidised housing for years. In 1998 some nine hundred of them, including Irene Grootboom, moved onto a vacant hillside that was privately owned and had been set aside for low cost housing. They were evicted in the middle of the winter rains, their shacks were bulldozed and their possessions destroyed, and they took shelter on a sports field under plastic sheeting.

The Constitutional Court gave judgment in October 2000. It declined to hold that the constitution entitled each person to a house on demand, or even to a defined minimum of shelter, since the text spoke of reasonable measures and available resources. But it held that the reasonableness of the government's programme was a question the court could examine. The state had a housing programme, and by many measures an impressive one. That programme, however, was aimed entirely at building permanent houses over the medium and long term, and it made no provision whatever for people in desperate need who had no roof at all. A programme that excluded those in the most urgent circumstances, the court ruled, could not be reasonable, however statistically successful it might be. The government was ordered to devise and fund measures of immediate relief.

The judgment has been studied around the world as a demonstration that social rights can be adjudicated without judges taking over the budget. The court did not say what should be built or how much should be spent. It asked whether the policy was coherent, whether it attended to the worst off and whether it was being carried out, questions of a kind that courts routinely ask of administrative action. Later decisions built on it. The court has held that a municipality may not evict the occupants of unsafe inner city buildings without first engaging meaningfully with them about alternatives, and that when a private owner obtains an eviction order the city must provide emergency accommodation for those who would otherwise be on the street. The limits are equally well known. Mrs Grootboom died in 2008, aged thirty-nine, still living in a shack. The judgment changed the law and the policy. It did not produce a house for the woman who won it.

France has taken the legislative route. In the winter of 2006 an association named after Don Quixote distributed several hundred red tents to homeless people along a canal in the east of Paris and invited the housed to spend a night in them. The spectacle, coming shortly before a presidential election, prompted parliament to pass within weeks a law creating an enforceable right to housing. A person who is homeless, threatened with eviction without rehousing, in unfit or severely overcrowded accommodation, or who has waited an abnormally long time for social housing, may apply to a commission in his department. If the commission recognises the claim as urgent and a priority, the prefect must offer suitable housing within a set period, and if he fails the applicant may go to the administrative court, which can order the state to house him on pain of a daily penalty. The law has rehoused more than two hundred thousand households since it took effect in 2008. In the regions where housing is scarce, above all around Paris, tens of thousands of households recognised as having priority remain unhoused for years. The penalties the courts impose are paid by the state into a fund for social housing, that is to say by the state to itself, and the claimant receives no dwelling from the judgment. The law has established a right to be placed at the head of the queue, and where there is nothing at the end of the queue it can do no more.

The United Kingdom has imposed statutory duties without using the vocabulary of rights. An act of 1977 required local authorities to secure accommodation for households that were homeless through no fault of their own and fell into categories of priority need, chiefly families with children, pregnant women and the vulnerable. Scotland extended the duty in stages and from the end of 2012 abolished the test of priority need, so that every unintentionally homeless person there has a legal entitlement to settled accommodation, the strongest such right in Europe. In England the duty has been met, as social housing has dwindled, by placing families in temporary accommodation, where well over a hundred thousand households now live at any one time, at a cost of more than two billion pounds a year.

In Spain the constitutional guarantee, as noted earlier in this book, is a guiding principle that binds the legislature and cannot be invoked directly before a court. Some regions have gone beyond it. A Basque law of 2015 created a subjective right to the legal occupation of a decent dwelling, enforceable against the regional government, which must either provide a home for rent or, failing that, pay an allowance. Spain has also been the subject of several findings by the United Nations committee that supervises the covenant. In a case decided in 2017, concerning a family with two small children evicted from a rented room in Madrid at the end of their lease with nowhere to go, the committee held that the state had violated the right to housing by failing to ensure alternative accommodation, and recommended that evictions of vulnerable people should not be carried out without it. Spanish courts have since given some weight to these views.

The United States stands at the opposite pole. In 1972 the Supreme Court considered a challenge by tenants in Oregon to a summary eviction procedure, in which they argued that the need for decent shelter was a fundamental interest deserving special constitutional protection. The court rejected the argument. The constitution, it said, does not provide judicial remedies for every social and economic ill, and it contains no guarantee of access to dwellings of a particular quality. The assurance of adequate housing and the definition of the relationship between landlord and tenant are functions of the legislature. There has been one significant exception at state level. A lawsuit brought in 1979 on behalf of homeless men in New York City, relying on a clause of the state constitution about the care of the needy, ended in a settlement by which the city undertook to provide shelter to every person who asks for it. New York consequently has by far the largest shelter system in the country, accommodating more than a hundred thousand people on some nights, and almost no one sleeping in the open by comparison with the cities of the west coast. It is a right to a bed in a shelter, however, and not to a home.

Elsewhere in the United States the legal contest has concerned not a right to housing but the right to be without it. As encampments spread in western cities, municipalities passed ordinances against sleeping in public places. A federal appeals court held in 2018 that to punish people for sleeping outdoors when no shelter bed was available to them was cruel and unusual punishment. In June 2024 the Supreme Court reversed that line of authority, ruling in a case from a small town in Oregon that such ordinances regulate conduct and may be enforced whether or not the person has anywhere else to go. Waldron's argument had been presented to the country's highest court in the form of a constitutional claim, and it was rejected.

#### Homelessness, and what a right can and cannot do

The sharpest test of any claim that housing is a right is the person who has none. Homelessness in the literal sense had become rare in the rich countries by the 1960s. It returned from about 1980 and has grown in most of them since, and the pattern of its growth says a good deal about its causes.

The figures are imperfect and are defined differently in each country, but the orders of magnitude are clear. In the United States the official count on a single night in January 2024 found about seven hundred and seventy thousand people in shelters or sleeping outside, the highest number since the count began and nearly a fifth more than the year before. In England more than a hundred and twenty thousand households, including well over a hundred and fifty thousand children, were living in temporary accommodation arranged by local authorities. France was estimated to have some three hundred and thirty thousand people without a home of their own, twice as many as a decade earlier. Germany, Ireland, the Netherlands, Spain and Canada have all recorded increases. The exceptions are few. Finland, as described in Part Six, has reduced its total to under four thousand, and Japan counts only a few thousand people living on the streets, a small fraction of the number at the start of the century.

For a long time the public discussion of homelessness, particularly in the United States, treated it as a problem of individuals: of mental illness, addiction and broken families. These conditions are indeed far more common among homeless people than in the population at large, and they help to explain which persons become homeless. They do not explain why there is so much more homelessness in some places than others. Two American researchers, Gregg Colburn and Clayton Page Aldern, compared metropolitan areas across the country in a study published in 2022. The cities with the highest rates of homelessness, such as San Francisco, Seattle, Los Angeles, New York and Boston, did not have higher rates of drug use, mental illness or poverty than those with the lowest, such as Detroit, Cleveland, Cincinnati or the cities of West Virginia, where addiction is rife and incomes are low. What distinguished them was the cost of housing. Rates of homelessness tracked rents and vacancy rates closely and tracked nothing else. The authors offered an analogy with a game of musical chairs. Personal vulnerabilities determine who is left standing when the music stops. The number left standing is determined by the number of chairs.

The same reasoning underlies the approach known as Housing First, which originated in New York in the early 1990s with a psychologist named Sam Tsemberis and was adopted nationally in Finland. Controlled trials, the largest of them conducted in five Canadian cities with more than two thousand participants, have found that giving chronically homeless people with mental illness an ordinary tenancy at once, with support but without preconditions, keeps a large majority of them housed, at a cost largely offset by savings on hospitals, shelters and prisons. The method is effective at ending the homelessness of the individuals it reaches. It cannot reduce the total where the supply of affordable dwellings is not growing, since each flat given to one person is then unavailable to another, which is the difference between the Finnish result and the American.

Against this background it is possible to say with some precision what a legal right to housing achieves. Its critics are correct on the main point. A right builds nothing. Where dwellings are scarce, a right enforced by a court moves the successful claimant ahead of others who may be in equal need, or produces an order that cannot be executed, or, as in New York and England, generates an expensive system of shelters and temporary rooms that satisfies the letter of the duty. The countries with the most emphatic constitutional language, among them several in Latin America and the former Soviet bloc, are not the best housed, and the best housed, such as Austria, Switzerland, Singapore and Japan, have for the most part no justiciable right at all. Good housing is the product of a housing system, meaning land, finance, builders and institutions that function over decades, and no declaration can substitute for one.

Yet the supporters are also correct on several points that matter. First, a right changes the terms on which people can be removed from their homes. The South African requirement of a court order and consideration of alternatives, the committee's doctrine on forced evictions and the Scottish duty to rehouse have each prevented a great deal of homelessness at its source, by obliging landlords and authorities to deal with occupants as persons with standing and not as obstacles. Second, a right obliges a government to have a policy and to account for it. The Grootboom standard, that a programme must be reasonable and must not ignore those in greatest need, is a modest demand, and it is one that many governments could not meet. Third, a right alters the burden of justification. Where housing is merely a good, the person without it must explain why he should be helped. Where it is a right, the state must explain why he has not been. Campaigners value this shift because it converts a plea into a claim, and politicians resist it for the same reason.

There is a further consideration that bears on the central theme of this book. In constitutional systems the right to housing does not stand alone. It stands beside the right to property, which is everywhere guaranteed and everywhere enforceable. When a tenant resists eviction, a municipality rezones a site, or a legislature caps a rent, the owner can go to court and invoke a fundamental right, and judges must weigh it. If the occupant's interest in his home has no comparable legal status, the scales are tilted from the outset. Several constitutional courts, including those of Germany and South Africa, have developed a doctrine of balance, in which property is protected but is understood to carry social obligations, and the more a piece of property serves as the essential living space of others, the more regulation its owner must accept. The practical function of a right to housing may lie less in what it entitles any individual to receive than in the weight it places in that balance against the claims of ownership.

A right to housing is thus best understood neither as an empty slogan nor as a remedy. It is a statement of priority, made in the language that modern legal systems reserve for their most serious commitments, that the use of dwellings as homes ranks above their use as investments when the two conflict. Whether a society honours the statement depends on what it then builds, taxes, regulates and spends, which is to say on the choices among the traditions described in the earlier parts of this book.

### Chapter 29: Today's Battlegrounds

The traditions traced in this book are not confined to libraries. In the years since the financial crisis of 2008, and with greater intensity since the rise in rents that followed the pandemic of 2020, housing has moved to the centre of politics in almost every rich country, and the old arguments are being conducted again over a handful of specific questions. This chapter examines four of them: the regulation of rents, the letting of homes to tourists and their use as second homes, the conflict between generations and its bearing on building, and the combinations of policy that governments are assembling in response.

#### Rent regulation revisited

No issue in housing has been argued over for so long. Part Three described the wartime freezes and the liberal case against them, and noted the distinction between those hard controls and the more flexible systems introduced since the 1970s. For some decades the subject seemed closed. Controls were being dismantled, and almost no economist defended them. Since about 2015 they have returned, in a variety of new forms, to an extent that would have astonished observers a generation ago, and the evidence about their effects is now better than it has ever been.

The most careful single study concerns San Francisco. In 1994 the voters of that city extended rent control to small apartment buildings constructed before 1980, which had previously been exempt, while similar buildings constructed after 1980 remained uncontrolled. The economist Rebecca Diamond and two colleagues used this accident of dates to compare the two groups over the following two decades, tracing individual tenants through address records. Their findings, published in 2019, gave something to each side. Tenants in the newly controlled buildings were between ten and twenty per cent more likely to be living at the same address years later, and more likely to have remained in the city at all. The benefit was greatest for older households and long term residents, the people the measure was meant to protect. The control worked as insurance against displacement.

The owners of the controlled buildings responded as the market tradition would predict. They converted flats to condominiums for sale, redeveloped sites with new buildings that fell outside the law, or bought out their tenants. The supply of rental housing in the affected buildings fell by about fifteen per cent, and the authors calculated that this reduction had raised rents across the city by about five per cent. The cost of protecting sitting tenants was borne by future tenants, in an amount roughly equal to the benefit. The policy had also accelerated the replacement of modest rented housing by more expensive owner occupied housing, and so had contributed to the very change in the character of the city that its supporters had hoped to resist.

Subsequent experiments have repeated the pattern with local variations. The city of St Paul in Minnesota adopted by referendum in 2021 one of the strictest laws in the United States, limiting all rent increases to three per cent a year with no exemption for new buildings and no adjustment when a tenant left. Permits for new housing fell by more than four fifths in the months that followed, while they rose in the neighbouring city of Minneapolis, and within a year the council exempted new construction for twenty years. Berlin's five year freeze of 2020, described in Part Seven, lowered rents in the regulated stock, cut the number of flats offered by something like half and pushed up rents in the exempt sector before it was annulled. Scotland imposed an emergency cap on increases within existing tenancies in 2022. Since the cap did not apply to new lettings, landlords raised the rent sharply whenever a tenant changed, and advertised rents in Scotland rose faster than anywhere else in Britain during the period.

Catalonia has provided a much studied case in two phases. A regional law of September 2020 capped rents on new contracts in some sixty municipalities at the level of a reference index, until the constitutional court struck it down in 2022 on grounds of competence. Under the national housing law of 2023, the region declared most of its urban area a stressed market, and from March 2024 new contracts there may not exceed the previous rent or, for large landlords, the official index. Researchers examining the first phase reached conflicting conclusions. One group found that rents in the regulated municipalities fell by about five per cent with no measurable loss of supply. Another found a significant drop in the number of contracts signed, concentrated among the more expensive dwellings. In the second phase, official figures showed average rents on new contracts in Barcelona falling by several per cent in the first year, and the number of new long term contracts falling by a larger proportion, while lettings for periods of less than a year, which were outside the cap, multiplied until the regional parliament moved to regulate them too.

The Netherlands extended its system of regulated rents in July 2024 from the cheapest dwellings to the middle of the market, covering flats that could previously be let freely for up to about eleven hundred euros a month. The declared aim was to help teachers, nurses and police officers. Within months, investors were reported to be selling tens of thousands of affected flats to owner occupiers as tenancies ended, since the regulated rent no longer covered their costs, and the stock of rental housing for precisely that middle group contracted. Ireland has limited increases in designated pressure zones since 2016 and found that small landlords left the market in large numbers. It announced in 2025 a recasting of the system to allow rents to reset between tenancies in return for stronger security.

In the United States, where most states forbid cities to regulate rents at all, a different and milder form has spread. Oregon enacted in 2019 the first statewide law, limiting annual increases to seven per cent plus inflation and exempting buildings less than fifteen years old. California followed the same year with a ceiling of five per cent plus inflation, and Washington adopted a similar measure in 2025. These laws are set well above normal market increases. Their purpose is not to hold rents down in general but to prohibit the sudden rise of thirty or fifty per cent that forces a household out, a practice their supporters call rent gouging, and they are usually coupled with a requirement that a landlord show cause before ending a tenancy. Because they seldom bind, they have had little detectable effect on supply, and economists who oppose traditional controls have found less to object to in them.

From this accumulated experience a degree of agreement has emerged among those who study the subject, if not among those who campaign about it. Regulation that limits increases within a tenancy and protects against arbitrary eviction gives tenants real security at modest cost, and most of the best functioning rental systems, including the German and Swiss, have it. Regulation that holds the rent of a dwelling well below the market level across changes of tenant benefits those in place, reduces the number of dwellings offered for rent, and makes entry harder for those who are looking. The severity of the second effect depends on the detail: whether new buildings are exempt, whether owners can easily sell to owner occupiers or switch to tourists, and whether the state is building rental housing of its own to make up the loss.

The disagreement that remains is at bottom about whom policy is for. The tenant who is settled in a neighbourhood, with children at the local school and elderly relatives nearby, has a great deal to lose from displacement, and regulation serves him. The young person arriving in the city, the migrant and the family that needs to move are served by a plentiful supply of vacancies, and regulation tends to reduce it. Both interests are legitimate. A measure of the difficulty is that they are often the same people at different stages of life.

#### Tourist lets and second homes

A dwelling can be put to uses other than housing a resident, and two of them have become objects of intense dispute: letting by the night to visitors, and occupation for a few weeks a year by an owner whose main home is elsewhere. Both raise in a direct form the question with which this book has been concerned throughout, whether the owner of a home may do with it whatever pays best.

Short term letting on any scale is a creation of the internet. A company founded in San Francisco in 2008 by three young men who had rented out air mattresses in their flat during a conference built a website on which anyone could offer a room or a whole home to travellers. Within ten years it listed several million properties in almost every country. The original picture, of residents earning a little from a spare room, soon ceased to describe the business. In the cities most popular with visitors, the majority of listings came to be entire flats available all year, and a large share were controlled by operators with many properties. A flat in the centre of Barcelona, Lisbon, Florence or Amsterdam let to tourists could earn in a week what a resident tenant paid in a month.

Research on the effects has reached reasonably consistent conclusions. A study covering the whole of the United States found that growth in short term listings raised local rents and house prices by a small amount on average, with larger effects in neighbourhoods where few owners lived on the premises. A study of Barcelona estimated that tourist lets had raised rents by about two per cent across the city and by around seven per cent in the districts where they were most concentrated, with somewhat larger effects on sale prices. The average effect on a metropolitan market is thus modest, and economists are right to say that tourist flats do not explain the general rise in housing costs. The effect in particular neighbourhoods is substantial, and it is accompanied by changes that do not show in prices: the disappearance of neighbours, the conversion of grocers and hardware shops into luggage stores and bars, the noise of wheeled suitcases at night, the sense among long term residents that they have become extras in someone else's holiday.

Cities have responded with increasing severity. Amsterdam allows a home to be let for no more than thirty nights a year. Paris reduced its limit from a hundred and twenty nights to ninety and employs a squad of inspectors. Berlin banned the letting of entire flats without a permit under a law against the misuse of housing. New York began enforcing in September 2023 a rule that a stay of less than thirty days is lawful only if the host is present and has registered, which removed something like four fifths of the listings in the city within months. Early assessments found that hotel prices rose and that there was no evident fall in rents, which opponents took as proof that the measure was misconceived and supporters as proof that the flats had not yet returned to the long term market.

Spain has been at the centre of the conflict. It is the second most visited country in the world, with more than ninety million foreign arrivals in a year, and tourism is its largest industry. The number of registered tourist dwellings rose to around four hundred thousand. In the Canary Islands in April 2024 tens of thousands marched under the slogan that the islands have a limit, and demonstrations followed in Málaga, Palma de Mallorca, San Sebastián and Barcelona, where in July of that year protesters sprayed diners on restaurant terraces with water pistols, an image that travelled around the world. The complaint of the demonstrators was only partly about crowds. Its core was that workers in the tourist industry could no longer find anywhere to live in the places where they were employed, and there were reports of hotel staff and teachers in Ibiza sleeping in vans and tents.

The mayor of Barcelona announced in June 2024 that the city would not renew any of its ten thousand or so licences for tourist flats when they expire in November 2028, with the aim of returning them all to residential use. The measure rests on a Catalan decree that makes such licences temporary and subject to municipal planning, and it has been challenged in the courts by owners who argue that it amounts to expropriation without compensation. The national government introduced in 2025 a compulsory register for all short term rentals and ordered the largest platform to remove tens of thousands of listings that lacked a valid licence number. Communities of owners in apartment buildings were given the power to prohibit tourist lets in their building by a vote of three fifths.

Second homes present the same issue in a slower form. In mountain and coastal regions across Europe, a growing share of the housing stock is owned by people from the cities or from abroad who use it for holidays, and local people on local wages cannot compete with them. Swiss voters approved in 2012, by a narrow majority and against the advice of their government, a constitutional initiative that prohibits the construction of new second homes in any commune where they already exceed a fifth of the stock, a limit that applies to most Alpine resorts. In England, the residents of the Cornish town of St Ives voted in 2016 to require that all new dwellings be occupied as a principal residence. Wales allows its councils to charge owners of second homes up to four times the ordinary local tax. Norway has long permitted municipalities to impose a duty of residence, under which a house in a designated area must be lived in all year round, and Denmark and parts of Austria restrict purchases by people who are not resident. The most recent variant concerns people who can work from anywhere. The arrival of foreign remote workers with salaries from richer countries in Lisbon, Mexico City and Medellín has provoked protests there against what is called gentrification by digital nomads.

The arguments on each side are by now familiar from the rest of this book. The owner invokes his right of property and the freedom to contract, and points out that tourism brings income and employment, that many hosts are ordinary families supplementing a pension or a wage, and that the numbers involved are too small to account for the shortage of housing, which results from the failure to build. The restrictions, on this view, are a way for politicians to appear to act without confronting the real problem. The resident replies that a dwelling was given planning permission, connected to public services and counted in the city's housing stock as a place for someone to live, and that converting it into a hotel room is a change of use that the community is entitled to control, as it would control the conversion of a flat into a factory. Where housing is short, each home withdrawn matters.

Whatever the economic weight of these uses, their political significance is considerable. The tourist flat and the empty holiday house make visible, in a way that statistics on land values do not, the difference between a dwelling as a home and a dwelling as a source of income. A neighbour replaced by a key safe on the wall is an argument that anyone can understand. That is why these issues have mobilised people who had never before taken part in a protest about housing, and why governments of every political colour, including those most attached to the rights of property, have found themselves restricting what owners may do.

#### Generations, demography and the politics of building

The housing question of the nineteenth century divided classes. The housing question of the twenty-first divides generations, and that change has rearranged the politics of the subject in ways that the inherited ideologies are still struggling to absorb.

The basic facts have been given in earlier parts. In Britain, the United States, Canada, Australia, Ireland, Spain and many other countries, people who bought homes before the long rise in prices that began in the 1980s and 1990s hold them at a value several times what they paid, largely free of debt. People who reached adulthood after about 2000 have faced prices that had risen far faster than earnings. In England a person born in the early 1980s was about half as likely to own a home by the age of thirty as one born twenty years earlier. In the United States the median age of a first time buyer has risen from the late twenties to around forty. The young rent for longer, and in most of southern and eastern Europe, where rented housing is scarce, they remain with their parents. The average age at which young people leave the parental home is about thirty in Spain, Italy and Greece and higher still in Croatia and Slovakia, against twenty-one or twenty-two in Sweden, Denmark and Finland, where rented flats and student housing are available.

The transmission of housing wealth through families has become correspondingly important. In Britain around half of first time buyers now receive money from parents or grandparents, a source so significant that it has been ranked among the country's larger mortgage lenders. Those whose families cannot help are at a disadvantage that no amount of saving from a salary can easily overcome. Inheritances, which are growing as the generation of owners ages, will go predominantly to people whose parents owned property in expensive places. Economists who study the distribution of wealth have observed that a society in which the main asset is passed down in this manner is moving back towards the condition that the liberal revolutions were supposed to have ended, in which a person's prospects depend more on birth than on effort.

The effect on family formation, discussed in connection with East Asia, is evident in Europe and North America as well. Studies in Britain, the United States and elsewhere have found that rising house prices reduce births among renters and raise them slightly among owners, with the net effect negative, and that young adults who cannot obtain independent housing postpone partnership and children. Governments worried about falling birth rates and the financing of pensions have begun to notice that their housing markets are working against them.

These changes have altered the political alignments. For most of the twentieth century housing was contested between left and right on lines of class. Today the more reliable predictor of a person's view on most housing questions is whether he owns. Owners of all incomes and parties tend to oppose new building near them, resist taxes on property and favour policies that support prices. Renters and those still with their parents tend to want more building, more regulation of landlords and lower prices. Since owners are older and older people vote in greater numbers, and since in most rich countries they are still a majority of households, their preferences have generally prevailed. Political scientists have connected the frustration of the excluded with the weakening of attachment to established parties among the young, and with support for insurgent movements on both the left and the right.

The question of building cuts across the traditional camps in a manner described in Part Three. A further complication has arisen with the growth of immigration to many rich countries. Where the population is rising quickly through migration and the housing stock is not, the pressure on rents is real, and parties of the nationalist right in the Netherlands, Ireland, Germany, Canada, Britain and elsewhere have made the link between immigration and the housing shortage central to their appeal. The government of Canada, long among the most favourable to immigration, announced in 2024 a reduction in the numbers of temporary workers and foreign students, citing the strain on housing. Opponents of this line of argument reply that migrants also build a large share of the houses, that the shortage long predates the recent arrivals and that the cause lies in decades of insufficient construction. Both statements can be true at once. A housing system that cannot expand will convert any increase in population, from whatever source, into higher prices and resentment.

Environmental concerns add another dimension. One strand of green thinking favours dense, compact cities, in which people live in flats near public transport and use less energy, and so supports building upward within existing urban areas. Another emphasises the protection of open land, trees, historic neighbourhoods and local character, and the carbon released in demolition and new construction, and so tends to oppose particular schemes. The two meet in conflict at countless planning hearings. Requirements that new buildings meet high standards of energy efficiency raise their cost, and the retrofitting of existing dwellings, which is necessary if emissions targets are to be met, raises the question of who pays, the owner, the tenant through the rent or the taxpayer.

Behind all of this lies a problem of capacity that receives less attention than it deserves. Even where land is made available and permission is granted, houses have to be built, and the building industry in most rich countries has not become more productive in half a century. Measured output per worker in construction in the United States is lower than it was in the 1960s, while in manufacturing it has multiplied. Dwellings are still largely assembled by hand on site by an ageing workforce of separate trades. Attempts to manufacture housing in factories have a long history of commercial failure, though they continue. Since 2021 the cost of materials, labour and borrowing has risen sharply, and in several countries the number of homes started has fallen in spite of acute shortage and government targets, because at current costs and prices many schemes do not pay. A group of American writers has argued under the banner of abundance that the liberal left in particular has for decades concentrated on subsidising demand and on procedures that allow projects to be blocked, and neglected the simple matter of whether anything gets built. The criticism applies more widely than to one party or one country.

The generational divide gives the present moment a particular character. In the past, those who suffered from bad housing and those who benefited from the system were separate groups with separate organisations. Now they are frequently members of the same family. Parents who have gained from rising prices watch their adult children unable to leave home, and find that their own interest as owners conflicts with their interest as parents. That personal experience, repeated in millions of households, may in time do more to change attitudes towards housing than any argument of economists or reformers.

#### The policy mixes governments reach for

Faced with these pressures, governments in the 2020s have rarely chosen among the traditions. They have drawn on all of them at once, and the programmes that result are a useful index of how the ideologies stand in practice.

A typical national housing plan of the present decade contains the following elements in some proportion. There is a target for construction, expressed as a number of homes over a term of office: a million and a half in five years in Britain, a similar figure in Germany, three million promised by a presidential candidate in the United States, several hundred thousand in Ireland, Canada and Australia. There are measures to speed the granting of permission, by overriding local objections, relaxing zoning near transport or reducing the scope for legal challenge, which come from the market tradition. There are subsidies and guarantees to help first time buyers with a deposit or a mortgage, which come from the conservative one. There is funding, usually modest, for social or affordable housing built by public and non-profit bodies, and a reference to Vienna, which come from the social democratic. There are new protections for tenants, such as limits on rent increases and on eviction without cause. There are restrictions on tourist lets, foreign buyers and institutional investors, and taxes on vacant homes and land, which express the view that housing is for residents first. And there is frequently a public body charged with assembling land or building directly.

The breadth of such programmes reflects a political calculation. Each element answers a constituency, and none can be omitted without offence. It also reflects a genuine shift in opinion. A generation ago the prevailing view in finance ministries and international organisations was that governments should withdraw from housing, deregulate, and assist the poor through allowances. That confidence has gone. Institutions that once recommended liberalisation now speak of the need for public investment in affordable housing, and governments of the centre right have introduced rent regulations and restrictions on purchasers that their predecessors would have denounced.

The difficulty is that several of these elements work against one another, and a government that adopts them all may find their effects cancelling out. Help for buyers adds to demand, and where supply is slow to respond it raises the prices that the construction target is meant to restrain. Controls on rents may reduce the private investment in new rental building on which the construction target depends. Obligations on developers to include affordable homes, pay for infrastructure and meet higher environmental standards each raise the cost of building and reduce the number of schemes that proceed. Measures to support the incomes of pensioners, protect the value of their homes and shield them from property taxes conflict directly with measures to make housing cheaper for their grandchildren. The typical plan contains no account of how these tensions are to be resolved, because to give one would be to say who is to lose.

The underlying dilemma was stated at the outset of this book and has reappeared in every part. In a country where most households own their homes and hold most of their wealth in that form, a policy that succeeded in making housing substantially cheaper would make the majority poorer, and a policy that protects the wealth of the majority keeps housing expensive for those who do not yet own. Politicians of all parties therefore promise that housing will become more affordable while prices continue to rise gently, a combination that is possible only if incomes rise faster than prices for a long period. Some ministers have begun to say this openly. The housing minister of Canada remarked in 2025 that the government did not want house prices to fall, and the statement, though widely criticised, was an honest description of the position of almost every government.

If there is a direction in which informed opinion has been moving, it could be summarised under three heads. The first is supply. There is now wide agreement, extending well beyond the market tradition, that prosperous cities have built too little for decades and that the rules and processes that prevent building must be changed. The second is a non-market sector. There is renewed acceptance that the market will not house those on the lowest incomes without help, and that the most durable form of help is a stock of dwellings held permanently by public or non-profit owners at rents related to cost, on the model of the systems that have lasted. The third is security for those who rent. As renting has become the long term condition of a large part of the population in countries where it was once a stage of youth, the case for tenancies that allow a family to stay and to plan has been accepted by governments that formerly regarded such protection as an interference with the market.

A fourth element, the treatment of land values, commands much less agreement, for the reasons set out in Part Eight. Yet without it the other three are constantly undermined. New supply on land bought at speculative prices is expensive supply. A non-market sector that must pay the market price for sites remains small. And the gains from every public improvement continue to flow to those who already own. The countries that have done best over the long run, as this book has tried to show, are those that found some way of preventing the value of land from being captured privately, whether by owning it, taxing it, limiting what could be charged for it or allowing so much to be built that it ceased to be scarce.

The argument is best pursued further in a particular case. The chapter that follows examines one country in which almost every policy described in this book has been tried within living memory, and in which the housing question has become, in the last few years, the first concern of the public.

### Chapter 30: Spain

Spain has appeared in almost every part of this book. Its liberal governments of the nineteenth century sold off the lands of the Church and the commons. Its dictatorship set out to create a nation of proprietors. Its constitution proclaims a right to housing and a public share in land values. It experienced one of the largest property bubbles in history and one of the most severe crashes, gave rise to one of the most effective housing movements in Europe, became the largest market on the continent for international funds buying distressed homes, and has lately adopted a national law regulating rents. Few countries illustrate so completely, within a single lifetime, the consequences of treating housing first as an asset. This chapter draws the threads together.

#### The inheritance and the bubble

The foundations of the Spanish housing system were laid between the 1950s and the 1970s, in the manner described in Part Five. Three features of that period proved lasting. The first was the choice of ownership as the goal of policy. Public support went to dwellings built for sale, and the ownership of individual flats was given a secure legal form in 1960. The second was the destruction of renting. Rents on existing contracts were frozen and tenancies made heritable by the law on urban leases of 1964, so that owning a building let to tenants became a liability, and the share of households renting fell from more than half in 1950 to under a fifth by the 1990s. The third was the temporary character of protection. A dwelling built with public subsidy carried restrictions on its price for a fixed term, typically twenty or thirty years, after which it could be sold freely. Spain subsidised the construction of more than six million such dwellings between the 1950s and the present century and retained almost none of them as a permanent public or social stock.

The democratic governments that followed the death of Franco in 1975 inherited these arrangements and reinforced them. Buyers of a main residence could deduct a proportion of their mortgage payments from income tax, a relief that lasted until 2013. A decree of 1985 freed new rental contracts from control, allowing landlords to let for a year at a time, which made renting insecure without making it plentiful, and a law of 1994 restored a minimum term of five years. Social housing for rent remained negligible, at about two per cent of the stock, against a fifth or more in the Netherlands, Austria or Denmark. A Spanish family that wished for security had one option, which was to buy, and to buy as early as possible. The country reached the end of the century with more than eighty per cent of households in owner occupation, the highest proportion in western Europe.

The boom that began in the late 1990s was built on this base. Its causes have been touched on in Part Four. Entry into the euro in 1999 brought Spanish interest rates down from double figures to the German level, and since Spanish inflation ran higher than the European average, the real cost of borrowing was for several years close to zero. Banks extended the term of mortgages to thirty, forty and even fifty years, lent the full value of the property and sometimes more, and financed themselves by selling bonds abroad. The population grew by about six million in a decade through immigration. Northern Europeans bought holiday and retirement homes along the Mediterranean. And a national land law of 1998, passed by the conservative government of José María Aznar, declared that all land not expressly protected for its environmental or other value was eligible for development, in the belief that a plentiful supply of building land would lower prices.

The response of the building industry was without precedent in Europe. Housing starts rose from about three hundred thousand a year in the mid 1990s to more than seven hundred and fifty thousand in 2006, a number exceeding those of Germany, France and Italy together, in a country with a fraction of their combined population. Construction came to employ about one worker in eight. Yet prices did not fall. The average price of housing roughly tripled in nominal terms between 1997 and 2007, and the price of urban land rose about fivefold. The episode is a difficulty for any simple version of the supply argument, and the explanation is the one given in Part Eight. When credit is abundant and buyers expect prices to keep rising, the value of every additional permission to build is captured by the owner of the land, who can hold out for a price that reflects the expected selling price of the finished homes. More land was made available, and it was bought and sold at ever higher prices in anticipation of the gains.

The institutions that financed the boom were the regional savings banks. These were foundations without shareholders, originally charitable, whose boards had come to be dominated by appointees of regional and municipal governments. They lent to developers who were often connected with the same politicians, on land that the same municipalities had reclassified for building, and the municipalities drew a large part of their revenue from the fees, taxes and compulsory cessions that development generated. The circle of interests was closed. A succession of corruption cases, of which the investigation into the town hall of Marbella in 2006 was the most spectacular, revealed how planning decisions had been bought.

The physical results can still be seen. At Seseña, on the dry plain south of Madrid, a single developer began an estate intended for more than thirteen thousand flats beside a motorway, far from any town, without an assured water supply. In Guadalajara province a new town was laid out around a station of the high speed railway for thirty thousand residents and attracted a small fraction of that number. Along the coast of Valencia and Murcia, resorts with thousands of apartments and golf courses were started and left unfinished. When the census was taken in 2011 it recorded about three and a half million empty dwellings in the country, more than one in eight, many of them in places where nobody wished to live, while in the large cities young people could not afford a home.

The boom changed the country in ways that outlasted it. It persuaded a generation that property could only rise in value and that a mortgage of forty years at a variable rate was a prudent commitment. It drew hundreds of thousands of young men out of education and into building work that would shortly disappear. It left the banking system holding loans to developers equal to something like a third of national output. And it used up, for a product that in large part proved worthless, a quantity of land, capital and public trust that would be badly needed in the years that followed.

#### The crash, the evictions and the movement of the mortgage victims

The Spanish property market stopped in the second half of 2007, and what followed was the deepest economic crisis in the country since the civil war. Sales of new homes fell by more than three quarters. Developers went bankrupt by the thousand, and with them the builders, suppliers and estate agents who had depended on them. Unemployment rose from eight per cent to twenty-six per cent of the workforce, and to more than half among those under twenty-five. House prices fell by more than a third on average and by half or more on the coast and the outer fringes of cities. The savings banks, loaded with loans to developers that would never be repaid and with repossessed land that nobody would buy, concealed their condition for several years. Seven of them were merged in 2010 into a new bank that was floated on the stock exchange and collapsed two years later, and in 2012 the government requested a loan of up to a hundred billion euros from its European partners to recapitalise the banking system. The toxic property assets were transferred to a publicly backed company created for the purpose.

For households the mechanism of the disaster lay in the law of mortgages. A family that had bought at the peak with a loan of the full price found, when one or both earners lost their jobs, that it could not meet the payments. The bank began proceedings, the home was put up for auction, and if nobody bid, as was normal in a collapsed market, the bank could take the property itself at a fraction of its appraised value, at that time half. The amount so credited was deducted from the debt, and the family remained liable for the rest, together with interest on arrears at penal rates and the costs of the proceedings. Spanish law, unlike that of several American states, gave the lender recourse to all the present and future assets of the borrower. A couple could lose their home and still owe the bank a sum they would never be able to pay, with their wages subject to attachment for life. Parents who had guaranteed their children's loans, as many had, were pursued for their own homes. Immigrant families, who had bought late and had commonly guaranteed one another's mortgages in chains, were hit hardest of all.

Several hundred thousand foreclosure proceedings were begun in the years after 2008, and tens of thousands of families were evicted from their main residence each year at the height of the crisis. The banks receiving these homes were at the same time being rescued with public money.

The response came from below. A small group in Barcelona, who had campaigned during the boom under a slogan warning the young that they would never be able to afford a home, founded in February 2009 an organisation called the Platform for People Affected by Mortgages, known by its Spanish initials as the PAH. Its method was the weekly assembly. People facing foreclosure, who typically arrived ashamed and convinced that their situation was their own fault, heard others describe the same experience and learned that it was shared by thousands. The assembly then advised each case collectively. The movement's first demand was a change in the law so that surrender of the property would cancel the debt, and it promoted a popular legislative initiative to that effect, which gathered almost a million and a half signatures, three times the number required, and was nevertheless set aside by the parliamentary majority in 2013.

Its second activity was direct. From November 2010 members and supporters gathered in numbers at the door of a family due to be evicted and physically prevented the court officers from entering, a tactic that stopped more than two thousand evictions in the following years and obliged banks to negotiate. When that failed, the platform rehoused families in empty blocks owned by rescued banks, as described in Part Seven. It also adopted from Argentina the practice of demonstrating outside the homes of members of parliament who opposed its proposals. The movement spread to more than two hundred local groups, kept strictly independent of political parties and trade unions, and won a degree of public sympathy that opinion polls put at over eighty per cent. It was a principal current in the wider protest that filled the squares of Spanish cities from 15 May 2011.

The courts gave the movement its most concrete victories. A Moroccan immigrant named Mohamed Aziz, evicted from his home near Barcelona in 2011, challenged the foreclosure with the help of a lawyer who argued that the mortgage contract contained unfair terms. The judge referred the question to the Court of Justice of the European Union, which ruled in March 2013 that the Spanish foreclosure procedure was contrary to European consumer law, because it did not allow the court hearing the repossession to consider whether the terms of the loan were abusive or to suspend the eviction while the matter was decided. Parliament had to amend the law. Later rulings from the same court condemned clauses that had prevented borrowers from benefiting when interest rates fell, and obliged the banks to refund several billion euros. Meanwhile, after a number of suicides of people about to be evicted, among them a former councillor in the Basque Country who threw herself from her balcony as the officers climbed the stairs in November 2012, the government decreed a moratorium on the eviction of especially vulnerable families, which has been extended repeatedly.

The movement entered the institutions in 2015, when its best known spokeswoman, Ada Colau, was elected mayor of Barcelona at the head of a citizens' platform, and allied lists won the town halls of Madrid, Valencia, Zaragoza and other cities. The Catalan parliament passed in the same year the law on housing emergencies that the platform had drafted, described in Part Seven. The experience of municipal government proved sobering. Spanish town halls have limited powers and budgets in housing, the main competences belong to the regions and the main levers of taxation and tenancy law to the state, and the new mayors found that they could build or buy only a few hundred homes a year.

A further consequence of the crash shaped the decade that followed. The banks and the public asset company needed to dispose of several hundred thousand repossessed dwellings and of the loans secured on many more. They sold them in large portfolios, at heavy discounts, to international investment funds, a process encouraged by a favourable tax regime for listed property companies.
