The city of Madrid sold some eighteen hundred publicly owned rental flats to one such fund in 2013, and the regional government sold nearly three thousand to another, transactions later criticised by the audit authorities and in part annulled by the courts. Within a few years a country that had scarcely known the corporate landlord had become one of its principal markets in Europe, and the largest private owner of rental housing in Spain was an American private equity firm. The homes built for sale in the boom, and lost by their buyers in the crash, returned to the market as rental property owned from abroad.

#### The rental crisis and the housing law of 2023

The crisis that grips Spain today is in a sense the reverse of the one that preceded it. The crash of 2008 was a crisis of owners with debts they could not pay. The present one is a crisis of people who cannot become owners and cannot afford to rent, and it has arisen in a country whose institutions were designed on the assumption that everyone would buy.

After the crash the banks, chastened and newly regulated, required deposits of a fifth of the price or more and proof of stable income. A generation that entered the labour market in the years of mass unemployment, on temporary contracts and low wages, could meet neither condition. They rented. The proportion of households renting, which had fallen to under a fifth at the height of the boom, rose to about a quarter, and among people under thirty-five to more than half. This new demand met a rental sector that was small, owned mostly by individuals with one or two properties, and lightly regulated. A reform of 2013 had cut the minimum term of a tenancy from five years to three in the hope of drawing more owners into the market. At the same moment the short term letting of flats to tourists was expanding rapidly in exactly the central districts of Madrid, Barcelona, Valencia, Málaga, Seville and the island capitals where the young wanted to live, and investment funds were buying buildings.

Rents rose accordingly. In the principal cities and the coastal and island provinces, the rents asked on new contracts roughly doubled in the ten years after 2014, a period in which wages rose by about a fifth. Households renting at market prices in Spain devote a larger share of their income to housing than those in almost any other European country, and around two fifths of them spend more than forty per cent. The average age at which young Spaniards leave their parents' home passed thirty, the highest in western Europe. Sharing a flat, once a stage of student life, became the normal condition of employed adults in their thirties, and rooms in shared flats in Madrid and Barcelona were advertised at prices that a decade earlier would have rented a whole apartment. The national statistical survey of public concerns placed housing first, above unemployment, for the first time since the question was asked.

Construction, meanwhile, never recovered. After building far too much in the wrong places, Spain has for fifteen years built about a hundred thousand dwellings a year, while the number of households has been growing by two hundred thousand or more, in large part through renewed immigration. The central bank estimated in 2024 that the country had accumulated a shortfall of some six hundred thousand homes, concentrated in the big cities and tourist areas. The construction workforce had aged or emigrated, developers found it hard to obtain finance, the cost of materials rose sharply after 2021, and the process of making land ready for building under the planning system takes a decade or more. The millions of dwellings recorded as empty stand for the most part in depopulated rural provinces and in the failed developments of the boom, far from where the need is.

The first legislative response extended the security of tenants. A decree of 2019 restored the minimum term of a tenancy to five years, or seven where the landlord is a company, and limited deposits. The larger measure took four years to negotiate within the governing coalition of socialists and the left. The Law for the Right to Housing, which came into force in May 2023, was the first national housing statute of the democratic period, and it draws on several of the traditions described in this book.

Its best known provisions concern rents. A regional government may declare an area to be a stressed residential market if the average cost of rent or mortgage plus basic utilities exceeds thirty per cent of average household income there, or if prices have risen more than three percentage points above inflation over five years. In such an area the rent on a new contract may not exceed that of the previous one, apart from the permitted annual adjustment, and where the landlord is a large holder, defined as an owner of more than ten dwellings or of five within the stressed area, the rent may not exceed a ceiling set by an official index of reference prices. Throughout the country, annual increases on existing contracts were limited to two per cent in 2023 and three per cent in 2024, and from 2025 are tied to a new index designed to rise more slowly than consumer prices. The fees of letting agents were made payable by the landlord.

Other provisions address the stock. Dwellings built with public subsidy on land reserved for the purpose are henceforth to keep their protected status permanently, and elsewhere for at least thirty years, which closes, for the future, the leak through which the public investment of sixty years passed into private hands. Publicly owned housing may not be sold to private investors. New developments must reserve a larger share of their floor area for protected housing, and half of that for rent. Municipalities may add a surcharge of up to one and a half times the property tax on dwellings left empty without good reason for more than two years. Before a vulnerable household can be evicted, the court must be informed of its situation and the social services given time to seek an alternative.

The law at once became a party question. Housing is a competence of the autonomous communities, and the national statute could only offer them instruments. Catalonia applied the rent ceilings from March 2024 to well over a hundred municipalities containing most of its population, and the Basque Country, Navarre and a few others have followed in particular cities. The regions governed by the conservative Popular Party, including Madrid, Andalusia, Valencia and Galicia, declined to declare any stressed areas. They argued that controls reduce supply and that the answer lies in making land available, cutting the taxes and delays that burden construction, giving owners confidence by speeding the recovery of dwellings from tenants who do not pay and from illegal occupants, and guaranteeing rents. Their critics replied that Madrid had the fastest rising rents in the country. The early results in Catalonia, discussed in the previous chapter, gave each side a statistic: rents on new contracts fell and so did the number of contracts, as owners moved to seasonal lettings, sold, or withdrew.

The public has not waited on the argument. Demonstrations called by tenants' unions and neighbourhood groups brought large crowds into the streets of Madrid in October 2024 and of Barcelona the following month, and in April 2025 marches took place simultaneously in some forty cities under a slogan calling for an end to the housing business. Speakers demanded a compulsory reduction in rents, indefinite tenancies, the recovery of tourist and empty flats for residents, and a prohibition on buying homes for purposes other than living in them, and some called for a rent strike. The prime minister announced in January 2025 a further package that included the creation of a state housing company to build and manage rental homes on public land, the transfer to it of tens of thousands of dwellings and plots held by the public asset company left over from the crash, tax advantages for landlords who let at regulated prices, tighter treatment of tourist flats, and a proposal to tax at up to the full value of the property purchases by buyers from outside the European Union who are not resident. The scheme of residence permits in exchange for property investment was ended in April of that year.

Spain today thus contains, in active contention, every position described in this book. There are those who hold that the country suffers from too little market and those who hold that it suffers from too much. There is the conservative attachment to the family that owns, embodied in the three quarters of households that still do, and in the parents and grandparents whose flats are the only route to a home for many of the young. There is a social democratic attempt, begun very late, to build a permanent stock of public rental housing where almost none exists. There is a movement that speaks the language of rights and of the social function of property, and that has shown it can fill the streets. And beneath all of them lies a history in which public money, public land and the savings of two generations were converted into private assets whose value the present generation is now being asked to pay.

### Conclusion

This book set out to explain the different approaches that different ideologies take to housing, and to follow each of them through the places and periods in which it was put into practice. The introduction proposed that the disagreements could be reduced to three questions: what a house is for, who should receive the value of land, and how homes should be allocated among those who need them. It remains to say what the record shows about the answers that each tradition gave, and what general conclusions the history supports.

#### What each tradition got right and wrong

The market liberal tradition holds that a house is a commodity, that land values belong to the owner and that allocation should be left to prices. It has been right about a number of things that its opponents were slow to admit. Freezing rents shrank and degraded the stock of rented housing wherever it was tried for long, from Vienna and Paris between the wars to Bombay and Cairo. Administrative allocation produced queues, privilege and immobility. And the restriction of building in prosperous cities, by zoning in the United States and discretionary planning in Britain, has raised the cost of housing for everyone outside the circle of existing owners, a point on which the evidence is now very strong. The tradition has been wrong, or silent, about the bottom of the market. No country has housed its poorest citizens decently through the market alone, and where markets operate with least interference, in the shack settlements of African cities, they deliver what the poor can pay for, which is very little. It has also underestimated the degree to which owners, acting rationally, will use political power to restrict supply, and it has had little to say about the consequences of making housing the foundation of a credit system.

The view of the house as a financial product, which is less a doctrine than a condition that arose from the way homes came to be paid for, has likewise a mixed record. Long mortgages, an invention of governments, enabled families of ordinary means to acquire homes and accumulate savings, and that achievement should not be belittled. But the expansion of credit after 1980 flowed mainly into the price of existing land, and the booms it financed in Japan, the United States, Ireland, Spain and China ended in crashes whose costs fell on the last to buy and on the public. A society whose savings, pensions and banks rest on the price of homes has, it turns out, made a commitment to keeping that price high, and has thereby given up much of its freedom to make housing affordable.

The conservative tradition holds that a house is the basis of family life and citizenship, and that policy should make as many people as possible the owners of their homes. It was right about what people want. Security, control over one's surroundings and something to hand on are deep and legitimate desires, and families who obtained them through ownership in the middle of the twentieth century valued them highly. It was right also that a society of small proprietors is a stable one. Its error lay in the means. Tax privileges, subsidised credit, grants to buyers and the discounted sale of public housing each raised what purchasers could pay without increasing what there was to buy, enriched the generation that received them and raised the barrier for the next. The property-owning democracy reached a ceiling in every country that pursued it and then began to recede, and the owners it created became the most determined opponents of the building that would have allowed their children to join them. Where the policy excluded a group at the outset, as in the United States, the exclusion was inherited.

The social democratic tradition holds that a home is a social right and that the community should guarantee it by providing a large stock of good housing outside the market and by regulating the rest. It has to its credit the abolition of the slum in the industrial countries, which was accomplished very largely by public and non-profit building, and the creation in cities such as Vienna, Zurich, Copenhagen and Helsinki of systems that have housed broad populations well for generations. Its failures were of two kinds. Where it built in haste and in bulk, on isolated sites, for the poor alone, the estates declined, and their decline was used to discredit the whole idea. And where it allocated by queue and held rents far below the market without continuing to build, it favoured those already housed over newcomers. The lesson of its successes is that a non-market sector must be broad, of good quality, steadily enlarged, held permanently and supplied with land at prices unrelated to speculation. Those conditions have been met in few places, and they require a continuity of purpose over decades that democratic politics does not easily supply.

The socialist tradition holds that housing should not be a commodity at all. In its comprehensive form it removed the landlord, reduced the cost of housing to a trifle and, after long delay, rehoused populations on a scale never seen before. It also produced permanent shortage, dependence on officials and employers, uniformity and neglect, because it abolished prices without finding any adequate substitute for the information and incentives they supply. When its citizens were offered ownership they took it, and the countries of state socialism are now the most thoroughly owner occupied in the world. In its smaller scale forms, the cooperative and the land trust, the same conviction has produced some of the most admirable housing anywhere, on the condition that resale at a profit is prevented and that a public authority supplies land and credit. These forms have stayed small, and whether they can be more than islands is an open question.

The tradition of Henry George holds that the building belongs to its maker and the value of the site to the community. Its analysis has been borne out by events more completely than that of any rival. The long rise in the price of housing is a rise in the price of land, the gains have gone to those who held it, and every subsidy and public improvement has tended to be absorbed into it. Its remedy has been enacted almost nowhere, because the unearned increment that once belonged to a small class of landlords is now the principal wealth of the majority of voters. Where communities have nonetheless found ways to keep land values in common, by taxation, by leasehold, by trusts or by conditions attached to the right to build, the results have generally vindicated the principle, subject to the important qualification that a public landowner seeking revenue can behave exactly like a private one.

Beyond Europe and North America, the developmental states of East Asia showed that outcomes depend less on whether the state or the market builds than on the rules governing land, credit and resale. Singapore and Hong Kong both have public land and large public housing sectors, with opposite results. Latin America showed that people excluded from the formal city will build their own, that what they most need from government is security, services and a decent location, and that mass programmes of finished housing repeatedly sacrifice location to numbers. Its jurists gave the world the doctrine that property carries a social function. Africa and South Asia showed each ideology at its limit: the unregulated market in the rental shack, the overreaching state in the land ceiling and the frozen rent, and the capacity of organised residents to do what neither had managed.

No tradition, then, emerges from the record as simply correct, and none as simply mistaken. Each grasped something true about housing and built a programme on it, and each programme, pursued in isolation and to its conclusion, ran into the truths that the others had grasped.

#### The enduring argument

Several patterns recur so often in this history that they may be offered as general findings.

The first is the frequency with which housing policies have produced the opposite of what their authors intended. Communist governments that abolished private property in housing left behind nations of homeowners. Conservative governments that set out to spread ownership made it harder for the next generation to attain. Rent controls introduced to protect the poor helped to create the informal settlements in which the poor of Mumbai and Cairo now live. Subsidies for protected housing in Spain built private fortunes and no public stock. A land law intended to lower Spanish prices accompanied their tripling. Public housing designed to end the slum became, in St Louis and the suburbs of Paris, a new kind of slum. Housing is a slow system. Buildings stand for a century, households move rarely, and the effects of a decision may not be visible for a generation, by which time those who made it have gone and its beneficiaries have become an interest that defends it. Anyone proposing a reform would do well to ask what the measure will look like in forty years and who will then have a stake in keeping it.

The second is the centrality of land. In every tradition and on every continent, the decisive question proved to be who captures the value of location. Subsidies to buyers and to tenants leaked into it. Public builders were defeated by it or succeeded by escaping it. The places that have housed their people best over long periods, however different their ideologies, each found some way to prevent the gains from urban growth from being taken privately: Vienna and Singapore by public ownership, Zurich by leasing to cooperatives, Tokyo by permitting so much building that location lost its scarcity. The places that have fared worst are those in which land became the principal store of private wealth.

The third is the importance of continuity. The admired systems were built over fifty or a hundred years by institutions that kept to a course through changes of government and fashion. The failures were, very often, the result of doing a great deal in a short time and then stopping, or of dismantling in one decade what had been assembled in the previous five. Housing rewards patience and punishes both the crash programme and the fire sale.

The fourth is the division between insiders and outsiders, which appears under every system. The sitting tenant with a controlled rent, the council tenant with a right to buy, the family with a place in the Stockholm queue, the owner who bought before the boom, the Soviet official with a flat in the centre and the lessee of public land in Canberra all occupy the same position. They hold something valuable that was distributed at a particular moment on particular terms, and they have every reason to defend it against those who came later. Each ideology, when it succeeds, creates a class of beneficiaries, and that class then resists the extension of the benefit. A just housing policy has to be judged by how it treats the person who is not yet housed, since that person is the one with no voice in the matter.

The fifth concerns the relation between the two views with which the book began. It is tempting to suppose that a society must choose whether a house is an asset or a home, and that the ideologies represent rival choices. The history suggests that the two cannot be separated so cleanly. A dwelling that a family owns will be its largest possession whatever the law calls it, and families will care about its value. A dwelling that nobody can live without will never be treated by the public as a mere investment, whatever the market prefers, and when the gap between the two becomes too wide, people take to the streets, as they did in Glasgow in 1915, in Buenos Aires in 1907 and in Madrid in 2024. What societies actually choose is a settlement between these two facts: how far the home may serve as a vehicle for accumulation, and what is to be protected from that use.

The settlements that have lasted share a recognisable shape. They allow people the security and control that the conservative tradition rightly prizes, whether as owners or as tenants with rights strong enough to make renting a permanent home. They let the market build, and do not forbid it to, because scarcity serves only those who already own. They maintain alongside the market a substantial stock of housing whose price does not rise with the price of land, so that those who cannot buy are not left to bid against those who can. They restrain the use of credit to inflate what already exists. And they return to the community, by one means or another, at least a part of the value that the community creates. None of these elements belongs to a single ideology. Each tradition supplied one of them and tended to neglect the rest.

There is a harder point, which every government knows and few state. In countries where most households own their homes and hold most of their wealth in them, housing cannot be made substantially cheaper without making the majority poorer, at least on paper. The conflict is not between a propertied few and everyone else, as the reformers of the nineteenth century believed. It runs between two large groups of ordinary people, those who own and those who do not, and very often between parents and their own children. A decline in the real price of housing spread gently over many years, with incomes rising faster than prices, would reconcile the two as far as they can be reconciled. It requires building steadily, taxing land sensibly and resisting each call to prop up values, and it asks the present generation of owners to accept that their homes should cease to enrich them. Whether democracies are capable of that restraint is the practical question on which the future of housing in the rich countries depends.

Beyond the rich countries the question is larger and plainer. Two and a half billion more people will live in cities by the middle of the century, most of them in Africa and Asia, and most of them will house themselves on whatever ground they can obtain. Whether that ground is safe, serviced and secure, or steep, flooded and liable to be cleared, will be decided by choices about land that are being made now, and the old argument over who has the right to occupy a place will be conducted there on a scale that dwarfs anything in this book.

The argument is as old as the first cities. The Babylonian king who cancelled debts and restored houses to their families, the legislator of Leviticus who let town houses be sold for ever and required fields to return, the tribune who tried to limit the estates of the rich, the settlers who marked out plots on the desert outside Lima overnight and the mothers who stood in a doorway in Barcelona to stop an eviction were all engaged in it. It will not be settled, because it arises from what a home is: the one possession that is at the same time a necessity of life, a piece of the earth, a place in a community and a store of wealth. Each generation has to decide for itself how those claims are to be weighed against one another. It will decide better if it knows what the alternatives are, where each came from, what each achieved and what each cost, and to supply that knowledge has been the purpose of this book.
